SIE Program

The SIE Study Guide

Published with Day 1 · 413 min read

Everything the 30-day program teaches, in one place and a little less detail — a chapter for each day, built to be worked through alongside the daily editions and kept for the rest of your career.

How to use this guide

Each teaching day of the program has a chapter here with the same number. Read the chapter before the day's edition to see the shape of the topic, or after it to consolidate; the edition goes deeper, adapts to your weak spots and carries the graded quiz. The Check-yourself questions at the end of each chapter hide their answers until you tap them — answer first, then open.

Every chapter separates four kinds of knowledge, always with the same labels: Understand (concepts to reason from), MEMORIZE (facts that cannot be derived), Exam traps (how the SIE baits the wrong answer) and Trader connection (why it matters on a trading, hedging or financing desk). Where a rule is simply an arbitrary exam number, the text says so.

The structure follows FINRA's official SIE Content Outline. Where a third-party prep convention disagrees with a FINRA or SEC rule, the rule is taught and the convention is named as a trap. Figures are current as of September 2026; rules that changed recently are marked, with the old number that many books still print.

The exam
  • 75 scored questions plus 5 unscored pretest items; 1 hour 45 minutes; passing score 70.
  • Section 1 Capital Markets 16% (12) · Section 2 Products and Their Risks 44% (33) · Section 3 Trading, Customer Accounts and Prohibited Activities 31% (23) · Section 4 Regulatory Framework 9% (7).
  • Valid for four years; no firm sponsorship needed.

Part I · Teaching days

Day 1 · Who's who: the machinery of the securities industry

FINRA 1.1, 1.2, 4 · pairs with the Day 1 edition (~95 min)

Before any product or rule makes sense you need the map: who issues, who stands between buyer and seller, where a trade goes after the fill, and which regulator watches each step. The SIE tests this map with vocabulary questions that look trivial and are easy to miss; on a desk the same map tells you who holds your securities, who owes you money, and who fails if something breaks.

Two markets, two cash flows

The single most useful distinction on the exam is primary market versus secondary market, and the cleanest way to hold it is to follow the cash.

In the primary market an issuer (a corporation, a municipality, the Treasury) sells new securities to raise capital. An underwriter (an investment bank acting as a broker-dealer) buys the securities or arranges their sale, and the investor's cash ends up with the issuer, minus the underwriter's compensation (the spread). This is the only time the issuer gets money from its own securities.

PRIMARY
Issuer --(new shares)--> Underwriter --(shares)--> Investor
Issuer <--(cash - spread)-- Underwriter <--(cash)-- Investor

In the secondary market investors trade already-issued securities. A broker-dealer (BD: a firm registered to act for customers and/or its own account) sits in the middle; cash goes from buyer to seller and the issuer receives nothing.

SECONDARY
Seller --(shares)--> BD / venue --(shares)--> Buyer
Seller <--(cash)---- BD / venue <--(cash)---- Buyer
Issuer: not in the chain, receives $0

Issuers still care: investors pay more for new securities they can resell, so secondary liquidity lowers the primary cost of capital.

Broker versus dealer: capacity decides the pay

Every trade a BD does for a customer is done in one of two capacities, and the capacity decides how the firm is paid and what risk it carries.

Broker (agent)Dealer (principal)
Acts forThe customer, finding the other sideItself, as the other side
Paid byCommissionMarkup (customer buys) or markdown (customer sells)
Inventory riskNone; never owns the securityYes; owns it, even if briefly

A firm can be a broker on one trade and a dealer on the next, but never both in the same trade: no commission plus markup on one execution. A markup is the amount a dealer adds above the prevailing market price when it sells to a customer; a markdown is the amount it deducts below the prevailing market price when it buys from a customer. OTC (over the counter) simply means "not on an exchange": a negotiated market where dealers quote and trade with each other and with customers.

A hybrid: the riskless principal trade. The firm gets a customer buy order, buys the shares for its own account, and immediately sells them to the customer at a marked-up price. Economically agency, legally principal, so the pay is a markup.

Markup arithmetic

The markup is measured against the prevailing market price (PMP): for an active stock, generally the inside market (the best offer when the customer buys), usually evidenced by the dealer's contemporaneous cost. Not the dealer's stale inventory cost, and not the customer's price.

Markup % = (Price to customer − Prevailing market price) ÷ Prevailing market price

Price to customer
the net price on the customer's purchase, in dollars per share or per bond
Prevailing market price
the current inside market for the security, the benchmark the markup is measured from
Markdown %
the mirror image when the customer sells: (PMP − price paid to customer) ÷ PMP
Worked example
  1. Inside market in XYZ: 39.95 bid – 40.00 ask. The customer buys 1,000 shares from the dealer at a net price of 40.60.
  2. PMP for a customer purchase = the offer, 40.00.
  3. Markup per share = 40.60 − 40.00 = 0.60. Total = 0.60 × 1,000 = $600.
  4. Markup % = 0.60 ÷ 40.00 = 0.015 = 1.5%. (Dividing by 40.60 would give 1.48%, the classic wrong denominator.)
  5. Same order done as agent: customer pays 40.00 × 1,000 = $40,000 plus a separate commission, say $60.

FINRA's fair-pricing rule (Rule 2121) carries the long-standing 5% markup policy, which is a guideline, not a ceiling: a markup under 5% can still be unfair and one above it can be fair, depending on the security, the price, the size and the dealer's cost of service. It covers principal and riskless principal trades, not securities sold at a fixed offering price under a prospectus (such as new issues or mutual fund shares). Just memorize "5% is a guideline."

What the confirmation shows

A confirmation is the written trade notice a BD must send a customer at or before completion of the transaction (settlement). It states the capacity. On an agency trade the commission appears as its own line, because the customer paid the market price plus a fee to an agent and is entitled to see the fee. On a principal trade the confirmation shows a single net price, with the markup built in; the markup is broken out only in specific cases (for example riskless principal equity trades, and retail bond trades a dealer offset the same day). For the SIE: commission is disclosed on the confirmation; a markup is part of the net price.

Market makers: selling immediacy

A market maker is a dealer that continuously quotes a bid (the price at which it will buy) and an ask or offer (the price at which it will sell) in a security, and stands ready to trade at those prices. The gap is the spread. On the NYSE the equivalent role on the floor is the designated market maker (DMM).

Why they exist: natural buyers and sellers rarely arrive at the same second. The market maker fills the gap by taking the other side now and laying the position off later. The customer pays the spread for immediacy; the market maker earns it for carrying risk. Two risks sit on its book:

  • Inventory risk: the stock it just bought can fall before it sells it.
  • Adverse selection: the counterparty who hits its bid may know something; informed flow systematically loses the market maker money.

More volatile, less liquid or more information-heavy stocks trade wider.

The four markets: a venue × intermediary map

The SIE sorts trading into four "markets." Treat them as a grid of what is being traded and where/how.

MarketWhat tradesWhere / how
FirstExchange-listed securitiesOn the exchange (auction market)
SecondUnlisted (non-exchange) stocksOTC, through dealers (negotiated market)
ThirdExchange-listed securitiesOTC, off the exchange, through BDs (often to institutions)
FourthAny securitiesInstitution to institution directly, with no BD in between (ATS/ECN)

An ATS (alternative trading system) is a non-exchange electronic venue registered as a BD; an ECN (electronic communication network) is a type of ATS that matches orders electronically. Use the SIE definition of the second market given here even if you have seen it defined elsewhere as "the whole secondary market." Just memorize The pairing that gets tested: third = listed security, OTC venue.

The life of a trade, end to end

A fill is the start of the process, not the end. The chain below is the one to be able to reproduce in order.

1 Customer --(order)--> BD
2 BD --(route)--> venue: exchange, ATS or market maker
3 Venue --(execution report)--> BD --(confirm)--> Customer
4 Trade report --> consolidated tape / FINRA TRF
5 Clearing firm --(trade data)--> NSCC
    NSCC steps in as buyer to every seller and
    seller to every buyer; nets each member's trades
6 T+1: DTC moves shares by book entry, cash moves
7 DTC (nominee Cede & Co.) --> clearing firm --> you
8 Transfer agent: Cede & Co. = registered holder
  • Trade reporting: the print that makes the price public. Exchange trades hit the consolidated tape through the exchange; off-exchange stock trades are reported through a FINRA Trade Reporting Facility (TRF); corporate bonds go to TRACE; municipal trades go to the MSRB's RTRS within 15 minutes.
  • Clearing: confirming the terms, calculating who owes what, and guaranteeing it. The NSCC (National Securities Clearing Corporation) is the central counterparty (CCP) for US stock, corporate bond and muni trades. Through novation it replaces the original contract with two new ones, becoming buyer to every seller and seller to every buyer, then nets each member's trades into one obligation per security per day (continuous net settlement).
  • Settlement: the actual exchange of securities for cash, T+1 for regular-way stock trades. The DTC (Depository Trust Company) is the central securities depository: it holds the securities in immobilised form and settles by book entry, moving positions between participant accounts. No certificates move.
  • Custody: safekeeping plus ownership records, at each level: DTC for its participants, the clearing firm for its customers.

Street name means the securities are registered in the name of the BD (and, through DTC, of Cede & Co.) rather than the customer. You are the beneficial owner; the chain of book entries is what proves it. Street name is what makes fast settlement, margin and lending possible.

Four back-office jobs the exam likes to confuse

PartyWorks forJob
Transfer agentIssuerKeeps the list of registered owners; issues and cancels certificates; records transfers; often distributes dividends and mails proxies
RegistrarIssuerAudits the transfer agent; makes sure shares outstanding never exceed shares authorized
CustodianAsset owner (a fund, a client)Holds and safeguards the assets; for a mutual fund, a bank custodian holds the portfolio
Clearing (carrying) firmOther BDs and their customersClears and settles trades, holds customer accounts and cash, extends margin, sends statements

An introducing broker keeps the customer relationship but passes clearing and custody to a clearing firm, which is why a statement can carry a different firm's name.

The regulator cast list

US securities regulation is layered. Congress passes laws; the SEC (Securities and Exchange Commission), a federal agency, writes rules under them and oversees everything below it. Below the SEC sit SROs (self-regulatory organizations): industry bodies that write and enforce rules for their members, subject to SEC approval.

Congress --(laws)--> SEC
SEC --(oversees)--> FINRA, MSRB, exchanges (SROs)
FINRA --(rules, exams, discipline)--> member BDs, reps
Fed --(credit rules: Reg T, Reg U)--> BDs, banks
Treasury --> FinCEN (AML), OFAC (sanctions)
States --(blue-sky laws)--> BDs, agents in the state
BodyWhat it isWhat to know
SECFederal agency, created by the '34 ActTop securities regulator; oversees SROs; enforces federal securities law
FINRASRO for broker-dealersFormed 2007 from the NASD and NYSE member regulation; writes and enforces rules for member firms and their registered people; overseen by the SEC
MSRBSRO for the municipal marketCreated 1975; writes muni rules but does not enforce them (FINRA, the SEC and bank regulators do)
ExchangesSROs (NYSE, Nasdaq, Cboe and others)Regulate their own members and listings
Federal ReserveCentral bankMonetary policy; securities credit: Reg T (credit extended by BDs), Reg U (bank loans on securities)
Treasury: FinCENFinancial Crimes Enforcement NetworkAnti-money-laundering rules under the Bank Secrecy Act; receives CTRs and SARs
Treasury: OFACOffice of Foreign Assets ControlSanctions; the SDN list firms must screen against
SIPCNonprofit membership corporationProtects customers of a failed BD: $500,000 per separate customer capacity, of which at most $250,000 cash
FDICFederal bank deposit insurer$250,000 per depositor, per insured bank, per ownership category
State administratorsEach state's securities regulatorEnforce the state's blue-sky law (Uniform Securities Act); NASAA writes model rules but enforces nothing

Futures are a separate world: the CFTC and the NFA regulate them, which is one reason SIPC does not cover them.

SIPC is not insurance against loss

SIPC steps in only when a BD fails and customer assets are missing; never for market losses or bad advice. A "separate customer capacity" is a distinct ownership role: one person's individual, joint and IRA accounts are three capacities, each with its own limit. Not covered: commodities and futures, currency, and fixed annuities. The $250,000 cash sub-limit sits inside the $500,000, not on top of it.

Worked example
  1. A failed BD owes a customer (one individual account) $420,000 in securities and $150,000 in cash. Claim = $570,000.
  2. Securities are protected first against the $500,000 ceiling: $420,000 covered, $80,000 of ceiling left.
  3. Cash is capped at the lesser of $250,000 and the remaining ceiling: $80,000 covered, $70,000 not covered by SIPC.
  4. SIPC protection = $500,000. The $70,000 is a general creditor claim against the firm's estate (plus any excess-SIPC policy the firm carries).

The eight laws in one sentence each

LawYearWhat it doesGoverns
Securities Act1933New issues must be registered and sold with a prospectus disclosing the facts; antifraud appliesPrimary market
Securities Exchange Act1934Created the SEC; registers exchanges, BDs and reps; gives the Fed margin authority; covers insider trading, proxies, short sales and net capitalSecondary market
Trust Indenture Act1939Publicly offered corporate bonds need an indenture with an independent trusteePrimary (corporate debt)
Investment Company Act1940Regulates investment companies: mutual funds, closed-end funds, UITs, face-amount companiesThe fund as an issuer and business
Investment Advisers Act1940Regulates investment advisers and imposes a fiduciary dutyAdvice for a fee
Securities Investor Protection Act1970Created SIPCCustomer protection when a BD fails
Bank Secrecy Act1970AML recordkeeping and reporting: CTRs and SARsCash and suspicious activity, all markets
USA PATRIOT Act2001Title III: AML programs and Customer Identification Programs for BDsAccount opening and AML

The '33 Act regulates the product at birth; the '34 Act regulates the marketplace and its participants afterward. The SEC did not exist until 1934 (the FTC administered the '33 Act for its first year), which is the source of a favourite trap.

Understand
  • Primary market: investor cash → underwriter → issuer (minus spread). Secondary market: buyer cash → seller; the issuer gets nothing.
  • Broker = agent, commission, no inventory risk. Dealer = principal, markup/markdown, carries inventory. Never both on one trade.
  • Order → venue → trade report → clearing at NSCC (novation, netting) → settlement at DTC (book entry, T+1) → custody in street name → transfer agent records Cede & Co.
  • First/second/third/fourth markets = listed or unlisted × exchange, OTC dealer, or direct institution-to-institution.
  • SEC over SROs (FINRA, MSRB, exchanges); Fed for credit; FinCEN and OFAC at Treasury; SIPC for failed BDs, FDIC for banks; states enforce blue-sky laws. '33 = primary, '34 = secondary.
MEMORIZE:
  • Broker → agent, commission, no inventory
  • Dealer → principal, markup/markdown, inventory risk
  • First market → listed securities on an exchange
  • Second market → unlisted stocks OTC
  • Third market → listed securities traded OTC
  • Fourth market → institution to institution, no BD (ATS/ECN)
  • Securities Act → 1933 → new issues, registration, prospectus
  • Securities Exchange Act → 1934 → created SEC; secondary market, BDs, margin, insiders
  • Trust Indenture Act → 1939 → corporate bond indenture + trustee
  • Investment Company Act → 1940 → funds
  • Investment Advisers Act → 1940 → advisers, fiduciary duty
  • SIPA → 1970 → created SIPC
  • Bank Secrecy Act → 1970 → CTRs, SARs
  • USA PATRIOT Act → 2001 → AML programs, CIP
  • SIPC → $500,000 per customer capacity, max $250,000 cash
  • FDIC → $250,000 per depositor, per bank, per ownership category
  • MSRB → writes muni rules, does not enforce
  • FINRA → formed 2007 (NASD + NYSE member regulation)
  • Markup % → (customer price − PMP) ÷ PMP
  • 5% markup policy → guideline, not a rule
Exam traps
  • "The firm acted as broker and dealer on the trade" → impossible; one capacity per trade, so commission OR markup.
  • "Third market = OTC stocks" → third market is a listed security traded OTC; the fourth market is institution-to-institution with no BD (ATS/ECN); unlisted stocks OTC is the second market.
  • "SIPC insures against market losses" → no; it protects custody when a BD fails, and it excludes commodities/futures, currency and fixed annuities.
  • "SIPC covers $500,000 cash" → cash is capped at $250,000 inside the $500,000.
  • "The '33 Act created the SEC" → the '34 Act did.
  • "The MSRB enforces its rules" → FINRA, the SEC and bank regulators enforce them.
  • "NASAA enforces the Uniform Securities Act" → each state's administrator does; NASAA writes model rules.
  • Custodian vs transfer agent vs registrar vs clearing firm → safekeeping vs owner records vs share-count audit vs clearing/settlement and customer accounts.
  • Markup computed on the customer's price or old inventory cost → measured from the prevailing market price.
Trader connection

Dealer P&L = spread capture (half the spread on each fill) − adverse selection (the move against it after informed flow trades) − inventory drift (mark-to-market on what it could not lay off). Skewing quotes when long, or hedging with futures and ETFs, is about keeping the first line and shrinking the other two.

Clearing as risk compression. Without a CCP, 5,000 trades against 200 counterparties means 200 bilateral credit exposures. Novation to the NSCC (stocks) or the OCC (listed options) collapses them into one netted exposure to a margined CCP, the same logic behind mandatory clearing of standardized swaps.

Where your securities sit. A long position in a prime brokerage account is a book entry at your prime broker, which holds it through its account at DTC, whose nominee Cede & Co. is on the issuer's register. When you lend or pledge securities, or your prime broker rehypothecates them against your margin debit, the position moves along that chain. Knowing the chain tells you whose failure you are exposed to.

Real-market places you will meet Day 1: the trading desk (capacity, markups, quoting), operations and settlements (NSCC, DTC, breaks and fails), prime brokerage (custody, financing, stock loan) and compliance (fair pricing, confirmations, who regulates what).

Check yourself

Q1 Which of the following is most likely to earn a commission?
A. A market maker selling from its inventory
B. A broker-dealer executing a customer order on an exchange as agent
C. A firm filling a customer order as riskless principal
D. An underwriter selling shares it bought in a firm-commitment offering

Answer: B. An agent finds the other side and is paid a commission. A market maker selling from inventory (A) is a principal and earns a markup. Riskless principal (C) is tempting because it looks like agency, but legally it is a principal trade, so the pay is a markup. An underwriter in a firm commitment (D) owns the shares and earns the spread.

Q2 The inside market in a stock is 49.90 – 50.00. A dealer sells 200 shares to a customer at a net price of 51.50. What is the markup percentage?
A. 2.91%
B. 1.50%
C. 3.21%
D. 3.00%

Answer: D. For a customer purchase, the prevailing market price is the offer, 50.00. Markup = 51.50 − 50.00 = 1.50; 1.50 ÷ 50.00 = 3.00%. A (2.91%) divides by the customer's price, 51.50. B confuses the dollar markup with a percentage. C (3.21%) measures from the bid, 49.90, which is the benchmark for a customer sale, not a purchase.

Q3 A pension fund buys 200,000 shares of an NYSE-listed stock from a broker-dealer that fills the order off the exchange. This trade took place in the:
A. third market
B. second market
C. fourth market
D. first market

Answer: A. A listed security traded OTC through a BD is the third market. The first market (D) would be on the exchange itself. The second market (B) is unlisted stock OTC. The fourth market (C) is tempting because an institution is involved, but it requires institution-to-institution trading with no BD in between.

Q4 SIPC protection covers all of the following held at a failed broker-dealer EXCEPT:
A. common stock held in street name
B. $40,000 of cash awaiting investment
C. futures contracts
D. Treasury bonds held for the customer

Answer: C. SIPC protects securities and cash held for customers of a failed BD. Commodities and futures are excluded (they sit under CFTC regulation), as are currency and fixed annuities. Street-name stock (A), cash within the $250,000 sub-limit (B) and Treasuries held for the customer (D) are all covered.

Q5 Which statements are true?
I. The MSRB writes rules for the municipal securities market
II. The MSRB enforces its rules against municipal dealers
III. FINRA was formed in 2007
IV. The Securities Act of 1933 created the SEC
A. I and II
B. II and IV
C. I and III
D. III and IV

Answer: C. The MSRB writes muni rules (I) but does not enforce them; FINRA, the SEC and bank regulators do, so II is false. FINRA formed in 2007 from the NASD and NYSE member regulation (III). The SEC was created by the Securities Exchange Act of 1934, not the '33 Act, so IV is false.

Q6 Which party keeps the issuer's list of registered shareholders and cancels old certificates and issues new ones on transfer?
A. Transfer agent
B. Registrar
C. Custodian
D. Clearing firm

Answer: A. That is the transfer agent's job. The registrar (B) is the neighbouring concept: it audits the transfer agent and makes sure outstanding shares never exceed authorized shares. A custodian (C) safeguards assets for their owner. A clearing firm (D) clears and settles trades and carries customer accounts.

Q7 Place these steps in the life of a stock trade in order:
I. Clearing and netting at the NSCC
II. Execution at a trading venue
III. Settlement by book entry at DTC
IV. Trade report to the tape
A. II, I, IV, III
B. II, IV, I, III
C. IV, II, I, III
D. II, I, III, IV

Answer: B. A trade must be executed before it can be reported (so C is wrong), reported promptly after execution, then cleared (novation and netting at the NSCC), and finally settled at DTC on T+1. A and D put clearing ahead of the trade report, which happens within seconds of execution; D also puts reporting after settlement.

Q8 A customer's individual account at a failed broker-dealer holds $400,000 of securities and $300,000 of cash. What is the maximum SIPC protection?
A. $650,000
B. $700,000
C. $250,000
D. $500,000

Answer: D. SIPC protects up to $500,000 per separate customer capacity, of which no more than $250,000 may be cash. Securities use $400,000 of the limit; only $100,000 remains, so cash coverage is $100,000; total $500,000. A adds the full $250,000 cash cap to the securities, which ignores the overall ceiling. B is the whole claim. C is the cash sub-limit alone. The uncovered $200,000 becomes a claim against the firm's estate.

Day 2 · Economics, the Fed and how macro moves prices

FINRA 1.3 · pairs with the Day 2 edition (~100 min)

The SIE's economics questions are mostly vocabulary and direction: which indicator leads, which tool eases, which way a curve slopes. On a desk the same material is the transmission mechanism: how a decision by twelve voters at the Fed turns into a move in bond prices, credit spreads, the dollar and equity multiples. Learn the direction of every arrow and the exam questions answer themselves.

The business cycle

Economic activity rises and falls around a long-run trend in four phases. The SIE wants the names, the order and what each phase does to the things you trade.

expansion --> peak --> contraction --> trough --> expansion
PhaseActivityEarningsCredit spreadsRates / Fed
ExpansionOutput, jobs, spending risingRisingTighteningDrifting up; Fed leans against inflation
PeakGrowth at its high; capacity strainedHigh, growth slowingTightest, starting to widenHigh; Fed tight; curve flattens or inverts
ContractionOutput and jobs fallingFallingWidening; defaults riseFed cuts; short yields fall
TroughBottom; activity stops fallingBottomingWidest, then tighteningLow; curve steep

A credit spread is the extra yield a corporate bond pays over a Treasury of the same maturity; it widens when investors fear defaults. A recession, by the exam's convention, is two consecutive quarters of declining real GDP. The official call in the US is made by the NBER (National Bureau of Economic Research), which looks at a wider set of data and often announces months after the fact. Just memorize Both halves: the two-quarter convention and the NBER as the official judge.

Industries react differently. Cyclical stocks (autos, housing, capital goods, travel) swing with the cycle. Defensive stocks (consumer staples, utilities, health care) hold up in contractions because people buy food, power and medicine regardless. Growth companies grow earnings faster than the economy in most phases.

Measuring the economy: GDP and prices

GDP (gross domestic product) is the value of all final goods and services produced in the country in a period. Nominal GDP is measured at current prices; real GDP strips out price changes, so it measures volume. If nominal GDP rose 5% and prices rose 3%, real output grew about 2%. Recessions are defined on real GDP because nominal GDP can rise during inflation while the economy is actually shrinking.

The CPI (Consumer Price Index) measures the price of a basket of goods and services bought by urban consumers. Three words describe it, and the exam separates them by level versus rate of change:

TermPrice levelRate of changeExample CPI path (year-over-year)
InflationRisingPositive+3%, +3%, +4%
DisinflationStill risingPositive but falling+6%, +4%, +2.5%
DeflationFallingNegative+1%, −0.5%, −1%

Disinflation is the one people get wrong: prices are still going up, just more slowly. Deflation is dangerous because it raises the real burden of debt and encourages people to delay purchases. Stagflation is high inflation combined with stagnant growth and rising unemployment. The Fed's stated goal is 2% inflation over time, and its dual mandate is maximum employment and stable prices.

Leading, coincident and lagging indicators

Indicators are grouped by when they turn relative to the cycle. The logic behind the grouping is almost always "who has to decide before whom."

GroupIndicatorsWhy they sit there
Leading (turn before the economy)Building permits; stock prices; new orders; average weekly initial jobless claims; average manufacturing workweek; consumer expectations; yield-curve spreadThey record decisions and expectations: a permit comes before construction, an order before production, overtime is cut before workers are laid off, stock prices discount future earnings
Coincident (turn with it)Nonfarm payrolls; industrial production; personal income less transfer payments; manufacturing and trade salesThey measure activity happening now
Lagging (turn after it)Average duration of unemployment; CPI for services; prime rate; commercial and industrial loans outstanding; labor cost per unit of output; consumer credit-to-income; inventories-to-salesThey respond to what already happened: unemployment spells lengthen after layoffs, the prime rate follows the Fed, inventories pile up after sales drop, service prices are sticky

The tested pairs: stock prices lead, and CPI and duration of unemployment lag. Initial jobless claims (new filings) lead; duration of unemployment (how long people stay jobless) lags. Same topic, opposite category, which is exactly why it is tested.

Two levers: monetary and fiscal policy

Monetary policyFiscal policy
WhoThe Federal ReserveCongress and the President
ControlsMoney supply, bank reserves, short-term interest rates, creditTaxes and government spending
StimulateCut rates, buy securities (add reserves)Cut taxes, raise spending
RestrainRaise rates, sell securities (drain reserves)Raise taxes, cut spending
SpeedDecided at a meeting, effective immediately in marketsRequires legislation

Fiscal deficits are financed by Treasury borrowing, which is why a large deficit shows up as heavy auction supply in the Treasury market. That is Treasury's business; the Fed does not set tax or spending policy, and the exam will offer "the Fed raises taxes" as a distractor.

The Fed's toolkit

The FOMC (Federal Open Market Committee) sets monetary policy. It has twelve voting members (the seven Fed governors, the president of the New York Fed and four rotating Reserve Bank presidents) and meets 8 times a year. Its main target is the federal funds rate, the overnight rate at which banks lend reserves to each other.

Open-market operations (OMO): the most used tool

The Fed buys or sells Treasury (and agency) securities in the open market. The key is what happens to bank reserves, the balances banks hold at the Fed.

EASING: Fed BUYS securities
Fed --(new reserves / cash)--> banks, dealers
banks --(securities)--> Fed
reserves up --> money supply up --> short rates down

TIGHTENING: Fed SELLS securities
banks --(reserves / cash)--> Fed
reserves down --> money supply down --> short rates up

Direction check that the exam loves: buying = easing (the Fed pays with newly created reserves), selling = tightening (the Fed absorbs reserves). The same logic runs at scale in quantitative easing and its reverse, balance-sheet runoff.

The other tools

  • Discount rate: the rate the Fed charges banks that borrow directly from it at the discount window. Raising it tightens; lowering it eases. Proposed by the regional Reserve Banks and approved by the Board of Governors.
  • Reserve requirements: the share of deposits banks must hold as reserves. Raising it tightens (less to lend). Set to 0% since March 2020, so today it is a tool on paper only.
  • Interest on reserve balances (IORB): the rate the Fed pays banks on reserves held at the Fed. No bank lends reserves overnight for much less than it can earn risk-free at the Fed, so raising IORB pulls the fed funds rate up. In practice this is how the Fed now holds the rate inside its target range.
  • Regulation T (exam convention): the SIE lists changing Reg T margin as a Fed tool. Reg T sets how much credit BDs may extend to customers buying securities; it has been 50% since 1974. In reality it is a credit control, not a monetary-policy lever, but accept it on the exam.

Just memorize Fed tools as the SIE lists them: open-market operations (most used), discount rate, reserve requirements, Reg T. Know IORB as the modern addition.

The rate ladder

RateWho lends to whomSet by
Federal funds rateBank to bank, overnight, unsecured reservesMarket rate; the FOMC sets a target range
Discount rateFed to banks (discount window)The Fed; sits above fed funds
Prime rateBanks to their best corporate customersBanks; follows fed funds (a lagging indicator)
Broker call rateBanks to BDs, secured by customer margin securitiesBanks; the base for customer margin interest

Fed funds is below the discount rate by design: the discount window is a backstop, not a cheap funding source. The broker call rate matters to anyone with a margin account, because the BD's cost of financing your debit balance is what your margin rate is built on.

From a Fed decision to every price you watch

Prep material sometimes says "interest rates and inflation are negatively correlated." That is loose. The Fed raises rates in response to inflation, so in the data rates and inflation often rise together. The causal lever the exam wants is: higher rates are used to bring inflation down, by making borrowing more expensive and cooling demand.

Fed hikes
 --> short-term yields up
 --> existing bond prices down (fixed coupons now below market)
 --> borrowing costs up for firms and households
 --> demand cools --> inflation eases (with a lag)
 --> equity multiples compress (higher discount rate)
 --> dollar tends to firm (higher USD yields draw capital)

The bond step is the see-saw: a bond paying a fixed 4% coupon is worth less when new bonds pay 5%, so its price must fall until its yield matches the market. Longer maturities fall more because the below-market coupon is locked in for longer.

Real versus nominal

The nominal rate is the stated rate. The real rate is what you earn after inflation erodes your purchasing power. Investors, and the Fed, care about the real rate because it is what actually changes behaviour.

Real rate ≈ Nominal rate − Inflation rate

Nominal rate
the quoted yield or interest rate
Inflation rate
the expected (or measured) rise in the price level, usually CPI year-over-year
Real rate
the gain in purchasing power; the approximation is fine for the SIE
Worked example
  1. A 1-year Treasury yields 4.5%; inflation is running at 3.0%.
  2. Real rate ≈ 4.5% − 3.0% = 1.5%: the policy is restrictive in real terms.
  3. If inflation instead ran at 6.0%: 4.5% − 6.0% = −1.5%. A negative real rate: savers lose purchasing power, borrowing is effectively subsidised, policy is still easy even though the nominal rate looks high.

The yield curve

The yield curve plots Treasury yields against maturity. Three shapes are tested:

ShapeSlopeUsual reading
Normal (positive)Long rates above short ratesInvestors demand more to lend longer; expansion expected
Inverted (negative)Short rates above long ratesFed is tight now; markets expect cuts and slower growth; recession signal
FlatShort and long rates about equalTransition, often late cycle

Why inversion predicts recession: short yields are pinned by today's policy rate, which is high because the Fed is fighting inflation. Long yields are an average of expected future short rates plus a term premium; if the market expects the Fed to be cutting later because growth will slow, long yields fall below short ones. An inverted curve also squeezes banks, which borrow short and lend long, so credit tightens, which helps cause the slowdown it predicts. The 2s10s spread (10-year yield minus 2-year yield) is the most-watched measure; negative means inverted.

The dollar

Currency moves change the translated value of trade and earnings. For a stronger dollar:

USD strengthens
 --> imports cheaper for US buyers (helps importers)
 --> US exports dearer abroad (hurts exporters)
 --> foreign earnings translate into fewer dollars
 --> US tourists abroad gain buying power

A weaker dollar reverses every arrow. The SIE framing: a US multinational earning in euros reports lower dollar earnings when the dollar rises, even if its euro sales are unchanged.

Market structures

The SIE occasionally asks how competitive an industry is, because pricing power drives margins.

StructureSellersPricing powerExample
MonopolyOne; often regulatedHigh (unless regulated)Local electric or water utility
OligopolyA few; high barriers to entrySignificant; watch rivalsAirlines
Monopolistic competitionMany; differentiated productsSome, via brandBreakfast cereal
Perfect competitionMany; identical productsNone; price takersCommodity farm products
Understand
  • Expansion → peak → contraction → trough: earnings follow the cycle, credit spreads move against it, the Fed leans against it.
  • Real GDP measures volume; CPI measures price level. Inflation vs disinflation vs deflation is level vs rate of change.
  • Leading indicators record decisions and expectations; coincident ones measure now; lagging ones react.
  • Monetary = Fed (reserves, rates). Fiscal = Congress and President (taxes, spending).
  • Fed buys → reserves up → easing. Fed sells → reserves down → tightening. IORB sets the floor in practice.
  • Inverted curve = short above long = market expects cuts and slower growth.
  • Stronger dollar → cheaper imports, weaker exporters, fewer dollars from foreign earnings.
MEMORIZE:
  • Leading → building permits, stock prices, new orders, initial jobless claims, manufacturing workweek, consumer expectations, yield-curve spread
  • Coincident → nonfarm payrolls, industrial production, personal income less transfers, manufacturing and trade sales
  • Lagging → duration of unemployment, CPI for services, prime rate, C&I loans outstanding, labor cost per unit, consumer credit-to-income, inventories-to-sales
  • Fed tools → OMO (most used), discount rate, reserve requirements (0% since March 2020), IORB; exam also lists Reg T
  • Reg T → 50%, unchanged since 1974
  • FOMC → meets 8 times a year
  • Recession (exam) → two consecutive quarters of declining real GDP; NBER makes the official call
  • Fed funds rate → overnight bank-to-bank reserves
  • Discount rate → Fed lending to banks; above fed funds
  • Prime rate → banks' best corporate customers
  • Broker call rate → bank loans to BDs; basis for margin interest
  • Real rate → nominal − inflation
Exam traps
  • "The Fed cut taxes" → fiscal policy is Congress and the President, never the Fed.
  • "The Fed sold securities to ease" → selling drains reserves and tightens; buying eases.
  • "Stock prices are a lagging indicator" → they lead. CPI and duration of unemployment lag.
  • "Initial jobless claims lag" → initial claims lead; duration of unemployment lags.
  • "Inverted means long rates above short" → inverted is short above long.
  • "Disinflation means falling prices" → that is deflation; disinflation is slowing inflation.
  • "Rates and inflation are negatively correlated" → the lever is causal: the Fed raises rates to bring inflation down.
  • "Reserve requirements are the most-used tool" → OMO is.
Trader connection

Transmission. The Fed sets one overnight rate; everything else reprices off expectations of its path. Front-end Treasury yields and SOFR futures move on every data surprise that changes the expected number of hikes or cuts; the dollar follows rate differentials; equity multiples compress as the discount rate rises; commodities priced in dollars tend to soften when the dollar firms.

The 2s10s as a positioning signal. A bull steepener (2-year falling faster than 10-year) usually means the market is pricing cuts; a bear flattener (2-year rising faster) means hikes. Curve trades (long one tenor, short another, DV01-weighted) express a view on the Fed without betting on the overall level.

Why rates desks hedge with futures. The curve moves on Fed expectations, not just the level, so a desk hedges each part of its exposure where it lives: SOFR futures for the expected policy path over the next few years, Treasury futures (2-year, 5-year, 10-year, bond) for duration further out. That is cheaper, more liquid and more precise than trading cash bonds against every position.

Where you will meet Day 2 in practice: rates trading (curve and front-end views around FOMC meetings), macro and portfolio management (positioning for the phase of the cycle), and risk management (rate and FX sensitivities in stress scenarios).

Check yourself

Q1 Which of the following is a leading economic indicator?
A. CPI for services
B. Average duration of unemployment
C. Building permits
D. Industrial production

Answer: C. Building permits are issued before construction starts, so they turn before the economy. CPI for services (A) and duration of unemployment (B) are lagging: they react after the cycle has turned. Industrial production (D) is coincident: it measures output happening now.

Q2 If the FOMC directs the purchase of Treasury securities in the open market, the most likely result is:
A. bank reserves fall and short-term rates rise
B. bank reserves rise and short-term rates tend to fall
C. the discount rate rises automatically
D. reserve requirements fall

Answer: B. When the Fed buys, it pays with newly created reserves, so reserves and the money supply rise and short-term rates tend to fall: easing. A describes a sale (tightening), the reversed relationship. C and D name other tools that are set separately; an open-market purchase does not change them.

Q3 All of the following are tools of the Federal Reserve EXCEPT:
A. changing the discount rate
B. changing reserve requirements
C. open-market operations
D. changing federal income tax rates

Answer: D. Tax rates are fiscal policy, set by Congress and the President. The discount rate (A), reserve requirements (B) and open-market operations (C) are Fed tools; OMO is the most used.

Q4 A customer buys a 1-year note yielding 5.2% when inflation is running at 3.4%. The approximate real rate of return is:
A. 1.8%
B. 8.6%
C. 3.4%
D. 5.2%

Answer: A. Real rate ≈ nominal − inflation = 5.2% − 3.4% = 1.8%. B adds inflation instead of subtracting it. C is the inflation rate and D the nominal rate: each is one input, not the answer.

Q5 Which statements about an inverted yield curve are true?
I. Short-term yields are above long-term yields
II. Long-term yields are above short-term yields
III. It is commonly read as a signal of a coming recession
IV. It usually means the market expects the Fed to keep raising rates for years
A. I and IV
B. II and III
C. II and IV
D. I and III

Answer: D. Inverted means short above long (I), and it is read as a recession signal (III). II describes a normal curve. IV is backwards: long yields sit below short ones because the market expects rates to be cut as growth slows.

Q6 Year-over-year CPI was 5.8% two years ago, 4.1% last year and 2.9% this year. This is best described as:
A. deflation
B. stagflation
C. disinflation
D. a recession

Answer: C. Prices are still rising each year, but more slowly: disinflation. Deflation (A) needs a falling price level, meaning negative CPI change. Stagflation (B) is high inflation with weak growth, and nothing here says growth is weak. A recession (D) is defined on real GDP, not CPI.

Q7 An industry with a few large sellers and high barriers to entry, such as airlines, is best described as:
A. a monopoly
B. an oligopoly
C. monopolistic competition
D. perfect competition

Answer: B. A few sellers with high barriers is an oligopoly. A monopoly (A) has one seller, often a regulated utility. Monopolistic competition (C) has many sellers of differentiated products, like cereal brands. Perfect competition (D) has many sellers of identical products who take the market price.

Q8 A sharp rise in the value of the US dollar is most likely to:
A. reduce the dollar value of a US multinational's foreign earnings
B. make imported goods more expensive for US consumers
C. help US exporters sell abroad
D. be caused by the Fed buying securities in the open market

Answer: A. Each euro or yen of foreign profit converts into fewer dollars when the dollar is strong. B and C reverse the effects: a strong dollar makes imports cheaper and US exports more expensive abroad. D is backwards too: Fed buying eases policy and tends to weaken, not strengthen, the dollar.

Day 3 · Common stock: ownership, votes and corporate actions

FINRA 2.1, 3.1 · pairs with the Day 3 edition (~90 min)

Common stock is the product you already trade; today is about the plumbing the SIE tests around it: the share-count vocabulary, how votes are counted, and the calendar of a dividend. The dividend dates and split arithmetic are near-certain exam questions, and on a desk the same dates drive option exercise, stock-loan recalls and corporate-actions breaks.

What a share actually is

A share of common stock is a residual claim on the company: shareholders own whatever is left after every creditor and every preferred holder has been paid. Two consequences follow.

  • Limited liability: the most a shareholder can lose is what was paid for the shares. The company's creditors cannot come after the owners' personal assets.
  • Last in liquidation: if the company is wound up, common holders are paid only after everyone else.
Liquidation priority (first paid --> last paid)
secured creditors
 --> unsecured creditors (general creditors, debentures)
 --> subordinated debt
 --> preferred stock
 --> common stock (whatever is left, often nothing)

In exchange for being last, common holders get the upside: all growth in the firm's value beyond the fixed claims accrues to them. That is why common is the growth instrument and why its value swings most.

Companies can issue more than one class of common. In a dual-class structure one class carries extra votes (often held by founders) and another carries fewer or none, so economic ownership and control are separated. Index providers and governance investors care; for the SIE, remember that the "one share, one vote" default can be changed by the charter.

The rights that come with common, as the SIE lists them: vote (for directors and on major matters such as mergers), receive dividends if and when declared by the board, transfer the shares freely, receive annual reports and inspect certain books and records, preemptive rights where the charter grants them, and the residual claim in liquidation.

Authorized, issued, outstanding, treasury

Four share counts describe a company's equity, and each is a subset of the one before it.

TermMeaning
AuthorizedThe maximum number of shares the corporate charter allows; raising it requires amending the charter
IssuedShares the company has actually sold or distributed at some point
TreasuryIssued shares the company has bought back and holds; no vote, no dividend, not counted in EPS
OutstandingIssued − treasury: the shares in investors' hands

Outstanding shares = Issued shares − Treasury shares

Issued shares
every share ever sold or distributed, never above authorized
Treasury shares
shares repurchased and held by the company, which may later reissue or retire them

Why buybacks raise EPS (earnings per share = net income ÷ shares outstanding): the numerator is unchanged but the denominator shrinks.

Worked example
  1. Net income $60 million; 120 million shares outstanding. EPS = 60 ÷ 120 = $0.50.
  2. The company buys back 20 million shares. They become treasury stock. Outstanding = 120 − 20 = 100 million.
  3. Same net income: EPS = 60 ÷ 100 = $0.60, a 20% rise with no change in the business.
  4. Caveat: the cash spent on the buyback no longer earns interest, so in reality net income dips slightly. The SIE ignores this.

Votes: statutory versus cumulative

Each common share normally carries one vote per director seat up for election. The difference between the two methods is only in how those votes can be distributed.

Total votes = Shares owned × Seats up for election

Shares owned
voting common shares held on the record date
Seats up for election
the number of director positions being filled at this meeting
  • Statutory voting: the votes must be split evenly, at most one vote per share for each seat.
  • Cumulative voting: the same total can be split any way, including all on one candidate.
Worked example
  1. You own 100 shares; 4 seats are up. Total votes = 100 × 4 = 400.
  2. Statutory: at most 100 votes for each of 4 candidates.
  3. Cumulative: any split of 400, for example all 400 for one candidate.
  4. Why this helps minorities: with 1,000 shares voting and 4 seats, a holder with just over 1,000 ÷ (4 + 1) = 200 shares (201) can concentrate 804 votes and guarantee one seat. Under statutory voting the majority holder wins every seat.

A proxy is a written authorization for someone else to vote your shares at a meeting you do not attend, in effect an absentee ballot. Proxies exist because most shareholders never attend: without them no meeting would reach a quorum. The SEC regulates proxy solicitations under the '34 Act. For shares held in street name, the BD forwards proxy materials to the beneficial owners and passes their instructions back; the issuer reimburses the cost.

Preemptive rights and dilution

A preemptive right is the right of existing shareholders to buy new shares before the public, in proportion to what they own, so their percentage stake is not diluted. If you own 5% and the company issues 10 million new shares, a preemptive right lets you buy 500,000 of them to stay at 5%. Whether shareholders have it depends on state law and the charter. The mechanism the company uses to honour it is a rights offering, covered on Day 4.

Dilution is the reduction in each existing share's slice of ownership and earnings when new shares are issued. It happens through new offerings, employee stock options, warrant exercises and convertible conversions.

The dividend calendar

A cash dividend is a distribution of profit that the board of directors must declare; common holders have no right to one until it is declared. Four dates follow, in this order:

DateSet byWhat it means
DeclarationBoardBoard announces the amount, record date and payable date
Ex-dividend (ex-date)Exchange (FINRA for OTC)First day the stock trades without the dividend; a buyer on or after this date does not get it
RecordBoardThe day you must be on the company's books as owner to receive the dividend
PayableBoardThe day the cash is paid; for an ordinary corporate dividend, the taxable event

Why the ex-date equals the record date under T+1

You are on the books only when your trade settles. Since May 28, 2024, regular-way stock trades settle T+1 (one business day after the trade date). So the last day to buy and still settle by the record date is one business day before the record date. The next day, the record date itself, is the first day a buyer would settle too late: that is the ex-date.

Record date Thu Oct 15 (regular way, T+1)
Buy Wed Oct 14 --(settles Thu Oct 15)--> on record: GETS it
Buy Thu Oct 15 --(settles Fri Oct 16)--> too late: NO dividend
=> ex-date = Thu Oct 15 = record date

Cash trade (settles same day)
Buy Thu Oct 15 --(settles Thu Oct 15)--> GETS it
=> cash ex-date = Fri Oct 16, day AFTER record

Under the old T+2 cycle the ex-date was one business day before the record date, and older prep books still say so. Today, regular way: ex-date = record date. Cash settlement: ex-date = the business day after the record date.

Mutual funds are different: the fund itself (its board) sets the ex-date, typically the business day after the record date. A fund does not trade on an exchange, so the settlement logic does not apply.

Tax timing: an ordinary cash dividend is taxable when it is received, on the payable date. The exception to remember: mutual fund and REIT dividends declared in October to December to holders of record then, and paid by the end of January, are taxed as if received on December 31 of the earlier year.

How the price adjusts on the ex-date

On the ex-date the stock no longer carries the right to the dividend, so it is worth that much less. The exchange lowers the reference (opening) price by the dividend amount. A stock that closed at $50.00 the day before going ex a $0.40 dividend opens with a reference price of $49.60. The holder is no worse off: $49.60 of stock plus a $0.40 receivable. Open buy limit and sell stop orders held below the market are also reduced by the dividend on the ex-date under FINRA Rule 5330, unless the customer marks the order DNR (do not reduce).

Dividend yield = Annual dividend ÷ Current market price

Annual dividend
the dividends per share paid over a year (quarterly × 4 for a regular payer)
Current market price
the share price today

A stock at $50 paying $0.50 a quarter: annual dividend $2.00; yield = 2 ÷ 50 = 4%.

Where the cash goes

Issuer --(cash)--> paying agent / transfer agent
 --(one payment to the holder of record)--> DTC (Cede & Co.)
 --(allocated by position)--> clearing firm / BD
 --(credited on payable date)--> customer account

Because Cede & Co. is the registered holder, the issuer pays one cheque; every allocation below that is a book entry. When a trade straddles the record date and fails, the dividend lands with the wrong party and operations must file a due bill claim to move it to the rightful owner.

Why "buy before the ex-date" is not free money

A naive dividend-capture trade buys the day before the ex-date and sells on it. On the ex-date the price drops by roughly the dividend, so the gross gain is about zero, and the investor has converted price into taxable income. Holding briefly also fails the holding-period test for a qualified dividend (more than 60 days in the 121-day window that starts 60 days before the ex-date), so the dividend is taxed as ordinary income. Dividend strategies that work do so because of tax differences between holders, not because of the calendar.

Stock dividends and splits: more slices, same pizza

A stock dividend pays shareholders in additional shares instead of cash. A forward split (for example 2-for-1) increases the share count and cuts the price proportionally; a reverse split (for example 1-for-5) does the opposite, usually to lift a low share price. None of these changes what the holder owns: the company's value is sliced into more or fewer pieces.

EventSharesPrice per shareHolder's total valueTotal cost basis
Cash dividendSameFalls by the dividend on ex-dateSame (stock + cash)Same; cash is taxable income
Stock dividendUpDownSameSame total, spread over more shares
Forward splitUpDownSameSame total
Reverse splitDownUpSameSame total

New shares = Old shares × (New ÷ Old) · New price = Old price × (Old ÷ New)

New ÷ Old
the split ratio as stated: in a 3-for-2 split, New = 3 and Old = 2
Old ÷ New
the inverse ratio, applied to price so that shares × price is unchanged
Stock dividend of x%
shares × (1 + x); price ÷ (1 + x)
Worked example
  1. 3-for-2 split: 200 shares at $60 (value $12,000). Shares = 200 × 3/2 = 300. Price = 60 × 2/3 = $40. Value = 300 × 40 = $12,000. Basis per share falls from $60 to $40.
  2. 1-for-5 reverse split: 1,000 shares at $2 (value $2,000). Shares = 1,000 × 1/5 = 200. Price = 2 × 5 = $10. Value = $2,000.
  3. 10% stock dividend: 1,000 shares at $44 (value $44,000). Shares = 1,000 × 1.1 = 1,100. Price = 44 ÷ 1.1 = $40. Value = $44,000.

Who approves a split is a matter of state corporate law and the company's charter, not a FINRA or SEC rule. As a general pattern, a forward split can often be done by the board (a shareholder vote is needed if the charter must be amended to authorize more shares), and a reverse split usually requires a shareholder vote. Some prep providers say every split needs shareholder approval; that is not a rule you should rely on, and it will not be tested here.

Understand
  • Common = residual claim, limited liability, last in liquidation, all the upside.
  • Outstanding = issued − treasury; buybacks cut the denominator and lift EPS.
  • Votes = shares × seats; cumulative lets a minority concentrate them.
  • Proxies make meetings possible; preemptive rights protect against dilution.
  • Settlement decides who is on the books: T+1 makes ex-date = record date; cash trades go ex the day after.
  • On the ex-date the price drops by the dividend; splits and stock dividends change the count and price, never the total value.
MEMORIZE:
  • Dividend sequence → declaration → ex-date → record → payable
  • Regular-way ex-date → same day as record date (T+1, since May 28, 2024)
  • Cash-settlement ex-date → business day after record date
  • Mutual fund ex-date → set by the fund, usually business day after record date
  • Ex-date set by → exchange (FINRA for OTC); fund's board for mutual funds
  • Cash dividend tax event → payable date
  • Cumulative voting → votes = shares × seats, any split
  • Statutory voting → at most one vote per share per seat
  • Outstanding → issued − treasury
  • Treasury stock → no vote, no dividend
  • Dividend yield → annual dividend ÷ price
Exam traps
  • "Buy on the ex-date to get the dividend" → a regular-way buyer on the ex-date settles after the record date and does not get it.
  • "The ex-date is one day before the record date" → that was T+2; under T+1 they are the same day.
  • "Treasury stock votes and receives dividends" → it does neither.
  • "A split or stock dividend makes the holder richer" → total value and total cost basis are unchanged.
  • "Preferred stock votes" → voting belongs to common; preferred generally does not vote.
  • Record date vs ex-date vs payable date → on the books by / first day without / cash arrives.
  • "Every split needs a shareholder vote per FINRA" → splits are governed by state law and the charter.
Trader connection

Options adjust for corporate actions, not ordinary dividends. The OCC adjusts listed options so that splits do not change a position's economics. A 2-for-1 split turns one 100-share contract with an $80 strike into two 100-share contracts at $40. A 3-for-2 split leaves one contract but changes the deliverable to 150 shares and the strike to $53.33. Stock dividends are adjusted the same way. Ordinary cash dividends are not adjusted, which is why dividends are priced into options and why the ex-date matters.

Early exercise. A holder of a deep in-the-money American BUY CALL exercises early only to capture a dividend: if the dividend exceeds the call's remaining time value, exercise on the business day before the ex-date (exercise settles T+1, landing on the record date). The mirror image: whoever is SELL CALL on those strikes should expect assignment the night before the ex-date.

Stock loan. The vote and the dividend go with the shares. When you lend stock, the borrower sells it, the buyer is on record, and the borrower owes you a manufactured dividend, taxed as ordinary income rather than as a qualified dividend. Lenders recall shares before a record date when they want to vote, and loan demand around record dates can be tax-driven (dividend arbitrage), which regulators watch closely. Index providers also react: buybacks and issuance change share counts and therefore index weights, and a split changes the divisor of a price-weighted index like the Dow.

Where you will meet Day 3: equity trading (ex-date price moves, dividend risk in options books), operations (the corporate-actions team processing splits, dividends and claims when a trade straddles a record date) and securities lending (recalls, manufactured dividends).

Check yourself

Q1 A shareholder owns 500 shares and three directors are up for election. Under cumulative voting, what is the most votes she may cast for a single candidate?
A. 1,500
B. 500
C. 166
D. 3

Answer: A. Total votes = shares × seats = 500 × 3 = 1,500, and cumulative voting lets her put them all on one candidate. B is the statutory maximum per candidate. C splits the shares across seats, and D is the number of seats.

Q2 A board declares a dividend with a record date of Thursday, October 15. For a regular-way trade, the ex-dividend date is:
A. Wednesday, October 14
B. Thursday, October 15
C. Friday, October 16
D. Tuesday, October 13

Answer: B. Under T+1, the ex-date is the same day as the record date: a buyer on Thursday settles Friday, too late. A is the old T+2 answer (one day before record) and the last day to buy with the dividend. C is the ex-date for a cash-settlement trade. D has no basis under current settlement.

Q3 A customer owns 300 shares at $90. After a 3-for-2 stock split, the customer owns:
A. 200 shares at $135
B. 450 shares at $90
C. 450 shares at $60
D. 600 shares at $45

Answer: C. Shares = 300 × 3/2 = 450; price = 90 × 2/3 = $60; value = $27,000 before and after. A inverts the ratio (a reverse split). B raises the count without cutting the price, which would create value from nothing. D is a 2-for-1 split.

Q4 All of the following are true of treasury stock EXCEPT:
A. it has been issued and then repurchased by the company
B. it is not counted in shares outstanding
C. it can be reissued later
D. it retains voting rights

Answer: D. Treasury stock has no vote and receives no dividend. A, B and C are all true: it is issued stock the company bought back, it is excluded from outstanding shares (and from EPS), and it can be reissued or retired.

Q5 For tax purposes, a customer receives an ordinary cash dividend from a corporation on which date?
A. The declaration date
B. The payable date
C. The ex-dividend date
D. The record date

Answer: B. A cash dividend is taxable when received, which is the payable date. The declaration (A), ex-date (C) and record date (D) each determine something else: the announcement, the first day without the dividend and who is on the books. (Fund and REIT dividends declared in Oct–Dec and paid in January are the exception: taxed as of Dec 31.)

Q6 A customer holds 1,000 shares at $55 when the company pays a 10% stock dividend. Afterward the customer holds approximately:
A. 1,100 shares at $50
B. 1,100 shares at $55
C. 1,000 shares at $50
D. 1,010 shares at $54.46

Answer: A. Shares = 1,000 × 1.1 = 1,100; price = 55 ÷ 1.1 = $50; value stays $55,000. B leaves the price unchanged, adding $5,500 of value from nothing. C cuts the price without adding shares. D applies 1% instead of 10%.

Q7 Which of the following are rights of common stockholders?
I. To vote for the board of directors
II. To receive a fixed dividend each quarter
III. Preemptive rights, where the charter provides them
IV. Priority over bondholders in liquidation
A. I and II
B. II and IV
C. III and IV
D. I and III

Answer: D. Common holders vote (I) and, where the charter grants them, have preemptive rights (III). Dividends on common are paid only if and when the board declares them; a fixed dividend (II) describes preferred. Common is last in liquidation, behind bondholders and preferred, so IV is false.

Q8 A company earns $50 million with 100 million shares outstanding, then buys back 10 million shares. If net income is unchanged, EPS becomes approximately:
A. $0.45
B. $0.50
C. $0.56
D. $0.60

Answer: C. Before: 50 ÷ 100 = $0.50. After the buyback, outstanding = 100 − 10 = 90 million, so EPS = 50 ÷ 90 = $0.556 ≈ $0.56. A divides by 110 million, adding instead of subtracting the repurchased shares. B ignores the buyback. D would need outstanding shares to fall to about 83 million.

Day 4 · Preferred stock, rights, warrants and ADRs

FINRA 2.1 · pairs with the Day 4 edition (~85 min)

Today covers the equity products that are not plain common stock: preferred, which prices like a bond; rights and warrants, which are call options issued by the company; and ADRs, which bring foreign shares into dollar markets. The SIE tests the features and the comparisons (rights versus warrants, callable versus convertible) plus three short calculations. On a desk each of these is a familiar risk (duration, optionality, currency) in an equity wrapper.

Preferred stock: a hybrid

Preferred stock is equity that pays a stated dividend and ranks ahead of common for dividends and in liquidation. It usually has no vote. Its par value is typically $100 (bonds are typically $1,000), and the dividend is quoted as a percentage of par: a 6% preferred pays $6 a year per share, usually in quarterly instalments of $1.50.

BondPreferredCommon
PaymentInterest: a legal obligationFixed dividend, paid only if declaredVariable dividend, if declared
Missed paymentDefaultNot a default (arrears if cumulative)Nothing owed
Liquidation rankAhead of all equityAfter debt, ahead of commonLast
VoteNoUsually noYes
MaturityYesUsually none (perpetual)None
Typical par$1,000$100Nominal; irrelevant to price

The key legal point: preferred dividends are not a legal obligation. The board can skip them without triggering default; skipping bond interest is a default. That is why preferred yields more than the same issuer's bonds.

Preferred suits income investors, not those seeking capital appreciation: the dividend is fixed, so holders do not share in growth (convertible preferred is the exception). Corporations like it for a tax reason: a corporate holder may deduct part of the dividends it receives from other domestic corporations (the dividends-received deduction), which individuals cannot.

Why issuers use it

From the issuer's side, preferred raises equity capital without giving away votes and without creating a payment whose omission is a default. The cost: preferred dividends are paid from after-tax profit, whereas bond interest is tax-deductible to the issuer, so preferred is usually the more expensive source of money. Some issues add protection for holders, such as the right to elect directors if dividends go unpaid for a period.

Why preferred trades like a bond

A preferred with a fixed dividend and no maturity is, economically, a perpetual bond. Its price is set by the yield investors require:

Preferred price ≈ Annual dividend ÷ Required yield

Annual dividend
stated rate × par, in dollars (6% × $100 = $6)
Required yield
the market yield investors demand today for this issuer's preferred, as a decimal
Preferred price
the market price in dollars per share
Worked example
  1. 6% preferred, $100 par: dividend = $6.
  2. At issue, required yield = 6%: price = 6 ÷ 0.06 = $100.
  3. Rates rise; required yield 7.5%: price = 6 ÷ 0.075 = $80. A 1.5-point rise in yield cut the price by 20%.
  4. Rates fall; required yield 5%: price = 6 ÷ 0.05 = $120, unless the issue is callable at $100, in which case buyers will not pay much above the call price.

Price and required yield move in opposite directions, exactly like a bond, and because there is no maturity the sensitivity is large. That is the interest-rate risk of preferred.

The features, and who each one favours

Every feature that helps the holder lowers the dividend the issuer must pay; every feature that helps the issuer raises it. That single rule answers most "which pays more?" questions.

TypeFeatureFavoursStated dividend vs straight
Straight (noncumulative)A skipped dividend is gone foreverIssuerBaseline
CumulativeSkipped dividends accrue as arrears; all arrears plus the current dividend must be paid before common gets anythingHolderLower
ParticipatingMay receive extra dividends above the stated rate when common receives moreHolderLower
ConvertibleHolder may exchange for a set number of common sharesHolderLower (the option has value)
CallableIssuer may redeem at a set price after a set dateIssuerHigher (compensates call risk)
Adjustable-rateDividend resets periodically with a benchmark rateHolder (price stability)Floats; price stays near par

Callable preferred gets called when rates fall, because the issuer can refinance cheaper. The holder loses the high dividend exactly when reinvestment rates are low, and the price is capped near the call price. Adjustable-rate preferred is the opposite trade-off: because the dividend moves with rates, the price barely moves, so it carries little interest-rate risk and little upside.

Cumulative arrears

Arrears = Missed dividends per share × Shares

Missed dividends per share
annual dividend × years (or quarters × quarterly amount) skipped
Shares
cumulative preferred shares outstanding (or held, for one investor)
Worked example
  1. 7% cumulative preferred, $100 par: $7 a year. The company skipped two full years.
  2. Arrears per share = 2 × $7 = $14.
  3. Before any common dividend this year: $14 arrears + $7 current = $21 per preferred share.
  4. With 50,000 preferred shares: 21 × 50,000 = $1,050,000 must go to preferred first.

Convertible preferred: conversion ratio and parity

The conversion price is the price per common share at which par converts. The conversion ratio is how many common shares one preferred converts into. Parity is the price at which the preferred and the common it converts into are worth exactly the same.

Conversion ratio = Par ÷ Conversion price · Parity price of preferred = Common price × Conversion ratio

Par
face value of the preferred, typically $100
Conversion price
the price per common share set at issue
Conversion ratio
common shares received per preferred share
Common price
current market price of the common
Parity price of common
the reverse: preferred price ÷ conversion ratio
Worked example
  1. $100 par convertible preferred, conversion price $25. Ratio = 100 ÷ 25 = 4 common shares.
  2. Common trades at $28. Parity price of the preferred = 28 × 4 = $112.
  3. If the preferred trades at $105, it is below parity: buy the preferred at $105, convert, sell 4 shares at $28 = $112, a $7 arbitrage before costs.
  4. Parity price of the common, given a $105 preferred = 105 ÷ 4 = $26.25.

Conversion ratios are adjusted for splits and stock dividends (anti-dilution provisions), so a 2-for-1 split would double the ratio to 8.

Rights: short-term, at a discount, to existing holders

A rights offering is how a company honours shareholders' preemptive rights when it raises new equity. Each existing share receives one right. A fixed number of rights plus a cash subscription price buys one new share. The subscription price is set below the market price, so rights have intrinsic value at issue. They are short-lived (a matter of weeks) and trade separately, so a holder can exercise, sell the rights, or let them expire worthless. Shares that holders do not take up are often bought by a standby underwriter under a standby commitment.

Company --(1 right per share)--> existing holders
Holder --(N rights + subscription cash)--> company
Company --(1 new share)--> holder
Unexercised rights --(sold)--> other investors
Unsubscribed shares --> standby underwriter

A holder who neither exercises nor sells is diluted twice: his percentage stake falls and the share price falls as new shares come in below market. Selling the rights is how a holder who does not want to invest more recovers that value.

Right value (cum rights) = (Market price − Subscription price) ÷ (Rights per new share + 1)

Market price
price of the stock while it still trades with the right attached ("cum rights")
Subscription price
cash paid per new share when exercising
Rights per new share
number of rights needed to buy one new share (N)
Ex rights
once the stock trades without the right, the divisor is N, not N + 1
Worked example
  1. Stock $50 cum rights; subscription price $44; 5 rights buy one new share.
  2. Value of one right = (50 − 44) ÷ (5 + 1) = $1.00.
  3. Ex rights the stock should open near $49 (the right has been detached): (49 − 44) ÷ 5 = $1.00, consistent.
  4. A holder of 500 shares gets 500 rights: exercise them for 100 new shares at $44 ($4,400), or sell them for about $500.

Warrants: long-term, above market, as a sweetener

A warrant gives the holder the right to buy the issuer's common stock at a set exercise price for a long period: typically several years, sometimes perpetual. Warrants are usually issued attached to a bond or preferred as a sweetener: investors accept a lower coupon or dividend because they also get the upside of the warrant. At issue the exercise price is set above the market price, so a warrant has no intrinsic value at issue; its value is all time value. Warrants are usually detachable and trade on their own. When exercised, the company issues new shares, which dilutes existing holders.

Worked example
  1. A bond issue comes with warrants to buy the stock at $30; the stock trades at $24. Intrinsic value = 0 (you would not pay $30 for a $24 stock), yet the warrant trades at, say, $3 because it has years to run.
  2. Three years later the stock is $38. Intrinsic value = 38 − 30 = $8 per share. The warrant trades above $8, the extra being remaining time value.
  3. Exercise: the holder pays $30 to the company and receives a newly issued share. Share count rises; existing holders are diluted.

Warrants also appear in units, such as those sold by SPACs (special purpose acquisition companies), where one unit is a share plus a fraction of a warrant.

RightsWarrants
LifeShort (weeks)Long (years; some perpetual)
Price vs market at issueBelow market (discount)Above market (premium)
Intrinsic value at issueYesNo
Who gets themExisting shareholders, one per shareBuyers of a new bond or preferred (or units)
PurposePreemptive right; raise equity from current holdersSweetener; lower the cost of the debt or preferred
Trade separately?YesYes, usually

ADRs: foreign shares in dollar form

An ADR (American Depositary Receipt) is a certificate issued by a US depositary bank that represents shares of a foreign company held on deposit by a custodian bank in the company's home market. ADRs trade in dollars in the US, settle through DTC, and pay dividends in dollars. Each ADR represents a stated number of foreign ("ordinary") shares, the ADR ratio.

Foreign issuer's ordinary shares (in euros)
 --(deposited)--> custodian bank, home market
 --(held for)--> US depositary bank --(issues)--> ADRs
 --(trade in USD)--> US investors via BDs, DTC
Dividends: EUR --(converted, net of foreign tax)--> USD

The dollar price does not remove currency risk; it just hides it. The ADR is worth the foreign price converted at today's exchange rate.

Worked example
  1. One ADR = 2 ordinary shares. Ordinary share: €20. EUR/USD = 1.10.
  2. ADR value ≈ 2 × 20 × 1.10 = $44.
  3. The euro falls to 1.00; the share is unchanged at €20. ADR ≈ 2 × 20 × 1.00 = $40: a 9% loss from currency alone.
  4. A €0.50 dividend per ordinary share at 1.00 = $1.00 per ADR, less foreign withholding tax (which the US holder may be able to claim as a foreign tax credit).
Sponsored ADRUnsponsored ADR
Created byDepositary bank with the foreign issuer's participation and agreementDepositary bank without the issuer's involvement
Where it tradesCan list on a US exchangeOTC only
VotingHolders can generally vote through the depositaryGenerally no voting rights
CostsIssuer bears much of the depositary costHolders bear the fees

ADR risks the SIE names: currency risk (above), political and country risk, and foreign withholding tax on dividends. Because ADRs can be converted into ordinary shares and back, arbitrage keeps the ADR close to the converted ordinary price. The same structure issued outside the US, often in London or Luxembourg, is a GDR (global depositary receipt).

Understand
  • Preferred = fixed dividend, priority over common, usually no vote, no legal obligation to pay; priced like a perpetual bond.
  • Features that help holders (cumulative, participating, convertible) lower the dividend; features that help the issuer (callable) raise it.
  • Rights: short term, below market, one per existing share, trade separately.
  • Warrants: long term, above market at issue, sweetener on bonds or preferred, dilutive on exercise.
  • ADRs: foreign shares held by a depositary, trade in dollars; currency risk and foreign withholding remain.
MEMORIZE:
  • Preferred par → typically $100
  • Preferred suits → income investors, not capital appreciation
  • Preferred dividend → not a legal obligation (bond interest is)
  • Cumulative preferred → all arrears paid before any common dividend
  • Callable preferred → higher dividend
  • Convertible preferred → lower dividend
  • Rights → short term, below market, intrinsic value at issue, to existing holders
  • Warrants → long term, above market at issue, no intrinsic value, sweetener
  • Conversion ratio → par ÷ conversion price
  • Parity price → common price × conversion ratio
  • ADR holders → currency risk and foreign withholding tax
  • Unsponsored ADR → no issuer involvement, OTC only
Exam traps
  • Rights vs warrants → rights = discount + short term; warrants = premium + long term.
  • "Callable preferred pays less" → it pays more, to compensate for call risk.
  • "Convertible preferred pays more" → it pays less; the conversion option is worth something.
  • "ADRs trade in dollars, so there is no currency risk" → the underlying currency still moves the dollar price and the dividend.
  • "Skipping a preferred dividend is a default" → only missed bond interest is a default.
  • "Preferred par is $1,000" → that is the bond convention; preferred is typically $100.
Trader connection

Preferred is rate plus credit exposure in equity clothing. A perpetual preferred's price is D ÷ y, so its modified duration is about 1 ÷ y: at a 6% yield, roughly 16.7. A 50 bp rise in the required yield therefore costs about 8%. Hedgers treat preferred as long-duration, deeply subordinated credit: the rate leg is hedged with Treasury futures, the credit leg with credit indices or single-name protection. Banks issue noncumulative perpetual preferred because it counts toward regulatory capital, which is why this market is dominated by financial issuers.

Warrants and convertibles are embedded long calls. A warrant is a company-issued BUY CALL; a convertible is a bond or preferred plus a BUY CALL on the stock. They carry delta (sensitivity to the stock), vega (sensitivity to volatility) and a dilution effect that listed options do not have, because exercise creates new shares. Convertible arbitrage buys the convertible and shorts delta-many shares of the common (which requires borrowing the stock, so it lives next to the securities-lending desk). It captures cheap volatility and the coupon, and rebalances as delta changes.

ADR vs ordinary. An ADR and its ordinary shares are the same risk in two currencies and two time zones. Desks arbitrage the gap by creating or cancelling ADRs with the depositary, and hedge the currency leg with FX forwards.

Where you will meet Day 4: capital markets (structuring hybrid capital such as bank preferreds and convertibles), convertible arbitrage desks and funds, and international equity (ADR/ordinary trading, depositary receipts, currency hedging).

Check yourself

Q1 A company has 8% cumulative preferred stock, $100 par. It paid no preferred dividends last year and wants to pay a common dividend this year. How much must it first pay per preferred share?
A. $8
B. $4
C. $16
D. $24

Answer: C. Annual dividend = 8% × $100 = $8. Arrears for last year = $8, plus the current year's $8 = $16 before any common dividend. A pays only the current year, which would be the answer for noncumulative preferred. B is half a year. D counts a third year that was never skipped.

Q2 A $100 par convertible preferred has a conversion price of $40. The common trades at $44. What is the parity price of the preferred?
A. $110
B. $100
C. $104
D. $176

Answer: A. Conversion ratio = 100 ÷ 40 = 2.5 shares; parity = 44 × 2.5 = $110. B is par. C adds the $4 gain on one share instead of 2.5 shares. D uses a ratio of 4, as if the conversion price were $25.

Q3 Which statements are true?
I. Rights typically have a short life
II. Warrants are typically issued with an exercise price above the market price
