III. Rights are typically issued with bonds as a sweetener
IV. Warrants are typically issued at a discount to the market price
A. I and III
B. II and IV
C. III and IV
D. I and II

Answer: D. Rights are short-lived (I) and warrants carry an exercise price above market at issue (II). III and IV swap the two products: it is warrants that sweeten bond and preferred issues, and rights that are priced at a discount.

Q4 A 5% straight preferred, $100 par, trades at par. If required yields on similar preferreds rise to 6.25%, the price should move to approximately:
A. $125
B. $80
C. $93.75
D. $98.75

Answer: B. Dividend = $5; price ≈ 5 ÷ 0.0625 = $80. A applies the move in the wrong direction (yields up, price down). C (100 − 6.25) subtracts the new yield from par as if it were dollars, and D (100 − 1.25) subtracts the yield change; both understate a perpetual's rate sensitivity.

Q5 All of the following are true of ADRs EXCEPT:
A. they are issued by a US depositary bank
B. they trade and pay dividends in US dollars
C. they eliminate currency risk for US investors
D. dividends may be subject to foreign withholding tax

Answer: C. The ADR's dollar price is the foreign price converted at the current exchange rate, so currency moves still change its value and its dividends. A, B and D are true: a US depositary bank issues them, they are dollar-denominated, and the home country may withhold tax on dividends.

Q6 Assuming the same issuer and credit quality, which preferred would most likely carry the lowest stated dividend rate?
A. Callable preferred
B. Convertible preferred
C. Noncumulative straight preferred
D. Callable noncumulative preferred

Answer: B. The conversion feature gives the holder an equity option, so investors accept a lower dividend. Callable features (A, D) favour the issuer and require a higher dividend. Noncumulative straight preferred (C) has no holder-friendly feature, so it pays more than the convertible.

Q7 Which statements about preferred stock are true?
I. Par value is typically $100
II. Par value is typically $1,000
III. It is suited to investors seeking capital appreciation
IV. It is suited to investors seeking income
A. I and IV
B. II and III
C. I and III
D. II and IV

Answer: A. Preferred par is typically $100 (I) and its fixed dividend makes it an income product (IV). $1,000 (II) is the bond convention. Preferred holders do not share in growth, so it does not suit capital appreciation (III); the exception is convertible preferred, which the question does not specify.

Q8 Which of the following best describes an unsponsored ADR?
A. It is listed on a US exchange at the foreign issuer's request
B. The foreign issuer pays the depositary's costs
C. Holders vote through the depositary on all matters
D. It is created by a depositary bank without the foreign issuer's involvement and trades OTC

Answer: D. An unsponsored ADR is set up by a depositary bank on its own initiative, trades only OTC, and holders bear the fees and generally cannot vote. A, B and C each describe features of a sponsored ADR.

Day 5 · Bond math: price, yield and why they move opposite

FINRA 2.1, 2.2 · pairs with the Day 5 edition (~120 min)

A bond is a fixed stream of cash that the market keeps repricing to whatever yield it demands today; almost every SIE bond question is that one sentence in disguise. Today you learn to read a bond quote, convert it to dollars, rank its four yields in either direction without memorizing blindly, and charge accrued interest correctly. On a desk the same mechanics become DV01, hedge ratios and the reason callable bonds behave like a short option.

The anatomy of a bond

A bond is a loan to the issuer, broken into tradable pieces. Four terms define it:

  • Par (face value, principal): the amount repaid at maturity. The SIE convention is $1,000 per bond, and every price is quoted as a percentage of par. A price of 97 means 97% × $1,000 = $970.
  • Coupon: the fixed annual interest rate stated on the bond, applied to par. A 6% coupon pays $60 a year per bond, usually as two $30 semiannual payments. The coupon is also called the nominal yield or stated yield. It never changes once the bond is issued (floating-rate notes aside).
  • Maturity: the date par comes back. After it, the bond is gone.
  • Price: whatever the market pays today. Above par = premium; below par = discount; at 100 = par.

The issuer's cash flows are fixed at issue. The only thing that moves is what a buyer will pay for them.

Bond issues are also organised by how their maturities are laid out:

StructureHow principal comes dueWhere you see it
TermThe whole issue matures on one dateMost corporates; often paired with a sinking fund
SerialSlices mature on a schedule, year after yearMunicipal general-obligation issues
BalloonSerial, but a large final slice matures lastIssuers who want small early repayments

Why price and yield move opposite

The coupon is frozen; market yields are not. When newly issued bonds pay more, nobody will pay par for your old, lower coupon, so its price drops until a buyer at the new price earns the new market yield. The price is the only adjustable part, so it does all the adjusting.

Market yields rise --> old coupon looks thin
  --> buyers pay less --> price falls until
  (coupons + pull to par) = new market yield

Market yields fall --> old coupon looks rich
  --> buyers pay more --> price rises
Worked example
  1. A 10-year bond, 5% coupon, $50 a year in two $25 payments, priced at par when market yields are 5%: price = $1,000.
  2. Market yields jump to 6%. Discounting the same 20 payments of $25 plus $1,000 at 6% (3% per half-year) gives a price of about $925.61.
  3. Market yields instead fall to 4%. Discounting at 2% per half-year gives about $1,081.76.
  4. Same cash flows, three prices. Yield up 1% → price down $74.39; yield down 1% → price up $81.76. The rise is bigger than the fall: that asymmetry is positive convexity, and it matters in the trader section.

The exam calls this the see-saw: price on one end, yield on the other. Remember it with the reason attached: a fixed coupon has to be repriced to the market's yield.

Three yields, and what each one ignores

The coupon tells you the cash. The other yields add price and time, one layer at a time.

Current yield = Annual interest ÷ Market price

Annual interest
coupon rate × par, in dollars ($60 for a 6% bond)
Market price
today's dollar price of the bond, not par

Current yield (CY) answers "how much cash income per dollar I pay today?" It ignores time entirely: it pretends the premium you paid is never lost, and the discount never gained.

Approx. YTM = [Annual interest + (Par − Price) ÷ Years] ÷ [(Par + Price) ÷ 2]

Annual interest
coupon dollars per year
Par − Price
the gain (discount bond, positive) or loss (premium bond, negative) you lock in by holding to maturity
Years
years to maturity; dividing spreads the gain or loss evenly per year
(Par + Price) ÷ 2
the average amount invested over the bond's life

Yield to maturity (YTM, also called the basis) is the single discount rate that sets the present value of every coupon plus par equal to today's price. It is the bond's true internal rate of return, assuming you hold to maturity and reinvest each coupon at the YTM. The approximation above is the SIE version; it is close enough to rank yields.

Yield to call (YTC) is the same calculation, except the bond ends on the first call date at the call price instead of at maturity at par. A call is the issuer's right to repay early; it does that when rates fall and it can refinance cheaper (Day 6 covers call features).

YTM carries a hidden assumption: every coupon is reinvested at the YTM itself. If rates fall and coupons can only be reinvested at lower rates, the realised return comes in below the quoted YTM. That is reinvestment risk, and it is worst for high-coupon bonds when rates fall. A zero-coupon bond has no coupons to reinvest, so it carries no reinvestment risk; it pays for that with the most interest-rate risk, as the duration section shows.

MeasureUsesIgnores
Coupon (nominal yield)ParPrice and time
Current yieldPriceGain or loss to maturity; timing
YTMPrice, coupons, par, years to maturityEarly call; reinvestment at other rates
YTCPrice, coupons, call price, years to callAnything after the call date

Deriving the premium ordering: Coupon > CY > YTM > YTC

Take one bond and walk through the yields. 6% coupon, 10 years to maturity, priced at 110 ($1,100), callable at par (100) in 5 years.

Worked example
  1. Coupon: $60 ÷ $1,000 par = 6.00%.
  2. Current yield: $60 ÷ $1,100 = 5.45%. Dividing the same $60 by a bigger number must give a smaller result.
  3. YTM: the $100 premium is lost by maturity, because you paid $1,100 and get back $1,000. Spread over 10 years: −$10 a year. ($60 − $10) ÷ [($1,000 + $1,100) ÷ 2] = $50 ÷ $1,050 = 4.76% (the exact semiannual YTM is 4.73%).
  4. YTC: the same $100 is lost in only 5 years: −$20 a year. ($60 − $20) ÷ $1,050 = $40 ÷ $1,050 = 3.81% (exact 3.79%).
  5. Ranking: 6.00% > 5.45% > 4.76% > 3.81%, so Coupon > CY > YTM > YTC.

Each step down the ladder adds one more piece of bad news that the step before ignored:

Coupon  --(divide by price > par)-->  CY lower
CY      --(charge premium loss / 10 yr)-->  YTM lower
YTM     --(same loss in only 5 yr)-->  YTC lowest

The whole logic: the premium is a loss you are certain to take, and a call makes you take it sooner. A loss spread over fewer years costs more per year. That is why the issuer's early call is the worst case for a premium buyer, and why YTC sits at the bottom.

Deriving the discount ordering: YTC > YTM > CY > Coupon

Mirror image. 6% coupon, 10 years, priced at 90 ($900), callable at par in 5 years.

Worked example
  1. Coupon: 6.00%.
  2. Current yield: $60 ÷ $900 = 6.67%. A smaller denominator gives a bigger yield.
  3. YTM: you gain the $100 discount by maturity: +$10 a year over 10 years. ($60 + $10) ÷ [($1,000 + $900) ÷ 2] = $70 ÷ $950 = 7.37% (exact 7.44%).
  4. YTC: the same $100 gain arrives in 5 years: +$20 a year. $80 ÷ $950 = 8.42% (exact 8.50%).
  5. Ranking: 8.42% > 7.37% > 6.67% > 6.00%, so YTC > YTM > CY > Coupon.

The discount is a gain you are certain to collect, and a call hands it to you sooner. A gain compressed into fewer years is worth more per year, so YTC is on top. (Discount bonds are rarely called in practice, since their coupon is below market and the issuer would not refinance, but the exam still asks for the ordering.)

Notice what the proof never needed: the actual discount rate math. Every ordering question reduces to three comparisons. Is the price above or below par (coupon vs CY)? Is the premium or discount charged over the life (CY vs YTM)? Is it charged over the shorter call period (YTM vs YTC)? Answer those three and you can rebuild the ladder under exam pressure instead of trusting a memorized string.

At par there is no premium to lose or discount to gain, so coupon = CY = YTM = YTC.

Reading the ordering in both directions

The exam asks for these rankings "highest to lowest" in one question and "lowest to highest" in the next. It is the same fact read backwards, and the reversal is the trap. Write the four in one fixed line: Coupon – CY – YTM – YTC. For a premium bond the line runs downhill left to right; for a discount bond it runs uphill.

BondHighest → lowestLowest → highest
PremiumCoupon, CY, YTM, YTCYTC, YTM, CY, Coupon
DiscountYTC, YTM, CY, CouponCoupon, CY, YTM, YTC
ParAll four equal

Quick reverse check: if a question says a bond's YTM is above its coupon, the bond is at a discount. If YTM is below the coupon, it is at a premium. The ordering is also a price detector.

Desk footnote, not an exam point: the SIE ordering assumes the call price is par or close to it. If the same $1,100 bond were callable at 105, the loss to the call date is only $50 over 5 years, the same $10 a year as to maturity, and the exact YTC (4.91%) actually comes out above YTM (4.73%). That is why dealers quote yield to worst: the lower of YTM and every YTC. On the exam, use the orderings in the tables.

Duration: which bonds move most

Interest-rate risk is the risk that rising rates push a bond's price down. How far depends on how much of the bond's value sits far in the future, since distant cash is discounted hardest. Duration measures that: the weighted-average time until you get your money back, weighted by the present value of each payment. Two drivers:

  • Longer maturity → more sensitivity. More cash sits far out.
  • Lower coupon → more sensitivity. Less cash arrives early, so more of the value is the distant par payment.

Push both to the extreme and you get a zero-coupon bond: one payment, at maturity. Its duration (Macaulay duration, to be precise) equals its maturity, the maximum possible for that term.

Bond (all priced at a 5% yield)PricePrice after +100 bp% change
2-year, 5% coupon$1,000.00$981.41−1.9%
10-year, 5% coupon$1,000.00$925.61−7.4%
30-year, 5% coupon$1,000.00$861.62−13.8%
30-year zero$227.28$169.73−25.3%

The practical form is modified duration: the approximate % price change for a 1% (100 bp) change in yield. A bond with modified duration 7.8 loses about 7.8% if yields rise 1%. Rank questions (an 80-day T-bill against a 25-year AAA corporate, say) are answered by maturity first and coupon second. Credit quality is a separate risk; the AAA does not save the 25-year bond from rates.

Rates up --> all bond prices fall
  --> most: long maturity + low coupon (zeros)
  --> least: short maturity + high coupon (T-bills)

Quote conventions: points, eighths, 32nds and basis points

A point is 1% of par, which is $10 on a $1,000 bond. Everything else is a fraction of a point.

Unit% of par$ per $1,000 bondUsed for
1 point1%$10.00All bond prices
1/8 point0.125%$1.25Corporate and muni fractions (exam style)
1/32 point0.03125%$0.3125Treasury notes and bonds
1 basis point (bp)0.01%$0.10 if applied to priceYields and spreads

The decoding rule: fraction → decimal → move the decimal one place right. Percent of $1,000 is the same as multiplying the decimal quote by 10.

Worked example
  1. Corporate at 86 5/8: 5/8 = 0.625, so 86.625% of $1,000 = $866.25.
  2. Corporate at 98-1/4: 1/4 = 0.25, so 98.25% = $982.50. The hyphen here separates the whole points from a fraction written out.
  3. Treasury at 106-16: the hyphen means 32nds. 16/32 = 0.5, so 106.5% = $1,065.00. Reading it as 106.16 ($1,061.60) is the classic mistake.
  4. Treasury at 101-08: 8/32 = 0.25, so $1,012.50. Treasury at 99-24: 24/32 = 0.75, so $997.50.
  5. Treasury quote 106-12 bid / 106-16 ask. A customer buys at the ask: $1,065.00. A customer sells at the bid: 12/32 = 0.375, so $1,063.75. The dealer earns the 4/32 spread, which is $1.25 per bond.
  6. Basis points: a yield moving from 4.25% to 4.60% is +35 bp. A spread of 1.20% over Treasuries is 120 bp.

Drill until the conversion is automatic. Cover the right column and check yourself:

QuoteTypeDecimalDollars per bond
92 3/8Corporate (eighths)92.375$923.75
101 7/8Corporate (eighths)101.875$1,018.75
97 1/2Corporate97.5$975.00
98-04Treasury (32nds)98.125$981.25
102-20Treasury (32nds)102.625$1,026.25
100-01Treasury (32nds)100.03125$1,000.3125

Size scales linearly: 25 bonds at 92 3/8 cost 25 × $923.75 = $23,093.75 before accrued interest and commission. Price moves scale the same way: a move from 98-04 to 98-12 is 8/32 = 1/4 point = $2.50 per bond, or $2,500 on $1 million face.

On a real Treasury screen you will also see a "+", as in 99-16+. It means half a 32nd (1/64). The SIE does not test it, but a rates desk quotes it all day.

Accrued interest: who pays whom and why

Coupons are paid only on coupon dates, to whoever holds the bond on the record date. If you sell between coupons, you held the bond for part of the period but the buyer will receive the whole next coupon. Accrued interest is the fix: at settlement the buyer pays the seller the interest earned so far, then collects the full coupon later.

Buyer --(price + accrued interest)--> Seller
Seller --(bond)--> Buyer
Issuer --(full coupon on next date)--> Buyer

Accrual runs from the last coupon date up to, but not including, the settlement date. Regular-way settlement is T+1 for corporates, munis and Treasuries alike. Two day-count conventions, which you Just memorize:

  • 30/360 for corporate and municipal bonds: every month counts as 30 days, the year as 360.
  • Actual/actual for U.S. government securities: real calendar days, divided by the real days in the coupon period.

Accrued interest = Principal × Coupon rate × Days ÷ 360

Principal
par amount traded (10 bonds = $10,000)
Coupon rate
annual coupon as a decimal
Days
30/360 days from the last coupon date up to the day before settlement
360
the 30/360 year (corporates and munis)
Worked example
  1. Corporate: 10 bonds ($10,000 par), 6% coupon paid Jan 1 and Jul 1. Trade Thursday, March 12, 2026; settles Friday, March 13 (T+1).
  2. 30/360 days: January 30 + February 30 + March 1–12 = 12 → 72 days. (February counts as 30 even though it had 28.)
  3. Accrued = $10,000 × 0.06 × 72 ÷ 360 = $600 × 0.2 = $120.00, paid by the buyer to the seller on top of the price.
  4. Settlement money: at a price of 101, the buyer pays $10,100 + $120 = $10,220 plus any commission; the seller receives $10,220 minus any commission. On July 1 the buyer collects the full $300 coupon, of which $120 simply repays what was advanced and $180 is earned.
  5. Treasury: $10,000 of a 4% note, coupons Jan 15 and Jul 15, same settlement date. Actual days Jan 15 → Mar 13 (excluding Mar 13) = 17 + 28 + 12 = 57. Days in the coupon period Jan 15 → Jul 15 = 181. Semiannual coupon $200 × 57 ÷ 181 = $62.98.

Some bonds trade flat, meaning without accrued interest: bonds in default and income bonds (interest is paid only if earned, Day 6). Zero-coupon bonds have no coupon, so there is nothing to accrue.

Understand
  • Par $1,000, prices quoted as % of par; the coupon (nominal yield) is fixed on par.
  • Price and yield move inversely because the fixed coupon has to be repriced to the market's yield.
  • CY adds price; YTM adds the gain or loss to maturity; YTC compresses that gain or loss into the years to call.
  • Premium: the loss comes sooner if called, so Coupon > CY > YTM > YTC. Discount: the gain comes sooner, so YTC > YTM > CY > Coupon.
  • Longer maturity and lower coupon → higher duration → bigger price moves; a zero's duration equals its maturity.
  • Point $10, eighth $1.25, 32nd $0.3125, basis point 0.01%.
  • The buyer pays the seller accrued interest because the buyer will receive the full next coupon.
MEMORIZE:
  • 1 bond point → 1% of par = $10
  • 1/8 point → $1.25
  • 1/32 point (Treasuries) → $0.3125 per $1,000
  • 1 basis point → 0.01%; 100 bp = 1%
  • Corporate and muni day count → 30/360
  • U.S. government day count → actual/actual
  • Accrual period → last coupon date up to but not including settlement date
  • Premium bond, high to low → Coupon, CY, YTM, YTC
  • Discount bond, high to low → YTC, YTM, CY, Coupon
  • Nominal yield → the coupon rate
Exam traps
  • "Nominal yield" sounds like a market yield → it is the coupon, fixed on par.
  • Current yield divided by par → current yield uses the market PRICE.
  • A premium bond's YTM is above its coupon → it is BELOW the coupon (you lose the premium).
  • The seller pays the buyer accrued interest → the BUYER pays the SELLER.
  • Rates up hurts short, high-coupon bonds most → long-maturity, low-coupon bonds (zeros) fall most.
  • "Lowest to highest" read as "highest to lowest" → reread the direction; reverse the list.
  • Treasury 106-16 read as 106.16 → it is 106 16/32 = $1,065.00; a customer buy uses the ask.
  • Accrued through the settlement date → up to but NOT including it.
Trader connection

DV01 (dollar value of a basis point) is duration in dollars: price change for a 1 bp move. A 10-year 5% bond at par has modified duration about 7.8, so $10 million face moves about $10,000,000 × 7.8 × 0.0001 ≈ $7,800 per bp. Rates desks, portfolio managers and risk managers run on DV01: risk limits are set in DV01, and a credit desk long $500 million of corporates carries rate risk it did not choose. It hedges by selling Treasury futures in the amount whose DV01 offsets the book's. That strips out the rate move and leaves the credit spread, the risk the desk actually wants to trade.

A callable bond = a straight bond + the investor has SOLD a CALL to the issuer. As rates fall, the price rises toward the call price and then stalls, because the issuer will call. Upside is capped, downside is not: negative convexity, the same payoff shape as a SELL CALL position. The investor is paid for that option through a higher yield than a non-callable bond offers. Day 7 shows the same shape in mortgage-backed securities.

Check yourself

Q1 A customer buys one corporate bond quoted at 86 5/8. What is the dollar price, before accrued interest and commission?
A. $806.25
B. $860.58
C. $866.25
D. $868.75

Answer: C. 5/8 = 0.625, so 86.625% × $1,000 = $866.25. B treats the numerator and denominator as decimals; D adds an extra eighth ($1.25 × 2 = $2.50 too much); A misplaces the fraction entirely.

Q2 A bond trades at 108 and is callable at par in four years. Rank its yields from highest to lowest.
A. Nominal yield, current yield, yield to maturity, yield to call
B. Yield to call, yield to maturity, current yield, nominal yield
C. Current yield, nominal yield, yield to call, yield to maturity
D. Yield to maturity, yield to call, nominal yield, current yield

Answer: A. At 108 it is a premium bond. CY is below the coupon because the price is above par; YTM subtracts the $80 premium lost over the life; YTC subtracts the same $80 over fewer years, so it is lowest. B is the discount ordering; C and D scramble the reasoning.

Q3 A Treasury note is quoted 106-12 bid, 106-16 ask. A customer buys one note ($1,000 par). What does the customer pay, ignoring accrued interest?
A. $1,061.60
B. $1,063.75
C. $1,064.00
D. $1,065.00

Answer: D. Customers buy at the ask. 106-16 = 106 16/32 = 106.5% of $1,000 = $1,065.00. B is the bid (106 12/32 = $1,063.75), the price the customer would get on a sale. A reads the quote as a decimal (106.16), the classic 32nds error.

Q4 Ms. Ortiz buys a 6% corporate bond at 95. What is her current yield?
A. 5.70%
B. 6.32%
C. 6.00%
D. 6.50%

Answer: B. Current yield = annual interest ÷ market price = $60 ÷ $950 = 6.32%. C is the nominal yield (coupon ÷ par). A multiplies instead of divides (6% × 0.95). D can only be reached by counting part of the $50 gain to maturity, which current yield ignores; that gain belongs in YTM.

Q5 A bond is trading at a discount. Rank its yields from lowest to highest.
A. Yield to call, yield to maturity, current yield, nominal yield
B. Current yield, nominal yield, yield to maturity, yield to call
C. Nominal yield, current yield, yield to maturity, yield to call
D. Nominal yield, yield to maturity, current yield, yield to call

Answer: C. Discount bond, highest to lowest, is YTC > YTM > CY > Coupon; read backwards for lowest to highest. A is the correct list in the wrong direction, which is exactly what this question is testing. D swaps CY and YTM: YTM is higher because it adds the discount gained by maturity.

Q6 Which statements about accrued interest are TRUE?
I. The buyer pays accrued interest to the seller.
II. The seller pays accrued interest to the buyer.
III. Corporate bonds accrue on a 30/360 basis.
IV. Treasury notes accrue on a 30/360 basis.
A. I and III
B. I and IV
C. II and III
D. II and IV

Answer: A. The buyer compensates the seller for interest earned since the last coupon, because the buyer collects the whole next coupon. Corporates and munis use 30/360; U.S. government securities use actual/actual, so IV is false.

Q7 All of the following are true of a bond trading at a premium EXCEPT:
A. Its current yield is lower than its coupon.
B. Its yield to call is lower than its yield to maturity.
C. Market rates have likely fallen since it was issued.
D. Its yield to maturity is higher than its coupon.

Answer: D. A premium bond's YTM is below its coupon, since the buyer loses the premium by maturity. A, B and C are all true: CY divides by a price above par, YTC takes the premium loss sooner, and prices rise above par when market yields fall below the coupon.

Q8 If interest rates rise sharply, which bond's price will fall the most?
A. A 5-year, 7% corporate bond
B. A 30-year zero-coupon Treasury
C. A 30-year, 8% corporate bond
D. A 90-day Treasury bill

Answer: B. Price sensitivity rises with maturity and falls with coupon. A 30-year zero has the longest maturity and no coupon, so its duration equals its 30-year maturity, the maximum. C matches the maturity but its coupons return cash earlier, lowering duration. D has almost no interest-rate risk. Note B is a Treasury: no default risk does not mean no rate risk.

Day 6 · Corporate debt: seniority, collateral, calls and conversions

FINRA 2.1, 2.2 · pairs with the Day 6 edition (~95 min)

Corporate bonds differ less in their math than in their rights: what backs them, where they stand in the queue if the company fails, and what options the issuer or investor holds. The SIE tests the vocabulary of those rights (debenture, equipment trust, indenture, call protection, parity, OID). A credit desk prices the same rights as recovery rates, embedded options and spread.

The capital structure is a queue

When a company is liquidated, its assets are sold and the cash is paid out in a strict order. Each class is paid in full before the next class gets anything. The SIE's version of that queue:

Liquidation cash --> 1. Secured creditors (their collateral)
                 --> 2. Unsecured: debentures + general
                        creditors (suppliers, etc.)
                 --> 3. Subordinated debentures
                 --> 4. Preferred stock
                 --> 5. Common stock (whatever is left)

Debt comes before equity because debt is a contract and equity is a residual claim. Within debt, collateral and contractual subordination set the order. Real bankruptcy law also places administrative costs, some employee wages and certain taxes ahead of general unsecured creditors; SIE questions stick to the five-rung ladder above. Recovery falls as you go down the queue, which is why yields rise as you go down it.

Secured bonds: what stands behind them

A secured bond is backed by specific assets pledged to bondholders. If the issuer defaults, the trustee can seize and sell the collateral for them. There are three types to know:

TypeCollateralTypical issuer
Mortgage bondReal property the corporation owns (plants, land, buildings)Utilities, industrials
Equipment trust certificateRolling stock or equipment; the trustee holds title until the certificates are paid offTransportation: airlines, railroads, trucking
Collateral trust bondSecurities (stocks or bonds, often of a subsidiary) deposited with a trusteeHolding companies

Match them by asking what the company owns that a trustee could sell. A railroad owns locomotives, so equipment trust. A holding company owns shares of its subsidiaries, so collateral trust. A utility owns power plants, so mortgage bond. Just memorize the names; the logic tells you which is which.

Unsecured bonds: debentures and below

A debenture is an unsecured bond, backed only by the issuer's full faith and credit: its general promise and earning power. Debenture holders rank with the other general creditors. For a strong issuer, unsecured does not mean risky; most investment-grade corporate debt is senior unsecured.

A subordinated debenture is unsecured and contractually placed behind the other unsecured debt. It is paid only after senior debenture holders and general creditors are made whole, so it pays a higher yield. Convertible bonds are often issued as subordinated debentures.

An income bond (also called an adjustment bond) promises principal at maturity but pays interest only if the company earns enough to pay it. Companies issue them in reorganizations, often exchanging them for older bonds that are in default. Missing an income-bond coupon is not a default, which is exactly why they exist. They trade flat (without accrued interest), since no one can be sure interest will be paid. They are never suitable for someone who needs steady income.

The indenture, the trustee and the Trust Indenture Act of 1939

The indenture (deed of trust) is the contract between the issuer and its bondholders. It states the coupon, the maturity, any call, put, sinking-fund or conversion terms, the collateral if any, and the covenants: the promises the issuer makes, such as limits on additional debt or requirements to maintain insurance on collateral.

Thousands of scattered bondholders cannot each police an issuer, so the indenture names a trustee, normally a bank or trust company. The trustee monitors covenants and acts for the holders if the issuer defaults.

The Trust Indenture Act of 1939 made this mandatory. Corporate bonds offered to the public must be issued under an indenture that names an independent trustee.

Issuer --(indenture: promises)--> Trustee
Trustee --(monitors, enforces)--> on behalf of holders
Issuer --(coupons, principal)--> Bondholders

The Act covers corporate bonds only. U.S. Treasuries, agency securities and municipal bonds are exempt from it. The exam likes a Roman-numeral question that lists munis or Treasuries alongside corporates.

Calls, puts, refunding and sinking funds

A call feature gives the issuer the right to redeem bonds before maturity at a set call price. Issuers call when rates have fallen: they retire an expensive coupon and replace it with a cheaper one. Doing that with the proceeds of a new bond issue is called refunding. So call risk rises when rates fall, which is also when the investor would least like the cash back, because it can only be reinvested at the new, lower rates.

  • Call protection is the period during which the bond cannot be called: the time from issue until the first call date.
  • Call premium is the amount by which the call price exceeds par. It compensates holders for losing the bond early.
Worked example
  1. A 20-year bond issued in 2026, maturing in 2046, is callable beginning in 2034 at 105, stepping down to 100 by 2040.
  2. Call protection = 2034 − 2026 = 8 years. It is not 20 years (that is the maturity) and not 14 years (the time until the call price reaches par).
  3. Call premium at the first call = 105 − 100 = 5 points × $10 = $50 per bond.

Because the investor has effectively SOLD a CALL to the issuer, a callable bond pays a higher yield than an otherwise identical non-callable one, and its price gets compressed near the call price when rates fall. That is the negative convexity from Day 5.

A put feature (a puttable bond) is the reverse. The investor may sell the bond back to the issuer at par on set dates. The investor holds the option, like a BUY PUT, so a puttable bond pays a lower yield. Investors use it when rates rise: put the bond back at par and reinvest at the higher rates.

A sinking fund requires the issuer to set money aside, usually through the trustee, to retire part of the issue over time by calling bonds or buying them in the market. It reduces credit risk (the debt shrinks before maturity) and adds a steady buyer, which supports the price. Holders can have their specific bonds called under it.

One table ties the features together. The rule underneath is always the same: whoever holds the option pays for it through the yield.

FeatureWho holds the optionUsed whenEffect on yield vs plain bond
CallIssuer (investor is short a call)Rates fallHigher
PutInvestor (long a put)Rates riseLower
ConversionInvestor (long a call on the stock)Stock risesLower
Sinking fundNot an option: an obligation of the issuerOn scheduleLower (less credit risk)
CollateralPledge to holdersOn defaultLower (higher recovery)
SubordinationHolders give up rankOn defaultHigher (lower recovery)

Note what bondholders do not get: a vote. They are creditors, not owners. Their protection is the indenture and the trustee, not the board. When a covenant is breached or a payment is missed, the trustee acts for them, and in a default bondholders can end up owning the company as the old equity is wiped out.

Convertible bonds

A convertible bond can be exchanged, at the holder's choice, for a fixed number of the issuer's common shares. It is a bond plus an embedded BUY CALL on the stock. Because investors get that equity upside, the issuer pays a lower coupon than on straight debt. The trade-off for existing shareholders is dilution if the bonds convert.

Conversion ratio = Par ÷ Conversion price

Conversion ratio
shares received per bond
Par
$1,000
Conversion price
the stock price at which par converts, fixed in the indenture

Parity stock price = Bond price ÷ Conversion ratio

Parity stock price
the stock price at which the shares received are worth exactly what the bond costs
Bond price
the convertible's market price in dollars
Conversion ratio
shares per bond
Worked example
  1. A $1,000 convertible with a conversion price of $40 has a ratio of $1,000 ÷ $40 = 25 shares.
  2. The bond trades at 110 ($1,100). Parity stock price = $1,100 ÷ 25 = $44. Below $44, converting loses money versus selling the bond.
  3. Other direction: the stock trades at $48. Parity bond price = 25 × $48 = $1,200, which is the bond's conversion value.
  4. If that bond traded at $1,150 with the stock at $48, an arbitrageur would buy the bond, sell 25 shares short and convert, locking in $50. That trade keeps convertibles at or above conversion value.

Issuers can force conversion: when the stock is well above the conversion price, they call the bond. Holders then convert, because the shares are worth more than the call price. The debt disappears without the issuer paying out cash.

Zero-coupon bonds and OID

A zero-coupon bond pays no interest. It is sold at a deep discount and matures at par; the difference is the investor's interest, called original issue discount (OID).

Annual accretion = (Par − Purchase price) ÷ Years to maturity

Annual accretion
the OID earned each year and added to cost basis (straight-line, the SIE teaching convention)
Par
$1,000 at maturity
Purchase price
the original discounted price
Years to maturity
years from purchase to maturity
Worked example
  1. Buy a 10-year corporate zero at issue for $600. OID = $400; accretion = $400 ÷ 10 = $40 a year.
  2. Each year the investor reports $40 of interest income for federal tax, even though no cash arrives. This is phantom income. The basis rises by $40 a year.
  3. After 3 years the basis is $600 + $120 = $720. Sold then for $780, the result is a $60 capital gain ($780 − $720), not $180.
  4. Held to maturity: basis has accreted to $1,000, par is repaid, no gain or loss.

The IRS actually accretes OID on a constant-yield basis rather than straight-line; use straight-line for the exam. Phantom income applies to corporate zeros and to Treasury STRIPS (Day 7). Accretion on municipal zeros is tax-exempt, though it still raises the basis. That is why taxable zeros suit tax-deferred accounts such as IRAs.

Risk profile, both halves tested: zeros have no reinvestment risk (there are no coupons to reinvest), but maximum interest-rate risk for their maturity (duration equals maturity).

Credit ratings and what a spread pays for

Credit risk (default risk) is the chance that the issuer fails to pay interest or principal. Rating agencies such as S&P, Moody's and Fitch grade it. The line that matters most:

BandS&P / FitchMoody's
Investment gradeAAA, AA, A, BBB (down to BBB−)Aaa, Aa, A, Baa (down to Baa3)
High-yield ("junk", speculative)BB+ and belowBa1 and below

Many institutions (some pension funds, insurers, bank portfolios) are restricted to investment grade by their mandates. A fall from BBB− to BB+ (a "fallen angel") can therefore force selling, and the price drop is larger than the one-notch change alone would suggest.

A credit spread is a bond's yield minus the yield of a Treasury with the same maturity. It compensates for:

Spread = expected default loss
         (probability x (1 - recovery))
       + downgrade risk
       + liquidity risk (harder to sell)
       + risk premium for bearing it at all

Lower rating → wider spread → higher yield → lower price for the same coupon. Seniority feeds straight into the recovery term: a secured bond of a given issuer yields less than its subordinated debenture.

Understand
  • Secured bonds: mortgage (real property), equipment trust (transport equipment, trustee holds title), collateral trust (securities in trust). Unsecured: debentures and subordinated debentures.
  • Liquidation order: secured → unsecured and general creditors → subordinated → preferred → common.
  • The indenture is the contract; the trustee enforces it; the Trust Indenture Act of 1939 requires both for publicly offered corporate bonds.
  • Call = issuer's option, used when rates fall, so the investor earns a higher yield. Put = investor's option, used when rates rise, so a lower yield. Sinking funds retire debt over time.
  • Convertibles trade a lower coupon for an equity call; ratio = par ÷ conversion price; parity links bond and stock prices.
  • Zeros: OID accretes and is taxed yearly as phantom income on corporate zeros; no reinvestment risk, maximum rate risk.
  • Investment grade is BBB−/Baa3 and above; spreads pay for default, downgrade and liquidity risk.
MEMORIZE:
  • Liquidation priority → secured, unsecured/general creditors, subordinated, preferred, common
  • Investment grade → BBB−/Baa3 and above
  • Equipment trust certificates → transportation (airlines, railroads)
  • Collateral trust bond → backed by securities held in trust
  • Mortgage bond → backed by real property the corporation owns
  • Trust Indenture Act of 1939 → corporate bonds; an independent trustee protects bondholders
  • Call protection → years until the FIRST call date
  • Call premium → call price minus par (105 → $50)
  • Conversion ratio → par ÷ conversion price
Exam traps
  • Debentures are secured by the issuer's assets → they are unsecured, backed only by full faith and credit.
  • Income bonds pay interest every period → only if earned; they come out of reorganizations and trade flat.
  • Zeros have no interest-rate risk because there is nothing to reinvest → no REINVESTMENT risk, but maximum interest-rate risk.
  • Munis and Treasuries fall under the Trust Indenture Act → both are exempt; it covers corporates.
  • Call risk rises when rates rise → it rises when rates FALL (issuers refinance).
  • Call protection runs to maturity or to the par call → it runs only to the first call date.
  • Convertibles pay a higher coupon to attract buyers → LOWER coupon; the conversion option is the sweetener.
Trader connection

Credit desks hedge default risk with credit default swaps (CDS): the protection buyer pays a running spread, and on default receives par minus the recovery value. Recovery is set by seniority: the same default that returns most of par to secured holders can leave subordinated holders with little. A CDS on senior unsecured debt is priced with a recovery assumption (market convention is often around 40%), and a subordinated CDS with a lower one. Distressed investors live on this queue, buying the tranche whose recovery they think the market has mispriced.

A convertible is three risks in one wrapper: equity delta (the embedded BUY CALL), credit (the bond floor) and volatility (option value). Convertible arbitrage buys the convert and sells short the stock in proportion to the option's delta. That isolates cheap volatility and credit, and the position is rebalanced as the stock moves. Leveraged-finance and DCM bankers choose between call schedules, conversion terms and seniority when they structure the deal in the first place.

Check yourself

Q1 A corporation is liquidated. Which of the following is paid first?
A. Preferred stockholders
B. Subordinated debenture holders
C. General creditors
D. Common stockholders

Answer: C. Order: secured creditors, then unsecured debt holders and general creditors, then subordinated debt, then preferred, then common. B is debt but subordinated to general creditors by contract. A is ahead of common but behind all debt.

Q2 A 20-year bond issued in 2026 is callable beginning in 2034 at 104, declining to par in 2038. How much call protection does it have?
A. 8 years
B. 12 years
C. 20 years
D. 4 years

Answer: A. Call protection is the time until the first call date: 2034 − 2026 = 8 years. B measures to the par call, which is irrelevant, since the bond can be called at 104 from 2034. C is the maturity. D confuses the 4-point call premium ($40 per bond) with years.

Q3 A $1,000 bond is convertible at $50 per share and trades at 112. What is the parity price of the common stock?
A. $50.00
B. $22.40
C. $20.00
D. $56.00

Answer: D. Conversion ratio = $1,000 ÷ $50 = 20 shares. Parity stock price = $1,120 ÷ 20 = $56. A is the conversion price, which equals parity only when the bond trades at par. C is the conversion ratio (shares), not a price. B divides the bond price by the conversion price, which is neither a share count nor a parity price.

Q4 All of the following are secured corporate bonds EXCEPT:
A. Equipment trust certificates
B. Debentures
C. Collateral trust bonds
D. Mortgage bonds

Answer: B. Debentures are unsecured, backed only by the issuer's full faith and credit. Equipment trusts are backed by transportation equipment, collateral trusts by securities deposited with a trustee, and mortgage bonds by real property.

Q5 Which statements about the Trust Indenture Act of 1939 are TRUE?
I. It applies to publicly offered corporate bonds.
II. It applies to municipal bonds.
III. It requires an independent trustee.
IV. It applies to U.S. Treasury bonds.
A. I and III
B. I and IV
C. II and III
D. II and IV

Answer: A. The Act requires a qualified indenture and an independent trustee for corporate bonds offered to the public. Municipal and U.S. government securities are exempt, so II and IV are false.

Q6 Mr. Chen buys a newly issued 10-year corporate zero-coupon bond for $600 in a taxable account. What is the tax consequence in the first year?
A. None until the bond matures
B. A $400 capital gain at maturity only
C. $40 of interest income, even though no cash is received
D. $40 of tax-exempt accretion

Answer: C. OID on a corporate zero accretes annually, $400 ÷ 10 = $40 using the straight-line convention, and is taxed each year as interest (phantom income); basis rises by the same $40. A and B describe tax deferral that corporate zeros do not get. D is true only of municipal zeros.

Q7 An airline raising money to buy new aircraft would most likely issue:
A. Collateral trust bonds
B. Income bonds
C. Mortgage bonds
D. Equipment trust certificates

Answer: D. Equipment trust certificates finance transportation equipment. The trustee holds title to the aircraft until the certificates are paid. A is backed by securities, not equipment. C is backed by real property. B is a reorganization instrument.

Q8 Which of the following ratings is the lowest that still counts as investment grade?
A. BB+
B. BBB−
C. Ba1
D. A−

Answer: B. Investment grade runs down to BBB− (S&P/Fitch) and Baa3 (Moody's). A and C are the top of high-yield, one notch below the line on each scale. D is investment grade but not the lowest.

Day 7 · Governments, agencies, mortgage-backed and money-market paper

FINRA 2.1, 2.2 · pairs with the Day 7 edition (~110 min)

Today covers the plumbing of the dollar system: Treasuries that set the risk-free curve, agency mortgage securities that turn home loans into tradable cash flows, and the money-market paper and repo that fund everyone overnight. The SIE tests maturities, tax treatment, who guarantees what and which risk shows up when rates move. A rates or funding desk uses the same facts to finance positions, hedge convexity and run the basis trade.

Treasury securities: one issuer, five shapes

U.S. Treasury securities are direct obligations of the federal government, backed by its full faith and credit, meaning its power to tax and borrow. They are unsecured (no specific collateral) yet treated as free of default risk. They are not free of interest-rate risk: a 30-year Treasury bond can lose more value than a junk bond when rates jump.

SecurityMaturityHow it paysHow it is quoted
Treasury bill4, 6, 8, 13, 17, 26, 52 weeksBought at a discount, matures at par; no couponDiscount yield
Treasury note2 to 10 yearsSemiannual coupon, par at maturityPrice in 32nds
Treasury bond20 and 30 yearsSemiannual coupon, par at maturityPrice in 32nds
TIPSMulti-yearFixed coupon rate on a CPI-adjusted principalPrice and real yield
STRIPSAny coupon or principal dateZero coupon: a single paymentPrice or yield

Tax: Treasury interest is taxable at the federal level and exempt from state and local income tax. The state exemption is a constitutional courtesy between governments: states do not tax federal debt, and the federal government does not tax most municipal interest. Just memorize the maturities; they are Treasury issuance choices, not logic. Occasional cash-management bills are issued off the regular schedule when the Treasury needs short-term cash.

T-bills and the discount-yield quote

A T-bill pays no coupon. You pay less than par and receive par at maturity, and the difference is your interest. T-bills are the benchmark money-market instrument. Bills are quoted not by price but by discount yield: the discount as a percentage of face value, annualised on a 360-day year.

Discount yield = [(Face − Price) ÷ Face] × (360 ÷ Days to maturity)

Face
par value received at maturity
Price
purchase price
Days to maturity
actual days until the bill matures
360
the money-market year convention

Bond-equivalent yield = [(Face − Price) ÷ Price] × (365 ÷ Days to maturity)

Face − Price
the dollar interest earned
Price
what you actually invested (the correct denominator for a return)
365
a calendar year, so the result compares with coupon Treasuries
Worked example
  1. A 26-week (182-day) bill, $10,000 face, quoted 4.02% bid / 4.00% ask.
  2. Ask price = $10,000 × (1 − 0.0400 × 182 ÷ 360) = $9,797.78. Bid price = $10,000 × (1 − 0.0402 × 182 ÷ 360) = $9,796.77.
  3. The bid yield is higher than the ask yield, yet the bid price is lower. A higher discount means a lower price. The dealer buys cheap at the bid and sells dear at the ask, exactly as with any quote; it only looks backwards because the quote is in yield.
  4. Bond-equivalent yield at the ask: ($202.22 ÷ $9,797.78) × (365 ÷ 182) = 4.14%, higher than the 4.00% discount yield for two reasons: it divides by the smaller price instead of face, and it uses 365 days instead of 360.

Rule of thumb for the exam: the discount yield understates the investor's true return, so a bill's bond-equivalent yield is always higher than its quoted discount yield.

TIPS: inflation adjusts the principal

Treasury Inflation-Protected Securities protect purchasing power. The principal is adjusted by the Consumer Price Index (CPI-U); the coupon rate is fixed, but it is paid on the adjusted principal, so the dollar coupon moves with inflation.

New principal = Principal × (1 + CPI change); Coupon $ = Rate × Adjusted principal

Principal
the principal before the adjustment ($1,000 at issue)
CPI change
percentage change in the index, as a decimal (negative in deflation)
Rate
the fixed coupon rate set at auction
Adjusted principal
the new principal the rate is applied to
Worked example
  1. $1,000 TIPS, 2% coupon. CPI rises 3% over the year.
  2. New principal = $1,000 × 1.03 = $1,030.
  3. Annual interest = 2% × $1,030 = $20.60 (paid semiannually, about $10.30 each, on the principal at each date).
  4. The $30 principal increase is taxable federal income that year even though it is not paid until maturity: phantom income, as with zeros.
  5. If deflation shrinks the principal, the Treasury still repays at least the original $1,000 at maturity.

Because TIPS carry little inflation risk, their yield is lower than a regular Treasury's of the same maturity. The gap between the two (the breakeven) is the market's inflation forecast plus a risk premium.

STRIPS: zeros made from Treasuries

STRIPS (Separate Trading of Registered Interest and Principal of Securities) are created when dealers split a Treasury note or bond into its individual coupon payments and its principal payment, each trading as a separate zero-coupon security through the Federal Reserve's book-entry system. The Treasury does not sell STRIPS directly. They are still full-faith-and-credit obligations. As with corporate zeros, the annual accretion is taxed federally each year (phantom income), which makes them a natural fit for IRAs. Pension funds and insurers buy them to match a known future liability to a single payment date.

How Treasuries are sold: the auction

Treasury sells new securities at regularly scheduled auctions, which the Federal Reserve Banks run as its fiscal agent. There are two kinds of bid:

  • Competitive bid: states the yield (or discount rate) the bidder will accept. It may be filled in full, in part or not at all. Primary dealers and large institutions bid this way.
  • Non-competitive bid: agrees to accept whatever yield the auction sets. It is guaranteed to be filled, up to a size cap ($10 million per auction). Small investors and TreasuryDirect buyers bid this way.

The Treasury uses a single-price auction (also called a uniform-price or Dutch auction). Every winning bidder receives the same yield, the highest yield accepted, called the stop-out or high yield.

Worked example
  1. Offering: $50 billion. Non-competitive bids: $5 billion, filled first. That leaves $45 billion for competitive bids.
  2. Competitive bids, lowest yield first: $15 billion at 4.10%, $20 billion at 4.12%, $15 billion at 4.13%, $10 billion at 4.15%.
  3. Fill from the lowest yield: $15 + $20 = $35 billion. The last $10 billion comes from the 4.13% bids, prorated (10 of 15). The 4.15% bids get nothing.
  4. Stop-out yield = 4.13%. Every winning bidder, including the non-competitive bidders and those who bid 4.10%, receives 4.13%. Everyone gets the same price.
Treasury --(new securities)--> Fed Banks (fiscal agent)
Bidders --(non-comp: filled first, at stop-out)--> auction
Bidders --(comp: lowest yields filled first)--> auction
All winners --(pay one price = stop-out yield)--> Treasury

Why single-price: bidders need not shade their bids down for fear of overpaying relative to others, so they bid closer to true value, and the Treasury borrows more cheaply.

Agencies: explicit guarantee versus implied backing

"Agency" securities come from two kinds of issuer, and the SIE cares about the difference.

GNMA (Ginnie Mae)FNMA (Fannie Mae), FHLMC (Freddie Mac)
What it isGovernment corporation inside HUDGovernment-sponsored enterprises (GSEs): chartered by Congress but shareholder-owned companies
BackingExplicit full faith and credit of the U.S.Implied: the market assumes support; no legal guarantee
What it doesGuarantees MBS that approved lenders issue from FHA/VA-insured loansBuys conventional mortgages from lenders, pools them, guarantees the MBS
Interest taxTaxable at federal, state and local levelsTaxable at federal, state and local levels

Two traps sit in this table. First, GNMA's government guarantee does not carry the Treasury's state tax exemption: GNMA interest is fully taxable. Second, "implied" backing is not a guarantee. The market's assumption was tested in September 2008, when Fannie and Freddie were placed in federal conservatorship. Some other agency debt (Federal Home Loan Banks, Farm Credit) is state-tax-exempt, but mortgage-backed securities from GNMA, FNMA and FHLMC are not.

Mortgage pass-throughs: prepayment and extension risk

A mortgage-backed security (MBS) pass-through gives its holder a pro-rata share of a pool of home loans. Homeowners' monthly payments flow through to investors, less servicing and guarantee fees.

Homeowners --(monthly principal + interest)--> Servicer
Servicer --(less servicing fee)--> Guarantor/pool
Pool --(monthly P&I, pro rata)--> MBS investors

Three things differ from a Treasury. Payments are monthly. Each payment includes principal, so the balance shrinks over time. And homeowners can prepay at any time, by refinancing, moving or paying down early. Investors therefore do not know when their principal will come back. MBS are analysed by average life (the expected average time until principal is returned) under an assumed prepayment speed, not by stated maturity.

Worked example
  1. An investor owns 1% of a $500 million pass-through pool.
  2. This month the pool collects $2.0 million of interest, $1.0 million of scheduled principal and $4.0 million of prepayments from homeowners who refinanced.
  3. The investor receives 1% × $7.0 million = $70,000, before fees. Only $20,000 of it is interest; $50,000 is the investor's own principal coming back.
  4. The investor's share of the pool falls by that $50,000. If rates are lower now, the returned principal can only be reinvested at those lower rates.
  5. Reading "yield" on an MBS without separating interest from returned principal overstates income. That is a suitability point as well as a math point.

The homeowner's right to prepay is a call option held against the investor. It creates two opposite risks:

Rates FALL --> homeowners refinance --> prepayments speed up
  --> principal back early, reinvested at lower rates
  --> PREPAYMENT RISK; price gains capped

Rates RISE --> refinancing stops --> prepayments slow
  --> average life lengthens at a below-market coupon
  --> EXTENSION RISK; price falls further

Either way, the investor ends up with the wrong duration: short when rates fall (when long would pay), long when rates rise (when short would help). That is negative convexity, the same shape as the callable bond on Day 5.

CMOs: slicing the risk into tranches

A collateralized mortgage obligation (CMO) takes a pool of mortgages or pass-throughs and divides its cash flows into classes called tranches, each with its own rules for receiving principal. The total prepayment and extension risk is unchanged; it is redistributed. Safer tranches exist only because a riskier tranche absorbs the difference.

TrancheHow it worksRisk it carries
PAC (planned amortization class)Principal paid on a fixed schedule across a range of prepayment speedsMost protection: shielded from both prepayment and extension risk; lowest yield
TAC (targeted amortization class)Principal paid to a target at one assumed speedProtected from prepayment risk, not extension risk
Companion (support)Absorbs excess prepayments and shortfalls so PACs and TACs stay on scheduleLeast protection: most prepayment and extension risk; highest yield
Z-tranche (accrual)Receives no cash until earlier tranches are retired; interest accrues onto its principalBehaves like a zero: longest life, most price volatility

In a plain sequential CMO, all principal goes to tranche A until it is retired, then to B, and so on. Short tranches suit investors who want short average lives; long ones suit liability matchers. CMOs are complex, and a rep must be able to explain that a tranche's average life is an estimate, not a promise.

Money-market instruments

The money market is the market for debt maturing in about a year or less. Short maturity means little interest-rate risk; high-quality issuers mean little credit risk; active dealer markets mean high liquidity. That combination is why money-market paper serves as a cash substitute, and why its yields are low. Most of it is issued at a discount, like T-bills.

InstrumentWho borrowsBackingKey facts
Treasury billU.S. TreasuryFull faith and credit (unsecured)4–52 weeks; discount; state-tax-exempt
Commercial paper (CP)Corporations, finance companiesUnsecured promissory noteExempt from registration if ≤ 270 days; typically $100,000+ denominations; buyers are institutions and money-market funds
Banker's acceptance (BA)An importer, with a bank's guaranteeThe accepting bank's obligationFinances international trade; time draft; generally 270 days or less; discount
Negotiable CDBanksThe bank; FDIC insurance only up to $250,000 per depositor, per bank, per ownership category"Jumbo" size (typically $100,000+); tradable before maturity
Repurchase agreement (repo)Dealers and other securities holdersCollateral: typically Treasuries or agency MBSA collateralized loan, often overnight
Fed fundsBanksUnsecuredOvernight loans of reserves between banks; the FOMC targets their rate

A banker's acceptance works like this: an importer needs to pay a foreign exporter in, say, 90 days. Its bank "accepts" a time draft, which makes the bank liable for payment. The exporter can hold it or sell it at a discount in the market. The bank's name, not the importer's, is what makes it tradable.

Repo: a sale on paper, a loan in substance

In a repurchase agreement, one party sells securities and agrees to buy them back at a slightly higher price on a set date. Legally it is two trades. Economically it is a collateralized loan: the cash borrower pledges securities, and the price difference is interest (the repo rate). The other side of the same trade, lending cash against collateral, is a reverse repo.

Start: Dealer --(Treasuries)--> Cash lender
       Cash lender --(cash: value minus haircut)--> Dealer
End:   Dealer --(cash + repo interest)--> Cash lender
       Cash lender --(same Treasuries)--> Dealer
Worked example
  1. A dealer holds Treasuries worth $100 million and repos them overnight. With a 2% haircut, the lender advances $98 million.
  2. At an illustrative 4.30% repo rate (repo accrues on actual/360): interest = $98,000,000 × 0.043 ÷ 360 = $11,705.56 for one night.
  3. The dealer repurchases for $98,011,705.56. If it defaults, the lender keeps the collateral, and the $2 million haircut is its cushion against a price drop.

Risk map: which risk lives where

Content Outline section 2.2 asks you to match products to risks. For today's securities:

SecurityDefault riskInterest-rate riskMain other risk
T-billNone in practiceVery lowReinvestment risk: it matures quickly and must be rolled
30-year Treasury bondNone in practiceHighInflation (purchasing-power) risk
TIPSNone in practiceModerate (to real yields)Low inflation risk; phantom income
STRIPSNone in practiceHighest for the maturityNo reinvestment risk; phantom income
GNMA pass-throughNone (explicit guarantee)ModeratePrepayment and extension risk
CMO companion trancheDepends on the collateralHigh and unstableThe most prepayment and extension risk
Commercial paperLow but real (issuer credit)Very lowRollover and liquidity risk in a crisis

The pattern: the U.S. guarantee removes default risk and nothing else. Maturity and the timing of cash flows decide the rest.

Why money funds are "safe," and what breaking the buck meant

Money-market mutual funds hold this paper and, for most of their history, kept a stable $1.00 net asset value (NAV) per share. SEC Rule 2a-7 restricts their maturities, credit quality and diversification so that the $1.00 stays credible. Breaking the buck means the NAV falls below $1.00. The famous case: in September 2008 the Reserve Primary Fund held Lehman Brothers commercial paper when Lehman failed, and its NAV fell to $0.97. Investors pulled cash from prime funds, the CP market froze, and the Treasury and Fed stepped in with emergency support. Later SEC reforms tightened liquidity requirements and moved institutional prime funds to a floating NAV. The exam lesson: money-market funds are low-risk, not riskless, and are not FDIC-insured.

Understand
  • Bills are discount instruments; notes and bonds pay semiannual coupons; TIPS index the principal to CPI; STRIPS are Treasury zeros.
  • Treasury auctions fill non-competitive bids first, then competitive bids from the lowest yield; every winner gets the single stop-out yield.
  • GNMA is explicitly guaranteed but fully taxable; FNMA and FHLMC are GSEs with implied backing.
  • Pass-throughs: rates fall → prepayment risk; rates rise → extension risk. CMOs reallocate both: PAC safest, companion riskiest, Z like a zero.
  • Money-market paper is short, liquid and high quality: T-bills, CP, BAs, negotiable CDs, repo, fed funds.
  • Repo is a collateralized loan with a haircut; breaking the buck means a money fund's NAV fell below $1.00.
MEMORIZE:
  • T-bill maturities → 4, 6, 8, 13, 17, 26 and 52 weeks
  • Treasury notes → 2–10 years
  • Treasury bonds → 20 and 30 years
  • Treasury interest → federally taxable; exempt from state and local tax
  • GNMA → the only agency with an explicit U.S. guarantee; interest taxable at all levels
  • Commercial paper → exempt from registration if maturity ≤ 270 days
  • Banker's acceptances → finance international trade
  • CMO protection → PAC most, companion least
  • Non-competitive bid → guaranteed fill at the auction's yield
Exam traps
  • A T-bill's ask yield is higher than its bid yield → the BID yield is higher (higher discount = lower price).
  • GNMA is government-guaranteed, so it is state-tax-exempt like Treasuries → GNMA interest is taxable at ALL levels.
  • CMOs eliminate prepayment risk → they redistribute prepayment AND extension risk; PACs have the most protection, companions the least.
  • A repo is a sale of securities → economically it is a collateralized loan.
  • TIPS adjust the coupon rate for inflation → the PRINCIPAL adjusts; the rate is fixed (the dollar coupon moves because the principal does).
  • Rates falling helps MBS holders the way it helps Treasury holders → prepayments cap the gain.
  • A T-bill has a coupon → it is bought at a discount and matures at par.
Trader connection

Repo is the financing engine of every rates desk. A dealer long $5 billion of Treasuries does not pay cash for them; it repos them out overnight and rolls the loan daily. The haircut is the collateral margin, the same idea as margin on a stock loan. When haircuts rise or repo rates spike, leveraged holders must sell. Funding desks and treasury/cash managers sit on the other side, lending cash against collateral.

MBS negative convexity moves the whole curve. When rates fall, MBS durations shorten as prepayments speed up. Servicers and MBS holders who hedge to a target duration must buy duration (buy Treasuries or receive fixed in swaps), which pushes rates down further. When rates rise, they sell. MBS analytics teams model prepayment speeds for exactly this reason.

The Treasury basis trade: buy the cash Treasury, finance it in repo, and sell the matching Treasury futures contract, collecting the small gap between the futures-implied price and the cash-plus-financing cost. It is highly leveraged, so it depends entirely on repo remaining available; when funding and margin tighten (as in March 2020), the unwind can move the Treasury market itself.

Check yourself

Q1 All of the following are standard Treasury bill maturities EXCEPT:
A. 4 weeks
B. 13 weeks
C. 52 weeks
D. 2 years

Answer: D. Bills run 4, 6, 8, 13, 17, 26 and 52 weeks. Two years is the shortest Treasury note, a coupon security, not a discount bill. A, B and C are all regular bill maturities.

Q2 A customer in a high-tax state buys a GNMA pass-through. The interest is:
A. Exempt from federal tax, taxable by the state
B. Taxable at the federal, state and local levels
C. Taxable federally, exempt from state and local tax
D. Exempt from all taxes because GNMA is government-guaranteed

Answer: B. GNMA has the explicit U.S. guarantee, but its interest is fully taxable. C describes Treasury interest, the tempting neighbour. D confuses credit backing with tax status. A describes a typical municipal bond held out of state.

Q3 Which of the following are money-market instruments?
I. Commercial paper
II. Treasury bills
III. Newly issued 10-year Treasury notes
IV. Bankers' acceptances
A. I and II only
B. II and III only
C. I, II and IV only
D. I, II, III and IV

Answer: C. Money-market instruments mature in about a year or less: CP (≤ 270 days to be exempt), T-bills (≤ 52 weeks) and BAs. A newly issued 10-year note is a capital-market security. A leaves out BAs; D wrongly includes the note.

Q4 An investor owns $1,000 of TIPS with a 1.5% coupon. Over the year the CPI rises 4%. What is the annual interest on the adjusted principal?
A. $15.60
B. $15.00
C. $55.00
D. $41.50

Answer: A. New principal = $1,000 × 1.04 = $1,040; interest = 1.5% × $1,040 = $15.60. B ignores the adjustment. C adds inflation to the coupon rate (5.5%), which is how TIPS do NOT work. D adds the $40 principal change to the unadjusted coupon.

Q5 Mortgage rates fall sharply. Which CMO tranche is most exposed to prepayment risk?
A. PAC
B. TAC
C. Z-tranche
D. Companion

Answer: D. Companion (support) tranches absorb the excess prepayments so PACs and TACs can stay on schedule, so they take the most prepayment and extension risk. A has the most protection. B is specifically protected against prepayment risk (though not extension). C gets principal last.

Q6 A 13-week T-bill is quoted 3.92 bid, 3.88 ask. A customer buying the bill will pay a price based on:
A. 3.92, the higher yield
B. 3.88, which gives the higher dollar price
C. 3.90, the midpoint
D. 3.88, which gives the lower dollar price

Answer: B. Bills are quoted on a discount-yield basis, so the ask yield is lower than the bid yield, and a lower discount means a higher price. The customer buys at the ask: 3.88, the higher price. A is the bid, where a customer would sell. D has the right number and the wrong price logic.

Q7 A dealer sells Treasuries to a money-market fund and agrees to buy them back the next day at a slightly higher price. In economic terms, this is:
A. A collateralized loan to the dealer
B. An outright sale followed by an unrelated purchase
C. An unsecured loan of reserves between banks
D. A banker's acceptance

Answer: A. A repo is a short-term loan: the fund lends cash, holds the Treasuries as collateral, and earns the price difference as interest. B describes the legal form, not the substance. C is fed funds, which are unsecured. D is a bank-guaranteed trade draft.

Q8 Which statement about a non-competitive bid in a Treasury auction is TRUE?
A. It specifies the lowest yield the bidder will accept.
B. It is filled only after all competitive bids.
C. It is guaranteed to be filled at the yield set by the auction.
D. It receives the yield the bidder submitted.

Answer: C. A non-competitive bidder accepts the stop-out yield in return for a guaranteed fill, up to the size cap. A and D describe competitive bids (and even competitive winners receive the single stop-out yield, not their own bid). B is backwards: non-competitive bids are filled first.

Day 8 · Municipal securities: tax-exempt credit

FINRA 2.1, 2.2, 1.1 · pairs with the Day 8 edition (~100 min)

Municipal bonds are ordinary credit instruments with one extraordinary feature: the interest is usually free of federal income tax, which changes who owns them, how they are priced and how they are regulated. The SIE tests the backing of each bond type, the alphabet of short-term notes, the tax arithmetic and the MSRB's odd position as a rule-writer with no enforcement arm. On a desk, the same ideas show up as credit analysis, tax-adjusted relative value and a hedge that never quite fits.

Who borrows, and why investors accept a lower coupon

A municipal security ("muni") is debt issued by a state, a city, a county, a school district, a public authority (toll roads, airports, water systems) or a U.S. territory. Munis are exempt securities: they do not register with the SEC under the Securities Act of 1933, and the issuers do not file periodic reports under the 1934 Act. What they are not exempt from is the antifraud rules. Lying in a muni offering document is securities fraud exactly as it would be for a corporation.

The investor gives up yield in exchange for the tax break. A AAA muni might yield less than a Treasury of the same maturity, even though the Treasury is the safer credit. The reason is that the muni buyer keeps all of the coupon and the Treasury buyer shares part of it with the IRS. So muni prices are set by the after-tax return that top-bracket buyers demand.

Issuer (city) --(bonds)--> Underwriter --(bonds)--> Investors
Investors --(cash)--> Underwriter --(cash - spread)--> Issuer
Issuer --(taxes or project revenue)--> Paying agent
Paying agent --(tax-free coupons)--> Investors
Bond counsel: legal opinion (validity + tax exemption)
Municipal advisor: advises the ISSUER, owes it a fiduciary duty

Deals are sold either negotiated (the issuer picks an underwriter and negotiates terms) or competitive (underwriters bid and the lowest borrowing cost wins). The distinction matters for the political-contribution rule later in the chapter.

General obligation vs revenue: what pays you back

Every muni question starts with one question of your own: which cash flow services this debt?

A general obligation (GO) bond is backed by the issuer's full faith, credit and taxing power. States pay GOs mainly from income and sales taxes; local governments mainly from property (ad valorem) taxes. Because a GO pledges taxpayers' money, it usually needs voter approval and must fit inside a statutory debt limit. GOs fund things that do not earn money: schools, police stations, parks.

A revenue bond is backed only by the revenue of the project it finances: tolls, water and sewer fees, airport landing fees, hospital revenue, stadium or convention-center income. No taxing power stands behind it. Because taxpayers are not on the hook, it normally needs no voter approval and sits outside debt limits. Instead, the investor gets protection from two documents: a feasibility study (an engineering and economic report showing the project can earn enough) and the bond resolution's covenants (promises such as a rate covenant to set fees high enough to cover debt service, plus insurance and maintenance covenants).

General obligationRevenue bond
BackingFull faith, credit and taxing powerUser fees and project revenue only
Voter approvalUsually requiredUsually not
Debt limitAppliesUsually not
Credit analysisTax base, debt per capita, collection rates, budgetFeasibility study, coverage ratio, covenants, competing facilities
Typical projectsSchools, public safety, parksToll roads, utilities, airports, convention centers
Typical structure/quoteSerial maturities, quoted on yieldOften term bonds, quoted as % of par

Revenue bonds pay out through a flow of funds. In the usual net revenue pledge, operating and maintenance costs come first, then debt service, then reserves:

Project revenue
  --> 1. Operations and maintenance
  --> 2. Debt service (bondholders)
  --> 3. Debt service reserve
  --> 4. Renewal/replacement, surplus

The coverage ratio (net revenue ÷ debt service) is the revenue-bond analyst's key number. Coverage of 1.5× means revenue could fall a third before bondholders go unpaid.

The hybrids, and the one that is really corporate credit

  • Double-barreled bond: a revenue bond that is also backed by taxing power if revenue falls short. Two sources of repayment; it is rated and treated like a GO.
  • Moral obligation bond: a revenue bond where the state legislature is permitted but not legally obligated to appropriate money to cover a shortfall. The backstop is political will, not law.
  • Special tax bond: backed by one specific tax, such as a gasoline, tobacco or hotel tax, and not by general taxing power. It is a revenue-type bond.
  • Special assessment bond: repaid by a charge on only the properties that benefit (new sidewalks, sewers or streetlights in one district).
  • Industrial development revenue bond (IDR or IDB): the municipality issues the bond, builds a facility and leases it to a corporation. The corporation's lease payments pay the bondholders. The credit you are underwriting is the company's, not the town's. Interest on such private-activity bonds may also be a preference item for the alternative minimum tax (AMT).

A convention center, stadium or airport financed by its own revenue is a revenue bond, and it is still an exempt security. The exam likes to ask whether "convention-center bonds" are revenue bonds. They are.

Short-term notes: bridging a cash-flow gap

Municipalities collect money in lumps (property taxes twice a year, grants when Washington pays) but spend it evenly. Short-term notes, usually a year or less, bridge the gap and are repaid from the specific inflow they anticipate. Match the letters to the cash flow and you have the whole topic.

NoteStands forRepaid from
TANTax anticipation noteUpcoming tax collections
RANRevenue anticipation noteOther expected revenue (for example, state aid)
TRANTax and revenue anticipation noteBoth taxes and revenue
BANBond anticipation noteProceeds of a future long-term bond sale
GANGrant anticipation noteA promised grant, often federal
CLNConstruction loan notePermanent financing once construction is done

Short-term muni notes carry their own rating scale: Moody's MIG ratings (Municipal Investment Grade, MIG 1 best down to MIG 3, then speculative grade). Long-term bonds use the familiar Aaa/AAA scales.

Tax treatment: what is exempt and what is not

Four rules cover almost every exam question:

  1. Interest is exempt from federal income tax.
  2. In-state interest is usually exempt from state (and local) tax too. A New York resident holding a New York City bond is "triple tax-exempt": free of federal, state and city tax. Buy an out-of-state muni and your own state usually taxes the interest.
  3. U.S. territories are triple tax-exempt everywhere. Bonds of Puerto Rico, Guam and the U.S. Virgin Islands are exempt from federal, state and local tax for residents of any state. That is why Puerto Rico debt sat in mutual funds all over the country before its 2016–2022 restructuring.
  4. Capital gains are taxable. Only the interest is exempt. Sell a muni for more than your adjusted cost basis and the gain is taxed like any other capital gain.

To compare a muni with a taxable bond you put them on the same after-tax footing. A taxable bond keeps (1 − t) of its yield after tax, so the muni's yield has to be scaled up by 1 ÷ (1 − t) to compare.

TEY = Muni yield ÷ (1 − t)

TEY
tax-equivalent yield: the taxable yield that would leave you with the same after-tax income
Muni yield
the tax-free yield on the municipal bond
t
the investor's marginal (top) federal tax rate, as a decimal

Muni-equivalent yield = Taxable yield × (1 − t)

Taxable yield
yield on a corporate bond or other fully taxable bond
t
marginal tax rate
Result
the tax-free yield that pays the same after tax
Worked example
  1. A 35%-bracket investor chooses between a muni at 3.50% and a corporate at 5.00%.
  2. TEY of the muni = 3.50% ÷ (1 − 0.35) = 3.50% ÷ 0.65 = 5.38%.
  3. Equivalently, the corporate after tax = 5.00% × 0.65 = 3.25%.
  4. 5.38% > 5.00% (or 3.50% > 3.25%): the muni wins for this investor.
  5. For a 12%-bracket investor: 3.50% ÷ 0.88 = 3.98%, below 5.00%. The corporate wins. The higher the bracket, the more a muni is worth.

Who munis suit: investors in high brackets with taxable accounts. Who they do not suit: anyone holding them inside a traditional IRA, 401(k) or other tax-deferred account. The account already shelters the income, and withdrawals from a traditional IRA are taxed as ordinary income, so the muni's exemption is thrown away and you are left with a lower yield. Tax-exempt institutions (pension funds, charities) have the same problem.

Premium bonds: basis steps down, and no loss at maturity

If you buy a muni above par, the tax code makes you amortize the premium: each year your cost basis is reduced by a slice of it. For munis the amortization is not deductible (the interest it offsets is tax-free anyway). By maturity the basis has fallen to par, you receive par, and there is no capital loss. The exam uses straight-line amortization.

Worked example
  1. Buy a muni at 110 ($1,100) with 10 years to maturity. Premium = $100.
  2. Annual amortization = $100 ÷ 10 = $10. Basis falls $10 a year.
  3. After 4 years: basis = $1,100 − 4 × $10 = $1,060.
  4. Sell then at 104 ($1,040): capital loss = $1,060 − $1,040 = $20 (not $60).
  5. Hold to maturity instead: basis = $1,000, redemption = $1,000, gain/loss = $0.

The mirror case: a muni issued at a discount (original issue discount) accretes that discount as tax-exempt interest. A discount picked up in the secondary market (market discount) is treated differently and is taxable when realized. Know that the distinction exists; the SIE concentrates on the premium case.

How munis are quoted

Many GO issues are serial bonds: one deal split into slices maturing every year. With dozens of maturities trading thinly, dealers quote each slice on a yield basis ("5s of 2031 at a 3.20 basis"), leaving the customer's price to be worked out from the yield. Term bonds, where the whole issue matures on one date (common for revenue bonds), trade actively enough to be quoted as a percentage of par, like corporates. They are called dollar bonds. Just memorize Serial = yield basis; term = dollar price.

Who writes the rules and who enforces them

The Municipal Securities Rulemaking Board (MSRB) was created by the Securities Acts Amendments of 1975. It writes rules for firms that deal in munis and for municipal advisors. It has no enforcement power. Enforcement is split by type of firm:

Who is being policedWho enforces MSRB rules
Broker-dealersFINRA (and the SEC)
Bank dealers (muni departments of banks)Bank regulators: Comptroller of the Currency, Federal Reserve, FDIC
Municipal advisors that are not dealersSEC

The MSRB runs EMMA (Electronic Municipal Market Access), the free public website for official statements, continuing disclosures and trade prices. Trades feed EMMA through the Real-Time Transaction Reporting System (RTRS): dealers must report muni trades within 15 minutes of execution.

The official statement is the muni equivalent of a prospectus. It is prepared by the issuer (with its advisors); it is not filed with or reviewed by the SEC, because the offering is exempt. Under SEC Rule 15c2-12 the underwriter of most new issues must obtain it and must make sure the issuer has committed to ongoing disclosure. Bond counsel's legal opinion (that the bonds are validly issued and the interest is tax-exempt) is printed with it.

Municipal advisors advise the issuer on structure and timing. Since the Dodd-Frank Act they must register with the SEC and the MSRB, and they owe a fiduciary duty to the municipal entities they advise. The underwriter is on the other side of the table, buying the bonds.

G-37: pay-to-play

A Municipal Finance Professional (MFP) is a person at a dealer primarily engaged in municipal underwriting, trading, sales to issuers, financial advisory or research, or who solicits municipal issuer business. A registered rep who only sells munis to retail customers is not an MFP. Rule G-37 stops dealers buying underwriting mandates with campaign cash: an MFP may give up to $250 per election to an official, but only one the MFP is entitled to vote for. A larger contribution (or any contribution to an official the MFP cannot vote for) triggers a 2-year ban on negotiated municipal business with that issuer. Just memorize $250, can-vote, 2 years, negotiated.

MFP gives $1,000 to mayor --> firm banned 2 years
  --> no NEGOTIATED deals with that city
  --> competitive bids still allowed

Where you would meet this
