Public finance banking structures GO and revenue deals and writes the official statement with bond counsel. Muni trading desks make markets in thousands of thinly traded CUSIPs, reporting each print within 15 minutes. Wealth management is where the tax arithmetic is sold: separately managed muni ladders for high-bracket clients, kept out of IRAs. Credit events are real: Detroit's 2013 Chapter 9 bankruptcy and Puerto Rico's restructuring both imposed losses on bondholders who had assumed a municipality could not default.

Understand
  • GO = taxing power, voter approval, debt limits. Revenue = project cash flow, feasibility study, covenants, coverage.
  • Double-barreled = revenue + taxes (treated as GO); moral obligation = legislature may, not must, pay; special tax = one named tax; special assessment = benefiting properties; IDR = a corporation's lease payments.
  • Notes are named after the cash flow that repays them: TAN, RAN, TRAN, BAN, GAN, CLN.
  • Interest is federally exempt; in-state usually state-exempt; territories triple-exempt; capital gains taxable.
  • TEY scales a muni yield up by 1 ÷ (1 − t); munis suit high brackets, never IRAs.
  • MSRB writes rules; FINRA, the SEC and the bank regulators enforce them. EMMA discloses; RTRS reports prices; G-37 polices contributions.
  • Official statement ≠ prospectus: munis are exempt from registration, not from antifraud. Municipal advisors owe issuers a fiduciary duty.
MEMORIZE:
  • Tax-equivalent yield → Muni yield ÷ (1 − tax rate)
  • Muni-equivalent yield → Taxable yield × (1 − tax rate)
  • MSRB created by → Securities Acts Amendments of 1975
  • MSRB enforcement → none; FINRA/SEC (BDs), bank regulators (banks)
  • G-37 de minimis → $250 per election, only where the MFP can vote
  • G-37 violation → 2-year ban on negotiated business with that issuer
  • Muni trade reporting → RTRS within 15 minutes
  • Short-term muni note ratings → MIG (Moody's)
  • Triple tax-exempt anywhere → Puerto Rico, Guam, USVI
  • BAN → repaid from a future bond sale
  • Serial bonds → yield basis; term bonds → dollar price (% of par)
  • Muni offering document → official statement
  • Muni premium → amortized, basis steps down to par, no loss at maturity
Exam traps
  • "IDRs are backed by the municipality" → backed by a CORPORATION's lease payments; rate the company.
  • "Munis are a good fit for an IRA" → the IRA already defers tax; the exemption is wasted and withdrawals are taxed as ordinary income.
  • "Munis are exempt from all federal securities law" → exempt from '33 Act registration, NOT from antifraud.
  • "The MSRB enforces its rules" → it writes them only.
  • "Muni gains are tax-free" → only interest is exempt; capital gains are taxable.
  • "A rep who sells munis to retail is an MFP" → only if primarily engaged in muni banking/trading/advice or soliciting issuer business.
  • "A premium muni held to maturity produces a capital loss" → basis has been amortized to par; no loss.
Trader connection

Muni pricing is credit plus tax. The muni/Treasury ratio (for example, 10-year AAA muni yield ÷ 10-year Treasury yield) is the desk's quick relative-value gauge: when the ratio rises toward or above 100%, munis are cheap even before the tax benefit, which usually happens when forced selling hits (fund outflows in March 2020 pushed ratios far above 100%). When the ratio is low, munis are rich and the tax break is fully priced.

Muni dealers hedge inventory rate risk with Treasury futures (or swaps) because there is no liquid muni futures market to use. The hedge carries basis risk: muni yields do not move one-for-one with Treasuries, and in a sell-off driven by muni fund redemptions munis can cheapen while Treasuries rally, so both legs lose together. Treat the hedge ratio as an estimate, not an identity.

Check yourself

Q1 An investor in the 32% bracket is considering a municipal bond yielding 4.00%. What is the tax-equivalent yield?
A. 5.88%
B. 5.28%
C. 2.72%
D. 6.40%

Answer: A. TEY = 4.00% ÷ (1 − 0.32) = 4.00% ÷ 0.68 = 5.88%. C (2.72%) multiplies by 0.68, which is the muni-equivalent formula run backwards. B is 4.00% × 1.32, a common shortcut error that understates the answer. D has no basis in the formula.

Q2 A county plans a long-term bond sale for a new jail next spring but needs to begin construction now. Which note is it most likely to issue?
A. TAN
B. RAN
C. BAN
D. GAN

Answer: C. A bond anticipation note is repaid from the proceeds of a future bond sale. A TAN is repaid from taxes and a RAN from other revenue, neither of which is the stated source here. A GAN depends on a grant.

Q3 Rules of the MSRB are enforced by all of the following EXCEPT:
A. FINRA
B. the SEC
C. the Comptroller of the Currency
D. the MSRB

Answer: D. The MSRB writes rules but has no enforcement arm. FINRA enforces them for broker-dealers, the SEC has overall authority, and bank regulators such as the Comptroller of the Currency enforce them for bank dealers. Choice C is tempting if you forget that banks deal in munis too.

Q4 Which statements about an industrial development revenue bond are TRUE?
I. It is issued by a municipality
II. It is backed by the municipality's taxing power
III. It is backed by lease payments from a corporation
IV. Its credit quality depends on the corporation
A. I and II
B. I, III and IV
C. II and IV
D. II and III

Answer: B. The municipality issues the bond and leases the facility to a company whose lease payments service the debt, so the credit analysis is of the corporation. II is false: no taxing power stands behind an IDR. Every choice containing II is wrong.

Q5 A customer buys a municipal bond at 108 with 8 years to maturity. Three years later she sells it at 103. What is the tax result?
A. $50 capital loss
B. No gain or loss
C. $20 capital loss
D. $30 capital gain

Answer: C. Premium $80 ÷ 8 years = $10 per year. After 3 years the basis is $1,080 − $30 = $1,050. Sale at $1,030 gives a $20 loss. A ignores amortization ($1,080 − $1,030). B would be right only if she held to maturity. D reverses the direction.

Q6 A municipal finance professional contributes $1,000 to the re-election campaign of the mayor of the city where she lives and votes. Her firm underwrites the city's bonds. What is the result under MSRB Rule G-37?
A. The firm is barred from negotiated municipal business with the city for 2 years
B. The contribution is allowed because she can vote for the mayor
C. The firm is barred from all municipal business with the city for 1 year
D. She must resign as an MFP

Answer: A. Being able to vote only unlocks the $250-per-election de minimis; $1,000 exceeds it, triggering a 2-year ban on negotiated business with that issuer. B misreads the vote condition. C gets both the length and scope wrong (competitive bids are not banned). D is not the sanction.

Q7 A municipal bond fund would be LEAST suitable for which customer?
A. A 60-year-old in the 37% bracket with a taxable brokerage account
B. A couple in the 35% bracket seeking tax-advantaged income
C. A retiree in the 32% bracket seeking steady income in a taxable account
D. A 30-year-old investing through her traditional IRA

Answer: D. Inside a traditional IRA the income is already tax-deferred and every withdrawal is taxed as ordinary income, so the muni's exemption is wasted and she simply earns a lower yield. A, B and C are high-bracket taxable investors, the natural buyers.

Q8 A dealer executes a municipal bond trade at 10:02 a.m. ET. By when must it be reported to the MSRB's RTRS?
A. 10:32 a.m. ET
B. 10:17 a.m. ET
C. End of the trading day
D. Settlement date

Answer: B. Muni trades are reported within 15 minutes of execution: 10:02 + 15 minutes = 10:17. A applies a 30-minute window that does not exist for munis. C and D describe much older or unrelated deadlines.

Day 9 · Risk: every way an investment can hurt you

FINRA 2.2 · pairs with the Day 9 edition (~85 min)

The SIE names eighteen risks and expects you to tell them apart, match each one to the products that carry it and know which ones diversification removes. A risk desk does the same job with more precision: break exposure into factors, then decide which to diversify away, which to hedge and which to be paid for. This chapter gives each risk a definition, what reduces it and a real episode where it cost investors money.

The master split: systematic vs unsystematic

Systematic risk hits the whole market or economy at once. You cannot diversify it away, because every holding is exposed; you can only hedge it (index puts, short futures) or accept less of it (hold more cash or short-duration assets). Unsystematic risk belongs to one company or industry and averages out as you add unrelated holdings. That is the entire case for diversification, and it is the SIE's favourite trap: diversification does not reduce systematic risk.

Systematic (non-diversifiable)Unsystematic (diversifiable)
MarketBusiness
Interest-rateCredit / default of one issuer
Inflation (purchasing power)Regulatory action aimed at one firm or industry
Currency (exchange-rate)
Political (for a whole country)

The other risks (liquidity, call, prepayment, reinvestment, counterparty, leverage) are usually tested on their own rather than sorted into a column. If you are forced to sort one, ask the test question directly: would a bigger portfolio of unrelated holdings make it disappear? Reinvestment risk, for example, rides on market-wide rates, so it behaves systematically.

Add stocks from different industries
  --> company-specific shocks offset
  --> unsystematic risk -> near zero (20-30 names)
  --> what remains = systematic risk
  --> measured by beta, hedged with index products

Two numbers measure risk. Standard deviation measures total volatility of returns (systematic plus unsystematic). Beta measures only the systematic part: how much a security moves with the market.

Expected move ≈ β × Market move

β (beta)
sensitivity to the market; the market itself has β = 1
β > 1
moves more than the market (aggressive)
β < 1
moves less (defensive, e.g. utilities)
β < 0
tends to move opposite the market (e.g. an inverse fund)
Worked example
  1. A stock has β = 1.5. The market rises 10%: expected move ≈ 1.5 × 10% = +15%.
  2. The market falls 20%: ≈ 1.5 × −20% = −30%.
  3. Hedge a $1,000,000 portfolio with β = 1.2: it behaves like $1,200,000 of the index, so the hedge is $1.2 million of index short exposure, not $1 million.

The eighteen risks

1. Market risk

What it is: the risk that the whole market falls and takes your holding with it. What reduces it: not diversification within stocks; hedging with index puts or short index futures, or allocating across asset classes. Real example: from February 19 to March 23, 2020, the S&P 500 fell about 34% as COVID-19 shut economies. Good and bad companies fell together.

2. Systematic risk

What it is: the umbrella term for every economy-wide risk (market, rates, inflation, currency, political). What reduces it: hedging or reducing exposure; never diversification alone. Real example: in 2022, rising inflation and Fed hikes hit everything at once: the S&P 500 returned about −18% and the Bloomberg U.S. Aggregate bond index about −13%. The traditional stock/bond mix gave little protection because one shock drove both.

3. Unsystematic risk

What it is: company- or industry-specific risk (also "specific," "nonsystematic" or "diversifiable" risk). What reduces it: diversification. Real example: Enron went from a Wall Street favourite to bankruptcy in late 2001. Employees whose 401(k)s were concentrated in Enron stock lost retirement savings; an index fund holder barely noticed.

4. Credit risk

What it is: the risk that an issuer's ability to pay weakens, usually seen as a rating downgrade and a wider credit spread, so the bond's price falls even though every coupon is still paid. What reduces it: higher-rated issuers, diversification across issuers, buying credit protection (credit default swaps). Real example: in May 2005 S&P cut both General Motors and Ford to junk. Their bonds fell, and funds limited to investment-grade debt had to sell them.

5. Default risk

What it is: the risk the issuer actually fails to pay interest or principal. Credit risk is the deterioration; default is the failure. What reduces it: the same tools, plus seniority and collateral (secured bonds recover more). Real example: Lehman Brothers filed for bankruptcy on September 15, 2008; the credit-default-swap auction valued its senior bonds at under 10 cents on the dollar.

6. Liquidity risk (and marketability)

What it is: the risk you cannot sell quickly without accepting a much lower price. Marketability is the narrower question of whether a secondary market exists at all; the exam sometimes treats the words as synonyms. What reduces it: holding listed, heavily traded securities, keeping a cash buffer, and not putting money you may need into illiquid products. Real examples: in March 2020 even Treasuries traded poorly and corporate-bond ETFs traded at large discounts to their NAVs until the Fed intervened. In December 2022 Blackstone's non-traded REIT (BREIT) hit its redemption limits and began paying out only part of the withdrawal requests: the classic marketability problem of non-traded REITs and direct participation programs (DPPs).

7. Interest-rate risk

What it is: when market rates rise, existing bond prices fall. Longer maturity and lower coupon mean more sensitivity; a long zero-coupon bond has the most. What reduces it: shorter maturities, a ladder, floating-rate instruments, or hedging with Treasury futures or interest-rate swaps. Real example: Silicon Valley Bank bought long-dated Treasuries and MBS when yields were low; the 2022 rate rise left large unrealized losses, and when depositors ran in March 2023 it had to sell at a loss and failed on March 10.

8. Reinvestment risk

What it is: the risk that coupons and returned principal must be reinvested at lower rates. It is highest for high-coupon callable bonds and zero for zero-coupon bonds held to maturity (there is nothing to reinvest). What reduces it: zeros, non-callable bonds, longer maturities. Real example: after the Fed cut its target to 0–0.25% in March 2020, investors rolling T-bills and CDs through 2021 earned close to nothing.

9. Inflation (purchasing-power) risk

What it is: the risk that rising prices erode what your fixed payments buy. It is highest for fixed-income securities and cash, including fixed annuities and money-market funds. What reduces it: TIPS (principal indexed to CPI), equities and real assets over long periods. Real example: CPI inflation reached 9.1% year-over-year in June 2022, the highest since 1981. A 10-year note bought in 2021 at about 1.5% lost real value every year.

10. Political risk

What it is: the risk that government instability or action (expropriation, sanctions, capital controls) destroys investment value, mainly in foreign markets. It is systematic for that country. What reduces it: diversifying across countries, avoiding markets with weak rule of law, political-risk insurance for direct investment. Real examples: in 2012 Argentina expropriated Repsol's majority stake in the oil company YPF. In 2022, after Russia invaded Ukraine, sanctions and Russian capital controls left foreign holders of Russian stocks unable to sell, and index providers removed Russia at an effectively zero price.

11. Legislative risk

What it is: the risk that a new law (usually tax law) passed by a legislature hurts an investment. What reduces it: diversification across tax treatments; not buying a product only for a tax benefit Congress can remove. Real examples: the Tax Reform Act of 1986 introduced the passive-activity loss rules, wiping out the tax-shelter value of many limited partnerships almost overnight. In 2017 Congress capped the federal SALT deduction at $10,000, which made in-state munis more valuable to residents of high-tax states.

12. Regulatory risk

What it is: the risk that a regulator (not a legislature) changes a rule or acts in a way that hurts a firm or industry. What reduces it: diversification across industries; it is unsystematic when it hits one firm or sector. Real examples: SEC money-market reform (adopted 2014, effective October 2016) forced institutional prime money funds to float their NAV, and investors moved hundreds of billions of dollars into government funds. In March 2019 the FAA and other regulators grounded Boeing's 737 MAX after two crashes.

13. Currency (exchange-rate) risk

What it is: the risk that a foreign investment loses value in dollars because the foreign currency weakens. ADRs carry it even though they trade in dollars. What reduces it: currency forwards or futures, currency-hedged funds. Real example: in July 2022 the euro fell below parity with the dollar for the first time in about 20 years. A U.S. investor in European stocks lost on the currency on top of the local-market decline.

14. Business risk

What it is: the risk that a company's own operations, products or strategy fail: competition, bad management, obsolescence. What reduces it: diversification. Real example: Eastman Kodak failed to profit from the digital camera it had helped invent and filed for Chapter 11 in January 2012.

15. Call risk

What it is: the risk that the issuer redeems a callable bond early, almost always because rates have fallen. You get your principal back just when reinvestment yields are lowest, and the bond's price stops rising near the call price. What reduces it: non-callable bonds, or bonds with long call protection (time until the first call date). Real example: when yields hit record lows in 2020–2021, corporations and municipalities called and refinanced higher-coupon debt in large volumes, handing investors cash to reinvest at far lower yields.

16. Prepayment risk

What it is: the mortgage-backed version of call risk. Homeowners refinance when rates fall, so MBS principal comes back early. Its mirror is extension risk: when rates rise, prepayments slow and the security's life lengthens. What reduces it: PAC tranches of CMOs, which absorb less prepayment variation, or avoiding MBS. Real example: with 30-year mortgage rates below 3% in 2020–2021, a refinancing wave returned MBS principal at a rapid pace; then in 2022 rates passed 7%, prepayments dried up and MBS durations extended.

17. Counterparty risk

What it is: the risk that the other side of a contract (a swap, forward or repo) fails to perform. What reduces it: collateral posted daily under a credit support annex (CSA), netting agreements, and central clearing. Listed options are guaranteed by the Options Clearing Corporation (OCC), so the buyer never relies on the individual writer. Real example: in September 2008 AIG could not meet collateral calls on credit default swaps it had written for banks; the Federal Reserve lent it $85 billion to stop the losses spreading to those counterparties.

18. Leverage risk

What it is: borrowing magnifies percentage gains and losses on the investor's equity, and margin calls can force selling at the worst moment. What reduces it: less borrowing, position limits, liquidity buffers. Real example: in March 2021 Archegos Capital held concentrated positions through total return swaps with several prime brokers. When the stocks fell it missed margin calls, the banks dumped the shares, and Credit Suisse alone lost about $5.5 billion.

Return on equity ≈ Asset return × (Assets ÷ Equity)

Asset return
percentage change in the value of the position
Assets ÷ Equity
the leverage ratio; 2:1 under Reg T's 50% initial margin
Ignored
interest on the loan, which makes the true result slightly worse
Worked example
  1. Buy $20,000 of stock with $10,000 equity and a $10,000 margin loan (2:1).
  2. Stock +10% → position $22,000; loan still $10,000; equity $12,000 → +20%.
  3. Stock −10% → position $18,000; equity $8,000 → −20%.
  4. Stock −50% → position $10,000; equity $0 → −100%. A 50% fall wipes out a 2:1 account.

Telling the look-alikes apart

ScenarioRisk
Congress raises the capital-gains rateLegislative
The SEC adopts a rule that squeezes an industry's feesRegulatory
A competitor's better product takes the company's customersBusiness
A recession drags down every stockMarket (systematic)
Moody's downgrades the issuer from A to BBBCredit
The issuer misses a couponDefault
Rates fall and the issuer redeems at 102Call (then reinvestment)
Rates fall and homeowners refinancePrepayment
A foreign government seizes the companyPolitical

Call and prepayment risk have the same shape: the borrower holds an option to repay, and uses it when rates fall, which is exactly when you would like to keep the high coupon. That is why callable bonds and MBS show negative convexity: limited price gains when rates fall, full losses when rates rise.

The product/risk matrix

ProductRisks the exam pairs with it
Common stockMarket, business; liquidity for small caps
Long Treasury bondInterest-rate, inflation (no meaningful default risk)
T-bills, money-market funds, cashInflation, reinvestment
Callable high-coupon corporateCredit, default, interest-rate, call, reinvestment
Zero-coupon bondHighest interest-rate risk for its maturity; no reinvestment risk
Municipal bondCredit, interest-rate, legislative (tax law), liquidity
MBS / CMOPrepayment, extension, interest-rate
TIPSReal-rate risk; low inflation risk
Foreign stock / ADRCurrency, political
DPP, non-traded REITLiquidity/marketability, legislative
OTC swaps and forwardsCounterparty, leverage
Margin account, leveraged ETFLeverage
Understand
  • Systematic (market, interest-rate, inflation, currency, political) cannot be diversified away; unsystematic (business, credit, one-firm regulatory) can.
  • Beta measures systematic risk; standard deviation measures total volatility.
  • Every one of the eighteen risks has a mechanism, a reducer and a real episode; reason from the mechanism.
  • Products carry bundles of risks; the matrix is what scenario questions test.
MEMORIZE:
  • Systematic → market, interest-rate, inflation, currency, political
  • Unsystematic → business, credit/default of one issuer, regulatory for one firm
  • Diversification reduces → unsystematic risk only
  • Reinvestment risk highest → high-coupon callable bonds
  • Reinvestment risk none → zero-coupon bonds
  • Purchasing-power risk highest → fixed income and cash
  • Liquidity/marketability examples → DPPs, non-traded REITs
  • Beta 1.5 → about 1.5× the market's move
  • 2:1 leverage → doubles percentage gains and losses
  • Legislative → new laws; regulatory → agency rules
Exam traps
  • "Diversification reduces market risk" → it only reduces unsystematic risk.
  • "A tax-law change is regulatory risk" → a law passed by Congress is legislative; a rule from a regulator is regulatory.
  • "Call risk hurts when rates rise" → call and prepayment risk hit when rates FALL; rising rates bring extension risk.
  • "Credit risk means the issuer defaulted" → credit risk is deterioration (downgrade); default is failure to pay.
  • "Liquid and marketable are always the same" → marketability = a market exists; liquidity = selling fast without a big price concession.
  • "Zero-coupon bonds have high reinvestment risk" → they have none; they carry the most interest-rate risk instead.
Trader connection

A risk manager rewrites this list as a factor map: rates, credit, equity, FX, volatility, liquidity, counterparty. Every position is decomposed into exposures (DV01 for rates, CS01 for credit spreads, delta for equity, vega for volatility) so they can be netted and hedged. The SIE's systematic/unsystematic split is the ancestor of that map.

Counterparty risk explains the plumbing of derivatives: bilateral OTC trades carry daily variation-margin posting under a CSA, and standardized products are cleared through a central counterparty so one failure cannot cascade. That is the lesson regulators took from AIG and Lehman.

Leverage converts price risk into liquidity risk. The Archegos pattern: price falls → margin call → the fund cannot pay → brokers sell → prices fall further. Treasury/ALM desks at banks face the same chain on the funding side, as SVB showed: rate risk became a funding run.

Check yourself

Q1 Which risk can an investor reduce by diversifying across many unrelated stocks?
A. Interest-rate risk
B. Inflation risk
C. Business risk
D. Market risk

Answer: C. Business risk is specific to one company and averages out across holdings. Interest-rate, inflation and market risk are systematic: every stock is exposed, so adding more stocks does not remove them. D is the classic distractor.

Q2 A stock has a beta of 1.5. If the market falls 8%, the stock would be expected to fall about:
A. 5.3%
B. 8.0%
C. 9.5%
D. 12.0%

Answer: D. Expected move ≈ β × market move = 1.5 × 8% = 12%. A divides by beta instead of multiplying. B assumes beta 1. C adds 1.5 points instead of scaling.

Q3 Which of the following are systematic risks?
I. Interest-rate risk
II. Business risk
III. Purchasing-power risk
IV. Credit risk of a single issuer
A. I and III
B. II and IV
C. I, II and III
D. I, III and IV

Answer: A. Rate and inflation risk affect the whole market. Business risk and one issuer's credit risk are unsystematic and diversifiable, so any choice containing II or IV is wrong.

Q4 Reinvestment risk is greatest for which bond?
A. A 20-year zero-coupon Treasury
B. A 20-year 8% callable corporate bond
C. A 20-year 3% non-callable corporate bond
D. A 20-year 8% non-callable Treasury bond

Answer: B. A high coupon means a lot of cash to reinvest, and the call feature means principal may come back early when rates have fallen. A has no coupons to reinvest. C pays small coupons. D pays large coupons but cannot be called away.

Q5 Congress passes a law eliminating a tax deduction that made a real estate limited partnership attractive. The partners have suffered from:
A. Legislative risk
B. Regulatory risk
C. Political risk
D. Business risk

Answer: A. A change in law by a legislature is legislative risk, as in the Tax Reform Act of 1986. B would fit a rule adopted by an agency such as the SEC. C refers to government instability or expropriation, usually abroad. D concerns the partnership's own operations.

Q6 All of the following carry significant purchasing-power risk EXCEPT:
A. A money-market fund
B. A fixed annuity
C. Treasury Inflation-Protected Securities
D. A 30-year Treasury bond

Answer: C. TIPS principal is indexed to CPI, so payments rise with inflation. A money fund, a fixed annuity and a fixed-coupon Treasury all pay nominal amounts whose real value falls as prices rise. D is tempting because it has no default risk, but that is a different risk.

Q7 A customer buys $20,000 of stock in a margin account, depositing the Reg T minimum. The stock falls 15%. Ignoring interest, what is the percentage loss on the customer's equity?
A. 15%
B. 30%
C. 7.5%
D. 45%

Answer: B. Reg T 50% → equity $10,000, loan $10,000 (2:1). A 15% fall is a $3,000 loss; $3,000 ÷ $10,000 = 30%. A ignores leverage. C divides instead of multiplying. D triples it.

Q8 Rank these by interest-rate risk, highest to lowest:
I. 13-week T-bill
II. 30-year zero-coupon Treasury
III. 30-year 6% Treasury bond
IV. 5-year Treasury note
A. I, IV, III, II
B. III, II, IV, I
C. II, IV, III, I
D. II, III, IV, I

Answer: D. Longer maturity and lower coupon mean more price sensitivity. The 30-year zero pays everything at the end, so it is most sensitive; the 30-year coupon bond is next; then the 5-year note; the T-bill least. A is the same list lowest to highest. B puts the coupon bond above the zero.

Day 10 · Options I: the contract, the premium and who owes what

FINRA 2.1, 2.2, 3.1 · pairs with the Day 10 edition (~110 min)

An option splits one side of a stock trade into a right and an obligation, and a premium is paid for the difference. The SIE tests the vocabulary (holder, writer, strike, intrinsic, time value), the standardized contract, the OCC's role in exercise and assignment, and the account-opening sequence. A desk runs on the same machinery, where "time value" becomes volatility, theta and gamma, and assignment timing becomes a real P&L risk.

Rights vs obligations: the four positions

An option is a contract giving its buyer the right, but not the obligation, to buy or sell an underlying asset at a fixed strike price (exercise price) until or on an expiration date. It is a derivative: its value comes from the underlying.

  • A call is the right to BUY the underlying at the strike.
  • A put is the right to SELL the underlying at the strike.
  • The buyer (also holder, long) pays the premium and gets the right.
  • The seller (also writer, short) receives the premium and takes the obligation: to sell the stock at the strike if a call is exercised against it, or to buy it at the strike if a put is exercised.

Buyers choose; writers wait. A writer can never force anything, only be assigned. Every position is one of four, and the market view follows from who profits when the stock moves:

PositionRight or obligationPremiumView
BUY CALLRight to buy at KPaysBullish
SELL CALLObligation to sell at KReceivesBearish / neutral
BUY PUTRight to sell at KPaysBearish
SELL PUTObligation to buy at KReceivesBullish / neutral

Read it diagonally: bullish = BUY CALL or SELL PUT; bearish = BUY PUT or SELL CALL. A SELL PUT is bullish because the writer keeps the premium only if the stock stays above the strike; a SELL CALL is bearish because the writer keeps it only if the stock stays below.

Opening trade (premium flow):
Buyer --(premium)--> Buyer's BD --> OCC
OCC --> Writer's BD --(premium)--> Writer
OCC stands between them: buyer to every seller,
seller to every buyer

Why anyone buys or writes one

Three kinds of user sit on the two sides. Hedgers buy options to cap a loss on something they already own or owe (Day 11). Speculators buy options for leverage: a small premium controls 100 shares, and the most they can lose is what they paid. Income writers sell options to collect premium, accepting an obligation in return. A writer who owns the stock behind a call, or who holds cash for a put, is covered; one who does not is uncovered (naked) and must post margin.

Worked example
  1. XYZ is 52. Choice 1: buy 100 shares for $5,200. Choice 2: BUY 1 XYZ 50 call at 3.40 for $340.
  2. At expiration XYZ is 57.20 (+10%). Shares: gain $520 on $5,200 = +10%.
  3. Call: intrinsic = 57.20 − 50 = 7.20 → worth $720; gain $380 on $340 ≈ +112%.
  4. If XYZ is instead 50 or lower: shares lose $200 or more; the call loses its entire $340 (−100%).
  5. Same exposure direction, very different shape: the call trades a small, certain maximum loss for a high chance of losing all of it.

In, at or out of the money

Moneyness compares the stock price (S) with the strike (K) from the holder's point of view: would exercising right now be worth anything?

CallPut
In the money (ITM)S > KS < K
At the money (ATM)S = KS = K
Out of the money (OTM)S < KS > K

Memory aid: "Call up, put down." A call is ITM when the market is up above the strike; a put is ITM when the market is down below it. Calls gain intrinsic value as the market rises; puts gain it as the market falls.

Premium = intrinsic value + time value

Intrinsic value is what the option would be worth if exercised immediately. It can never be negative, because a holder would never exercise at a loss; they would simply walk away. With XYZ at 52, exercising a 55 call means paying $55 for a $52 stock. Nobody does that, so the 55 call's intrinsic value is zero, not −3.

Call intrinsic = max(0, S − K) · Put intrinsic = max(0, K − S)

S
current market price of the underlying
K
strike (exercise) price
max(0, …)
the larger of zero and the bracket: intrinsic value has a floor of zero

Time value (extrinsic value) is everything in the premium above intrinsic value: what the market pays for the chance that the option becomes more valuable before expiration.

Time value = Premium − Intrinsic value

Premium
the option's market price, quoted per share
Intrinsic value
from the formula above
Time value
never negative for a normal listed option; falls to zero at expiration

Contract cost = Premium × 100 × Number of contracts

Premium
quoted in dollars per share (3.40 = $3.40 a share)
100
shares per standard equity option contract

Here is an option chain for stock XYZ at 52.00:

StrikeCall premiumCall intrinsicCall time valuePut premiumPut intrinsicPut time value
457.607.00 (ITM)0.600.400 (OTM)0.40
503.402.00 (ITM)1.401.200 (OTM)1.20
551.200 (OTM)1.204.053.00 (ITM)1.05
600.350 (OTM)0.358.158.00 (ITM)0.15

Three patterns to notice. First, an OTM option's premium is all time value. Second, time value is largest for strikes near the stock price and smallest for deep ITM or deep OTM strikes; deep ITM options behave almost like stock, deep OTM ones almost like lottery tickets. Third, calls and puts at the same strike carry similar time value.

Worked example
  1. BUY 1 XYZ 50 call at 3.40 with XYZ at 52.
  2. Intrinsic = max(0, 52 − 50) = 2.00. The call is in the money by $2.
  3. Time value = 3.40 − 2.00 = 1.40.
  4. Cost = 3.40 × 100 = $340, paid in full (see settlement below).
  5. BUY 1 XYZ 55 put at 4.05: intrinsic = max(0, 55 − 52) = 3.00; time value = 4.05 − 3.00 = 1.05; cost $405.

What drives time value: more time to expiration and higher expected volatility of the underlying both raise it, because both widen the range of outcomes and the holder keeps the upside while the downside is capped at the premium. Interest rates and dividends matter at the margin. As expiration approaches, time value decays, slowly at first and fastest in the final weeks, and at expiration it is zero: the option is worth exactly its intrinsic value.

An illustrative decay path for an ATM option, holding the stock price and volatility constant (time value roughly tracks the square root of time remaining):

Days to expiration906030100
ATM premium (all time value)4.003.272.311.330
Lost in the period–0.730.960.981.33

The first 30 days cost the holder 0.73; the last 10 cost 1.33. That acceleration is why sellers like short-dated options and buyers of short-dated options need the move to come quickly.

What the holder earns at expiration

At expiration only intrinsic value is left, so profit is intrinsic value minus the premium paid. Here are two long positions at several closing prices, per contract:

XYZ at expiry50 call valueBUY CALL 50 @ 3.40 P/L50 put valueBUY PUT 50 @ 1.20 P/L
400−$34010.00+$880
450−$3405.00+$380
48.800−$3401.20$0 (breakeven)
500−$3400−$120
522.00−$1400−$120
53.403.40$0 (breakeven)0−$120
555.00+$1600−$120
6010.00+$6600−$120
7020.00+$1,6600−$120

Notice the kink. Below the strike the call's loss is flat at the premium; above it the call gains $100 per contract for each $1 the stock rises. The put is the mirror image. That kink is what "nonlinear" means, and it is why options can hedge in ways stock cannot. The writer of each option has exactly the opposite P/L in every row: options are a zero-sum transfer before costs. (Day 11 builds all four positions and the combinations out of this table.)

The writer's side: what assignment really costs

A writer thinks in effective prices. The premium received is kept whatever happens, so it adjusts the price at which the writer ends up trading stock if assigned:

  • SELL 1 XYZ 55 call at 1.20. If assigned, the writer sells 100 shares at 55 but has already received 1.20, so the effective sale price is 56.20. Above 56.20 an uncovered writer loses money; the loss has no ceiling because the stock has none.
  • SELL 1 XYZ 50 put at 1.20. If assigned, the writer buys 100 shares at 50 less the 1.20 received: effective purchase price 48.80. Below 48.80 the writer loses, down to a worst case of $4,880 if the stock goes to zero.

These effective prices are the breakevens, and they are the same numbers the buyers of those options use: 55 + 1.20 for the call, 50 − 1.20 for the put. One contract, two sides, one breakeven. Day 11 turns this into the four breakeven formulas.

The standardized contract

Listed options trade on exchanges because every term except price is fixed in advance, which lets thousands of traders trade the same instrument and close positions with anyone:

  • Size: one standard equity option covers 100 shares. Premiums are quoted per share, so multiply by 100.
  • Expiration: standard monthly equity options expire on the third Friday of the month. The last time to trade is 4:00 p.m. ET on that day; the option formally expires at 11:59 p.m. ET. Answer choices often convert these: 4:00 p.m. ET = 3:00 p.m. CT = 1:00 p.m. PT; 11:59 p.m. ET = 8:59 p.m. PT. (Weekly and other short-dated series also exist.)
  • Exercise style: American options can be exercised any time up to expiration; European options only at expiration. Standard equity options are American. Many index options (for example, those on the S&P 500 index itself) are European.
  • Strikes: set by the exchange at standard intervals around the current price, narrower for lower-priced and heavily traded stocks.
  • Adjustments: for a stock split the OCC adjusts the contract (a 2-for-1 split turns one 50 call into two 25 calls on 100 shares each). Ordinary cash dividends do not change the contract.

A contract is named underlying, expiration month, strike, type: "1 XYZ Oct 50 call at 3.40" means one contract, expiring the third Friday of October (October 16, 2026), strike 50, premium $340.

The OCC, exercise and assignment

The Options Clearing Corporation (OCC) is the issuer and guarantor of every listed option. When a trade clears, the OCC becomes the buyer to every seller and the seller to every buyer, so a holder never depends on whether the particular writer can pay. OCC's own guarantee is backed by margin posted by its clearing members.

Opening and closing matter. An opening transaction creates or increases a position (BUY to open, SELL to open); a closing transaction offsets it. Most options are closed in the market rather than exercised: closing captures intrinsic value and any remaining time value, while exercising throws the time value away.

Holder's choicesWriter's choices
Sell the option to closeBuy the option back to close
Exercise itWait; may be assigned at any time (American style)
Let it expire (worthless if OTM)Keep the premium if it expires OTM
Worked example
  1. A month before expiration XYZ is 55 and the 50 call trades at 5.80 (intrinsic 5.00 + time 0.80).
  2. Sell to close: receive $580.
  3. Exercise instead: pay $5,000 for 100 shares worth $5,500 = $500 of value.
  4. Exercising early throws away the $80 of time value. Unless a dividend is coming (see the trader note), closing beats exercising.
Exercise of 1 XYZ 50 call:
Holder --(exercise notice)--> Holder's BD --> OCC
OCC --(assigns at random)--> a BD with short 50 calls
That BD --(random, FIFO or other fair method)--> Writer
Settlement, T+1:
Writer --(100 XYZ shares)--> Holder
Holder --($5,000 = 50 x 100)--> Writer

Assignment is two-stage: the OCC assigns exercise notices to broker-dealers at random; each broker-dealer then assigns them to its customers by a random, first-in-first-out or other fair method it discloses. Only a writer can be assigned; a buyer has nothing to be assigned. The OCC also automatically exercises options that finish in the money by a small threshold at expiration unless the holder instructs otherwise, so a writer of an ITM option should expect assignment.

Settlement, cash and index options

  • Option trades settle T+1: the buyer's premium is due the business day after the trade.
  • Exercise of an equity option settles T+1 in the stock: shares against the strike price.
  • Exercising takes resources: a customer exercising a call must pay the full strike amount (or finance it on margin) by settlement; one exercising a put must deliver the shares, or the exercise creates a short position.
  • Paid in full: long options with 9 months or less to expiration cannot be bought on margin. Just memorize
  • Index options settle in cash. Nobody delivers 500 stocks. The assigned writer pays the holder the intrinsic value times the $100 multiplier.
Worked example
  1. A customer holds 1 index call, strike 5,100, bought at 60.00 ($6,000).
  2. The index settles at 5,200 and the call is exercised.
  3. Cash settlement = (5,200 − 5,100) × $100 = $10,000, paid by the assigned writer.
  4. Net profit = $10,000 − $6,000 = $4,000.

Opening an options account

Options can lose money faster than the customer's cash, so FINRA requires a specific sequence. Before approving the account the firm gathers the customer's investment objectives, employment, income, net worth, liquid assets and investing experience. Then:

1. Deliver the ODD (no later than account approval)
2. ROP approves the account (and the level of trading)
3. Customer may trade
4. Signed options agreement back within 15 days
   of approval (if late, closing trades only)
  • ODD = the Options Disclosure Document, "Characteristics and Risks of Standardized Options," published by the OCC. It must reach the customer at or before account approval.
  • ROP = Registered Options Principal, the supervisor who approves options accounts.
  • Options agreement: the customer's signed promise to follow exchange and OCC rules, including position and exercise limits. It must be returned within 15 days after approval. Just memorize

The ROP's approval is a suitability judgment: the firm must have a reasonable basis to believe the customer can evaluate options risk and bear the losses of the strategies allowed. Uncovered writing, where losses can exceed everything the customer has deposited, gets stricter criteria and minimum-equity requirements under each firm's written procedures. Discretionary options orders need the same written authorization and supervision as any other discretionary trading.

Firms also grant approval in levels (covered calls at the bottom, uncovered writing at the top). That tiering is firm practice; the rule-based sequence is ODD, ROP approval, agreement.

Understand
  • Call = right to BUY; put = right to SELL. Holders have rights and pay premium; writers have obligations and receive it.
  • Premium = intrinsic + time value; intrinsic = max(0, S − K) for calls, max(0, K − S) for puts; never negative.
  • Call ITM when S > K; put ITM when S < K. OTM premium is all time value.
  • Time value reflects time and volatility; it decays to zero at expiration.
  • OCC issues and guarantees; exercise → OCC assigns BDs at random → BDs assign customers randomly, FIFO or other fair method.
  • 100 shares; third Friday; last trade 4:00 p.m. ET; expires 11:59 p.m. ET; American vs European.
  • Trades and exercises settle T+1; index options settle in cash; short-dated long options are paid in full.
  • Account opening: ODD at or before approval → ROP approval → signed agreement within 15 days.
MEMORIZE:
  • Standard equity option contract → 100 shares
  • Contract dollars → premium × 100
  • ODD delivered → no later than account approval
  • Account approved by → Registered Options Principal (ROP)
  • Signed options agreement → within 15 days after approval
  • American style → exercise any time up to expiration
  • European style → exercise only at expiration
  • Index options → settle in cash
  • Option trades and exercises → settle T+1
  • Long options ≤ 9 months → paid in full, not marginable
  • Standard expiration → third Friday; last trade 4:00 p.m. ET; expires 11:59 p.m. ET
  • Issuer and guarantor of listed options → OCC
  • OCC assigns BDs → at random; BDs assign customers → random, FIFO or other fair method
Exam traps
  • "Selling a put is bearish" → SELL PUT is bullish (wants the stock to stay up); SELL CALL is the bearish sale.
  • "Time value grows as expiration nears" → it decays toward zero and is zero at expiration.
  • "An OTM option has some intrinsic value" → none; its whole premium is time value.
  • "The buyer can be assigned" → only writers are assigned; buyers exercise.
  • "A put is ITM when the market is above the strike" → put ITM when market < strike; call ITM when market > strike.
  • "Intrinsic value can be negative for an OTM option" → the floor is zero.
  • "The options agreement must be signed before the first trade" → it is due within 15 days after approval; the ODD is the document due by approval.
  • "Expiration 11:59 p.m. ET is the last time to trade" → trading ends 4:00 p.m. ET; convert time zones carefully.
Trader connection

"Time value" is the SIE name for a set of sensitivities traders manage directly. Delta is how much the option price moves per $1 in the stock (about 0.5 for an ATM call, near 1 deep ITM, near 0 deep OTM); it is also the hedge ratio: a desk long 10 ATM calls is roughly long 500 shares' worth of exposure. Gamma is how fast delta changes: long options have positive gamma, so the position gets longer as the stock rises and shorter as it falls. Theta is the daily decay of time value, the rent a buyer pays for that convexity. Vega is sensitivity to implied volatility. Buying options is buying gamma and paying theta; selling options is the reverse. Because the other inputs (stock price, strike, time, rates) are observable, professionals quote and compare options by implied volatility, the one number that makes a pricing model match the premium. "The 50 calls are trading at 28 vol" is a price; the dollar premium is just its translation.

Assignment around dividends: an ordinary cash dividend lowers the stock on the ex-date but does not adjust the contract. If an ITM call's remaining time value is less than the dividend, holders exercise on the last business day before the ex-date so the T+1 stock settlement makes them holders of record. Short-call writers, including covered-call and spread traders, get assigned that night and wake up short stock that owes the dividend. Checking time value against upcoming dividends is routine risk control on an equity-derivatives desk.

You would meet all of this in equity derivatives (market making and client flow), volatility trading (trading implied against realized volatility), and hedging overlays, where portfolio managers buy puts or sell calls on positions they already own.

Check yourself

Q1 XYZ trades at 47. A customer buys an XYZ 45 call at 3.50. What is the option's time value?
A. 1.50
B. 2.00
C. 3.50
D. 0

Answer: A. Intrinsic = 47 − 45 = 2.00; time value = 3.50 − 2.00 = 1.50. B is the intrinsic value. C is the whole premium, correct only for an OTM option. D would be true only at expiration.

Q2 Which positions are bullish?
I. BUY CALL
II. SELL CALL
III. BUY PUT
IV. SELL PUT
A. I and III
B. II and III
C. I and IV
D. II and IV

Answer: C. The call buyer profits as the stock rises; the put writer keeps the premium if the stock stays above the strike. II and III are the bearish pair. A pairs the two "buys," a common confusion between buying and bullishness.

Q3 XYZ trades at 38. Which option is in the money?
A. XYZ 40 call
B. XYZ 35 put
C. XYZ 37.50 put
D. XYZ 40 put

Answer: D. A put is ITM when the market is below the strike: 38 < 40, so the 40 put has $2 of intrinsic value. A is OTM (a call needs the market above the strike). B and C are puts with strikes below the market, so they are OTM.

Q4 A customer's options account is approved by a ROP on June 1. By when must the signed options agreement be returned?
A. Before June 1
B. June 16
C. June 11
D. June 30

Answer: B. The agreement is due within 15 days after approval: June 1 + 15 = June 16. A describes the ODD, which must be delivered at or before approval. C and D are made-up windows.

Q5 All of the following are true of the Options Clearing Corporation EXCEPT:
A. It issues listed options
B. It guarantees performance on listed options
C. It assigns exercise notices directly to the customers of broker-dealers
D. It acts as buyer to every seller and seller to every buyer

Answer: C. The OCC assigns exercise notices to broker-dealers at random; each broker-dealer then assigns its customers by a random, FIFO or other fair method. A, B and D are the OCC's actual roles.

Q6 A customer buys 2 XYZ Jan 60 puts at 2.15. What does she pay?
A. $2.15
B. $21.50
C. $215
D. $430

Answer: D. 2.15 × 100 shares × 2 contracts = $430. C is the cost of one contract. A and B forget the 100-share multiplier.

Q7 A customer in California wants to close a standard equity option on its expiration Friday. What is the latest time, Pacific time, at which the option trades?
A. 1:00 p.m. PT
B. 4:00 p.m. PT
C. 8:59 p.m. PT
D. 11:59 p.m. PT

Answer: A. Trading ends at 4:00 p.m. ET, which is 1:00 p.m. PT. B keeps the number but ignores the time zone. C is the formal expiration time (11:59 p.m. ET) converted to Pacific; D is that same time left in Eastern.

Q8 A customer exercises an in-the-money index call option. What does she receive?
A. The stocks in the index, T+1
B. Cash equal to the intrinsic value times the multiplier
C. Shares of an index ETF
D. The index level times 100 in cash

Answer: B. Index options are cash-settled: (index level − strike) × $100. A describes equity-option exercise. C is not a settlement method. D pays the full index value instead of only the in-the-money amount.

Day 11 · Options II: four positions, hedges and spreads

FINRA 2.1, 2.2 · pairs with the Day 11 edition (~120 min)

Every options question on the SIE reduces to three numbers: maximum gain, maximum loss and breakeven. This chapter derives them for the four basic positions, then for stock-plus-option hedges, spreads and straddles, with payoff tables at several expiration prices so you can check each formula yourself. On a desk the same tables are the language of hedging: floors, caps, collars and the idea that one exposure can be rebuilt from others.

The four positions side by side

One running example: stock XYZ, strike 50, a 50 call priced at 3 and a 50 put priced at 2, one contract (100 shares) each, held to expiration. Premium is quoted per share; multiply by 100 for dollars.

PositionViewMax gainMax lossBreakeven
BUY CALL 50 @ 3BullishUnlimitedPremium: $300K + prem = 53
SELL CALL 50 @ 3 (naked)Bearish / neutralPremium: $300UnlimitedK + prem = 53
BUY PUT 50 @ 2Bearish(K − prem) × 100 = $4,800Premium: $200K − prem = 48
SELL PUT 50 @ 2Bullish / neutralPremium: $200(K − prem) × 100 = $4,800K − prem = 48

The logic behind each cell:

  • A buyer can lose only the premium. The worst case is walking away from a worthless option.
  • A call's upside has no ceiling, because a stock has no ceiling. So the call buyer's gain and the naked call writer's loss are unlimited.
  • A put's upside stops at zero. The stock can fall only to $0, so the most a put can be worth is the strike. Subtract the premium: max gain for the put buyer, max loss for the put writer, is strike − premium.
  • A writer's best case is keeping the premium, when the option expires worthless.
  • Breakeven is the same for both sides of one contract. The call needs the stock to rise above the strike by the premium; the put needs it to fall below the strike by the premium.

Call breakeven = Strike + Premium · Put breakeven = Strike − Premium

Strike
exercise price, K
Premium
per-share price paid by the buyer and received by the writer
Memory aid
"Calls up, puts down": add for calls, subtract for puts, whether you bought or sold

Payoff tables: checking every formula

P/L per contract at expiration for all four positions. Each row sums to zero across a buyer and a writer: options move money between the two sides.

XYZ at expiryBUY CALL 50 @ 3SELL CALL 50 @ 3BUY PUT 50 @ 2SELL PUT 50 @ 2
0−$300+$300+$4,800−$4,800
40−$300+$300+$800−$800
45−$300+$300+$300−$300
48−$300+$300$0$0
50−$300+$300−$200+$200
53$0$0−$200+$200
55+$200−$200−$200+$200
60+$700−$700−$200+$200
80+$2,700−$2,700−$200+$200

Read the rows as scenarios. At 50 every option expires worthless and both buyers lose their premium. At 80 the naked call writer has lost $2,700 and the loss keeps growing with every dollar. At 0 the put buyer earns the maximum, $4,800. This asymmetry is behind a whole family of exam traps: naked call writing has unlimited risk; put writing has large but limited risk.

Two general rules follow. Buyers want volatility: a big move in the right direction pays, and the loss is capped. Sellers want time decay: the option's time value bleeds toward zero every day the stock sits still, and that decay is the writer's income.

Closing before expiration

Most positions are closed rather than held to expiration, and then the arithmetic is simpler: profit = sale price − purchase price, whatever the stock did. BUY 1 XYZ 50 call at 3, later SELL it at 5.50: gain = (5.50 − 3) × 100 = $250. SELL 1 XYZ 50 put at 2, later BUY it back at 0.40: gain = (2 − 0.40) × 100 = $160. The exam signals this with "closes the position" or "sells the option"; do not reach for the breakeven formula.

Stock plus one option: hedges and income

Combine a stock position with an option and you change its shape. There are four combinations, two for protection (you BUY an option) and two for income (you SELL one).

StockOptionName / purposeBreakeven
LongBUY PUTProtective put: insurance against a fallCost + premium
LongSELL CALLCovered call: income, upside cappedCost − premium
ShortBUY CALLProtection against a riseSale price − premium
ShortSELL PUTIncome, downside profit cappedSale price + premium

The breakeven rule is intuitive: buying protection costs money, so it moves your breakeven against you (a long needs a higher price, a short a lower one). Selling an option brings in money, so it moves the breakeven in your favour.

Covered call: long stock + SELL CALL

Own 100 XYZ at 50; SELL 1 XYZ 55 call at 2. You collect $200 now and promise to sell your shares at 55 if called.

XYZ at expiryStock P/LShort 55 call P/LTotal
30−$2,000+$200−$1,800
40−$1,000+$200−$800
48−$200+$200$0 (breakeven)
50$0+$200+$200
55+$500+$200+$700 (max)
70+$2,000−$1,300+$700
Worked example
  1. Breakeven = stock cost − premium = 50 − 2 = 48.
  2. Max gain = (strike − cost + premium) × 100 = (55 − 50 + 2) × 100 = $700, reached at 55 or above.
  3. Max loss = (cost − premium) × 100 = 48 × 100 = $4,800, if XYZ goes to zero.
  4. The call is "covered" because the shares are there to deliver if assigned.

The covered call gives only a cushion of the premium. At 30 the owner still loses $1,800. It is an income strategy for a neutral-to-mildly-bullish view, not protection against a large decline.

Protective put: long stock + BUY PUT

Own 100 XYZ at 50; BUY 1 XYZ 45 put at 2. You pay $200 for the right to sell at 45 whatever happens.

XYZ at expiryStock P/LLong 45 put P/LTotal
20−$3,000+$2,300−$700 (max loss)
40−$1,000+$300−$700
45−$500−$200−$700
50$0−$200−$200
52+$200−$200$0 (breakeven)
70+$2,000−$200+$1,800
Worked example
  1. Breakeven = stock cost + premium = 50 + 2 = 52.
  2. Max loss = (cost − strike + premium) × 100 = (50 − 45 + 2) × 100 = $700, however far XYZ falls.
  3. Max gain = unlimited, less the $200 premium.

This is insurance: a known cost (the premium) plus a deductible (cost − strike = $5 a share) buys a hard floor.

Protecting a short: short stock + BUY CALL

A short seller's danger is a rising price, so the protection is the right to buy: BUY CALL, not BUY PUT. A put pays off when the stock falls, the same direction the short already profits from, so it would add to the bet instead of hedging it.

Sell short 100 XYZ at 52; BUY 1 XYZ 55 call at 3.

XYZ at expiryShort stock P/LLong 55 call P/LTotal
0+$5,200−$300+$4,900 (max)
40+$1,200−$300+$900
49+$300−$300$0 (breakeven)
52$0−$300−$300
55−$300−$300−$600 (max loss)
70−$1,800+$1,200−$600
Worked example
  1. Breakeven = short sale price − premium = 52 − 3 = 49.
  2. Max gain = (sale price − premium) × 100 = (52 − 3) × 100 = $4,900, if XYZ goes to zero.
  3. Max loss = (strike − sale price + premium) × 100 = (55 − 52 + 3) × 100 = $600: above 55 the call buys back the shares at 55, so the short's loss stops at $3 a share plus the $3 premium.
  4. Without the call, the short's loss would be unlimited.

The fourth combination, short stock + SELL PUT, is the short seller's income trade. Short at 52, SELL a 50 put at 2: breakeven 54 (sale price + premium), max gain (52 − 50 + 2) × 100 = $400 if assigned at 50, and the loss on a rally remains unlimited.

The collar: floor and cap together

Own XYZ at 50, BUY a 45 put at 2 and SELL a 55 call at 2. The call premium pays for the put (a "zero-cost collar"). The result: loss capped at $500 (below 45), gain capped at $500 (above 55), breakeven 50. Executives with concentrated stock use collars to lock in a range without selling.

The T-chart: one method for every combination

For any mix of stock and options, track cash per share in two columns: money out (stock bought, premium paid) and money in (stock sold short, premium received). Then test the two extremes.

Worked example
  1. Short 100 XYZ at 52 (money in 52); BUY 55 call at 3 (money out 3). Net: 49 in.
  2. Breakeven: the short must be bought back at the net, 49.
  3. Stock goes to zero: buy back at 0, keep 49 → max gain $4,900.
  4. Stock soars: exercise the call, buy at 55 (money out 55). Net: 49 − 55 = −6 → max loss $600.
  5. Same method, covered call (buy at 50, SELL 55 call at 2): net 48 out → BE 48; called away at 55 → 55 − 48 = 7 → max gain $700; stock to zero → lose 48 → max loss $4,800.

The T-chart turns every combination into two questions: where does the net cash put the breakeven, and what happens at the extremes (stock at zero, stock at infinity, or an option exercised)? It works on any strategy the exam invents.

Spreads: one long option, one short

A spread is buying one option and selling another of the same type (two calls or two puts) on the same underlying. A vertical (price) spread uses different strikes and the same expiration; a horizontal (calendar, time) spread uses the same strike and different expirations; a diagonal spread differs in both. The SIE concentrates on verticals.

Two classifications, each with a quick test:

  • Debit or credit? Which leg costs more? A lower-strike call is always worth more than a higher-strike call; a higher-strike put is always worth more than a lower-strike put. If you buy the more expensive leg you pay a net debit; if you sell it you receive a net credit.
  • Bull or bear? Look at the strike you buy. Buy the lower strike = bull; buy the higher strike = bear. This works for calls and puts alike.
SpreadLegsNetMax gainMax lossBreakeven
Bull call (debit)BUY 50 call @ 3, SELL 55 call @ 12 debit$300$20050 + 2 = 52
Bear call (credit)SELL 50 call @ 3, BUY 55 call @ 12 credit$200$30050 + 2 = 52
Bear put (debit)BUY 50 put @ 2, SELL 45 put @ 0.501.50 debit$350$15050 − 1.50 = 48.50
Bull put (credit)SELL 50 put @ 2, BUY 45 put @ 0.501.50 credit$150$35050 − 1.50 = 48.50

Debit spread: Max loss = Net debit · Max gain = Strike difference − Net debit

Net debit
premium paid − premium received
Strike difference
higher strike − lower strike (the most a vertical spread can ever be worth)

Credit spread: Max gain = Net credit · Max loss = Strike difference − Net credit

Net credit
premium received − premium paid

Call spread BE = Lower strike + Net premium · Put spread BE = Higher strike − Net premium

Net premium
the debit or credit per share
Why
calls add, puts subtract, anchored on the strike of the more expensive leg

Bull call spread at expiration (BUY 50 call, SELL 55 call, 2 debit):

XYZLong 50 callShort 55 callSpread valueP/L
45000−$200
52202$0
55505+$300
6515−105+$300

Bull put spread at expiration (SELL 50 put, BUY 45 put, 1.50 credit):

XYZShort 50 put owesLong 45 put paysNet owedP/L
401055−$350
48.501.5001.50$0
50000+$150
60000+$150

Compare the bull call spread with the plain BUY 50 call at 3. At 45 the spread loses $200 against the call's $300; at 55 the spread makes $300 against the call's $200; at 65 the call makes $1,200 while the spread stops at $300. The short 55 call sold off the upside beyond 55 to make the position cheaper: a trader who expects a moderate rise, not a boom, prefers the spread.

What each side wants: the debit spread holder wants the premiums to widen (the spread's value rises toward the strike difference) and both options to finish in the money. The credit spread holder wants the premiums to narrow and both options to expire worthless, keeping the credit. Spreads trade away some upside for a cheaper or safer position: the short leg finances the long one, and the long leg caps the short one's risk.

Straddles: betting on size, not direction

A long straddle is BUY CALL + BUY PUT, same strike and expiration. You profit if the stock moves far enough in either direction. A short straddle (SELL CALL + SELL PUT) profits if it stays close to the strike.

Straddle breakevens = Strike ± Total premium

Total premium
call premium + put premium
Long straddle
max loss = total premium (at the strike); max gain unlimited up, (strike − total premium) × 100 down
Short straddle
max gain = total premium; max loss unlimited

BUY 1 XYZ 50 call at 3 and BUY 1 XYZ 50 put at 2: total premium 5, breakevens 45 and 55.

XYZCall valuePut valueTotal valueLong straddle P/LShort straddle P/L
3501515+$1,000−$1,000
45055$0$0
50000−$500+$500
55505$0$0
6515015+$1,000−$1,000

Long straddles are bought before binary events such as earnings or a court ruling, when you expect a large move but not its direction. The catch: the market knows too, and prices that expectation into the premium. The short straddle is the mirror: it collects the most premium of any simple strategy but loses without limit on a large move. If XYZ gaps to 70 on earnings, the short straddle above loses (20 − 5) × 100 = $1,500 and keeps losing as the gap widens. A strangle (or combination) uses different strikes, typically an OTM call and an OTM put: cheaper, with wider breakevens.

Hedging logic: transforming exposure

A hedge does not remove risk for free; it swaps the exposure you have for one you prefer, and the price is either premium paid or upside given away.

You haveYou fearOption tradeYou give up
Long stockA fallBUY PUT (floor)The premium
Long stockFlat returnsSELL CALL (sold cap)Upside above the strike
Short stockA rallyBUY CALL (ceiling on loss)The premium
Diversified portfolioA market fall (systematic risk)BUY index putsThe premium
Long stockA fall, but no budgetCollar: BUY PUT + SELL CALLUpside above the call strike
Worked example
  1. A $1,000,000 stock portfolio has beta 1.5, so it behaves like $1,500,000 of the index.
  2. The index is at 5,000; one index option covers 5,000 × $100 = $500,000.
  3. Contracts needed = $1,500,000 ÷ $500,000 = 3. BUY 3 index puts.
  4. If the index falls 10%, the portfolio should fall about 15% ($150,000); three puts struck at the money gain about 500 points × $100 × 3 = $150,000 at expiration, before premium.
Long stock  --(+ BUY PUT)-->  floor + unlimited upside
            = same shape as BUY CALL + cash
Long stock  --(+ SELL CALL)--> capped upside, full downside
            = same shape as SELL PUT + cash
BUY CALL + SELL PUT (same K, same expiry)
            = synthetic long stock

Those identities are put-call parity: for European options on a non-dividend stock, Call − Put = Stock − present value of the strike. Rearranged, long stock plus a protective put has exactly the payoff of a call plus enough cash (or a Treasury bill) to pay the strike. If the prices ever disagree, arbitrageurs buy the cheap side and sell the rich one until they line up. Parity is why a covered call and a naked put at the same strike are near-identical trades, and why a desk can hedge an option with stock, or build stock exposure out of options, whichever is cheaper to finance or borrow.

Understand
  • BUY CALL: bullish; max loss premium; max gain unlimited; BE = strike + premium.
  • SELL CALL (naked): bearish/neutral; max gain premium; max loss unlimited; BE = strike + premium.
  • BUY PUT: bearish; max loss premium; max gain strike − premium; BE = strike − premium.
  • SELL PUT: bullish/neutral; max gain premium; max loss strike − premium; BE = strike − premium.
  • Covered call = income with a capped upside; protective put = insurance; BUY CALL protects a short stock position.
  • Spreads: buy the lower strike = bull; buy the more expensive leg = debit. Debit wants widening and exercise; credit wants narrowing and expiration.
  • Long straddle = expect volatility; short straddle = expect calm; BEs = strike ± total premium.
  • Hedging transforms exposure: premium buys a floor; a sold option trades upside for income; parity links stock and options.
MEMORIZE:
  • Call breakeven (buyer or writer) → strike + premium
  • Put breakeven (buyer or writer) → strike − premium
  • BUY CALL → max loss premium; max gain unlimited
  • SELL CALL naked → max gain premium; max loss unlimited
  • BUY PUT → max loss premium; max gain strike − premium
  • SELL PUT → max gain premium; max loss strike − premium
  • Covered call breakeven → stock cost − premium
  • Protective put breakeven → stock cost + premium
  • Short stock + BUY CALL breakeven → sale price − premium
  • Call spread BE → lower strike + net premium
  • Put spread BE → higher strike − net premium
  • Straddle breakevens → strike ± total premium
  • Protect short stock → BUY CALL
Exam traps
  • "Selling a put has unlimited risk" → a SELL PUT's loss is limited to strike − premium; it is the naked SELL CALL that is unlimited.
  • "A covered call protects against a decline" → only by the premium received; below breakeven the loss is nearly the whole stock loss.
  • "A credit spread wants to be exercised" → a debit spread wants to WIDEN (and be exercised); a credit spread wants to EXPIRE (and narrow).
  • "Option buyers profit from time decay" → buyers want volatility; sellers want time decay.
  • "To protect a short stock position, BUY PUT" → BUY CALL; the put pays off in the direction you are already winning.
  • "The writer's breakeven differs from the buyer's" → both sides of one contract share one breakeven.
  • "Protective put BE = cost − premium" → premium paid raises it: cost + premium. Covered call subtracts.
Trader connection

Portfolio managers think in floors and caps. A protective put is a floor you buy; a covered call is a cap you sell; a collar finances one with the other, and it is the standard hedge for a concentrated or restricted stock holding. The trade-off is always the same: premium spent, or upside surrendered.

Behind that sits exposure transformation. Structured products are built from these parts: a principal-protected note is roughly a zero-coupon bond plus a BUY CALL; a reverse convertible is roughly a bond plus a SELL PUT, with the put premium paid out as a high coupon. Derivatives traders use put-call parity to move exposure between stock and options, for instance creating synthetic long stock (BUY CALL + SELL PUT) when stock is hard to borrow or cheap to finance through options, and they hedge the residual with stock (delta hedging) so that what is left is volatility, not direction.

For portfolio hedging, index puts neutralize the systematic risk that Day 9 showed diversification cannot touch: size the notional as portfolio value × beta, then accept that the premium is a running cost, the same way an insurer's premium is.

Check yourself

Q1 A customer sells short 100 XYZ at 52 and buys 1 XYZ 55 call at 3. What is the maximum potential gain?
A. $4,900
B. $5,200
C. $600
D. $300

Answer: A. If XYZ goes to zero the short earns 52 × 100 = $5,200, less the $300 call premium = $4,900. B ignores the premium. C is the maximum loss: (55 − 52 + 3) × 100. D is only the premium.

Q2 A customer buys 100 shares of ABC at 40 and sells 1 ABC 45 call at 2.50. What is the breakeven?
A. 42.50
B. 47.50
C. 37.50
D. 40.00

Answer: C. Covered call BE = stock cost − premium = 40 − 2.50 = 37.50; the premium received cushions the stock. A adds the premium (the protective-put rule). B is the call's own breakeven (strike + premium) and ignores the stock. D ignores the premium.

Q3 Rank these positions by maximum potential loss, largest first:
I. SELL 1 XYZ 50 call at 3, uncovered
II. SELL 1 XYZ 50 put at 3
III. BUY 1 XYZ 50 call at 3
IV. BUY 1 XYZ 50 call at 3 and SELL 1 XYZ 55 call at 1
A. II, I, III, IV
B. I, II, IV, III
C. IV, III, II, I
D. I, II, III, IV

Answer: D. The naked call is unlimited; the short put can lose (50 − 3) × 100 = $4,700; the long call can lose its $300 premium; the bull call spread can lose only its $200 net debit. A treats the short put as the riskier writer position. B swaps the last two. C is the reverse order.

Q4 Which of the following are credit spreads?
I. SELL 1 XYZ 40 call, BUY 1 XYZ 45 call
II. BUY 1 XYZ 40 call, SELL 1 XYZ 45 call
III. SELL 1 XYZ 40 put, BUY 1 XYZ 35 put
IV. BUY 1 XYZ 40 put, SELL 1 XYZ 35 put
A. II and IV
B. I and III
C. I and IV
D. II and III

Answer: B. A credit spread sells the more expensive leg. The lower-strike call (40) costs more than the 45 call, so I is a credit; the higher-strike put (40) costs more than the 35 put, so III is a credit. II and IV buy the expensive leg and are debit spreads. I is a bear call spread and III a bull put spread.

Q5 A customer is short 200 shares of DEF at 30 and is worried about a sharp rally. Which option trade best protects the position?
A. BUY 2 DEF 30 puts
B. BUY 2 DEF 30 calls
C. SELL 2 DEF 30 calls
D. SELL 2 DEF 30 puts

Answer: B. A short seller loses as the price rises; the right to buy at 30 caps that loss. A pays off only if DEF falls, doubling the bet. C adds more upside risk. D brings in premium but protects only by that premium.

Q6 A customer buys 1 XYZ 60 call at 4 and 1 XYZ 60 put at 3. What are the breakevens at expiration?
A. 56 and 64
B. 57 and 63
C. 53 and 67
D. 60 and 67

Answer: C. Straddle BEs = strike ± total premium = 60 ± 7. A applies only the call premium and B only the put premium. D uses the strike as one breakeven, but at 60 both options are worthless and the loss is the full $700.

Q7 A customer sells 1 XYZ 50 put at 4 and buys 1 XYZ 45 put at 1. What are the breakeven and maximum loss?
A. 47; $200
B. 53; $300
C. 48; $200
D. 47; $300

Answer: A. Net credit = 4 − 1 = 3. Put spread BE = higher strike − net premium = 50 − 3 = 47. Max loss = strike difference − credit = (5 − 3) × 100 = $200. B adds the premium as for calls. C subtracts 2 instead of the 3-point net credit. D takes the credit as the loss (that is the maximum gain).

Q8 All of the following are true of writing a covered call EXCEPT:
A. It generates premium income
B. It limits the upside on the stock
C. It lowers the breakeven by the premium received
D. It protects fully against a large decline in the stock

Answer: D. The only downside protection is the premium; below breakeven the position loses almost dollar for dollar with the stock. A, B and C are the defining features: income, a sold cap at the strike, and a breakeven of cost − premium.

Day 12 · Mutual funds: NAV, loads and share classes

FINRA 2.1, 3.2 · pairs with the Day 12 edition (~110 min)

A mutual fund is a pool that sells and buys back its own shares every day at a price struck once, after the close. The SIE tests the pricing arithmetic (NAV, POP, sales charge), the share classes and discounts a rep must get right for the customer, who does what inside the fund, and how distributions are taxed. On a desk, the same plumbing explains why fund flows move markets: when investors redeem, somebody has to sell.

The legal map: the Investment Company Act of 1940

The Investment Company Act of 1940 regulates companies whose business is investing other people's money in securities. It sorts them into three types, and the exam wants the tree cold:

Investment Company Act of 1940
 |-- Face-amount certificate company
 |     pay in now, get a fixed face amount later (rare)
 |-- Unit investment trust (UIT)
 |     fixed portfolio, no board, redeemable units
 `-- Management company (a manager picks the portfolio)
       |-- Open-end   = mutual fund (new shares daily,
       |                 redeemed by the fund)
       `-- Closed-end = fixed share count, trades on
                         an exchange

A face-amount certificate company sells a certificate: the investor pays a lump sum or installments and the company promises a fixed face amount at maturity. It is almost extinct; know that it exists. UITs and closed-end funds are Day 13. Today is the open-end management company, which is what everyone means by "mutual fund".

The defining feature is that the fund is continuously offering new shares and stands ready to redeem (buy back) shares on demand. There is no secondary market: you never buy a mutual fund share from another investor, you buy it from the fund (through its distributor) and sell it back to the fund. Because new shares are always being issued, a mutual fund is permanently "in registration" under the 1933 Act and must deliver a prospectus. It also means shares cannot be bought on margin; they can serve as margin collateral only once held 30 days, the same rule that applies to any new issue.

Diversified vs non-diversified: 75-5-10

A fund may call itself diversified only if it passes the 75-5-10 test. Read it as three nested numbers:

  • 75: at least 75% of total assets must be invested under the next two limits (cash and government securities count toward the 75%).
  • 5: within that 75%, no more than 5% of the fund's assets in any one issuer.
  • 10: within that 75%, no more than 10% of any one issuer's voting stock.

The remaining 25% is unconstrained, so a diversified fund can still hold a 20% position in one stock. A fund that fails the test is non-diversified; that is legal, it just has to say so. Just memorize the three numbers and which is "of the fund's assets" (5) versus "of the issuer's voting stock" (10).

Who does what inside a fund

A mutual fund is a shell. It usually has no employees; everything is contracted out, and each contract is a separate company with a separate job. Follow the cash and the securities:

Investor --($ at POP)--> Broker-dealer
Broker-dealer --($)--> Principal underwriter
Principal underwriter --($ at NAV)--> Fund
Fund's cash & securities held at --> Custodian (bank)
Share ownership records kept by --> Transfer agent
Portfolio decisions made by --> Investment adviser
All of the above overseen by --> Board of directors
PartyJobPaid by
Board of directorsElected by shareholders; oversees the fund, approves the advisory contract and any 12b-1 plan, sets dividend policy (including the fund's ex-date). At least 40% must be independent.Fund expenses
Investment adviserManages the portfolio to the stated objective; buys and sells securities.Management fee, a % of net assets; usually the largest expense
CustodianPhysically/legally holds the fund's cash and securities, settles trades, collects income. Usually a bank.Fund expenses
Transfer agentKeeps the shareholder register; issues and cancels (redeems) shares; sends distributions, statements and tax forms.Fund expenses
Principal underwriter (distributor, sponsor)Markets the shares; buys them from the fund at NAV and sells at POP through dealers.The sales charge and 12b-1 fees

Why split custody from management? So the person who decides what to buy never holds the assets. A manager who wanted to steal would have to get past an independent bank. That is the same segregation logic as customer-asset protection at a broker-dealer. The classic exam confusion is custodian vs transfer agent: the custodian holds assets; the transfer agent holds records of who owns the fund and pays distributions out to them.

Shareholders vote on the board, on changes to the fund's fundamental investment objective, on the initial advisory contract and on adopting a 12b-1 plan. They do not vote on which stocks the adviser buys.

Fund types by objective

Every fund states an investment objective in its prospectus, and a recommendation question usually hinges on matching objective to customer. The exam list:

TypeHoldsFits a customer who wants
GrowthStocks expected to grow earnings; low dividendsCapital appreciation, long horizon
IncomeHigh-dividend stocks (and sometimes bonds)Current income
Growth & incomeStocks with both growth and dividendsSome of each, all equity
BalancedStocks and bonds in a set mixGrowth and income across asset classes, moderate risk
Asset allocationStocks, bonds, cash; mix shifts with the manager's viewA managed mix
Sector (specialized)One industryA concentrated bet; highest risk of the stock funds
IndexReplicates an indexMarket return, low cost, low turnover
BondCorporate, government or muni bondsIncome; muni bond funds for high-bracket investors
Money marketVery short-term debt (T-bills, commercial paper, repo)Liquidity, parking cash

Note the pair the exam likes: growth & income is all stock; balanced mixes stocks and bonds. "Growth and income across asset classes" points to balanced. Money market funds aim for a stable $1.00 share price (government and retail funds), usually carry no sales charge, and are not FDIC-insured; a fund can "break the buck", as one did in September 2008.

NAV, POP and forward pricing

NAV per share = (Total assets − Total liabilities) ÷ Shares outstanding

Total assets
market value of every security plus cash and receivables, marked at the close
Total liabilities
accrued expenses, payables for securities bought, any borrowings
Shares outstanding
shares held by all investors at the moment of calculation

NAV (net asset value) is what one share is worth in liquidation: the bid, the price the fund pays you when you redeem. POP (public offering price) is what a buyer pays: NAV plus any front-end sales charge. For a no-load fund, POP = NAV.

Forward pricing: every order executes at the next NAV computed after the fund receives it. Funds compute NAV at least once each business day, typically at the 4:00 p.m. ET close. An order received at 2:00 p.m. gets today's 4:00 p.m. NAV; one received at 4:05 p.m. gets tomorrow's. You never know your price when you place the order. Why? If the fund priced at the last NAV, anyone could buy at yesterday's price after seeing today's rally, diluting existing holders. Taking orders after 4:00 p.m. at today's price ("late trading") is illegal for exactly that reason.

Redemption: the investor receives the next computed NAV, minus any back-end charge, and the fund must pay within 7 calendar days of receiving the request.

Sales charges and the arithmetic

A sales charge (load) pays the distributor and the selling broker-dealer. It is quoted as a percent of POP, not NAV. That convention makes the load look smaller, and it is the most tested calculation of the day.

Sales charge % = (POP − NAV) ÷ POP

POP
public offering price the buyer pays per share
NAV
net asset value per share, the amount actually invested
POP − NAV
the sales charge in dollars per share

POP = NAV ÷ (1 − Sales charge %)

NAV
net asset value per share
Sales charge %
the load as a decimal fraction of POP

Shares purchased = Dollars invested ÷ POP

Dollars invested
the check the customer writes
POP
offering price for the customer's breakpoint level; funds issue fractional shares
Worked example
  1. NAV $18.40, POP $20.00. Charge in dollars = 20.00 − 18.40 = $1.60.
  2. Sales charge % = 1.60 ÷ 20.00 = 8.0% of POP. (As a percent of NAV it would be 1.60 ÷ 18.40 = 8.7%. That is the distractor.)
  3. Another fund: NAV $9.50, load 5%. POP = 9.50 ÷ (1 − 0.05) = 9.50 ÷ 0.95 = $10.00. Adding 5% to NAV (9.50 × 1.05 = $9.975) is the wrong method.
  4. A customer invests $25,000 at POP $10.00: 25,000 ÷ 10.00 = 2,500 shares. Of the $25,000, 2,500 × $9.50 = $23,750 goes into the fund and $1,250 is the load.
