The FINRA ceiling is 8.5% of POP. In practice almost no fund charges it; the full 8.5% is allowed only when the fund offers breakpoints, rights of accumulation and reinvestment of dividends at NAV, and the ceiling drops when it does not. Front-end loads of 5–6% on stock funds are common.

Share classes: who pays, when

The same portfolio is sold in several classes that differ only in how the customer pays for distribution. The class decision is a suitability decision, and FINRA takes it seriously.

ClassFront-end loadBack-end load (CDSC)12b-1 feeBest for
AYes, with breakpointsUsually noneLow (often 0.25%)Large amounts, long horizons
BNoneYes, declines each year to zero; then shares convert to AHigher until conversionSmaller amounts held many years
CNoneSmall, typically only in the first yearHighest, often the full 1%, forever (level load)Short horizons
No-loadNoneNone0.25% or lessSelf-directed investors

A CDSC (contingent deferred sales charge) is a back-end load: charged on redemption, "contingent" on selling before the schedule runs out. A B-share CDSC might start at 5% and fall a point a year. When it reaches zero, B shares convert to A shares, which carry the lower 12b-1 fee. Many fund families have stopped selling new B shares, but the exam still asks who suits them: a smaller purchase held for a long time, too small to reach an A-share breakpoint.

The economics: an A share pays once up front; a C share pays roughly 1% every year. Over one or two years C is cheaper; over a decade the recurring 1% overtakes a one-time load, and breakpoints make A even cheaper for large checks. A C share has no front load, so breakpoints do nothing for a C-share buyer. Recommending C shares for a large, long-term position is a classic enforcement case.

12b-1 fees

A 12b-1 fee (named for the SEC rule) is an annual charge deducted from fund assets to pay for distribution and shareholder service. It is part of the fund's expense ratio (annual operating expenses ÷ average net assets; management fee + 12b-1 + other costs). Sales loads and the fund's own brokerage commissions are not in the expense ratio.

  • Maximum 1% per year: up to 0.75% for distribution + up to 0.25% for service.
  • A fund may call itself "no-load" only if it has no front or back load and its 12b-1 fee is 0.25% or less.
  • The plan must be approved by the board (including the independent directors) and by shareholders, and reviewed periodically.

Breakpoints, rights of accumulation and letters of intent

A breakpoint is the dollar level at which the Class A sales charge steps down. A hypothetical schedule:

Amount investedSales charge (% of POP)
Under $25,0005.75%
$25,000 to under $50,0005.00%
$50,000 to under $100,0004.50%
$100,000 and over3.50%

The whole purchase gets the lower rate; it is not tiered like a tax bracket. Purchases can usually be aggregated across the investor's household (spouse, minor children) and across funds in the same family, as the prospectus defines. Investment clubs and unrelated people pooling money do not qualify.

Breakpoint selling is prohibited: recommending a purchase just below a breakpoint (say $48,000 against a $50,000 level) or splitting money across fund families so the customer never qualifies, when that earns the rep a higher commission. The rep's duty is to tell the customer about breakpoints and how to reach them.

Two ways to reach a breakpoint without writing one large check:

  • Rights of accumulation (ROA): existing holdings in the fund family count toward the next purchase. Usually the current value counts (some funds use cost). Hold $40,000, add $15,000, and the new $15,000 is priced at the $50,000 rate. ROA looks backward and generally has no time limit.
  • Letter of intent (LOI): the customer signs a statement of intent to invest enough to reach a breakpoint within 13 months, and gets the reduced charge on every purchase in that window. The letter can be backdated up to 90 days to capture a recent purchase. The fund holds some shares in escrow; if the customer falls short, it redeems escrowed shares to collect the higher charge. The LOI binds the fund, not the customer: no one can be forced to finish buying.
Worked example
  1. On March 1 a customer invests $20,000 (NAV $9.50, 5.75% load): POP = 9.50 ÷ 0.9425 = $10.08.
  2. On May 15 (75 days later) she signs an LOI for $50,000, backdated to March 1. The 13-month window ends April 1 of next year.
  3. Every purchase from May 15 on is priced at the 4.50% rate: POP = 9.50 ÷ 0.955 = $9.95; $30,000 buys 30,000 ÷ 9.95 = 3,015.08 shares. (Whether the March purchase is re-priced depends on the prospectus.)
  4. If she stops at $40,000 total, the fund redeems enough escrowed shares to collect the difference between the 5.00% and 4.50% charges on the amount actually bought.

Other shareholder features

  • Reinvestment: dividends and capital gains are usually reinvested at NAV (no load).
  • Exchange (conversion) privilege: move money between funds in the same family at NAV, with no new load. It is still a sale for tax purposes.
  • Withdrawal plans: fixed-dollar, fixed-share or fixed-period payouts from the fund; none is guaranteed to last.

How fund distributions are taxed

A fund that distributes at least 90% of its net investment income qualifies as a regulated investment company and pays no tax on what it passes through. This is the conduit (pipeline) theory: the tax moves to the shareholder. The shareholder faces three taxable events:

Portfolio earns dividends/interest --> income dividend
   --> taxed to holder (ordinary or qualified)
Manager sells winners at a net gain --> cap-gain distribution
   --> taxed LONG-TERM, regardless of holder's period
Holder redeems or exchanges shares --> own gain or loss
   --> long/short by holder's own holding period
  • Reinvested distributions are still taxable, in the year paid, exactly as if taken in cash. The reinvested amount is added to the holder's cost basis, so it is not taxed twice when the shares are sold.
  • Capital-gain distributions are long-term because the fund held the securities; the shareholder's own holding period is irrelevant.
  • Distributions declared in October–December to holders of record then and paid by the end of January are taxed as if received December 31.
  • The fund's board sets the ex-date, typically the business day after the record date. On the ex-date, NAV drops by the distribution.

"Buying the dividend" is a trap for the customer and a violation for the rep: buy a fund a few days before a large distribution and NAV falls by the amount paid out, while the customer owes tax on money that was already his. Recommending the purchase to "capture" the distribution misrepresents it.

Fund flows as market flows

The daily redemption promise is a liquidity mismatch: shareholders can leave in 7 days, but the fund may hold assets (high-yield bonds, small caps, bank loans) that take much longer to sell at fair prices. The SEC's liquidity rule caps a fund's illiquid investments at 15% of net assets, but the mismatch never fully goes away:

Bad news --> redemptions --> fund must raise cash
 --> sells the most liquid holdings first
 --> remaining portfolio is less liquid
 --> prices marked lower --> NAV falls --> more redemptions

Remaining holders bear the trading costs caused by those who left, which is why early redeemers have an advantage in a run. The money-market run of 2008 and the bond-fund outflows of March 2020 are the textbook cases.

Understand
  • The 1940 Act has three types: face-amount, UIT, management company; management companies are open-end (mutual funds) or closed-end. Diversified = 75-5-10.
  • Buyers pay POP; redeemers receive NAV; orders fill at the next computed NAV (forward pricing); redemptions are paid within 7 days.
  • Loads are a percent of POP, capped at 8.5%. Class A = front load with breakpoints; B = declining CDSC then converts to A; C = level 12b-1 load; no-load = no load and 12b-1 ≤ 0.25%.
  • 12b-1 fees are capped at 1% a year (0.75% distribution + 0.25% service).
  • Breakpoints, ROA and a 13-month LOI (backdate 90 days) lower Class A loads; breakpoint selling is prohibited.
  • Board oversees; adviser manages; custodian holds assets; transfer agent keeps records and pays distributions; underwriter distributes.
  • Distributions are taxable when paid even if reinvested; capital-gain distributions are always long-term.
MEMORIZE:
  • Maximum mutual fund sales charge → 8.5% of POP
  • 12b-1 maximum → 1% a year (0.75% distribution + 0.25% service)
  • "No-load" label allowed → no sales charge and 12b-1 ≤ 0.25%
  • Letter of intent → 13 months; may be backdated 90 days; shares held in escrow
  • Redemption payment → within 7 calendar days
  • Independent directors → at least 40% of the board
  • Diversified fund → 75-5-10
  • Pricing → forward: next computed NAV
  • Fund ex-date → set by the board, typically the business day after the record date
  • Sales charge % → (POP − NAV) ÷ POP; POP → NAV ÷ (1 − sales charge %)
Exam traps
  • Sales charge as a percent of NAV → it is a percent of POP; dividing by NAV overstates it.
  • "C shares are always cheapest because there is no front load" → C suits short horizons; for large, long-term purchases Class A usually wins.
  • "Breakpoints lower the cost of C shares" → C shares have no front load; breakpoints only reduce Class A loads.
  • "Reinvested dividends are not taxed until the shares are sold" → they are taxed in the year paid, reinvested or not.
  • "The transfer agent holds the fund's securities" → the custodian holds assets; the transfer agent keeps shareholder records and pays distributions.
  • "An LOI obligates the customer to buy" → it binds only the fund; a shortfall just costs the higher load from escrow.
  • "Capital-gain distributions are short-term if you held the fund under a year" → always long-term.
Trader connection

The 4:00 p.m. NAV strike is a daily liquidity event. Index and active funds that receive net inflows buy at or near the close to match the price they credit investors, which is part of why closing auctions now carry a large share of daily volume. Outflows run the other way: a fund facing redemptions sells what it can, not what it wants, so outflows from credit funds show up as forced selling in the most liquid bonds and in CDX/ETF hedges. Watching weekly fund-flow data is watching who will be a forced buyer or seller next. The liquidity mismatch you learned here is also the reason ETFs (Day 13) handle redemptions in kind: the redeeming party, not the remaining holders, takes the securities and the trading cost.

Check yourself

Q1 A fund has a NAV of $23.00 and a POP of $25.00. What is the sales charge percentage?
A. 2.0%
B. 8.0%
C. 8.5%
D. 8.7%

Answer: B. Sales charge % = (POP − NAV) ÷ POP = (25.00 − 23.00) ÷ 25.00 = 2.00 ÷ 25.00 = 8.0%. D divides by NAV (2.00 ÷ 23.00 = 8.7%), the classic error. C is the regulatory maximum, not this fund's charge. A is the dollar charge, not a percentage.

Q2 A customer plans to invest $200,000 in a fund family and hold it for at least 15 years. Which share class is generally most suitable?
A. Class A
B. Class B
C. Class C
D. Any class, because all classes hold the same portfolio

Answer: A. A large, long-term purchase reaches deep breakpoints, and the one-time front load is then cheaper than paying a recurring 12b-1 fee. C's level 1% a year adds up over 15 years and gets no breakpoint benefit. B suits smaller purchases held long, and many firms cap B purchases well below $200,000. D is wrong: the portfolio is identical but the cost to the customer is not, which is exactly why class selection is a suitability issue.

Q3 Which party is responsible for holding a mutual fund's cash and securities?
A. The transfer agent
B. The investment adviser
C. The principal underwriter
D. The custodian

Answer: D. The custodian, usually a bank, safeguards the fund's assets and settles its trades. The transfer agent (A) keeps shareholder records and pays distributions. The adviser (B) decides what to buy and sell but does not hold the assets. The underwriter (C) distributes shares.

Q4 All of the following are true of a letter of intent EXCEPT:
A. It covers purchases made over 13 months.
B. It may be backdated up to 90 days.
C. It legally obligates the customer to complete the stated purchases.
D. Shares may be held in escrow to cover the higher sales charge if the customer falls short.

Answer: C. An LOI binds the fund to offer the reduced charge, not the customer to keep buying. If she falls short, escrowed shares are redeemed to collect the higher load. A, B and D are the standard LOI terms.

Q5 A fund's NAV is $14.25 and its sales charge is 5%. What is the POP?
A. $14.96
B. $15.00
C. $14.25
D. $13.54

Answer: B. POP = NAV ÷ (1 − 0.05) = 14.25 ÷ 0.95 = $15.00. A adds 5% to NAV (14.25 × 1.05 = 14.96), which treats the load as a percent of NAV. C ignores the load. D subtracts it.

Q6 Which of the following are true of mutual fund distributions? I. Income dividends reinvested in additional shares are taxable in the year paid. II. Reinvested distributions are not taxable until the shares are redeemed. III. Capital-gain distributions are taxed as long-term gains regardless of how long the investor has held the fund. IV. Capital-gain distributions are short-term if the investor has held the fund under one year.
A. I and III
B. I and IV
C. II and III
D. II and IV

Answer: A. Reinvestment does not defer tax (I true, II false); the reinvested amount is added to cost basis. Capital-gain distributions reflect the fund's holding period, so they are long-term (III true, IV false). Every other pair includes one of the false statements.

Q7 A customer's order to redeem fund shares arrives at 4:20 p.m. ET. The fund prices at the 4:00 p.m. close. At what price is the redemption executed?
A. Today's 4:00 p.m. NAV
B. Today's POP
C. The next NAV computed, on the following business day
D. The price of the last trade on the exchange

Answer: C. Forward pricing: orders execute at the next NAV computed after receipt. The order missed today's 4:00 p.m. strike, so it gets tomorrow's. A would be late trading, which is prohibited. B is the buying price, not the redemption price. D is wrong because mutual fund shares do not trade on an exchange.

Q8 A rep recommends that a customer invest $97,500 in a fund whose next breakpoint is $100,000, without mentioning the breakpoint. This is best described as:
A. Acceptable because the amount is below the breakpoint
B. Acceptable if the customer signs a letter of intent afterward
C. Front-running
D. Breakpoint selling, which is prohibited

Answer: D. Recommending a purchase just below a breakpoint, or failing to disclose it, so the customer pays a higher load is breakpoint selling. A describes the violation rather than excusing it, and a later LOI (B) does not cure a failure to disclose. C is trading ahead of a customer order, a different violation.

Day 13 · Exchange-traded, closed-end, UITs and the alternatives

FINRA 2.1, 2.2 · pairs with the Day 13 edition (~100 min)

Day 12's mutual fund redeems its own shares at NAV. Everything today solves the same problem, turning a pool of assets into something an investor can own, but with a different exit: an exchange (closed-end funds, ETFs, listed REITs), redemption of a fixed trust (UITs), or no easy exit at all (non-traded REITs, limited partnerships, hedge funds). The exam tests how each is priced, how it is taxed, and who it is unsuitable for. On a desk, the ETF creation/redemption mechanism is a basis trade you will see every day.

Closed-end funds: a fixed pool that trades like a stock

A closed-end fund (CEF) is a management company that raises money once, in an IPO with a prospectus, and then stops issuing. After that the share count is fixed (it changes only through events like rights offerings or buybacks), and the shares trade on an exchange between investors. The fund does not redeem them. To get out, you sell to another investor at whatever the market pays.

Mutual fund:  Investor --(redeem)--> Fund --(NAV)--> Investor
Closed-end:   Seller --(shares)--> Exchange --> Buyer
              price set by supply/demand, not by NAV

Because nothing forces price to NAV, CEFs trade at a premium (price above NAV) or discount (below NAV). Discounts are common; they reflect fees, leverage, illiquid holdings and sentiment. With a fixed pool the manager never faces redemptions, so a CEF can hold illiquid assets and borrow (issue debt or preferred shares) to add leverage. You buy it through a broker and pay a commission, not a sales charge; you can buy on margin and sell short once it trades in the secondary market.

Why do discounts persist if the assets are worth NAV? Nobody can force the fund to hand over the assets at NAV, so the gap can only close through the market: the board buying back shares, a tender offer, converting the fund to open-end, or an activist campaign demanding one of those. Until then a buyer of a 10% discount collects income on $1.00 of assets for $0.90, and a seller of a premium fund is paid more than the assets are worth. The same missing redemption mechanism is what ETFs were designed to fix.

Premium/discount = (Market price − NAV) ÷ NAV

Market price
last price on the exchange
NAV
net asset value per share of the underlying portfolio
Result
positive = premium; negative = discount
Worked example
  1. CEF NAV $20.00, market price $18.00: (18.00 − 20.00) ÷ 20.00 = −2.00 ÷ 20.00 = −10%, a 10% discount.
  2. Same fund later trades at $21.00 with NAV $20.00: (21.00 − 20.00) ÷ 20.00 = +5%, a 5% premium.
  3. Note the denominator is NAV. That is the opposite of Day 12's sales-charge formula, which divides by POP.

ETFs: a fund that trades on screen and redeems in bulk

An exchange-traded fund (ETF) is legally usually an open-end fund (a few, including the oldest S&P 500 ETF, are UITs). It issues and redeems shares, but only in large blocks called creation units and only with authorized participants (APs), big broker-dealers that have signed an agreement with the fund. Everyone else buys and sells on the exchange, intraday, at market prices, with commissions, limit orders, margin and short sales all available.

CREATION (ETF trading ABOVE NAV)
AP buys basket in market --(basket)--> ETF
ETF --(creation unit of new shares)--> AP
AP sells ETF shares on exchange --> premium closes

REDEMPTION (ETF trading BELOW NAV)
AP buys cheap ETF shares --(creation unit)--> ETF
ETF --(basket of securities, in kind)--> AP
AP sells basket --> discount closes

That arbitrage is what keeps an ETF's price close to NAV, and it is the key difference from a closed-end fund, which has no such mechanism. Exchanges also publish an intraday indicative value during the day so traders can see the gap. Because redemptions are usually in kind (securities, not cash), the fund does not have to sell winners to pay out, so ETFs typically distribute fewer capital gains than comparable mutual funds.

ETNs (exchange-traded notes) look similar on screen but are completely different on the balance sheet. An ETN is unsecured debt of an issuing bank that promises to pay an index return minus fees. There is no portfolio behind it. If the issuer defaults, holders are unsecured creditors. ETN = issuer credit risk, on top of index risk.

Leveraged and inverse ETFs

A leveraged ETF targets a multiple (2x, 3x) of an index's return; an inverse ETF targets the opposite (−1x, −2x). The target is for one day. To hit it, the fund resets its exposure every day, and over longer periods the compounding makes returns path-dependent: in a volatile, sideways market the fund loses value even if the index ends flat. FINRA Regulatory Notice 09-31 warned that these products are typically unsuitable for retail investors who plan to hold them longer than one trading session, particularly in volatile markets.

Worked example: two volatile days
  1. Index starts at 100. Day 1: +10% → 110. Day 2: −10% → 99. Index return: −1%.
  2. 2x ETF starts at 100. Day 1: +20% → 120. Day 2: −20% → 120 × 0.80 = 96. Return: −4%, not 2 × (−1%) = −2%.
  3. −1x ETF starts at 100. Day 1: −10% → 90. Day 2: +10% → 99. Return: −1%, even though the index also fell 1%.
  4. Both lost money on a path where the index went nowhere. That drag grows with volatility and time, which is why buy-and-hold is the wrong use.

Unit investment trusts

A UIT is the third 1940 Act type. A sponsor buys a portfolio (often bonds, sometimes a basket of stocks), deposits it in a trust and sells redeemable units. Then the portfolio is essentially fixed: no board of directors, no investment adviser, no active management. A bond UIT liquidates as the bonds mature; an equity UIT has a set termination date. Units are sold with a prospectus, and holders redeem them with the trustee at NAV; sponsors often maintain a secondary market to buy units back. The trap is to treat UITs like mutual funds: there is no manager making decisions, and the trust does not issue shares continuously once the offering ends.

Mutual fundClosed-end fundETFUIT
Share countChanges dailyFixed after IPOChanges via APsFixed at offering
How you exitRedeem to fund at NAVSell on exchangeSell on exchange (APs redeem)Redeem to trustee
PriceNAV (buy at POP)Market; premium/discountMarket, near NAVNAV
ManagerAdviser, boardAdviser, boardAdviser (often indexed)None active; no board
Buyer paysPOP (sales charge, if any)Market price + commissionMarket price + commissionOffer price incl. sales charge

REITs: real estate in a corporate wrapper

A real estate investment trust (REIT) is a company that owns or finances real estate and elects special tax treatment. It is not a 1940 Act investment company. If it meets the tests, it deducts the dividends it pays, so most of its income is taxed once, at the shareholder:

  • 75% asset test: at least 75% of assets in real estate, mortgages, cash and government securities.
  • 75% income test: at least 75% of gross income from real-estate sources (rents, mortgage interest, property sales).
  • 90% distribution: must distribute at least 90% of taxable income as dividends.

REIT dividends are generally taxed as ordinary income, not at the lower qualified-dividend rate, because the REIT itself paid no corporate tax on that income. The pass-through is one-way: a REIT passes through income, not losses. If the REIT loses money, the loss stays inside it. That is the key contrast with a limited partnership.

TypeOwnsEarnsMain risk
Equity REITPropertiesRents, property gainsOccupancy, property values
Mortgage REITMortgages, mortgage-backed securitiesInterestRates, leverage, funding
Hybrid REITBoth properties and mortgagesRents and interestBoth

Traded REITs are listed and priced every second. Non-traded REITs are registered with the SEC and sold with a prospectus, but not listed: they often carry high up-front costs, value their shares infrequently and offer only limited redemption programs that can be suspended. That is serious liquidity risk. Private REITs are neither registered nor listed.

A mortgage REIT is a leveraged rate trade in a stock wrapper: it holds mortgages or agency MBS, funds them with short-term borrowing (often repo), and earns the spread. Rising short rates squeeze that spread, and falling collateral values bring margin calls from its lenders, which is why mortgage REITs fall hard when rates spike.

Direct participation programs (limited partnerships)

A direct participation program (DPP) is a business, usually a limited partnership, whose income and losses flow through to the investors' own tax returns. The partnership itself pays no entity-level tax. Unlike a REIT, a DPP passes through both income and losses, which is historically why people bought them: to use the losses.

General partner --(manages; unlimited liability)--> LP
Limited partners --(capital)--> LP
LP income, losses, deductions --(K-1)--> each partner
General partner (GP)Limited partner (LP)
RoleRuns the business; fiduciary to the LPsPassive investor; supplies capital
LiabilityUnlimitedLimited to the investment
ManagementMakes all decisionsNone; an LP who manages risks losing limited status
RightsCompensation per agreementVote on major matters, inspect books, sue the GP

Documents: the partnership agreement (rights and duties), the certificate of limited partnership (filed with the state) and the subscription agreement (the investor's application; the GP signs it to accept the investor). DPPs are sold publicly with a prospectus or privately under Reg D, and there is little or no secondary market: illiquidity is the main risk after the business itself.

Program types and tax items

  • Real estate: raw land (speculative, no depreciation, no income), new construction, existing properties (income), government-assisted housing (tax credits). Tax items: depreciation of buildings, deductible interest on mortgages, pass-through losses.
  • Oil and gas: exploratory (wildcat, highest risk), developmental, income programs. Tax items: depletion (deduction as the resource is used up) and intangible drilling costs. Depletion belongs to natural-resource programs, never real estate.
  • Equipment leasing: steady lease income plus depreciation.

Passive-loss rule: income from an LP interest or rental activity is passive. Passive losses can offset only passive income, not wages or portfolio income (interest, dividends, capital gains). Unused losses carry forward and are released when the investor disposes of the interest. Just memorize the three buckets: earned, portfolio, passive. A DPP should make economic sense before any tax benefit.

Hedge funds and other private funds

A hedge fund is a private pool that avoids registering as an investment company by limiting who can invest: either no more than 100 beneficial owners, or only qualified purchasers (individuals with at least $5 million in investments). Interests are sold as private placements, usually under Reg D to accredited investors (income over $200,000, or $300,000 joint, in each of the last two years with the same expected; or net worth over $1 million excluding the primary residence; or a Series 7, 65 or 82 holder). The manager is typically a registered investment adviser.

  • Strategies: shorting, leverage, derivatives, concentrated positions, illiquid assets; nothing like a mutual fund's limits.
  • Liquidity: lockups (no redemptions for an initial period), periodic redemption windows with advance notice, and gates that limit how much can leave at once.
  • Fees: a management fee plus a performance fee on profits.
  • Funds of hedge funds: registered products that hold several hedge funds, with lower minimums and a second layer of fees.

Lockups and gates are the answer to Day 12's liquidity mismatch: a fund that holds illiquid or leveraged positions cannot promise daily exit, so it contracts out of it. For the investor that is the cost; for the manager it prevents a forced fire-sale. Private equity and venture funds follow the same private-fund template with even longer lockups (capital committed for years).

Understand
  • Closed-end funds: fixed shares after the IPO, trade on an exchange at a premium or discount to NAV; the fund does not redeem.
  • ETFs: APs create and redeem in creation units, in kind; the arbitrage holds price near NAV. ETNs are unsecured issuer debt.
  • Leveraged and inverse ETFs reset daily; returns are path-dependent; RN 09-31 flags them as generally unsuitable for holding beyond a day.
  • UITs: fixed portfolio, no board, no active adviser, redeemable units.
  • REITs: 75% asset and income tests, 90% distribution; equity, mortgage or hybrid; traded, non-traded or private.
  • DPPs: flow-through of income and losses; GP manages with unlimited liability; LPs passive and limited; passive losses offset passive income; illiquid.
  • Hedge funds: private, accredited or qualified investors, leverage, lockups, limited liquidity.
MEMORIZE:
  • REIT distribution → at least 90% of taxable income
  • REIT tests → 75% of assets and 75% of income from real estate
  • REIT dividends → generally ordinary income (not qualified)
  • LP liability → limited to the investment; GP liability → unlimited
  • Passive losses → offset only passive income
  • ETN → unsecured debt; issuer credit risk
  • Leveraged/inverse ETF guidance → FINRA Regulatory Notice 09-31
  • Depletion → oil and gas programs; depreciation and interest → real estate programs
  • Premium/discount → (price − NAV) ÷ NAV
Exam traps
  • "Sell a closed-end fund back to the fund" → no redemption; you sell on the exchange.
  • "REITs pass through losses like partnerships" → REITs pass through income only; DPPs pass through income and losses.
  • "A 2x ETF is a good long-term way to double the index" → daily reset makes it generally unsuitable for buy-and-hold.
  • "A UIT's adviser rebalances the portfolio" → no adviser actively manages it and there is no board.
  • "A non-traded REIT is liquid because it is registered" → registered is not listed; liquidity risk is high.
  • "Depletion is a benefit of a real estate program" → depletion is oil and gas; real estate gets depreciation and interest deductions.
  • "An ETN holds the index stocks" → it holds nothing; it is the issuer's IOU.
Trader connection

AP arbitrage is a basis trade: long the cheap leg, short the rich leg, converge through creation or redemption. ETF market makers quote the fund tight because they can hedge with the basket, futures or correlated ETFs, which is why an ETF's real liquidity lives in the underlying, not in its screen volume. A thinly traded ETF on liquid stocks can absorb a large order; a heavily traded high-yield ETF can gap to a discount in a stress event when the bonds themselves cannot trade. Leveraged and inverse ETFs rebalance near the close, and both kinds trade in the direction of the day's move: after an up day a 2x long fund must buy exposure and a −1x fund must cover shorts; after a down day both sell. That pro-cyclical late-day flow is visible in the closing auction on volatile days.

Check yourself

Q1 A closed-end fund has a NAV of $25.00 and trades at $22.50. What is its premium or discount?
A. 10% premium
B. 11.1% discount
C. 10% discount
D. 11.1% premium

Answer: C. (Price − NAV) ÷ NAV = (22.50 − 25.00) ÷ 25.00 = −2.50 ÷ 25.00 = −10%, a 10% discount. B divides by the market price (2.50 ÷ 22.50 = 11.1%). A and D have the sign wrong: trading below NAV is a discount.

Q2 All of the following are tax features of a real estate limited partnership EXCEPT:
A. Depreciation
B. Deductible mortgage interest
C. Pass-through of losses
D. Depletion

Answer: D. Depletion is a deduction for using up a natural resource, so it belongs to oil and gas programs. Real estate programs offer depreciation (A), interest deductions (B) and pass-through of losses (C).

Q3 An index rises 5% one day and falls 5% the next. A 2x leveraged ETF on that index starts at $50.00. Where does it end?
A. $49.50
B. $50.00
C. $49.88
D. $49.75

Answer: A. Day 1: +10% → 50.00 × 1.10 = 55.00. Day 2: −10% → 55.00 × 0.90 = 49.50. The index ended at 100 × 1.05 × 0.95 = 99.75, down 0.25%; twice that would be −0.5% ($49.75, D), but the daily reset produced −1%. B ignores compounding; C is the index path applied to $50.

Q4 Which of the following are true of REITs? I. They must distribute at least 90% of taxable income. II. They pass losses through to shareholders. III. Their dividends are generally taxed as ordinary income. IV. They are registered as open-end investment companies.
A. I and II
B. I and III
C. II and IV
D. III and IV

Answer: B. The 90% distribution (I) and ordinary-income dividends (III) are core REIT rules. REITs pass through income but not losses (II false), and they are companies electing REIT tax status, not 1940 Act investment companies (IV false).

Q5 The main risk an ETN investor bears that an ETF investor does not is:
A. Market risk
B. Tracking error
C. Credit risk of the issuer
D. Inflation risk

Answer: C. An ETN is unsecured debt of the issuing bank with no portfolio behind it, so an issuer default can wipe out holders regardless of the index. Both products carry market (A) and inflation (D) risk, and both can show tracking differences (B).

Q6 A limited partner in an oil and gas program has $30,000 of passive losses, $80,000 of salary and $10,000 of passive income from a real estate LP. How much of the loss can she deduct this year?
A. $30,000
B. $3,000
C. $20,000
D. $10,000

Answer: D. Passive losses offset only passive income, so $10,000 of the loss offsets the $10,000 of passive income; the remaining $20,000 carries forward. A assumes it can offset salary, which it cannot. B is the capital-loss limit against ordinary income, a different rule. C is the carryforward, not the deduction.

Q7 Which is true of a unit investment trust?
A. It has a board of directors that approves the advisory contract.
B. It issues a fixed portfolio of redeemable units and has no active manager.
C. It trades only on an exchange and cannot be redeemed.
D. It continuously offers new shares at NAV plus a sales charge.

Answer: B. A UIT holds a fixed portfolio, has no board and no adviser actively managing, and its units are redeemable with the trustee. A describes a management company. C describes a closed-end fund. D describes a mutual fund.

Day 14 · Annuities and insurance-linked products

FINRA 2.1, 2.2, 3.2 · pairs with the Day 14 edition (~75 min)

An annuity is a contract with an insurance company: you pay in, money grows tax-deferred, and later the insurer can turn it into income for life. The exam cares about one dividing line (who bears the investment risk, which decides whether it is a security), the unit mechanics of a variable annuity, the payout options, and the tax rules. On a desk, variable annuity guarantees are one of the largest sources of options and swap hedging in the market.

Fixed vs variable: who carries the risk

The question that decides everything is where the money sits and who eats a bad return.

FIXED:    Premium --> insurer's GENERAL account
          insurer guarantees the rate --> insurer bears risk
          --> insurance product, NOT a security

VARIABLE: Premium --> SEPARATE account --> sub-accounts
          value moves with the sub-accounts
          --> contract owner bears risk --> a SECURITY

A fixed annuity pays a guaranteed rate from the insurer's general account, the insurer's own balance sheet. The customer's risk is the insurer's credit, backed by state guaranty associations, not SIPC. It is regulated by state insurance commissioners and needs only an insurance license to sell.

A variable annuity (VA) puts premiums in a separate account, walled off from the insurer's creditors, divided into sub-accounts that work like mutual funds. Because the owner bears the investment risk, a VA is a security: the separate account is registered under the Investment Company Act of 1940, the contracts are registered under the Securities Act of 1933, a prospectus is required, and the rep needs both a securities registration and a state insurance license.

The balance-sheet logic: if the insurer fails, fixed-annuity owners are creditors of the general account, while separate-account assets belong to the VA owners and are shielded from the insurer's creditors. SIPC does not cover fixed annuities; it protects customers of failed broker-dealers, not insurance contracts.

Between the two sit indexed annuities (fixed indexed annuities): the insurer credits interest linked to an index, subject to a cap (maximum credited return) and a participation rate (share of the index gain credited), with a floor that protects principal. Fixed indexed annuities are generally treated as insurance, not securities. Products that can lose principal in exchange for higher caps (registered index-linked annuities) are registered with the SEC.

Two phases, two kinds of unit

A deferred annuity has an accumulation phase (paying in and growing) and a payout (annuity) phase. A VA measures the owner's interest in different units in each.

  • Accumulation units: each purchase payment buys accumulation units at their current value, like fund shares. The number of units grows with each payment; their value moves with the sub-accounts.
  • Annuity units: at annuitization (converting the contract into an income stream), the insurer converts accumulation units into a fixed number of annuity units. Each payment = number of annuity units × current annuity unit value. The number stays fixed; the value changes monthly.

To size the first payment, the insurer assumes a return for the sub-accounts: the assumed interest rate (AIR). After that, each payment moves according to how the actual return compares with the AIR:

Sub-account return this period vs AIR (4% example)Next payment
Actual 6% > AIR 4%Rises
Actual 4% = AIR 4%Unchanged
Actual 2% < AIR 4%Falls
Worked example (simplified, one period)
  1. At annuitization the owner receives 100 annuity units worth $10.00 each: first payment 100 × $10.00 = $1,000.
  2. AIR 4%. The sub-account earns 6%. The unit value adjusts by the excess over the AIR: $10.00 × 1.06 ÷ 1.04 = $10.19.
  3. Next payment: 100 × $10.19 = $1,019. Had the sub-account earned 2%: $10.00 × 1.02 ÷ 1.04 = $9.81, payment $981.

The comparison is always to the AIR, never to last period's return. If returns fall from 8% to 5% with a 4% AIR, the payment still rises, just by less. And the AIR is a planning assumption, not a guarantee: nothing promises the sub-accounts will earn it. A higher AIR means a larger first payment but a higher hurdle for increases.

Buying and settlement options

An immediate annuity is bought with a single lump-sum premium and starts paying promptly. A deferred annuity delays payout and can be funded with a single premium or periodic purchase payments; if the customer is paying in over time, it is deferred by definition.

At annuitization the owner picks a settlement option. The logic: the more the insurer might have to pay after the annuitant dies, the smaller each monthly check.

OptionPaysMonthly amount
Life only (straight life)For the annuitant's life; stops at death, nothing to heirsHighest
Unit refundFor life; if death comes before the contract value is paid out, the rest goes to a beneficiaryLower
Life with period certainFor life, with a guaranteed minimum period (e.g. 10 years) paid to a beneficiary if the annuitant dies earlyLower
Joint and last survivorFor two lives; continues until the second deathTypically lowest

Life only has the highest payout and the most risk to heirs: an annuitant who dies after one payment leaves nothing. Once annuitized, the choice is generally irrevocable.

Costs and variable life

VAs carry layers of cost: a mortality and expense (M&E) risk charge (paying for the insurance guarantees), administrative fees, sub-account fund expenses, and fees for optional riders. Most contracts impose a surrender charge on withdrawals in the early years, declining on a schedule to zero, though many allow a small free withdrawal each year. A standard VA death benefit pays the beneficiary at least the premiums paid (less withdrawals) if the owner dies during accumulation, even if the sub-accounts fell.

Variable life insurance applies the same separate-account idea to life insurance: premiums go into sub-accounts, the cash value is not guaranteed, and the death benefit has a guaranteed minimum that can grow with performance. It is a security, sold with a prospectus by someone holding both licenses. Variable universal life adds flexible premiums.

Taxation

A nonqualified annuity (bought outside a retirement plan) is funded with after-tax dollars; contributions are not deductible and there is no annual contribution limit. Earnings grow tax-deferred. When money comes out:

  • Withdrawals are LIFO (last in, first out): earnings come out first and are taxed as ordinary income; only after all earnings are out does the owner recover cost basis tax-free.
  • Earnings withdrawn before age 59½ also incur a 10% penalty (exceptions include death and disability).
  • Annuity gains are never capital gains, however long held; heirs get no step-up in basis. A beneficiary who inherits a contract pays ordinary income tax on the gain as it is paid out (no 10% penalty on death).

When the contract is annuitized, each payment is part return of basis (tax-free) and part earnings (ordinary income). The split uses the exclusion ratio:

Exclusion ratio = Cost basis ÷ Expected total payout

Cost basis
after-tax dollars paid into the contract
Expected total payout
payment × number of payments expected (from IRS life-expectancy tables)
Result
the fraction of each payment excluded from tax
Worked example
  1. Basis $60,000. Payout $1,000 a month, expected over 10 years: 120 × $1,000 = $120,000.
  2. Exclusion ratio = 60,000 ÷ 120,000 = 50%.
  3. Each $1,000 payment: $500 tax-free return of basis, $500 ordinary income.
  4. Contrast a $1,000 lump withdrawal from the same contract before annuitizing: under LIFO it is all earnings, all ordinary income (plus 10% if under 59½).

A qualified annuity sits inside an IRA or employer plan and was funded pre-tax, so every dollar out is ordinary income. Putting a VA inside an IRA adds the M&E cost for deferral the IRA already provides; it is justified only by features such as a guaranteed income or death benefit, and a rep must be able to say so.

1035 exchanges and suitability

A 1035 exchange (Internal Revenue Code §1035) swaps one contract for another without triggering tax: annuity → annuity, life insurance → annuity, life → life. Annuity → life insurance is not allowed. The basis carries over.

Tax-free is not cost-free. A new contract usually starts a new surrender-charge schedule, and the customer may give up guarantees on the old one. FINRA Rule 2330 governs recommendations of deferred variable annuities: the rep must have a reasonable basis that the customer benefits from the features, and a principal reviews the transaction, including whether the customer made another annuity exchange within the preceding 36 months. Red flags: long surrender periods for elderly or liquidity-needy customers, churning exchanges for commission, and VAs in IRAs without a feature-based reason.

Understand
  • Fixed annuity = insurer's general account, insurer's risk, not a security. Variable = separate account, owner's risk, a registered security.
  • Accumulation units during pay-in; a fixed number of annuity units at payout, with payments moving against the AIR.
  • Settlement options trade monthly size against what heirs might receive; life only pays most.
  • Indexed annuities credit capped index-linked interest with a floor; variable life is a separate-account security.
  • Nonqualified: after-tax in, tax-deferred growth, LIFO withdrawals taxed as ordinary income, 10% penalty before 59½.
  • 1035 exchanges defer tax but can restart surrender charges; suitability is closely supervised.
MEMORIZE:
  • VA sub-account return > AIR → payment rises; = AIR → unchanged; < AIR → payment falls
  • Life only → pays the most per month; stops at death
  • Joint and last survivor → typically the smallest payment
  • Nonqualified annuity withdrawals → earnings first (LIFO), ordinary income; 10% penalty before 59½
  • 1035 exchange → tax-free annuity-to-annuity, life-to-annuity, life-to-life (not annuity-to-life)
  • Immediate annuity → single lump-sum premium; periodic payments → deferred annuity
  • Exclusion ratio → basis ÷ expected total payout
Exam traps
  • "Fixed annuities are securities" → no; variable annuities are.
  • "Annuity gains held over a year are long-term capital gains" → annuity gains are always ordinary income.
  • "A VA in an IRA gets extra tax deferral" → the IRA already defers; the VA only adds cost.
  • "The AIR is the guaranteed minimum return" → it is an assumption for sizing payments, not a guarantee.
  • "Returns fell from 8% to 5%, so the payment falls" → compare with the AIR; above it, the payment still rises.
  • "Life only is safest for the family" → highest payout, most risk to heirs: nothing after death.
Trader connection

A VA with a guaranteed death or withdrawal benefit is the insurer SELLING a long-dated PUT on the customer's sub-accounts, plus an interest-rate guarantee. Insurers hedge that book dynamically: they BUY PUT on equity indices, sell index futures as markets fall (the put's delta grows, so the short hedge must grow), and receive fixed on long-dated rate swaps or buy swaptions because falling rates make the guarantees costlier. The hedge is pro-cyclical: selling futures into a falling market and receiving fixed into a rally in bonds. Insurer hedging demand is a large, persistent buyer of long-dated equity volatility and of duration at the long end of the swap curve.

Check yourself

Q1 Which of the following is a security?
A. A fixed annuity
B. A variable annuity
C. A fixed indexed annuity with a principal floor
D. A bank certificate of deposit

Answer: B. In a variable annuity the owner bears investment risk in a separate account, so it is a registered security. Fixed annuities (A) and fixed indexed annuities with a floor (C) are insurance products backed by the insurer's general account. A CD (D) is a bank deposit.

Q2 A variable annuity has an AIR of 4%. Sub-account returns were 7% last month and 5% this month. This month's payment compared with last month's will:
A. Decrease, because returns fell
B. Stay the same
C. Increase
D. Decrease to the AIR level

Answer: C. The payment moves relative to the AIR, not relative to last month: 5% is above the 4% AIR, so the payment rises, by less than last month's increase. A compares with the prior return, the classic trap. B would require exactly 4%. D invents a floor.

Q3 A 55-year-old withdraws $20,000 from a nonqualified annuity with $50,000 of basis and a $80,000 value. How is the withdrawal taxed?
A. $20,000 ordinary income plus a 10% penalty
B. $20,000 tax-free return of basis
C. $20,000 long-term capital gain
D. $12,500 tax-free and $7,500 ordinary income

Answer: A. LIFO: the $30,000 of earnings come out first, so the whole $20,000 is earnings, taxed as ordinary income, and she is under 59½, so the 10% penalty applies. B treats it as FIFO. C is wrong because annuity gains are never capital gains. D applies an exclusion-ratio split, which is only for annuitized payments.

Q4 Which settlement option pays the largest monthly amount?
A. Joint and last survivor
B. Life with 10-year period certain
C. Unit refund
D. Life only

Answer: D. Life only carries no obligation after the annuitant's death, so the insurer pays the most per month. Every other option promises something to a beneficiary or a second life, which lowers the payment; joint and last survivor (A) is typically the lowest.

Q5 Which of the following qualify as tax-free 1035 exchanges? I. Variable annuity to fixed annuity II. Whole life policy to variable annuity III. Variable annuity to variable life policy IV. Whole life policy to variable life policy
A. I, II and IV
B. I and III
C. II and III
D. I, II, III and IV

Answer: A. Annuity to annuity (I), life to annuity (II) and life to life (IV) qualify. Annuity to life insurance (III) does not, which rules out B, C and D.

Q6 A customer who is making monthly purchase payments into an annuity owns:
A. An immediate annuity
B. A deferred annuity
C. A single-premium immediate annuity
D. A fixed annuity only

Answer: B. An immediate annuity requires a single lump-sum premium; periodic purchase payments mean the payout is deferred. A and C contradict the payment pattern. D is wrong because deferred annuities can be fixed or variable.

Q7 An annuitant's cost basis is $90,000 and expected total payments are $150,000. How much of a $1,500 payment is taxable?
A. $900
B. $1,500
C. $600
D. $0

Answer: C. Exclusion ratio = 90,000 ÷ 150,000 = 60% excluded, so 0.60 × 1,500 = $900 is tax-free and $1,500 − $900 = $600 is ordinary income. A is the excluded portion, not the taxable one. B ignores basis recovery; D ignores earnings.

Day 15 · How securities are born: underwriting and registration

FINRA 1.4 · pairs with the Day 15 edition (~110 min)

Every security starts life in the primary market: an issuer sells new securities to investors, usually through investment banks that take on some or all of the risk. The SIE tests the Securities Act of 1933 timeline (what you may and may not do in each period), the types of underwriting commitment, the spread arithmetic, stabilization, and who may buy an IPO. On a desk, a firm-commitment deal is a block of inventory on the bank's balance sheet, and the greenshoe is the hedge that manages it.

The Securities Act of 1933: disclosure, not approval

The Securities Act of 1933 governs the primary market: the sale of new issues by issuers. Its core rule (Section 5) is simple: a non-exempt security may not be offered or sold to the public unless a registration statement has been filed with the SEC, and it may not be sold until that statement is effective. The statement must disclose the business, risks, use of proceeds, management and audited financials. Part I of it, the part investors receive, is the prospectus. An IPO issuer files on Form S-1; larger, already-public companies can use a shorter form that incorporates their existing SEC filings by reference.

The SEC reviews for completeness of disclosure, sends comment letters, and can delay effectiveness if something is missing. It never judges whether the deal is a good investment, and it never guarantees that the disclosure is true. That is why every prospectus carries a legend saying, in substance, that the SEC has neither approved nor disapproved of the securities or passed on the accuracy or adequacy of the prospectus, and that any statement to the contrary is a crime. A rep who says "the SEC approved this offering" is committing a violation.

Responsibility for accuracy sits with the people who sign and sell: the issuer, its directors and signing officers, the underwriters, and experts such as the auditors. Investors who buy on a registration statement with a material misstatement can sue them. The underwriters' defense is due diligence: showing they made a reasonable investigation. That legal exposure is why the diligence process below exists.

The players

Issuer --(new shares)--> Managing underwriter
Managing underwriter --(allocations)--> Syndicate members
Syndicate --(shares, as agents)--> Selling group
Syndicate + selling group --(shares at POP)--> Investors
Investors --(cash at POP)--> Syndicate
Syndicate --(POP minus spread)--> Issuer
  • Issuer: the company (or government) raising money.
  • Managing underwriter (lead manager, book-runner): negotiates with the issuer, forms and runs the syndicate, keeps the book of orders, allocates shares, and stabilizes.
  • Syndicate: the group of underwriters that commit capital. They sign an agreement among underwriters (the syndicate agreement), and in a firm commitment they are on the hook for unsold shares.
  • Selling group: broker-dealers brought in to sell to their customers. They act as agents, earn only the selling concession, and are not at risk for unsold shares.

Syndicate liability for unsold securities is split in one of two ways. In a Western (divided) account, each member is responsible only for its own allocation. In an Eastern (undivided) account, each member is also responsible for its percentage share of whatever remains unsold across the whole syndicate. Memory hook: Western = divided, like the West split into states.

The registration timeline

PRE-FILING   no offers, no sales; draft the S-1
   |
FILING       registration statement goes to the SEC
   |
COOLING-OFF  at least 20 days; red herring, IOIs only
   |
EFFECTIVE    SEC declares effective; price, sell, confirm
   |
AFTERMARKET  trading; prospectus-delivery window

1. Pre-filing period

The issuer picks its underwriters, negotiates the type of commitment, and drafts the registration statement with lawyers and auditors. The issuer and managing underwriter often sign a letter of intent setting out the deal terms (not to be confused with Day 12's mutual fund letter of intent). No offers and no sales are allowed, and publicity designed to condition the market for the offering (gun-jumping) is prohibited. The SEC has carved out limited "test-the-waters" talks with large institutional investors; the exam rarely goes further than "no offers to the public before filing".

2. Filing and the cooling-off period

Filing starts the cooling-off (waiting) period: at least 20 calendar days, longer if the SEC issues a deficiency letter and the issuer must amend. The purpose is to give investors time to read the disclosure before anyone can take their money. During cooling-off:

AllowedProhibited
Distributing the preliminary prospectus (red herring)Any sale, or taking money
Taking indications of interest (IOIs), which are non-bindingBinding commitments or accepted orders
Tombstone announcementsSales literature or advertising beyond the permitted forms
Free-writing prospectuses and road shows, under SEC conditionsHighlighting, annotating or summarizing the red herring for a customer
The due diligence meeting; state (blue-sky) registrationGuaranteeing an allocation or a price

Red herring: the preliminary prospectus, named for the red legend on its cover stating that the registration is not yet effective and the information may change. It contains nearly everything except the final public offering price (POP) and effective date; it usually shows an expected price range. A rep may send it but may not mark it up.

Indication of interest (IOI): a customer's statement that she would like, say, 1,000 shares. It is non-binding on both sides: the customer can walk away and the firm need not fill it. IOIs are how the book-runner builds the book and gauges demand to set the price.

Tombstone: a bare-bones announcement of the deal: issuer, security, amount, price or range, underwriters' names, where to get a prospectus, and a statement that it is not an offer to sell. It is permitted during cooling-off and after; it is not required.

Free-writing prospectus (FWP): any written offer other than the statutory prospectus, such as a term sheet or electronic road-show materials. For an IPO issuer, an FWP must be accompanied or preceded by the statutory prospectus (a hyperlink satisfies this), and many FWPs must be filed with the SEC.

Due diligence meeting: held near the end of cooling-off. The underwriters question the issuer's management about the business and the registration statement. It builds the underwriters' due-diligence defense.

Blue-sky: state registration, under each state's securities law. Most IPOs register by coordination, which runs the state filing alongside the federal one so both become effective together. Securities listed on a national exchange are federal "covered securities", for which states can require only a notice filing and fee.

3. Effective date and pricing

When the SEC's comments are resolved, the registration is declared effective. The deal is priced (typically after the close, the evening before trading), the final prospectus with the POP and the spread is prepared, orders are confirmed, and trading begins. Only now can money change hands. Effectiveness is permission to sell, not approval.

How the price is set: during cooling-off the book-runner collects IOIs from institutions and retail desks at different prices and sizes (book-building). If the book is several times oversubscribed, the deal may be priced at the top of the range or above it, or upsized; a weak book means pricing below the range or postponing. The manager then allocates shares, with discretion, subject to Rule 5130 below. IPOs are usually priced a little below where the book suggests the stock will trade, so that buyers see a first-day gain; that "pop" is money the issuer left on the table and the reason IPO allocations are rationed. Insiders normally sign lock-up agreements with the underwriters, commonly for 180 days, promising not to sell in the aftermarket. A lock-up is a contract, not an SEC rule.

4. The aftermarket and prospectus delivery

After effectiveness, dealers selling the security in the secondary market must still deliver a prospectus for a period, so early aftermarket buyers get the same disclosure as IPO buyers. The length depends on whether it is an IPO and whether the security is listed. Under SEC rules the final prospectus filed with the SEC counts as delivered ("access equals delivery"), with a notice of the sale sent to buyers.

OfferingListed on an exchange (incl. Nasdaq)Not listed (OTC)
IPO25 days90 days
Follow-on (issuer already public)0 days40 days

Just memorize 25 / 90 / 0 / 40. The logic: less information exists about an IPO issuer or an unlisted one, so the window is longer.

Types of underwriting commitment

The commitment decides who bears the risk that the deal does not sell, and whether the underwriter acts as principal or agent.

TypeCapacityWhat happens to unsold securities
Firm commitmentPrincipalUnderwriters buy the whole issue from the issuer and resell; unsold shares stay in their inventory. Risk on the underwriters.
Best effortsAgentUnderwriters sell what they can; unsold shares are returned to the issuer. Risk on the issuer.
All-or-noneAgentBest efforts, but if the full amount is not sold the deal is cancelled and investors' money (held in escrow) is returned.
Mini-maxAgentBest efforts with a minimum that must be sold (money in escrow until reached), then sales continue up to a maximum.
StandbyPrincipalUsed in rights offerings: the underwriter buys whatever shares existing holders do not subscribe for.
 
FIRM COMMITMENT (principal)
Issuer --(all shares)--> Underwriter, pays POP - spread
Underwriter --(resells at POP)--> Investors
Unsold? --> underwriter's inventory, underwriter's loss

BEST EFFORTS (agent)
Issuer --(shares on consignment)--> Underwriter
Underwriter --(sells what it can)--> Investors
Unsold? --> back to issuer, issuer's shortfall

Note what a firm commitment does to the bank's balance sheet: at pricing it owes the issuer cash for every share, whether or not it has buyers, and it must hold capital against that position. That capital charge and the market risk are what the underwriting fee pays for. Large, established issuers get firm commitments; small or speculative issuers often get best efforts, AON or mini-max because no bank will put its own capital behind the deal.

The spread and who earns it

The underwriting spread is the difference between the POP and the price the underwriters pay the issuer. It is divided into three pieces:

  • Manager's fee: to the managing underwriter, on every share in the deal, for running it. Smallest piece.
  • Underwriting fee: to the syndicate members, for committing capital and bearing the risk.
  • Selling concession: to whichever firm actually sells the share to a customer (syndicate member or selling-group member). Largest piece.

Spread = POP − Price paid to issuer = Manager's fee + Underwriting fee + Selling concession

POP
public offering price investors pay per share
Price paid to issuer
what the underwriters remit per share
Manager's fee
per-share fee to the managing underwriter
Underwriting fee
per-share fee to syndicate members for bearing risk
Selling concession
per-share fee to the firm that sells the share

Proceeds to issuer = Shares × (POP − Spread)

Shares
number of shares sold in the offering
POP − Spread
net price per share paid to the issuer
Worked example
  1. IPO of 10,000,000 shares at a POP of $20.00. The spread is $1.40: manager's fee $0.20, underwriting fee $0.30, selling concession $0.90.
  2. Price to issuer = 20.00 − 1.40 = $18.60. Proceeds to issuer = 10,000,000 × 18.60 = $186,000,000.
  3. Gross spread to the banks = 10,000,000 × 1.40 = $14,000,000, which is 1.40 ÷ 20.00 = 7% of the offering.
  4. Per share, when a firm sells its own allocation: the manager earns 0.20 + 0.30 + 0.90 = $1.40; a syndicate member earns 0.30 + 0.90 = $1.20; a selling-group member earns only the $0.90 concession.

The investor pays the POP and no separate commission: the spread is built into the price.

Stabilization, the greenshoe and Regulation M

A new issue that breaks below its POP embarrasses the issuer and leaves the syndicate holding losses. The SEC allows one narrow exception to its anti-manipulation rules: the managing underwriter may enter a stabilizing bid to support the price, at or below the POP, never above it. Stabilization must be disclosed in the prospectus. The syndicate can also impose a penalty bid, taking back the selling concession from a member whose customers quickly flip their shares into the stabilizing bid.

In practice most stabilization runs through the greenshoe (overallotment option): the issuer grants the underwriters an option to buy additional shares at the POP less the spread, typically up to 15% of the deal for about 30 days.

Underwriters sell 115% of the base deal
 --> syndicate is SHORT 15%
Price falls below POP --> buy shares in the market
 --> covers the short AND supports the price
Price rises above POP --> exercise the greenshoe
 --> buy from issuer at POP - spread, cover the short

Regulation M is the SEC rule set behind this: it restricts underwriters, issuers and other distribution participants from bidding for or buying the offered security (or inducing others to) during a restricted period around the pricing, so they cannot pump the price they are about to sell at, and it sets the conditions for legitimate stabilization.

Quiet periods: informally, the period from pre-filing through the end of the aftermarket prospectus-delivery window, during which the issuer avoids promotional statements. FINRA's research rule adds a specific one: the managers and co-managers of an IPO may not publish research on the issuer for 10 days after the IPO (3 days after a follow-on offering).

After the IPO: follow-ons, shelves, primary vs secondary

  • Primary offering: the issuer sells new shares. Proceeds go to the company; existing holders are diluted.
  • Secondary offering (in this sense): existing holders (founders, private-equity owners) sell shares they already own. Proceeds go to them; the share count does not change. A split offering combines both.
  • Follow-on offering: any registered offering by a company that is already public. Note that "secondary" is also used loosely for any follow-on; the exam uses the primary/secondary distinction above.
  • Shelf registration (SEC Rule 415): a seasoned issuer registers securities in advance and sells them off the shelf in pieces ("takedowns") over up to three years, when market conditions suit. This lets the company raise money within hours instead of waiting through a new cooling-off period.
Primary:   Issuer --(new shares)--> Investors
           Investors --($)--> Issuer (balance sheet grows)
Secondary: Selling holder --(existing shares)--> Investors
           Investors --($)--> Selling holder (company: $0)
Worked example: a split offering
  1. A company has 100,000,000 shares outstanding. It sells 10,000,000 new shares (primary) and a private-equity owner sells 5,000,000 existing shares (secondary), all at $40.00 with a $1.20 spread.
  2. Shares outstanding after the deal: 100,000,000 + 10,000,000 = 110,000,000. The secondary shares add nothing.
  3. Company proceeds: 10,000,000 × (40.00 − 1.20) = $388,000,000. Selling holder: 5,000,000 × 38.80 = $194,000,000.
  4. Existing holders' ownership is diluted by 10 ÷ 110 = about 9.1%, caused only by the primary piece.

Who may buy an IPO: FINRA Rule 5130

Hot IPOs often open above the POP, so an allocation is valuable. FINRA Rule 5130 stops the industry from keeping that value for itself: a member firm may not sell a new equity IPO to an account in which a restricted person has a beneficial interest. Restricted persons include:

  • Broker-dealers and their personnel (employees, officers, registered persons);
  • The immediate family of those personnel: spouse, parents, in-laws, siblings, children, and anyone materially supported by the person;
  • Certain others with influence over allocations, such as portfolio managers buying for their own accounts.

Grandparents, aunts and uncles, and cousins are not restricted. A rep's grandmother may buy the IPO; the rep's sister may not. The rule covers new equity IPOs, not bond deals or follow-ons. A companion rule (5131) bars "spinning": allocating hot IPO shares to executives of companies in exchange for investment-banking business.

Other 1933 Act material (exempt securities, Reg D private placements, Rule 144) is covered on later days; today's rule is that everything not exempt must go through the timeline above.

Understand
  • The 1933 Act requires registration and disclosure of new issues; the SEC reviews disclosure but never approves the securities.
  • Timeline: pre-filing (no offers) → filing → cooling-off (at least 20 days; red herring, non-binding IOIs, tombstones, FWPs, due diligence, blue-sky) → effective (pricing, sales) → aftermarket (prospectus delivery).
  • Firm commitment and standby are principal deals with risk on the underwriter; best efforts, AON and mini-max are agency deals with risk on the issuer.
  • Syndicate members commit capital; selling-group members are agents with no risk for unsold shares.
  • Spread = manager's fee + underwriting fee + selling concession; the concession is the largest.
  • Stabilization is legal only at or below the POP; Reg M restricts distribution participants' trading; the greenshoe finances stabilization.
  • Primary shares raise money for the company; secondary shares raise money for selling holders; shelves allow fast follow-ons.
MEMORIZE:
  • Cooling-off period → at least 20 calendar days
  • Aftermarket prospectus delivery → IPO 25 days (listed) / 90 days (unlisted); follow-on 0 (listed) / 40 (unlisted)
  • Stabilizing bid → at or below the POP
  • SEC disclaimer → "neither approved nor disapproved"
  • Largest part of the spread → selling concession
  • Selling group → agents; earn only the concession; not at risk for unsold shares
  • IOI → non-binding; the only "order" allowed during cooling-off
  • IPO restricted family → spouse, parents, in-laws, siblings, children, anyone materially supported (not grandparents, aunts/uncles, cousins)
  • Western account → divided liability; Eastern → undivided
Exam traps
  • "A customer can place a binding order during cooling-off" → only non-binding IOIs; no sales, no money.
  • "Firm commitment underwriters act as agents" → firm commitment = principal; best efforts = agency.
  • "Tombstones must wait for the effective date" → tombstones are allowed during cooling-off.
  • "Selling-group members must buy unsold shares" → they are agents and bear no underwriting risk.
  • "The effective date means the SEC approved the offering" → effectiveness permits sales; it is not approval.
  • "Stabilizing bids may be above the POP to show strength" → at or below the POP only.
  • "A rep's grandparent cannot buy an IPO" → grandparents are not restricted persons; siblings and in-laws are.
  • "The manager's fee is the largest piece" → the selling concession is.
Trader connection

A firm commitment is a principal position: from pricing until the last share is placed, the syndicate is long the issue and exposed to market risk, which is why deals are priced after the close and allocated overnight. Banks manage that risk by overallotting: selling 115% makes the syndicate net short 15% against a call option (the greenshoe) struck at the issuer's net price. If the stock trades down, the syndicate buys in the aftermarket to cover, and that buying is the stabilization; if it trades up, it exercises the option. The short plus greenshoe is a hedged position in which the underwriter is paid either way. For a trader, the first days after an IPO carry predictable flows: short covering near the POP, flipping by fast-money accounts, the end of the research quiet period, and later lock-up expiries that release insider supply.

Check yourself

Q1 An issuer sells 5,000,000 shares at a POP of $30.00. The underwriting spread is $1.80 per share. What are the proceeds to the issuer?
A. $150,000,000
B. $141,000,000
C. $159,000,000
D. $9,000,000

Answer: B. Proceeds = shares × (POP − spread) = 5,000,000 × (30.00 − 1.80) = 5,000,000 × 28.20 = $141,000,000. A is gross proceeds before the spread. C adds the spread instead of subtracting it. D is the underwriters' total spread (5,000,000 × 1.80).

Q2 During the cooling-off period, a registered representative may do all of the following EXCEPT:
A. Send a customer a preliminary prospectus
B. Take an indication of interest
C. Accept a customer's check to reserve shares
D. Provide a tombstone announcement

Answer: C. No sales and no money may be taken before the effective date; accepting payment turns an IOI into a sale. A, B and D are all permitted during cooling-off.

Q3 Arrange the stages of a new issue in order. I. Effective date II. Pre-filing III. Cooling-off IV. Aftermarket V. Filing of the registration statement
A. II, III, V, I, IV
B. V, II, III, I, IV
C. II, V, I, III, IV
D. II, V, III, I, IV

Answer: D. Pre-filing → filing → cooling-off (at least 20 days) → effective date → aftermarket. A places cooling-off before filing, but filing is what starts it. B puts filing first. C puts the effective date before cooling-off.

Q4 A registered representative wants to allocate shares of a hot equity IPO to relatives. Which may buy? I. His grandmother II. His brother-in-law III. His cousin IV. His adult daughter, whom he does not support
A. I and III
B. II and IV
C. I, II and III
D. I, III and IV

Answer: A. Under FINRA Rule 5130, immediate family of industry personnel includes spouse, parents, in-laws, siblings and children (whether or not supported), plus anyone materially supported. Grandparents and cousins are outside it. The brother-in-law (II) and the daughter (IV) are restricted, which eliminates B, C and D.

Q5 A company already listed on an exchange completes a follow-on offering. For how long must dealers deliver a prospectus in the aftermarket?
A. 0 days
B. 25 days
C. 40 days
D. 90 days

Answer: A. For a follow-on of a listed security there is no aftermarket prospectus-delivery period. B is a listed IPO, C an unlisted follow-on, D an unlisted IPO.

Q6 In a firm-commitment offering, which party bears the risk of unsold shares?
A. The issuer
B. Members of the selling group
C. The underwriting syndicate
D. The SEC

Answer: C. In a firm commitment the syndicate buys the entire issue as principal, so unsold shares are its loss. The issuer (A) bears the risk in best-efforts deals. The selling group (B) acts as agent and never bears underwriting risk. The SEC (D) reviews disclosure only.

Q7 An IPO is priced at $15.00. The managing underwriter may place a stabilizing bid at:
A. $15.25, to signal demand
B. $15.00 or less
C. Any price, if disclosed in the prospectus
D. Only the opening trade price

Answer: B. A stabilizing bid may be entered at or below the POP, never above it. A is above the POP, which would be manipulation. C is wrong because disclosure is required but does not lift the price cap. D invents a rule.

Q8 The spread on an offering is $1.50: manager's fee $0.25, underwriting fee $0.35, selling concession $0.90. A selling-group member sells 1,000 shares. What does it earn?
A. $1,500
B. $1,250
C. $600
D. $900

Answer: D. Selling-group members earn only the selling concession: 1,000 × $0.90 = $900. A is the full spread, which the manager earns on its own sales. B (0.35 + 0.90) is what a syndicate member earns on its own sales. C (0.25 + 0.35) is the non-selling portion.

Day 16 · The exemptions: private placements, Rule 144 and tender offers

FINRA 1.4, 3.3 · pairs with the Day 16 edition (~100 min)

Under the Securities Act of 1933 every offer and sale of a security must be registered unless an exemption applies. Today is about the exits from that rule: securities that never register, deals that do not have to, and the conditions for reselling what came through those exits. Then the other end of corporate control: tender offers, the 5% disclosure line and Section 16 insiders. On a desk this is the plumbing of private placements, 144A bond deals, executives' block sales and merger arbitrage.

Two different things called "exempt"

The exam separates two ideas and loves to blur them.

An exempt security is exempt because of what it is. It never registers, no matter who sells it or when. The antifraud rules still apply: exempt from registration never means exempt from fraud liability.

An exempt transaction is exempt because of how this particular deal is done: few buyers, rich buyers, one state, small size. The security itself is not exempt. Shares sold in a private placement come out restricted, and the buyer needs another exemption (usually Rule 144) to resell them.

Exempt securities (never register)Why
U.S. Treasury and agency securitiesThe government is the issuer
Municipal securitiesStates and localities; sold with an official statement, not a prospectus
Commercial paper and bankers' acceptances maturing in 270 days or lessShort-term, sold to institutions (usually $100,000+ pieces)
Securities issued by a bankBank regulators already supervise the issuer. A bank holding company is not a bank: its stock registers.
Securities of non-profit religious, charitable and educational issuersPolicy choice
Insurance policies and fixed annuitiesThe insurer carries the risk. A variable annuity is a security and must register.

Commercial paper at 271 days is no longer exempt. The line is Just memorize 270 days (nine months).

Regulation D: the private placement

Regulation D (Reg D) is the workhorse transaction exemption for raising private capital. The issuer (or a placement agent) sells directly to investors who are presumed able to look after themselves. The document is an offering memorandum, also called a private placement memorandum (PPM), not a prospectus.

An accredited investor (Rule 501) is, for individuals: income over $200,000 in each of the last two years ($300,000 joint with a spouse) with a reasonable expectation of the same this year; or net worth over $1 million excluding the primary residence; or a holder in good standing of the Series 7, 65 or 82 licence. Directors and executive officers of the issuer also count. Institutions qualify on their own tests.

Rule 506(b)Rule 506(c)
BuyersUnlimited accredited + up to 35 non-accredited who are sophisticatedAccredited only
General solicitation (ads, open websites)Not allowedAllowed
Accredited statusReasonable belief is enoughIssuer must take reasonable steps to verify
DisclosureNon-accredited buyers must get registration-style disclosureNo mandated disclosure package
Size capNone

After the first sale the issuer files Form D with the SEC within 15 days. Form D is a notice, not a registration: the SEC neither reviews nor approves the deal. Rule 506 securities are also "covered securities," so states cannot demand registration, only a notice filing and a fee.

Issuer --(PPM + shares)--> accredited buyers
Issuer --(Form D, 15 days after 1st sale)--> SEC
Buyer holds RESTRICTED stock --> resale needs Rule 144/144A

Rule 144: selling restricted and control stock

Rule 144 is the safe harbor that lets two kinds of holders sell into the public market without registering.

  • Restricted stock: acquired in an unregistered transaction (a Reg D purchase, pre-IPO founder or employee stock). The problem is the security's origin.
  • Control stock: any stock held by an affiliate, meaning a person who controls the issuer. For the exam, affiliates are officers, directors and 10%+ holders. The problem is the holder. Control stock can be fully registered stock the CEO bought on the NYSE.

A share can be both (a director's pre-IPO shares). The rule stacks up to five conditions: holding period, current public information about the issuer, volume limit, manner of sale (ordinary brokers' transactions or sales to a market maker), and the Form 144 notice.

Holder / stockHolding periodVolume limit and Form 144
Non-affiliate, restricted6 months (reporting issuer) or 1 year (non-reporting)None. Between 6 and 12 months the issuer must be current in its filings; after 1 year the stock is free.
Affiliate, restricted6 months / 1 yearYes, for as long as the holder is an affiliate
Affiliate, control stock (registered shares)NoneYes

The holding period starts when the securities are bought and fully paid. Form 144 is required when an affiliate's sales in any 90-day period exceed 5,000 shares or $50,000, and it is filed with the SEC no later than when the sell order is placed.

Max affiliate sales per 90 days = greater of (1% × shares outstanding) or (average weekly volume over the prior 4 weeks)

Shares outstanding
the latest figure the issuer has published
Average weekly volume
total reported trading volume for the 4 calendar weeks before filing Form 144, ÷ 4
90 days
a rolling window: sales in the previous 90 days count against the limit
Worked example
  1. A director of a listed company wants to sell. Shares outstanding: 48,000,000. 1% = 480,000.
  2. Weekly volume, last four weeks: 410,000 + 520,000 + 465,000 + 505,000 = 1,900,000. Average = 1,900,000 ÷ 4 = 475,000.
  3. Greater of 480,000 and 475,000 → limit = 480,000 shares per 90 days.
  4. She sold 100,000 shares 50 days ago, which still counts: 480,000 − 100,000 = 380,000 shares available now.
  5. 380,000 is well above 5,000 shares, so she files Form 144.

Shares sold under Rule 144 lose their restriction: the public buyer gets freely tradable stock.

Rule 144A and Regulation S: the institutional and offshore exits

Rule 144A lets restricted securities be resold, with no holding period, to qualified institutional buyers (QIBs): institutions that own or invest at least $100 million in securities. It created a deep private institutional market. Much high-yield bond issuance runs this way, as do many foreign issuers' U.S. deals.

Issuer --(private sale)--> banks ("initial purchasers")
Banks --(144A resale)--> QIBs (funds, insurers, pensions)
QIBs --(trade among themselves)--> other QIBs

Regulation S covers offers and sales made outside the United States. Because the sale is offshore, U.S. registration does not apply, provided there are no selling efforts directed into the U.S. and the securities do not flow back to U.S. buyers too early. A global bond is often "144A/Reg S": one tranche for U.S. QIBs, one for offshore investors.

Small and local offerings: Reg A, Rule 147/147A, Reg CF

ExemptionCap (12 months)Key features
Reg A Tier 1$20 million"Mini-IPO." Offering circular (Form 1-A) qualified by the SEC; the states also review it. Public can buy.
Reg A Tier 2$75 millionAudited financials, ongoing reports; no state registration; non-accredited buyers face investment limits unless the stock lists.
Rule 147 / 147A (intrastate)No federal capIssuer does business in one state and sells only to residents of that state. It registers with the state, not the SEC. Out-of-state resales are restricted for a period. 147A also allows out-of-state incorporation and internet offers, but sales still only to in-state residents.
Reg CF (crowdfunding)$5 millionMust run through one online intermediary: a registered funding portal or broker-dealer. Per-investor limits for non-accredited buyers; securities generally cannot be resold for one year.

Reg A shares are not restricted securities; buyers can resell them. Match each offering to its document:

Registered offering  --> prospectus
Reg D private placement --> offering memorandum (PPM)
