Reg A              --> offering circular
Municipal issue    --> official statement

Tender offers: the takeover route

A tender offer is a public offer to buy a company's shares directly from its holders, usually at a premium and often conditional on a minimum number being tendered. It goes around a target board that will not negotiate. The Williams Act amendments to the 1934 Act set the rules:

  • The offer stays open at least 20 business days, and at least 10 business days after any change in the price or the percentage sought.
  • It is open to all holders, and every holder gets the best price paid to anyone.
  • If a partial offer is oversubscribed, shares are bought pro rata.
  • Holders may withdraw tendered shares while the offer is open. The target board must tell holders its position within 10 business days.

The net-long rule (Rule 14e-4). You may tender only shares you are net long: long shares plus equivalents (such as calls you could exercise) minus any short position. Proration is the reason. If 25 million shares are tendered into an offer for 10 million, each tendering holder gets 40% taken at the premium. Without the rule, an arbitrageur could borrow shares, short them, and tender both the real and the borrowed shares. That would win a bigger slice of the premium and dilute every genuine holder's proration. Tendering borrowed shares ("short tendering") and tendering while hedged beyond your net position are both barred.

Worked example
  1. Bidder offers $50 for 10,000,000 shares; 25,000,000 are tendered. Proration = 10 ÷ 25 = 40%.
  2. An arb is long 300,000 shares and short 100,000 in another account. Net long = 300,000 − 100,000 = 200,000 → the most she may tender.
  3. Accepted: 200,000 × 40% = 80,000 shares at $50. The other 120,000 are returned to her.

Beneficial ownership: the 5% line

Anyone who acquires more than 5% of a class of voting equity must disclose it. The long form, Schedule 13D, is due within 5 business days of crossing 5% (the deadline was 10 days before the 2024 amendments; older books still say 10). 13D discloses identity, the source of funds and the purpose, such as seeking control or board seats. Schedule 13G is a short form for passive holders and qualified institutions with no control intent. Beneficial ownership counts shares you vote or dispose of, including shares you can acquire within 60 days (for example, through options or convertibles). Holders acting together are aggregated as a group.

Buy quietly --> cross 5% --> 13D within 5 business days
--> market sees stake + intent --> price reprices
--> cheap accumulation ends

That chain is why a secret takeover stake is impossible: you can build a toehold, but past 5% the market finds out within days.

Section 16 insiders and short-swing profits

Section 16 insiders are officers, directors and beneficial owners of more than 10% of a registered class of equity. They report on Form 3 when they become insiders (within 10 days), on Form 4 within 2 business days of any change in holdings, and on an annual Form 5 (within 45 days of fiscal year-end) for anything not already reported.

Short-swing rule (16(b)): any profit from a purchase and a sale, or a sale and a purchase, within 6 months belongs to the company. It is strict liability: no one has to show the insider used inside information. Section 16(c) also bars insiders from selling their own company's stock short.

Worked example
  1. A director buys 10,000 shares at $30 in February and sells 10,000 at $42 in June. Four months apart, so it is inside 6 months.
  2. Short-swing profit = (42 − 30) × 10,000 = $120,000, paid to the company, even if he sold to pay tuition.
  3. The June sale is also reported on Form 4 within 2 business days.

Separately, insider trading (Rule 10b-5) catches anyone, insider or not, who trades on material non-public information in breach of a duty, including tippees. Under ITSFEA (1988) the civil penalty can reach 3× the profit gained or loss avoided; criminal penalties include up to 20 years in prison. Section 16 is mechanical; 10b-5 is about information.

Understand
  • Exempt security = the instrument never registers. Exempt transaction = this deal is excused, but the paper comes out restricted.
  • Reg D trades a registration statement for buyer quality: accredited investors, a limited number of sophisticated non-accredited buyers in 506(b), and a Form D notice.
  • Rule 144 is the resale valve. Restricted stock is a problem of origin; control stock is a problem of who holds it. Affiliates face volume limits and Form 144 forever.
  • 144A (QIBs) and Reg S (offshore) move unregistered paper among professionals and foreign buyers.
  • Reg A, 147/147A and Reg CF are small-offering routes with size caps or geographic limits.
  • Tender-offer rules give holders time (20 business days), equal treatment and pro rata fills. The net-long rule stops borrowed shares from gaming proration.
  • 13D is disclosure of a stake. Section 16 is reporting plus short-swing disgorgement for insiders.
MEMORIZE:
  • Accredited individual → income > $200k ($300k joint) for 2 years, or net worth > $1M ex-primary residence, or Series 7/65/82 holder
  • 506(b) → up to 35 non-accredited; no general solicitation
  • 506(c) → general solicitation OK; all buyers accredited and verified
  • Form D → within 15 days after first sale
  • Rule 144 holding period → 6 months (reporting issuer) / 1 year (non-reporting)
  • Rule 144 volume → greater of 1% of outstanding or 4-week average weekly volume, per 90 days
  • Form 144 → sales > 5,000 shares or > $50,000 in 90 days
  • 144A QIB → $100 million in securities
  • Reg A → Tier 1 $20M, Tier 2 $75M per 12 months
  • Reg CF → $5M per 12 months, via funding portal or BD
  • Commercial paper exempt → maturity ≤ 270 days
  • Tender offer → open ≥ 20 business days; +10 business days after a price or percentage change
  • Schedule 13D → 5 business days after crossing 5% (was 10 days before 2024)
  • Section 16 insider → officer, director, 10%+ holder; Form 4 in 2 business days; short-swing = 6 months
  • PPM → Reg D; official statement → munis; prospectus → registered; offering circular → Reg A
Exam traps
  • "Commercial paper is exempt" → only if it matures in 270 days or less.
  • "Reg D securities are exempt securities" → Reg D is a transaction exemption; the stock is restricted and resale needs 144/144A.
  • "Control stock is unregistered stock" → control stock can be registered stock; it is the affiliate holder that triggers 144.
  • "Control stock has a 6-month holding period" → no holding period, but volume limits, manner of sale and Form 144 apply.
  • "Rule 147 means SEC registration for one state" → intrastate offerings register with the state, not the SEC.
  • "Bank holding company stock is exempt like bank stock" → only the bank's own securities are exempt.
  • "13D within 10 days" → 5 business days since the 2024 amendments; the 10-day figure is outdated prep material.
  • "Short-swing profits require proof of inside information" → strict liability; the profit goes to the company regardless.
Trader connection

A founder or executive with a large, concentrated, restricted position cannot simply dump it. Rule 144 caps the pace, blackout windows block timing, and Section 16 makes every move public. The equity-derivatives desk's answer is to hedge instead of sell. A collar is BUY PUT (floor) + SELL CALL (cap), often struck so the call premium pays for the put. A prepaid variable forward advances the holder most of the stock's value today in exchange for delivering a variable number of shares at maturity. The dealer hedges by borrowing and shorting stock, so its hedge sales are usually run within the holder's 144 volume and manner-of-sale limits, and an insider reports the derivative on Form 4. In merger arbitrage, a cash tender offer is a bet on completion plus proration. The net-long rule is why an arb's tender is capped at the real long position, and a 13D filing is often the first hard signal that a bid is coming. Around all of this sit the ECM and DCM desks, which choose between a registered deal, a 144A or a Reg D placement for each issuer. Compliance keeps restricted lists and checks every affiliate sale against 144 before the order leaves the desk.

Check yourself

Q1 An affiliate of a listed company wants to sell under Rule 144. The company has 26,000,000 shares outstanding. Weekly volume over the last four weeks was 250,000, 310,000, 280,000 and 360,000 shares. What is the most the affiliate may sell in the next 90 days, assuming no prior sales?
A. 260,000
B. 300,000
C. 360,000
D. 1,200,000

Answer: B. The limit is the greater of 1% of outstanding (26,000,000 × 1% = 260,000) and the 4-week average weekly volume ((250,000 + 310,000 + 280,000 + 360,000) ÷ 4 = 1,200,000 ÷ 4 = 300,000). The greater is 300,000. A is the 1% test alone, C is the single highest week, and D is the four-week total, not the average.

Q2 All of the following are exempt securities under the Securities Act of 1933 EXCEPT:
A. a 10-year Treasury note
B. a city's general obligation bond
C. 180-day commercial paper
D. common stock of a bank holding company

Answer: D. Securities issued by a bank are exempt, but a bank holding company is an ordinary corporation and its stock must register. Treasuries and munis are exempt securities. Commercial paper is exempt when it matures in 270 days or less, and 180 days qualifies.

Q3 Which statements about a Rule 506(b) offering are true?
I. Up to 35 non-accredited purchasers may participate
II. General solicitation is permitted
III. Form D is filed within 15 days after the first sale
IV. Purchasers may freely resell immediately
A. I and III
B. I and II
C. II and IV
D. III and IV

Answer: A. 506(b) allows unlimited accredited buyers plus up to 35 sophisticated non-accredited buyers, and Form D follows within 15 days of the first sale. General solicitation (II) is the feature of 506(c), not 506(b). The securities are restricted, so immediate free resale (IV) is wrong.

Q4 Which of the following individuals is an accredited investor?
A. A single engineer earning $180,000 in each of the last two years
B. A married couple with joint income of $280,000 in each of the last two years
C. A registered representative holding a Series 65 licence, net worth $150,000
D. A retiree with $1.1 million net worth, $400,000 of it equity in her home

Answer: C. Holders in good standing of the Series 7, 65 or 82 qualify regardless of wealth. A is below $200,000 and B below the $300,000 joint test. D looks tempting, but net worth excludes the primary residence: $1.1M − $400,000 = $700,000, under $1 million.

Q5 A director buys 5,000 shares of his company at $20 in January and sells them at $26 in May of the same year. What is the consequence?
A. The $30,000 profit must be paid to the company
B. None, provided he had no inside information
C. He must file Form 3 within 10 days
D. He owes a civil penalty of three times the profit

Answer: A. A purchase and sale within 6 months by a Section 16 insider produces short-swing profit: (26 − 20) × 5,000 = $30,000, disgorged to the company. It is strict liability, so B is wrong. Form 3 is the initial insider filing (the sale goes on Form 4). The 3× penalty in D belongs to insider trading under ITSFEA, which requires trading on inside information.

Q6 An issuer selling shares to accredited investors under Regulation D would typically provide which document?
A. A statutory prospectus
B. An official statement
C. An offering circular
D. A private placement memorandum

Answer: D. Reg D placements use an offering memorandum, also called a private placement memorandum. A prospectus goes with a registered offering, an official statement with a municipal issue, and an offering circular with Reg A.

Q7 On the 15th business day of a tender offer the bidder raises its price. What is the earliest the offer may close?
A. On the 20th business day, as originally scheduled
B. 10 business days after the price change
C. 20 business days after the price change
D. Immediately, since holders now get more

Answer: B. A change in price or percentage sought requires the offer to stay open at least 10 more business days. Day 15 + 10 = day 25, later than the original day 20, so A is wrong. The offer does not restart a full 20 days, so C is wrong. Holders must have time to react, so D is wrong.

Q8 An arbitrageur holds 50,000 shares of a target long in one account and is short 20,000 shares of the same stock in another. In a partial tender offer, how many shares may she tender?
A. 50,000
B. 70,000
C. 30,000
D. 20,000

Answer: C. Under Rule 14e-4 only the net long position may be tendered: 50,000 − 20,000 = 30,000. A ignores the short, which is exactly what the rule prevents. B adds the short as if borrowed shares could be tendered. D confuses the short with the permitted amount.

Day 17 · Orders, quotes and the people who make markets

FINRA 3.1, 1.2 · pairs with the Day 17 edition (~110 min)

Every trade is a customer's order meeting someone's quote. Today covers how quotes are built and who stands behind them, what each order type guarantees (the execution or the price, never both), where stops and limits sit, and the rules that govern routing and ticketing an order. The SIE asks at least one stop-order sequence and one "where does this order go" question. On a desk, this chapter is the difference between paying the spread and earning it.

Reading a quote: bid, ask, spread, size

A quote has two sides. The bid is the highest price a buyer (often a dealer) will pay, so it is where you can sell right now. The ask (or offer) is the lowest price a seller will accept, so it is where you can buy right now. The spread is the gap between them, and it is the dealer's gross compensation for standing there. Size is how much is available at each price, quoted in round lots of 100 shares.

Spread = Ask − Bid

Ask (offer)
the lowest price at which someone will sell to you
Bid
the highest price at which someone will buy from you
Worked example
  1. Quote: 42.10 – 42.14, size 8 × 5.
  2. Spread = 42.14 − 42.10 = $0.04 per share.
  3. Size 8 × 5 = 800 shares bid at 42.10, 500 shares offered at 42.14.
  4. A customer who buys 500 at 42.14 and immediately sells 500 at 42.10 loses 0.04 × 500 = $20. That is the round-trip cost of immediacy, before commissions.

Several dealers quote the same stock at once. The best of them together form the inside market: the highest bid and the lowest ask anywhere. Across all U.S. venues this is the NBBO, the national best bid and offer.

DealerBidAsk
MM142.0942.14
MM242.1042.16
MM342.0842.13
Inside market42.10 (MM2)42.13 (MM3)

The inside spread (3 cents) is narrower than any single dealer's spread. Competition among quoters is what tightens it, which is why a customer selling should reach MM2's bid and a customer buying should reach MM3's offer.

Quotes come in three strengths:

QuoteMeaningObligation
Firm"I will trade at this price for this size."Must honour it for at least the displayed size. Refusing is backing away, a violation.
Subject (workout)"Around 98–99, subject to confirmation." Common in thin bonds.None until it is firmed up
NominalAn indication of value for information onlyNone; must be clearly labelled as nominal

Who makes markets

A market maker is a dealer that continuously posts firm two-sided quotes in a security and trades from its own inventory. Several compete in the same stock (the Nasdaq and OTC model), and each earns the spread while carrying inventory risk.

On the NYSE, each listed stock has one designated market maker (DMM), the successor to the old specialist. The DMM keeps a fair and orderly market in its stocks: it runs the opening and closing auctions and commits its own capital when natural buyers and sellers are out of balance. The exam still describes an exchange as an auction market (buyers and sellers meet through a central order book) and OTC as a negotiated or dealer market.

TermMeaning
First marketExchange-listed stock traded on the exchange
Second marketOTC trading of unlisted securities
Third marketExchange-listed stock traded OTC by broker-dealers
Fourth marketInstitutions trading directly with each other, today through electronic venues

Capacity is what the firm was on your trade, and it decides how it gets paid:

AGENT (broker):  customer --order--> firm --finds--> other side
                 firm earns a COMMISSION
PRINCIPAL (dealer): customer <--trades with--> firm inventory
                 firm earns a MARKUP (sell to you) or
                 MARKDOWN (buy from you)

A firm is never both broker and dealer on the same trade. Capacity is disclosed on the confirmation. A hybrid you will meet: riskless principal. The firm receives a customer order, buys the stock from the market as principal and immediately sells it to the customer at a markup. Legally it is a principal trade; economically the firm took almost no inventory risk.

Interpositioning is placing an unnecessary third party between the customer and the best available market, for example routing through a friendly dealer who adds its own spread. The customer ends up with a worse price, so it violates best execution. It is permitted only when it actually produces a better net price for the customer.

The four order types: execution guarantee vs price guarantee

This is the most important idea of the day. Every order trades off two things: certainty of execution and certainty of price. No order gets both.

  • Market order = EXECUTION GUARANTEE. "Fill me now at the best available price." In a normal, open market with a quote it will execute, but the price is whatever the book gives you. You are paying for immediacy and accepting price risk (slippage).
  • Limit order = PRICE GUARANTEE. "Fill me only at my price or better." A buy limit at 50 fills at 50 or lower; a sell limit at 60 fills at 60 or higher. The price is guaranteed, but execution is not. If the market never comes to you, or comes and leaves before your turn in the queue, you get nothing.
  • Stop order = a trigger, then a market order. Dormant until the stop price is reached, then it becomes a market order. Once triggered ("elected"), it has an execution guarantee and no price guarantee.
  • Stop-limit order = a trigger, then a limit order. Once triggered it becomes a limit order. It has a price guarantee but no execution guarantee, and it can be triggered and never fill.

See the difference against a real order book. Offers in XYZ: 300 at 50.02, 500 at 50.05, 1,000 at 50.10.

Worked example
  1. Market buy 1,000: takes 300 @ 50.02 + 500 @ 50.05 + 200 @ 50.10. Cost = 15,006 + 25,025 + 10,020 = $50,051; average $50.051. Fully filled (execution guaranteed), but the average is almost 3 cents above the best offer (no price guarantee).
  2. Buy limit 1,000 at 50.03: takes 300 @ 50.02 (better than the limit, which is allowed). Nothing else is offered at or below 50.03, so 700 rest on the book as the new best bid. Price guaranteed, only 30% executed.
  3. Same wish, opposite guarantees. The customer chooses which risk to keep: price risk (market) or execution risk (limit).

Two refinements. A limit order priced through the market (a buy limit at or above the ask) is a marketable limit order: it executes at once like a market order but cannot pay more than its limit, a common way to cap slippage. And a resting limit order waits in a queue with price-time priority: better prices fill first, and at the same price, earlier orders fill first. A price guarantee does not help you when the market trades at your price but 20,000 shares were ahead of you in the queue.

OrderGuaranteesDoes not guaranteeMain risk
MarketExecutionPriceSlippage in a thin or fast market
LimitPrice (or better)ExecutionMissing the trade
StopNothing until triggered; then executionPriceFilled far through the stop in a gap
Stop-limitNothing until triggered; then priceExecutionTriggered, never filled, still exposed

Where each order sits relative to the market

Limits are placed where you would be happier to trade than at today's price. Stops are placed where you need to act if the market moves against you or breaks out. For stocks trading at 50:

         SELL LIMIT (take profit, sell higher)
         BUY STOP (cap a short's loss / buy a breakout)
   ---------------- market 50 ----------------
         BUY LIMIT (buy cheaper)
         SELL STOP (cap a long's loss / protect profit)

Memory aid: BLiSS = Buy Limit and Sell Stop sit below the market; the other two sit above. A buy limit above the market or a sell limit below it would simply execute immediately as a marketable order. A buy stop below the market would trigger at once.

  • Long 100 shares at 50, afraid of a drop: SELL STOP at 45, below the market.
  • Short 100 shares at 50, afraid of a rally: BUY STOP at 55, above the market. Buying back closes the short.
  • Want to buy only on a dip: BUY LIMIT at 47.
  • Want to take profit on a rally: SELL LIMIT at 58.

Stop sequences: when does the stop trigger?

Exam convention: a stop is elected when a trade occurs at or through the stop price. A buy stop needs a trade at or above it; a sell stop needs a trade at or below it. The electing trade is not your fill. Once elected, your order becomes a market (or limit) order and executes on a later trade.

Worked example: sell stop at 36
  1. Trades: 36.40, 36.10, 35.90, 35.75, 36.05.
  2. 36.40 and 36.10 are above 36, so nothing happens.
  3. 35.90 is at or below 36, so the stop is elected and becomes a market sell order.
  4. It executes on the next trade: 35.75, 25 cents worse than the stop price. That is the lack of a price guarantee.
Worked example: buy stop-limit, stop 52, limit 52.25
  1. Trades: 51.80, 52.30, 52.40, 52.20.
  2. 51.80 is below 52, so nothing happens. 52.30 is at or above 52, so the order is elected and becomes a buy limit at 52.25.
  3. 52.40 is above the limit, so no fill. 52.20 is at or below 52.25, so it fills at 52.20.
  4. If the stock had gapped to 53 and stayed there, the order would be live, unfilled and useless: a stop-limit can be triggered and never fill.

The quick test for any sequence: a buy stop waits for the first print at or above the stop; a sell stop waits for the first print at or below it. A trader who wants to buy only if a stock breaks out above resistance at 80 enters a BUY STOP at 80.25. Prints of 79.90, 80.10 and 80.20 do nothing. A print of 80.25 elects it, and it fills at the next price, perhaps 80.40.

Gaps are the real-world hazard. A stock closing at 40 with a sell stop at 38 that opens at 31 after bad news fills near 31, not 38. Some brokers elect stops off quotes rather than trades, but the exam uses trades.

Open orders and dividends (FINRA Rule 5330). When a stock goes ex-dividend, it drops by roughly the dividend. So orders resting below the market (buy limits, sell stops, sell stop-limits) are reduced by the dividend amount on the ex-date, unless the customer marked them DNR (do not reduce). Orders above the market are not adjusted.

Time in force and special instructions

InstructionMeaningImmediate?Partial fill OK?
DayExpires at the close if unfilled (the default)NoYes
GTC (good 'til cancelled, "open")Stays until filled or cancelled; firms typically cap how long it stays openNoYes
FOK (fill or kill)The whole order immediately, or cancel itYesNo
IOC (immediate or cancel)Fill whatever can be filled immediately; cancel the restYesYes
AON (all or none)The whole order or nothing, but it may waitNoNo

Separate them by two questions: must it be immediate, and may it be partial? FOK = immediate, not partial. IOC = immediate, partial OK. AON = not immediate, not partial.

A not-held order (market not held) gives the rep or trader discretion over time and price to work the order for a better result. The customer cannot hold the firm to the price available when the order was given. Time-and-price discretion is valid only for the business day it is granted unless the customer specifies otherwise in writing. It is not full discretion: the customer still chose the security, the side and the amount.

Best execution and the plumbing of U.S. equity markets

Best execution (FINRA Rule 5310): a firm must use reasonable diligence to find the best market for a customer order, so that the price is as favourable as possible under prevailing conditions. Factors include price, speed, likelihood of execution, the character of the market, size and the number of markets checked. Routing to a venue because it pays the firm (payment for order flow) is allowed only if best execution is still met, and it must be disclosed.

Regulation NMS (the SEC's national market system rules) ties the venues together:

  • Order protection rule (Rule 611): a venue may not execute a trade at a price worse than a protected, displayed quote elsewhere, called a trade-through. The best prices across venues form the NBBO (national best bid and offer).
  • Example: the NBBO is 30.00 – 30.02, with the 30.02 offer displayed on Exchange A. A dark venue may not sell to a buyer at 30.03 while that protected 30.02 offer is still displayed. It must route to Exchange A, or fill at 30.02 or better itself.
  • Access rule (Rule 610): venues must give fair access to their quotes and cap access fees.
  • Rules 605/606: public reports on execution quality (605, by venues) and on where brokers route orders and what they are paid for it (606).

Alternative trading systems (ATSs) match orders without being registered exchanges. They are run by broker-dealers and file Form ATS with the SEC. Dark pools are ATSs that display no pre-trade quotes, so large orders can trade without showing their hand. They still report every trade after the fact. Off-exchange equity trades are reported to a FINRA trade reporting facility, as soon as practicable and no later than 10 seconds after execution. Corporate bonds report to FINRA's TRACE; municipal trades report to the MSRB's RTRS within 15 minutes.

Two conduct rules protect resting customer orders. Trading ahead (Rule 5320): a firm holding a customer limit order may not trade for its own account at that price or better without filling the customer first. Front-running: trading ahead of a customer's large block because you know it is coming is prohibited.

The order ticket

Before an order goes out, the rep fills in an order ticket (paper or electronic). It is kept for 3 years. It must show:

  • Account name or number, and the security
  • Buy or sell. Every sell is marked long (the customer owns it) or short (it will be borrowed). Reg SHO requires this marking.
  • Quantity, order type and any limit or stop price, and time in force
  • Solicited or unsolicited. An order the customer initiated without a recommendation is marked "unsolicited", which matters in suitability disputes.
  • Whether discretion was exercised. Choosing the security, side or amount needs the customer's prior written authorization and principal approval of the account. Choosing only time and price does not.
  • Time of entry and, when filled, time of execution and price

What is not on the ticket: the commission. It appears on the confirmation the customer receives. The solicited/unsolicited mark matters later. If an unsuitable trade was unsolicited, the rep did not recommend it, and that changes the suitability analysis. Mis-marking a solicited trade as unsolicited to dodge suitability is a books-and-records violation, and a classic enforcement case.

Understand
  • Bid = where you can sell; ask = where you can buy; spread = ask − bid = the price of immediacy. Size is in round lots.
  • Firm quotes bind (no backing away); subject quotes need confirmation; nominal quotes are information only.
  • Market makers compete and carry inventory; the NYSE DMM (ex-specialist) runs one stock's auction and order.
  • Market = execution guarantee; limit = price guarantee; stop = trigger to market; stop-limit = trigger to limit.
  • BLiSS: Buy Limit and Sell Stop go below the market; sell limit and buy stop go above.
  • Stops elect on a trade at or through the stop price and fill on a later trade.
  • Best execution, Reg NMS order protection, ATSs and trade reporting govern where orders go and how trades become public.
  • Tickets show account, security, side (long/short on sells), quantity, type and price, time in force, solicited/unsolicited and discretion. The commission goes on the confirmation.
MEMORIZE:
  • Market order → execution guarantee, no price guarantee
  • Limit order → price guarantee (or better), no execution guarantee
  • Stop → becomes a market order when elected; stop-limit → becomes a limit order
  • Buy limit → below market; sell limit → above
  • Buy stop → above market; sell stop → below
  • Sell stop → protects a long; buy stop → protects a short
  • FOK → all, immediately, or cancel
  • IOC → fill what you can now, cancel the rest
  • AON → all or nothing, not necessarily immediately
  • Sell orders → marked long or short
  • Customer-initiated order → marked "unsolicited"
  • Commission → on the confirmation, not the ticket
  • Ex-dividend → buy limits and sell stops reduced unless DNR
  • Muni trades → reported to MSRB RTRS within 15 minutes
Exam traps
  • "A stop order guarantees the stop price" → once triggered it is a market order: no price guarantee.
  • "A buy limit is placed above the market" → below. Above the market it is just a marketable order.
  • "A stop-limit order is sure to execute once triggered" → it becomes a limit order and can be triggered and never fill.
  • "A buy stop protects a long position" → a sell stop (below) protects a long; a buy stop (above) protects a short.
  • "The bid is what the dealer sells at" → the bid is where the dealer buys from you; the ask is where it sells to you.
  • "The trade that elects the stop is the customer's fill" → election and execution are separate prints.
  • "AON must fill immediately" → that is FOK; AON can wait.
  • "The commission is recorded on the order ticket" → it is disclosed on the confirmation.
Trader connection

A market maker's P&L is spread capture minus adverse selection minus inventory cost. Quoting 42.10–42.14 earns 4 cents on a round trip against uninformed flow. Against an informed trader who knows the stock is going to 43, the dealer that just sold at 42.14 loses 86 cents. So dealers widen spreads and cut size when volatility or information risk rises, and liquidity thins exactly when everyone needs it. Stop orders make that worse: stops cluster at round numbers and below obvious support. A drop through 40 elects a layer of sell stops, which become market orders, which hit thinning bids, which push the price through the next layer of stops. The chain is price falls → stops elect → market sells → bids exhausted → price falls further. That stop cascade is why institutional desks rarely rest naked stops or send large market orders. They work orders through algorithms (VWAP, TWAP, participation) that slice size over time, choosing their point on the price-versus-execution frontier deliberately.

Check yourself

Q1 A customer is long 500 shares of DEF, currently trading at 64, and wants to limit her loss if the stock falls. Which order should she enter?
A. Buy stop at 60
B. Sell limit at 60
C. Sell stop at 60
D. Buy limit at 60

Answer: C. A sell stop below the market protects a long: if a trade occurs at or below 60 it becomes a market sell. B, a sell limit at 60 with the stock at 64, is marketable and would sell immediately at 64 or better, which is not protection. A and D are buy orders, which add to the position rather than exit it.

Q2 A customer enters a sell stop at 28. Subsequent trades: 28.30, 28.10, 27.95, 27.80, 28.05. At what price is the order most likely executed?
A. 27.80
B. 27.95
C. 28.00
D. 28.05

Answer: A. The stop is elected by the first trade at or below 28, which is 27.95. It then becomes a market order and fills on the next trade, 27.80. B is the electing print, not the fill. C assumes a price guarantee that stop orders do not have. D comes after the fill.

Q3 Which order type guarantees execution but not price?
A. Market order
B. Limit order
C. Stop-limit order
D. All-or-none limit order

Answer: A. A market order executes at the best available price: an execution guarantee with no price guarantee. A limit order is the reverse. A stop-limit becomes a limit order once triggered, so it also guarantees price, not execution. AON adds a size condition that makes execution even less certain.

Q4 A dealer quotes a stock 18.25 – 18.40, 10 × 4. Which statement is true?
A. The dealer will sell 1,000 shares at 18.25
B. The dealer will buy 1,000 shares at 18.25
C. The spread is $0.25
D. The dealer will buy 400 shares at 18.40

Answer: B. The bid (18.25) is where the dealer buys, for the first size figure, 10 round lots = 1,000 shares. The ask (18.40) is where it sells 400 shares. Spread = 18.40 − 18.25 = $0.15, so C is wrong. A and D reverse the sides.

Q5 All of the following are required on an order ticket EXCEPT:
A. whether the order was solicited or unsolicited
B. whether a sell order is long or short
C. whether discretion was exercised
D. the commission charged

Answer: D. The commission is disclosed on the confirmation, not the ticket. The ticket records the account, security, side (sells marked long or short), quantity, order type and price, time in force, solicited/unsolicited status and use of discretion.

Q6 An institution wants to buy 50,000 shares. It will accept any part that can be filled right away, but wants nothing left working afterward. Which instruction fits?
A. Fill or kill
B. All or none
C. Immediate or cancel
D. Good 'til cancelled

Answer: C. IOC fills whatever is available immediately and cancels the balance. FOK is tempting but requires the entire 50,000 at once or nothing. AON requires the whole order but may wait. GTC leaves the order working, which the institution does not want.

Q7 A customer who is short 300 shares at 45 enters a buy stop-limit order, stop 50, limit 50.50. Trades then print at 49.80, 50.60, 51.00, 51.20 and the stock closes at 51.20. Which is true?
A. The order filled at 50.60
B. The order filled at 50.50
C. The order was never triggered
D. The order was triggered but did not fill

Answer: D. 50.60 is at or above 50, so the order is elected and becomes a buy limit at 50.50. No later trade prints at 50.50 or lower, so it never fills and the short is still exposed. A treats the electing print as the fill. B assumes the limit price is guaranteed to execute. C ignores that 50.60 is through the stop.

Q8 Which orders are placed below the current market price?
I. Buy limit
II. Buy stop
III. Sell limit
IV. Sell stop
A. II and III
B. I and IV
C. I and III
D. II and IV

Answer: B. BLiSS: Buy Limit and Sell Stop sit below the market. A buy limit waits for a cheaper price, and a sell stop waits to exit a long on a decline. Buy stops and sell limits (A) sit above the market. C and D each pair one correct order with one wrong one.

Day 18 · Clearing, settlement and custody: what happens after the trade

FINRA 3.1, 3.2 · pairs with the Day 18 edition (~90 min)

A trade is a promise: shares for cash, on a date. Clearing and settlement turn that promise into ownership, and custody keeps track of who owns what afterwards. The SIE tests the settlement calendar, the ex-date that falls out of it, confirmations and statements, and delivery vocabulary. On a desk, settlement is where counterparty risk, funding cost and operational failure all meet.

Trade date, settlement date and the T+1 calendar

Trade date (T) is the day the order executes. Settlement date is the day the buyer pays and becomes the owner of record, and the seller delivers and gets paid. They are different days. The standard ("regular-way") gap is set by SEC Rule 15c6-1 and has shrunk over time: T+3 → T+2 (2017) → T+1 since May 28, 2024.

Trade typeSettles
Regular way: stocks, corporate bonds, municipal bonds, U.S. government securities, listed optionsT+1 (one business day)
Option exercise (stock delivered or paid for)T+1
Cash settlement (by special agreement)Same day as the trade
When-issuedWhen the security is actually issued
Reg T payment deadline (customer must pay)Settlement + 2 business days = T+3

Count business days: skip weekends and market holidays. Reg T (the Federal Reserve rule on broker-dealer credit) gives the customer two business days beyond settlement to pay. The firm itself must still settle at T+1, so it fronts the money in between.

Worked example: settlement around Thanksgiving 2026
  1. A customer buys stock on Wednesday, November 25, 2026 (T).
  2. T+1 would be Thursday, November 26, but that is Thanksgiving and markets are closed. It is not a business day.
  3. Settlement = Friday, November 27. It is a 1:00 p.m. early close for trading, but a full business day for settlement.
  4. Reg T payment = settlement + 2 business days: Monday November 30 (1), Tuesday December 1 (2) → due Tuesday, December 1.

The chain after execution: clearing and settlement

Clearing is everything between the trade and settlement: matching the two sides' records, working out who owes what, and guaranteeing it. Settlement is the actual exchange of securities and cash. In U.S. equities, both run through subsidiaries of the Depository Trust & Clearing Corporation (DTCC):

Execution (exchange / dealer / ATS)
   --> trade reported and compared (both sides agree)
   --> NSCC: becomes buyer to every seller and seller
       to every buyer (central counterparty)
   --> CNS netting: one net position per firm per stock
   --> DTC: book-entry moves shares; cash settles
   --> customer accounts credited (street name)
  • NSCC (National Securities Clearing Corporation) clears equities, corporate and muni bonds. By stepping in as central counterparty (CCP), it replaces thousands of bilateral exposures with one exposure to NSCC. Members post clearing-fund margin so a member's default is absorbed.
  • CNS (Continuous Net Settlement) nets each firm's buys and sells in each security into one net delivery or receipt per day.
  • DTC (Depository Trust Company) is the central securities depository. Certificates are immobilized in its vault or never printed. Ownership moves by book entry, debiting one participant's account and crediting another's.
  • Other clearing houses: FICC for Treasuries and agency mortgage-backed securities; OCC for listed options, where it is the issuer and guarantor of every contract.
Worked example: why netting matters
  1. On one day Broker A buys 10,000 XYZ from B, sells 7,000 to C and buys 2,000 from D.
  2. Gross: three separate deliveries totalling 19,000 shares, each with its own counterparty risk.
  3. Net: +10,000 − 7,000 + 2,000 = +5,000. Broker A receives 5,000 shares from NSCC and pays for 5,000 at the net value. One movement, one counterparty.

Options add one more step, because an exercise creates a stock trade. The OCC is the counterparty to every listed contract, so an exercise runs through it:

Holder (BUY CALL) exercises --> holder's firm --> OCC
OCC assigns at random --> a clearing firm short that series
Firm assigns its customer (random, FIFO or other fair method)
Writer (SELL CALL) delivers stock; holder pays strike: T+1

Treasuries, by contrast, move on the Federal Reserve's book-entry system, and FICC nets and guarantees dealer trades much as NSCC does for stocks.

Not every broker-dealer clears. An introducing firm holds the customer relationship and sends its trades to a clearing firm, which is a member of NSCC and DTC and carries the accounts and custody (a "fully disclosed" arrangement). A self-clearing firm does both. The customer's confirmations and statements often come from the clearing firm.

Where the risk sits between trade and settlement

Between T and settlement, each side holds an unsettled contract. Two different risks live there:

  • Replacement-cost risk: the counterparty defaults before settlement, and you must redo the trade at today's price. If you bought at 50 and the stock is now 54, replacing the purchase costs you $4 a share. The longer the window and the higher the volatility, the bigger this risk.
  • Principal risk: you hand over your leg (cash or shares) and never receive the other. That is the total amount, not just a price move. DVP and CCP settlement exist to remove it.
Longer settlement window --> more unsettled trades open
--> bigger price moves possible before settlement
--> more margin NSCC must collect --> more capital tied up

This is the regulatory logic of T+1: fewer days of open exposure means lower clearing-fund margin for the whole market. The cost is a tighter operational timeline for everyone.

Institutional settlement: DVP and RVP

Institutions (funds, pensions, insurers) usually do not keep assets at the executing broker. Their assets sit at a custodian bank, so settlement is a simultaneous swap between the broker and the custodian:

DVP (institution BUYS):
  broker --shares--> custodian ; custodian --cash--> broker
  payment only against delivery
RVP (institution SELLS):
  custodian --shares--> broker ; broker --cash--> custodian
  delivery only against payment

DVP (delivery versus payment, also called cash on delivery) means the institution pays only when the securities are delivered. RVP (receive versus payment) is the mirror image: the institution delivers only against payment. Neither side is ever out both the cash and the securities, which removes principal risk. Under T+1, SEC Rule 15c6-2 pushes the institutional allocation, confirmation and affirmation steps onto trade date itself. There is no longer a spare day to fix a mismatched instruction.

Custody: street name, registered name, transfer agent, registrar

Street nameRegistered (customer) name
Owner on the issuer's booksThe firm or its depository nominee (DTC's nominee is Cede & Co.)The customer
Customer's statusBeneficial ownerRegistered and beneficial owner
FormBook entry at DTCPhysical certificate, or book entry on the transfer agent's books (Direct Registration System, DRS)
Why use itFast sales, safekeeping, required for marginDirect relationship with the issuer

With street name, the firm forwards proxies, annual reports and dividends to the beneficial owners. Two issuer-side agents keep the register honest:

  • Transfer agent: keeps the list of registered owners, records transfers, issues and cancels certificates, and often pays dividends and mails proxies.
  • Registrar: makes sure the number of shares outstanding never exceeds the number authorized in the charter, in effect auditing the transfer agent. It is traditionally a separate entity, often a bank.

Paper trail: confirmations and statements

A confirmation (SEC Rule 10b-10) is a per-trade disclosure sent at or before completion of the transaction, meaning by settlement. It shows the security, quantity, price, trade date (and time or availability on request) and account. It also shows the firm's capacity: agent or principal. As agent the firm discloses its commission. As principal, the markup or markdown must be disclosed in specified cases (riskless-principal equity trades, many retail bond trades). It says whether the firm receives payment for order flow. For debt it shows the yield and whether the bond is callable.

An account statement (FINRA Rule 2231) is a periodic summary of positions, balances and activity, sent at least quarterly. A confirmation is about one trade; a statement is about the account. One never substitutes for the other.

ConfirmationAccount statement
CoversOne transactionThe whole account for the period
TimingAt or before completion (settlement)At least quarterly
Key contentsSecurity, quantity, price, capacity, commission or markup, yield for bondsPositions, market values, cash and margin balances, activity
RuleSEC Rule 10b-10FINRA Rule 2231
Kept by the firm3 years

When settlement breaks

  • Fail to deliver: the seller's side did not deliver on settlement date. The buyer's side has a fail to receive.
  • Buy-in: after written notice, the buyer's firm buys the securities in the market and charges any price difference to the failing seller.
  • Worked buy-in: a firm sold 1,000 shares at $30 and failed to deliver. After notice, the buyer's firm buys 1,000 in the market at $33. The failing seller owes the $3,000 difference (1,000 × $3), on top of the funding and penalty costs of carrying the fail.
  • Reg SHO Rule 204 forces close-out of fails at a clearing agency: fails from short sales by the start of trading on S+1 (the business day after settlement), fails from long sales by S+3. Day 19 goes further.
  • Good delivery (the FINRA Uniform Practice Code): physical certificates must be undamaged, endorsed exactly as registered or accompanied by a signed stock power, carry a guaranteed signature, and come in denominations adding up to round lots. A muni certificate needs its legal opinion attached unless traded "ex-legal". Otherwise the buyer may reject it.
  • When-issued trades: trades in securities authorized but not yet issued (a new muni, a spin-off). The confirmation carries no settlement date and no final money amount until the issue date is set. The trade settles when the security is issued.
  • Due bill: if a trade executed before the ex-date settles late, the seller is still registered on the record date and receives the dividend. A due bill obliges the seller to pass it to the buyer.

Corporate actions and why the ex-date moved

A dividend has four dates: declaration (the board announces), ex-date (first day a buyer does not get the dividend), record date (owners of record on this day are paid) and payable date. To be an owner of record, your purchase must have settled by the record date. So the ex-date is set by the settlement cycle:

T+3 era: ex-date = 2 business days before record
T+2 era: ex-date = 1 business day before record
T+1 now: ex-date = SAME DAY as record date
Cash trades: ex-date = business day AFTER record date

On the ex-date the stock opens lower by roughly the dividend, because the buyer no longer receives it. Mutual funds are the exception: the fund sets its own ex-date, typically the business day after the record date. For stock dividends or splits of 25% or more, the ex-date is the business day after the payable date. The shares have to exist before the stock can trade without them.

Worked example: record date after Labor Day 2026
  1. Record date: Tuesday, September 8, 2026. Monday, September 7 is Labor Day.
  2. Regular way under T+1: ex-date = record date = Tuesday, September 8.
  3. Check: buy Friday, September 4 → T+1 skips the weekend and the holiday → settles Tuesday, September 8, on the record date → gets the dividend.
  4. Buy Tuesday, September 8 → settles Wednesday, September 9, after the record date → no dividend. So September 8 is the first day without it.
  5. A cash trade settles the same day, so buying on September 8 for cash still makes the record date. The cash-trade ex-date is Wednesday, September 9.

Operationally, the issuer's paying agent pays the dividend on the record-date position of Cede & Co. DTC allocates it to its participants by their positions, and each firm credits its customers. Voluntary actions (tender offers, rights offerings, conversions) run the same chain in reverse: customer instructions flow up to DTC, which responds to the agent in bulk.

Understand
  • Regular-way T+1 (since May 28, 2024) for stocks, corporates, munis, governments and options; cash settles same day.
  • NSCC is the central counterparty and nets through CNS; DTC holds securities and moves them by book entry; FICC and OCC do the same jobs for governments and options.
  • DVP/RVP: institutions settle through custodian banks, exchanging cash and securities simultaneously.
  • Street name = firm (Cede & Co.) on the issuer's books, customer is beneficial owner. The transfer agent keeps the register; the registrar polices authorized shares.
  • Confirmation: per trade, at or before completion, discloses capacity and commission or markup. Statement: at least quarterly.
  • Fails lead to buy-ins and Reg SHO close-outs; good delivery rules for certificates; when-issued settles on issuance; due bills fix late settlement around a record date.
  • Ex-date follows the settlement cycle: under T+1 it equals the record date.
MEMORIZE:
  • Regular way → T+1: equities, corporates, munis, options, governments
  • Option exercise → settles T+1
  • Cash settlement → same day
  • Confirmation → at or before completion of the transaction
  • Account statements → at least quarterly
  • Reg T payment → settlement + 2 business days (T+3)
  • Ex-date (regular way, T+1) → same day as record date
  • Ex-date (cash trade) → business day after record date
  • Mutual fund ex-date → set by the fund
  • DVP → institution pays against delivery; RVP → delivers against payment
  • Registrar → shares issued ≤ shares authorized; transfer agent → ownership records
Exam traps
  • "The customer owns the stock of record on trade date" → ownership of record changes on settlement date.
  • "Option exercises settle in two days" → exercise settles T+1 in the stock, like the options themselves.
  • "A monthly statement satisfies the confirmation requirement" → confirmations are per trade, at or before completion; statements are separate.
  • "DVP means the broker is paid first" → the institution pays only against delivery.
  • "When-issued trades settle T+1" → they settle when the security is issued.
  • "Ex-date is one business day before the record date" → that was the T+2 rule; under T+1 they are the same day.
  • "The transfer agent confirms shares don't exceed authorized" → that is the registrar.
Trader connection

Settlement is where a P&L becomes cash, and every link is a risk. Counterparty risk is why the CCP exists: if a member defaults between T and settlement, NSCC finishes the trade from margin and the clearing fund. Operational risk is a fail. A fail costs money: the buyer can buy you in at the market, a Reg SHO close-out forces a purchase, and until it is cured you are funding a position you cannot deliver. T+1 squeezed every timeline. A securities lender that sells a loaned stock must recall and receive it back in one business day instead of two, which raises fail risk on hard-to-borrow names. A foreign buyer paying in dollars has to execute its currency trade on trade date, often in its own evening, to fund settlement the next morning. That is why treasury, operations and prime brokerage desks changed more for T+1 than trading desks did.

Check yourself

Q1 A customer buys 200 shares regular way on Wednesday, November 25, 2026. Thursday, November 26 is Thanksgiving. When does the trade settle?
A. Thursday, November 26
B. Friday, November 27
C. Monday, November 30
D. Wednesday, November 25

Answer: B. Regular way is T+1 business day. Thanksgiving is not a business day, so settlement rolls to Friday, November 27. That day is an early close for trading but a normal settlement day. A ignores the holiday. C skips the Friday as if the early close were a holiday. D is cash settlement.

Q2 A company sets a record date of Tuesday, September 8, 2026. Monday, September 7 is Labor Day. What is the regular-way ex-dividend date?
A. Friday, September 4
B. Thursday, September 3
C. Tuesday, September 8
D. Wednesday, September 9

Answer: C. Under T+1 the ex-date equals the record date. A purchase on Friday, September 4 settles Tuesday, September 8 (skipping the weekend and the holiday) and receives the dividend, so it is the last day to buy with the dividend, not the ex-date (A). B uses the old T+2 logic. D is the ex-date for a cash trade.

Q3 All of the following settle T+1 when traded regular way EXCEPT:
A. a corporate bond
B. a municipal bond
C. a listed equity option
D. a stock traded for cash settlement

Answer: D. Cash settlement is by special agreement and settles the same day. Corporate bonds, munis, listed options, government securities and stocks all settle T+1 regular way.

Q4 Which statements about DVP and RVP settlement are true?
I. Under DVP the institution pays when the securities are delivered to its custodian
II. DVP is typical of retail margin accounts
III. Under RVP the institution's custodian delivers securities against payment
IV. DVP means securities are delivered first and paid for days later
A. I and III
B. I and II
C. II and IV
D. III and IV

Answer: A. DVP (institution buying) and RVP (institution selling) make delivery and payment simultaneous through a custodian bank. They are institutional arrangements, not retail margin (II). "Delivered first, paid later" (IV) describes exactly the risk DVP removes.

Q5 Which party ensures that the number of shares an issuer has outstanding does not exceed the number authorized?
A. The transfer agent
B. The registrar
C. The Depository Trust Company
D. The National Securities Clearing Corporation

Answer: B. The registrar checks issued shares against authorized shares, in effect auditing the transfer agent. The transfer agent (A) is the tempting neighbour: it keeps the ownership records and processes transfers. DTC is the depository, and NSCC is the clearing corporation for trades.

Q6 A customer's stock purchase executes on Monday. Under SEC rules, when must the confirmation be sent?
A. At or before completion of the transaction
B. Within 3 business days after settlement
C. With the next quarterly statement
D. Within 10 calendar days of trade date

Answer: A. Rule 10b-10 requires the confirmation at or before completion of the transaction, meaning by settlement. C confuses the confirmation with the account statement, which is at least quarterly. B and D are invented deadlines that fall after settlement.

Q7 A customer buys a new municipal bond issue on a when-issued basis. When does the trade settle?
A. T+1
B. T+3
C. The same day
D. When the bonds are issued

Answer: D. A when-issued trade occurs before the securities exist, so it settles on the issue date, which is set later. The confirmation therefore carries no settlement date or final money amount at first. T+1 (A) is regular way. T+3 (B) is the Reg T payment date. Same day (C) is cash settlement.

Q8 A customer buys stock in a cash account on Wednesday, November 25, 2026 (Thanksgiving is the next day). By what date must the customer pay under Regulation T?
A. Friday, November 27
B. Monday, November 30
C. Tuesday, December 1
D. Wednesday, December 2

Answer: C. Reg T payment = settlement + 2 business days. Settlement is Friday, November 27 (T+1, skipping Thanksgiving). Two business days later: Monday, November 30 (1), Tuesday, December 1 (2). A is the settlement date. B is only one business day after settlement. D counts one day too many.

Day 19 · Short selling and securities lending

FINRA 3.1, 3.3 · pairs with the Day 19 edition (~110 min)

A short sale is a sale of borrowed stock, so every short is also a securities loan, with a lender, collateral, a fee and a recall right. The SIE tests the Reg SHO rules (locate, marking, close-out, the alternative uptick rule), short margin, the dividend a short seller owes, and the unlimited-loss profile. You already know the trade; today attaches the regulatory labels and runs the loan economics to the penny, because the lending desk is where the short's real costs live.

The short sale is a secured loan

Follow the shares and the cash. A customer shorts 1,000 XYZ at $40 through a prime broker:

1 LOCATE: broker confirms shares can be borrowed
2 BORROW: lender --1,000 XYZ--> broker (for customer)
3 SELL:   customer --1,000 XYZ--> market buyer
          market buyer --$40,000--> customer's account
4 COLLATERAL: broker --cash (~102% of value)--> lender
          lender pays back a REBATE on that cash
5 MARK DAILY: price up --> borrower posts more cash
              price down --> lender returns cash
6 BUY TO COVER: customer buys 1,000 XYZ in market
7 RETURN: 1,000 XYZ --> lender ; collateral --> borrower

Look at the balance sheet. The short seller holds cash (sale proceeds, locked up) and owes shares, a liability whose value floats with the stock price. Being short is being short a liability that can grow without limit. The lender swapped a stock for cash collateral and keeps the economic exposure to the stock (it still gains or loses on the price, since it gets the shares back). What the lender gives up is the vote and physical possession.

Regulation SHO: the rules around every short

Regulation SHO is the SEC's short-sale rulebook. Four pieces matter for the exam:

RuleRequirement
Rule 200(g): markingEvery sell order is marked long, short or short exempt. "Long" means the customer owns the shares and they will be delivered on time.
Rule 203(b): locateBefore accepting or effecting a short sale, the broker must have reasonable grounds to believe the stock can be borrowed and delivered on settlement date, and must document it. An "easy-to-borrow" list can support this. Bona fide market making is excepted.
Rule 204: close-outA fail to deliver from a short sale must be closed out, by borrowing or buying shares, by the start of regular trading on S+1: the business day after settlement date, so T+2 under T+1. A firm that misses it goes in the "penalty box": it must pre-borrow, not merely locate, before further shorts in that stock until the fail is cured.
Rule 201: alternative uptick ruleIf a stock falls 10% or more from the prior day's close, a short-sale price test applies for the rest of that day and all of the next: short sales may execute only at a price above the current national best bid. Longs can still sell at the bid.

A locate is not a borrow. A locate is the broker's documented belief that shares can be borrowed, often from a daily easy-to-borrow list or a lender's indication. The actual borrow only has to be in place to deliver on settlement date. The gap between them is where fails come from: a stock that looked available at 9:45 can be gone by settlement if other shorts took the supply. In hard-to-borrow names, prime brokers therefore make clients pre-borrow (lock the shares before trading) or pay for a locate reservation.

Worked example: Rule 201 trigger
  1. XYZ closed Monday at $50.00. Trigger = 50.00 × (1 − 10%) = $45.00.
  2. Tuesday at 11:20 it trades at $44.95 → the restriction is on for the rest of Tuesday and all of Wednesday.
  3. Tuesday afternoon the NBBO is 45.10 – 45.14. A short sale may print at 45.11 or higher (above the 45.10 bid), not by hitting the 45.10 bid.
  4. A long holder can still sell at 45.10. The rule slows shorts pressing a falling stock; it does not ban them.

Threshold securities are stocks with large, persistent fails: at least 10,000 shares and 0.5% of shares outstanding, failing for five consecutive settlement days. They appear on a daily list published by the listing markets, and fails in them that persist for 13 consecutive settlement days must be closed out.

Naked short selling means selling short without borrowing, or without a locate, and then failing to deliver. Beyond Reg SHO's mechanics, SEC Rule 10b-21 makes it fraud to deceive a broker about your intent or ability to deliver. Naked shorting is not a strategy; it is a delivery failure the rules exist to prevent.

Two more labels. Short against the box means shorting a stock you also own. Tax law treats it as a constructive sale today, so it no longer defers a gain. Section 16 insiders may not short their own company's stock at all, against the box or otherwise.

The stock-loan market

The shares come from long-only holders who would otherwise sit on them:

Beneficial owners (pensions, mutual funds, ETFs, insurers)
   --shares--> AGENT LENDER (usually the custodian bank)
   --shares--> BORROWER (prime broker / broker-dealer)
   --shares--> short-selling client, then sold in market
Cash collateral flows the opposite way, back to the lender.

Brokers also lend from inside the firm: customers' margin securities (within the rehypothecation limit below) and, with a separate written agreement, customers' fully paid securities in a fully paid lending program.

Collateral. By market convention, U.S. loans are collateralized at about 102% of the stock's value (105% for foreign securities). That is industry practice, not an SIE rule. The loan is marked to market daily: if the stock rises, the borrower sends more collateral; if it falls, the lender returns some. In the U.S. the collateral is mostly cash. Elsewhere, bonds and equities as collateral ("non-cash") are common, and then the borrower pays an explicit fee.

The rebate. With cash collateral, the lender does not charge a fee directly. It invests the cash (in money-market instruments or reverse repo), keeps the yield, and pays the borrower a rebate: interest on the cash, at a rate below what the lender earns. The gap is the lender's fee.

Rebate ($) = Cash collateral × Rebate rate × Days ÷ 360

Cash collateral
cash posted by the borrower, about 102% of the stock's value, marked daily
Rebate rate
annual rate the lender pays the borrower on the cash = reinvestment rate − borrow fee
Days ÷ 360
money-market day count, the usual convention on U.S. loans
Borrow fee
reinvestment rate − rebate rate: the lender's spread, and the true cost of the borrow
Worked example: general collateral vs hard-to-borrow
  1. Borrow 10,000 shares at $50 = $500,000 value. Cash collateral at 102% = $510,000.
  2. Easy stock ("general collateral", GC): the lender reinvests at 4.00% and charges a 0.25% fee, so the rebate is 3.75%. Over 30 days the borrower receives 510,000 × 0.0375 × 30 ÷ 360 = $1,593.75. The lender keeps 510,000 × 0.0025 × 30 ÷ 360 = $106.25.
  3. Hard-to-borrow stock: the fee is 14%, so the rebate is 4.00% − 14% = −10%, a negative rebate. Instead of receiving interest, the borrower pays 510,000 × 0.10 × 30 ÷ 360 = $4,250 for 30 days.
  4. Same collateral, same stock count. The difference is scarcity: a borrow fee is a price, set by supply of lendable shares against demand from shorts.

With non-cash collateral the arithmetic is simpler: the borrower pays a fee on the loan value. Borrow $500,000 of stock against $510,000 of Treasuries at a 0.50% fee for 30 days: 500,000 × 0.005 × 30 ÷ 360 = $208.33.

The lender's risks. If the borrower defaults, the lender keeps the collateral and buys its shares back in the market. The 2% over-collateralization and the daily mark cover an ordinary one-day move, but not a gap. Agent lenders commonly indemnify their clients against a borrower default, which is part of why pensions lend at all. The bigger risk is often on the cash side: if the lender reinvests the collateral in something that loses value, it still owes the borrower 100% of the cash back. Stock loan is a secured funding trade on both legs.

Retail short sellers rarely see the rebate: the broker usually keeps it and charges a hard-to-borrow fee when the stock is scarce. Hedge funds negotiate rebates through their prime broker. Utilization (shares on loan ÷ lendable supply) and the fee rate are the desk's scarcity gauges.

Recalls, buy-ins and votes

Most stock loans are open: either side can end them at any time. The borrower returns shares whenever it covers. The lender can recall the shares, for example because it sold them, wants to vote, or has a tax reason. The borrower then has the standard settlement cycle (now one business day) to return them. The broker can borrow elsewhere to replace them, but if no replacement borrow exists:

Lender recalls --> no replacement borrow available
--> borrower cannot return shares
--> BUY-IN: shares bought in the market, charged to the short
--> customer is covered out at whatever the price is

The margin agreement lets the broker buy in the customer without consent. That is the SIE trap: a forced buy-in is not a choice the short seller makes. Recalls cluster before contested shareholder votes, because loaned shares carry no vote for the lender. The borrower's buyer gets the vote, which is why a lender wanting to vote must recall before the record date. Under T+1 the recall-to-return window shrank to one business day.

Manufactured dividends: short sellers pay the dividend

When XYZ pays a dividend, the company pays it to whoever owns the shares on the record date: the buyer from the short sale. The lender, who still expects the economics of ownership, is owed the same amount. The short seller pays it, through the borrow, as a payment in lieu of dividend (a manufactured dividend).

  • Short seller → pays the dividend amount to the lender.
  • Lender → receives it as ordinary income, not a qualified dividend, because it did not come from the issuer. That is a real cost to a taxable lender, and a reason to recall over record dates. Tax-exempt lenders such as pensions do not care.
  • Economically, the stock drops by roughly the dividend on the ex-date, which gains the short about what it pays. The payment is not an extra market loss, but it is a real cash outflow to budget.
Worked example
  1. Short 1,000 XYZ, which closes at $40.00 the day before the ex-date. The dividend is $0.60.
  2. With no other news, XYZ opens around $39.40 on the ex-date: mark-to-market gain = 0.60 × 1,000 = +$600.
  3. On the payable date the short pays the lender 0.60 × 1,000 = −$600 through the borrow.
  4. Net ≈ $0. The trap is thinking a short "collects" the price drop for free. The trade-off is timing: shorting just before a large special dividend is not an arbitrage.

Margin on a short: the 150% rule

Shorts must be done in a margin account. Reg T requires the customer to deposit 50% of the short market value (SMV). With the proceeds held, the account's credit balance equals 150% of SMV. The FINRA minimum equity for a short account is $2,000. Maintenance is 30% of SMV for stocks at $5 or more.

Equity = Credit balance − SMV · Maintenance call when Equity < 30% × SMV

SMV
short market value: shares short × current price
Credit balance
short-sale proceeds + Reg T deposit; fixed until more cash moves (ignoring interest)
Maintenance call price
per share = Credit balance ÷ 1.30 ÷ shares
Worked example
  1. Short 1,000 XYZ at $40: SMV = $40,000. Reg T deposit = 50% = $20,000. Credit balance = 40,000 + 20,000 = $60,000 (150%).
  2. Equity = 60,000 − 40,000 = $20,000.
  3. Stock rises to $45: SMV = 45,000, equity = 15,000. The requirement is 30% × 45,000 = 13,500, so no call.
  4. Maintenance call price = 60,000 ÷ 1.30 ÷ 1,000 = $46.15.
  5. At $50: equity = 10,000 against a 15,000 requirement → $5,000 call. If it is not met, the broker covers the position.

P&L and the unlimited loss

Short P&L = (Sale price − Cover price) × Shares − Borrow costs − Manufactured dividends

Sale price
price at which the borrowed shares were sold
Cover price
price paid in the buy-to-cover
Borrow costs
fees, or forgone or negative rebate, over the life of the loan
Manufactured dividends
dividends paid to the lender while short
Worked example
  1. Short 1,000 at $40; hold 90 days; borrow fee 3% a year on $40,000 = 40,000 × 0.03 × 90 ÷ 360 = $300; one $0.50 dividend = $500 manufactured.
  2. Cover at $32: (40 − 32) × 1,000 = 8,000 − 300 − 500 = +$7,200.
  3. Cover at $58 instead: (40 − 58) × 1,000 = −18,000 − 300 − 500 = −$18,800.
  4. Maximum gain is capped at $40,000 (stock to zero, before costs). Loss has no ceiling, because the price has no ceiling. A long stock can lose only what was paid.

Hedging a short. The hedge must gain as the stock rises. BUY CALL fixes a maximum purchase price. A buy stop above the market also caps the loss, but only as a market order once elected, so a gap can blow through it. Example: short 100 at $60 and BUY CALL 65 for $2. Maximum loss = (65 − 60) + 2 = $7 × 100 = $700. Breakeven = 60 − 2 = $58. Maximum gain = (60 − 2) × 100 = $5,800. SELL PUT against a short only brings in premium. It caps the gain and does not limit the loss.

The full cost of carrying a short

CostWho receives itWhen it bites
Borrow fee / forgone or negative rebateLender (and agent lender)Every day the loan is open; spikes in crowded names
Manufactured dividendsLenderEach payable date
Margin deposit tied up (50% under Reg T)Opportunity costFor the life of the position
Maintenance callsMore cash posted to the accountWhen the price rises
Recall / buy-inMarket (forced purchase)At the lender's choice, often at the worst moment

A long can hold a loser for years at no carrying cost. A short pays rent every day and can be evicted, which is why short theses need a catalyst and a time horizon.

Short squeezes: a liquidity cascade

A short squeeze is a feedback loop in which shorts are forced to buy into a rising market:

Price rises --> short equity falls --> margin calls
--> forced buy-to-cover --> price rises further
Lenders recall / borrow fee spikes --> buy-ins
--> more forced buying --> price rises further
Dealers who SELL CALL buy stock to hedge --> adds fuel

Signals desks watch: short interest as a percentage of the public float (FINRA collects short interest from firms twice a month); days to cover = short interest ÷ average daily volume; utilization; and the borrow fee. For example, 12 million shares short against average daily volume of 1.5 million = 12 ÷ 1.5 = 8 days to cover: even if every share traded went to shorts, covering would take eight sessions. A high fee plus high utilization plus a catalyst is the classic setup. In GameStop in January 2021, reported short interest exceeded 100% of the float. That is possible because shares lent and sold can be bought by someone who lends them again. When shorts ran for the same exit, the price moved by multiples in days.

Customer protection and rehypothecation

SEC Rule 15c3-3 (the customer protection rule) makes a broker-dealer keep customers' assets separate from its own business:

  • Possession or control: the firm must hold customers' fully paid and excess margin securities in good control locations (such as DTC), not lend or pledge them.
  • Reserve formula: customer cash the firm is using, net of what customers owe it, must be deposited in a special reserve bank account for the exclusive benefit of customers.

Hypothecation is the customer pledging margin securities to the broker as collateral for the margin loan, under the margin agreement. Rehypothecation is the broker re-pledging those securities to a bank to fund the loan, or lending them to short sellers. The limit is 140% of the customer's debit balance. Anything above that is excess margin securities and must stay segregated.

Worked example
  1. Customer owns $100,000 of stock with a $40,000 debit balance.
  2. Rehypothecation limit = 140% × 40,000 = $56,000 of the customer's securities.
  3. Excess margin securities = 100,000 − 56,000 = $44,000, which must be kept in possession or control.

The cap has a history behind it. When Lehman Brothers failed in 2008, hedge funds whose prime broker was its U.K. arm, where no equivalent U.S. cap applied, found their rehypothecated assets tied up in the insolvency for years. The 140% limit is the U.S. answer: the broker can use enough customer collateral to fund the customer's own loan with a cushion, and no more.

A fully paid lending program needs a separate written agreement. The customer earns a fee and receives collateral, but securities on loan may not be protected by SIPC the way ordinary custody is, and the agreement must say so.

Understand
  • Short = locate → borrow → sell → post collateral (marked daily) → buy to cover → return shares, collateral released.
  • Reg SHO: mark every sell long, short or short exempt; locate before shorting; close out short fails by the open on S+1; threshold securities; Rule 201 limits shorts to above the best bid after a 10% drop.
  • Stock loan: lender → agent lender → borrower. Cash collateral earns a rebate; fee = reinvestment rate − rebate; hard-to-borrow stocks can carry negative rebates.
  • Recalls can force buy-ins; lenders lose the vote on loaned shares.
  • Short sellers pay manufactured dividends, which lenders receive as ordinary income.
  • Loss is unlimited and gain capped; Reg T 150% credit, 30% maintenance, squeezes feed on forced buying.
  • 15c3-3 protects customer assets; rehypothecation capped at 140% of the debit balance.
MEMORIZE:
  • Locate → required before a short sale
  • Sell orders → marked long, short or short exempt
  • Rule 201 → 10% drop from prior close; shorts only above the national best bid, rest of day + next day
  • Short-sale fail close-out → by start of trading on S+1
  • Short margin → Reg T 150% of SMV (50% deposit); maintenance 30%; $2,000 minimum
  • Rehypothecation → up to 140% of debit balance
  • Manufactured dividend → paid by the short to the lender; ordinary income, not qualified
  • Stock-loan collateral → ~102% domestic, 105% foreign (market convention)
  • Protect a short → BUY CALL (or a buy stop)
  • Short risk → unlimited loss; gain capped at sale price
Exam traps
  • "The short seller receives the dividend" → the short seller PAYS it (to the lender).
  • "Naked shorting is shorting without owning the stock" → every short is without owning; naked means without borrowing or locating.
  • "Short risk equals long risk" → short loss is unlimited; long loss is limited to the investment.
  • "A buy-in is a voluntary cover" → recall + no replacement borrow → forced purchase at market.
  • "SELL PUT or BUY PUT protects a short" → BUY CALL protects a short; puts gain as the stock falls, which a short already does.
  • "Rule 201 bans short selling for the day" → it restricts price (above the best bid) and lasts through the next day.
  • "Manufactured dividends are qualified dividends" → ordinary income.
  • "102% collateral is an SEC margin rule" → it is lending-market convention; the SIE margin number is Reg T 150%.
Trader connection

The borrow market is an information market. A rising fee, falling rebate or climbing utilization means shorts are crowding in, or lendable supply is leaving. Either way it signals both conviction and squeeze risk, often before the price shows it. Squeezes are liquidity cascades: margin calls, recalls and dealer hedging all force buying at the same moment, the mirror image of a stop cascade. The collateral mechanics are a repo. A cash-collateralized stock loan is economically a repo in which the security is an equity instead of a Treasury. The rebate plays the role of the repo rate. A hard-to-borrow stock is exactly like a bond "on special" in repo: the scarce security's owner can borrow cash below the general-collateral rate, even at a negative rate. Learn one and you have learned the other: collateral haircuts, daily marks, recalls, fails and funding all carry over to prime brokerage, repo desks and the treasury function that funds them.

Check yourself

Q1 A customer sells short 500 shares at $70 and later covers at $61. While short, she paid one manufactured dividend of $0.80 per share and $150 in borrow fees. What is her net profit?
A. $4,500
B. $4,350
C. $3,950
D. $4,900

Answer: C. Gross = (70 − 61) × 500 = $4,500. Manufactured dividend = 0.80 × 500 = $400. Net = 4,500 − 400 − 150 = $3,950. A ignores all costs. B subtracts only the borrow fee. D adds the dividend, as if the short seller received it.

Q2 ABC closed yesterday at $50.00. This morning it trades at $44.90. Which is true?
A. Short sales in ABC are prohibited for the rest of the day
B. For the rest of today and all of tomorrow, short sales may execute only above the national best bid
C. Short sales may execute only on an uptick for the next five trading days
D. Trading in ABC is halted for 15 minutes

Answer: B. A decline of 10% or more from the prior close ($45.00 trigger) activates Rule 201's price test for the rest of the day and the following day. A overstates it, since shorts are allowed above the bid. C mixes up the old tick-test uptick rule and invents a duration. D describes a volatility or circuit-breaker halt, a different mechanism.

Q3 All of the following are true of a customer's short sale EXCEPT:
A. the broker must locate the shares before the sale
B. the order ticket must be marked short
C. the customer receives any dividend paid while the position is open
D. the maximum potential loss is theoretically unlimited

Answer: C. The short seller pays a manufactured dividend to the lender and never receives one. A (Rule 203(b) locate) and B (Rule 200(g) marking) are Reg SHO requirements. D is true because the stock price has no ceiling.

Q4 Which of the following limit the risk of a short stock position?
I. BUY CALL
II. BUY PUT
III. A buy stop order above the market
IV. A sell stop order below the market
A. I and III
B. I and II
C. II and IV
D. III and IV

Answer: A. A short loses when the price rises, so the protection must buy or gain on a rise. BUY CALL caps the purchase price, and a buy stop above the market triggers a buy-to-cover (with no price guarantee once elected). BUY PUT and a sell stop both profit or act on declines, which is the direction the short already wins.

Q5 A customer opens a margin account and sells short 1,000 shares at $30, meeting the Reg T requirement. What is the credit balance?
A. $15,000
B. $30,000
C. $60,000
D. $45,000

Answer: D. SMV = $30,000. Reg T deposit = 50% × 30,000 = $15,000. Credit balance = proceeds + deposit = 30,000 + 15,000 = $45,000, which is 150% of SMV. A is the deposit alone, B is the proceeds alone, and C doubles the SMV.

Q6 A customer's margin account has a debit balance of $40,000 and securities worth $90,000. What is the maximum value of the customer's securities the broker-dealer may rehypothecate?
A. $40,000
B. $56,000
C. $60,000
D. $90,000

Answer: B. Rule 15c3-3 limits rehypothecation to 140% of the debit balance: 1.40 × 40,000 = $56,000. The remaining $34,000 is excess margin securities and must be segregated. A uses 100% of the debit. C uses 150%, mixing in the Reg T short-sale figure. D would pledge everything.

Q7 A hedge fund borrows stock worth $1,000,000 and posts $1,020,000 of cash collateral. The rebate rate is 3.6% a year. Using a 360-day year, what rebate does the fund earn over 30 days?
A. $3,060
B. $36,720
C. $3,000
D. $3,018

Answer: A. Rebate = cash collateral × rate × days ÷ 360 = 1,020,000 × 0.036 × 30 ÷ 360 = $3,060. B is a full year's rebate. C applies the rate to the stock value instead of the cash collateral. D uses a 365-day year.

Q8 A customer is short 2,000 shares of a hard-to-borrow stock. The lender recalls the shares, and the broker cannot find a replacement borrow. What is the most likely result?
A. The customer may keep the position until choosing to cover
B. The broker pays the lender cash equal to the shares' value and the loan ends
C. The position may stay open as a naked short for up to 13 days
D. The position is bought in at the market for the customer's account

Answer: D. Without shares to return, the broker buys them in at the market and charges the customer, as the margin agreement allows. A ignores the lender's right to recall. B is tempting because collateral exists, but the lender is owed shares, not cash. C misuses the threshold-security 13-day figure; failing to return is never a permitted strategy.

Day 20 · Margin: leverage, collateral and the margin call

FINRA 3.2, 3.1 · pairs with the Day 20 edition (~120 min)

A margin account is a secured loan: the broker-dealer lends cash against the customer's securities, and a small set of percentages decides how much it lends and when it takes collateral back. The SIE tests the account arithmetic (long and short), the difference between a Reg T call and a maintenance call, and which accounts and securities are shut out of margin. On a desk the same logic runs prime brokerage, futures clearing and repo, so learn the mechanics, not just the numbers.

What margin actually is
