In a cash account the customer pays 100% of every purchase. In a margin account the customer pays part and the broker-dealer (BD) lends the rest. The loan is the debit balance (DR). The purchased securities are the collateral: the customer hypothecates them (pledges them to the BD), which is why margin securities must be held in street name (registered to the BD, with the customer as beneficial owner). The BD then usually finances that loan itself by rehypothecating the securities (re-pledging them to a bank). The rate the bank charges the BD is the broker call rate; the customer's margin interest is quoted as a spread over it.

Customer --(cash 50%)--> BD --(cash 100%)--> Seller
Seller --(shares)--> BD, held in street name
BD --(pledges shares)--> Bank --(loan)--> BD
Customer pays interest on DR = broker call rate + spread

So there are two loans stacked on the same shares: bank → BD and BD → customer. The BD may rehypothecate customer margin securities worth up to 140% of the customer's debit balance (SEA Rule 15c3-3); anything beyond that is excess collateral it must segregate. The customer's upside and downside are both magnified because the debt is fixed while the asset moves.

Leverage = Market value of position ÷ Customer equity

Market value
current value of the securities held (long) or owed (short)
Customer equity
what the customer would have left if everything were closed out today

At 50% initial margin the leverage is 2:1, so a 10% move in the stock is a 20% move in equity. That is the whole point of margin, and the whole danger.

Who writes which rule

Four layers, from the Federal Reserve down to the firm. Keep them separate; the exam likes to swap them.

LayerWhoWhat it sets
Regulation TFederal Reserve Board (authority from the Securities Exchange Act of 1934)Initial margin on credit extended by BDs: 50%; payment deadline; cash-account rules (freeriding)
Regulation UFederal Reserve BoardBanks lending against securities (the bank → BD leg and bank loans to customers)
FINRA Rule 4210FINRA$2,000 minimum equity; maintenance 25% long / 30% short; pattern day trader rules
House requirementThe individual BDStricter maintenance (often 30–40%, higher for volatile names); can be raised without notice

The Fed writes Reg T but does not examine broker-dealers; FINRA enforces Reg T on its member firms. Reg T has been 50% since 1974. The exam still lists "changing margin requirements" as a Fed tool; accept that on the exam, but in practice it is a dormant credit control, not monetary policy.

Opening the margin account

The margin agreement is really three documents. Only two are mandatory.

PartWhat it doesRequired?
Credit agreementTerms of the loan: how interest is computed and chargedYes
Hypothecation agreementCustomer pledges the securities as collateral and lets the BD rehypothecate themYes
Loan consentLets the BD lend the customer's securities to short sellersNo — optional

The firm must also give the customer a margin disclosure statement (FINRA Rule 2264) at or before the opening of the account and annually after. It spells out the risks in plain words: you can lose more than you deposit; the firm can raise its house requirement at any time without advance notice; the firm can sell your securities without contacting you; you do not get to choose what is sold; you are not entitled to an extension on a call.

The $2,000 minimum

FINRA requires $2,000 of equity before the firm extends credit. Combine it with Reg T's 50% and the required deposit for a first purchase is the larger of 50% or $2,000, but never more than the full price. For short sales the $2,000 floor applies even when the trade is smaller, because a short has unlimited risk.

First trade in a new margin accountReg T 50%Required depositWhy
Buy $1,500 of stock$750$1,500Below $2,000: pay in full, no loan
Buy $3,000$1,500$2,000FINRA minimum beats Reg T
Buy $10,000$5,000$5,000Reg T beats the minimum
Short $1,500$750$2,000Short accounts: $2,000 regardless

The long account: a three-line balance sheet

LMV − DR = EQ

LMV
long market value: current value of the securities held
DR
debit balance: the loan owed to the BD
EQ
equity: the customer's ownership stake

Think of it as a personal balance sheet: asset (LMV) = liability (DR) + owner's equity (EQ). The key behaviour: market moves change LMV and EQ only. The debit balance does not move with the market. DR changes only when cash moves: more borrowing (purchases, cash withdrawals) raises it; sale proceeds, deposits, dividends and interest received lower it; margin interest charged raises it.

Worked example — the long account
  1. Buy 1,000 ABC at $40 on margin. LMV = $40,000. Reg T 50% → deposit $20,000; DR = $20,000; EQ = $20,000 (50%).
  2. ABC rises to $50. LMV = $50,000; DR still $20,000; EQ = $30,000 (60%). A 25% rise in the stock became a 50% rise in equity.
  3. ABC falls to $30. LMV = $30,000; DR still $20,000; EQ = $10,000 (33%). A 25% fall in the stock became a 50% fall in equity.

Excess equity and the SMA

Excess equity is equity above what Reg T would require today.

Excess equity = EQ − (50% × LMV)

EQ
current equity
50% × LMV
the Reg T requirement on today's market value

The Special Memorandum Account (SMA) is a side ledger that records the customer's right to borrow. Treat it as a line of credit attached to the account. It is credited by:

  • excess equity created when LMV rises (the SMA is raised to match the new excess);
  • cash deposits that are not needed to meet a call;
  • dividends and interest received;
  • 50% of the proceeds of a sale of long stock.

The SMA is sticky: once credited it does not shrink when the market falls. It is used up only when the customer uses it (withdraws cash or buys on it). Using it increases the debit balance, because using credit means borrowing more. A firm will let the customer use SMA only if the account stays above maintenance afterwards.

Buying power = SMA ÷ 0.50 = 2 × SMA

SMA
the credit line recorded in the Special Memorandum Account
0.50
Reg T initial margin: each $1 of SMA supports $2 of new stock
Worked example — SMA from a rally
  1. Start from the $40 purchase: LMV $40,000, DR $20,000, EQ $20,000, SMA $0.
  2. ABC rises to $50: LMV $50,000, EQ $30,000. Reg T requirement = 50% × $50,000 = $25,000. Excess equity = $30,000 − $25,000 = $5,000 → SMA = $5,000. Buying power = 2 × $5,000 = $10,000.
  3. Choice A — withdraw $5,000 cash: DR $25,000, LMV $50,000, EQ $25,000 (exactly 50%), SMA $0.
  4. Choice B — buy $10,000 more stock with no deposit: LMV $60,000, DR $30,000, EQ $30,000 (50%), SMA $0.
  5. Choice C — do nothing, and ABC falls back to $40: LMV $40,000, EQ $20,000, excess equity $0, but SMA is still $5,000. Using it now (withdraw $5,000) gives DR $25,000, EQ $15,000 = 37.5% of LMV, which is above the 25% maintenance level, so the firm allows it.

Restricted accounts and the retention rule

An account is restricted when equity is below the Reg T 50% level but above maintenance. It is not a call and nothing is due. It is a status. The customer can still trade: a new purchase simply needs its own 50% deposit, and sales are allowed.

The catch is the retention requirement: when the customer sells securities in a restricted account and wants to take money out, at least 50% of the sale proceeds must stay in the account to reduce the debit. The other 50% is credited to SMA and can be withdrawn (subject to maintenance).

Worked example — restricted account
  1. New account: 1,000 ABC bought at $40 with $20,000 down, so DR $20,000. ABC falls to $35. LMV $35,000, EQ $15,000 (42.9%).
  2. Reg T requirement 50% × $35,000 = $17,500 → the account is restricted by $2,500.
  3. Maintenance requirement 25% × $35,000 = $8,750 → equity of $15,000 is well above it: no call.
  4. Customer sells $10,000 of ABC. Proceeds pay DR down to $10,000; SMA is credited 50% × $10,000 = $5,000.
  5. Customer withdraws the $5,000. DR goes back up to $15,000; LMV $25,000; EQ $10,000 (40%). $5,000 of the sale — the retained half — permanently reduced the loan.
EQ ≥ 50% of LMV .............. unrestricted (excess → SMA)
25% ≤ EQ < 50% .............. restricted: no call, 50% retention
EQ < 25% (or house level) ... maintenance call

Maintenance: the line that triggers the call

Reg T governs the day you borrow. After that, FINRA's maintenance requirement governs: equity must stay at or above 25% of LMV for long positions and 30% of SMV for short positions (stocks $5 and over). Firms usually set a higher house requirement. When equity drops below the requirement, the firm issues a maintenance call (on desks, "the margin call") for the deficiency.

Because the debit is fixed, you can solve for the market value at which a call is triggered. At that point EQ = 25% of LMV, so DR = 75% of LMV.

Long maintenance call price: LMV = DR ÷ 0.75

DR
debit balance, which does not change with the market
0.75
1 − 25% maintenance: at the trigger, the loan is 75% of the collateral
LMV
market value at which equity equals exactly 25%; divide by shares for a price per share
Worked example — when does the call come, and how big is it?
  1. DR = $20,000 on 1,000 ABC. Trigger LMV = $20,000 ÷ 0.75 = $26,666.67 → $26.67 per share.
  2. Check: at $26,666.67, EQ = $6,666.67, and 25% × $26,666.67 = $6,666.67. Equity is exactly at maintenance.
  3. ABC falls to $24. LMV $24,000; EQ $4,000; requirement 25% × $24,000 = $6,000 → maintenance call for $2,000.
  4. Three ways to meet it: deposit $2,000 cash; deposit fully paid marginable securities worth $2,000 ÷ 0.75 = $2,666.67 (securities only count for 75% of their value at maintenance); or sell stock worth 4 × $2,000 = $8,000 (proceeds cut DR and LMV equally, so each $1 sold frees only $0.25 of requirement).

A quick sense check: starting from 50% equity, the stock must fall one third (from $40 to $26.67) before a long account at 25% maintenance gets a call.

The short account: the credit is fixed

A short seller borrows shares, sells them, and owes the shares back. The sale proceeds stay in the account as collateral, and Reg T requires another 50% on top. So the account holds 150% of the short market value (SMV) at the start, and that total is the credit balance (CR).

SMV + EQ = CR (equivalently EQ = CR − SMV)

SMV
short market value: current cost to buy back the shares owed
EQ
the customer's equity
CR
credit balance: short-sale proceeds + the customer's Reg T deposit; it does not change when the stock price moves

Now the liability (SMV) moves and the asset (CR) is fixed. The stock rising hurts; the credit balance does not change with price. (In live accounts the firm and the stock lender mark the loan collateral daily; for exam math, CR is fixed.)

Worked example — the short account
  1. Short 1,000 XYZ at $30. SMV = $30,000. Reg T deposit 50% = $15,000. CR = $30,000 + $15,000 = $45,000; EQ = $15,000.
  2. XYZ falls to $25: SMV $25,000; EQ = $45,000 − $25,000 = $20,000. Reg T requirement 50% × $25,000 = $12,500; excess $7,500 → SMA $7,500.
  3. Instead XYZ rises to $36: SMV $36,000; EQ $9,000; maintenance 30% × $36,000 = $10,800 → call for $1,800.

Short maintenance call price: SMV = CR ÷ 1.30

CR
credit balance, fixed
1.30
1 + 30% maintenance: at the trigger, CR = SMV + 30% of SMV
SMV
short market value at which equity equals exactly 30%

For the XYZ short: $45,000 ÷ 1.30 = $34,615.38 → $34.62 per share. Check: EQ = $45,000 − $34,615.38 = $10,384.62, which is 30% of $34,615.38. The long formula divides by 0.75, the short by 1.30: long uses (1 − 25%), short uses (1 + 30%).

Reg T call vs maintenance call

Reg T call (federal call)Maintenance call (house call)
Triggered byA new purchase or short sale in the margin accountMarket move pushes equity below 25% long / 30% short (or house level)
Amount50% of the trade, less any SMA usedThe deficiency below the requirement
DeadlineSettlement + 2 business days (T+3 under T+1 settlement)Promptly, as the firm demands; the firm can act immediately
If unmetFirm sells out enough of the position; the firm can ask FINRA for an extensionFirm liquidates, without having to notify the customer first
Worked example — a Reg T call reduced by SMA
  1. The ABC account has SMA of $5,000 from the earlier rally. The customer buys $16,000 of GHI on margin.
  2. Reg T requirement on the new trade = 50% × $16,000 = $8,000.
  3. The $5,000 SMA is applied first (and is used up); the remaining Reg T call is $8,000 − $5,000 = $3,000, due by settlement + 2 business days.
  4. After payment: DR rises by $16,000 − $3,000 = $13,000. The $5,000 of SMA became borrowed money, which is exactly why using SMA raises the debit.
Price falls --> LMV falls --> EQ falls (DR fixed)
  --> EQ < 25% --> maintenance call --> cash due now
  --> no cash --> forced sale into a falling market
  --> price risk has become liquidity risk

What cannot use margin

Accounts

  • IRAs and other retirement accounts: no borrowing. Tax law treats a loan secured by the account as a prohibited transaction. They can hold limited-risk options (for example SELL CALL covered by owned stock), but not margin.
  • UGMA/UTMA custodial accounts: no margin; the custodian manages a minor's irrevocable gift and may not leverage it.
  • 529 and Coverdell education accounts: no margin.
  • Corporate, partnership and trust accounts can use margin only if their governing documents (charter, partnership agreement, trust agreement) permit it.

Securities

  • New issues cannot be bought on margin for 30 days after the offering. Open-end mutual fund shares are continuously offered, so they are treated as new issues: bought in full, usable as collateral after 30 days.
  • Options with 9 months or less to expiration must be paid in full. Just memorize

The cash account: freeriding and the 90-day freeze

Reg T also governs cash accounts. A cash-account buyer must pay within the same Reg T period: settlement + 2 business days. Freeriding is buying in a cash account and selling before paying, using the sale proceeds to cover the purchase. The penalty is a 90-day freeze: for 90 days the customer must have the full purchase price in the account before the trade date of any buy. If a cash-account customer simply fails to pay, the firm sells the position out and the same freeze applies. Extensions, if any, are requested by the firm from FINRA.

Buy Mon (cash acct) --> sell Tue --> never paid for Mon
  --> freeriding --> account frozen 90 days
  --> each buy must be paid in full before trade date

Pattern day traders

A pattern day trader (PDT) is a margin customer who executes four or more day trades (open and close the same position the same day) within five business days, or whom the firm reasonably believes will. A PDT must hold $25,000 minimum equity in the margin account before day trading; day-trading buying power is up to 4 times the maintenance margin excess. Below $25,000, day trading stops until the equity is restored. FINRA has proposed changes to the PDT framework; the exam tests $25,000.

Understand
  • Cash account = pay in full; margin account = secured loan from the BD. The margin agreement has a credit agreement and a hypothecation agreement (both required) and an optional loan consent.
  • Reg T (Fed) sets 50% initial; FINRA sets the $2,000 minimum and 25% long / 30% short maintenance; firms may set stricter house levels.
  • Long: LMV − DR = EQ, with DR fixed by the market. Short: SMV + EQ = CR, with CR fixed by the market.
  • SMA is a sticky line of credit, created by excess equity, deposits, income and 50% of sale proceeds; using it raises DR; buying power is 2 × SMA.
  • Restricted = below 50% but above maintenance: no call, trading allowed, 50% of sale proceeds retained if withdrawing.
  • Reg T call follows a trade and is due by settlement + 2; maintenance call follows a price move and is due immediately; unmet calls end in liquidation.
  • No margin in IRAs/retirement accounts, UGMA/UTMA or education accounts; no margin on new issues for 30 days or options of 9 months or less.
  • Freeriding in a cash account → 90-day freeze. Pattern day traders need $25,000.
MEMORIZE:
  • Reg T initial margin → 50% (short: 150% of SMV held)
  • FINRA minimum equity → $2,000 (100% of the purchase if under $2,000; $2,000 for shorts regardless)
  • Maintenance → 25% of LMV long; 30% of SMV short
  • Long call price → DR ÷ 0.75; short call price → CR ÷ 1.30
  • Buying power → 2 × SMA
  • Pattern day trader minimum equity → $25,000
  • Reg T payment → settlement + 2 business days (T+3)
  • Freeriding → 90-day freeze
  • New issues → not marginable for 30 days
  • Options ≤ 9 months → paid in full
  • Margin disclosure statement → at opening and annually
  • Rehypothecation cap → 140% of the customer's debit balance
Exam traps
  • "The debit balance falls when the stock falls" → No. The DEBIT balance does not change when the market moves; equity does.
  • "The short credit balance rises when the stock rises" → No. The CR does not change with price; SMV and equity move.
  • "An IRA can be a margin account if the customer is sophisticated" → IRAs can NOT have margin (they can hold limited-risk options).
  • "FINRA sets Reg T" or "the Fed enforces Reg T at the firm" → Reg T is a Federal Reserve rule, enforced by FINRA on BDs.
  • Minimum maintenance price computed as DR ÷ 0.25 or DR × 1.25 → The long trigger is LMV = DR ÷ 0.75; the short trigger is CR ÷ 1.30, not SMV × 1.30.
  • "A restricted account must deposit cash" → Restricted is a status, not a call.
  • "SMA falls when the market falls" → SMA is sticky; only use reduces it.
Trader connection

Every leveraged position in finance is this chapter with different percentages. A prime broker lends to a hedge fund against its portfolio, applies haircuts by security, and calls for more collateral when the portfolio falls; portfolio margin sets those requirements from a stress test of the whole book instead of fixed 50/25 percentages. Futures initial margin is the Reg T analogue (a performance bond rather than a loan) and maintenance margin plus daily variation margin is the maintenance call, settled in cash every day through the clearinghouse. A repo haircut is the equity cushion. The failure mode is always the same chain: price falls → equity falls against fixed debt → call → forced selling → lower prices → more calls. In March 2021 Archegos Capital could not meet its prime brokers' calls on concentrated, swap-financed positions, the banks dumped blocks of stock, and some lost billions. Know your call price before you size a position; margin calls are how price risk becomes liquidity risk.

Check yourself

Q1 A customer buys 500 shares of DEF at $60 in a new margin account and meets the Reg T requirement. Assuming 25% minimum maintenance, at what price does the account receive a maintenance call?
A. $30.00
B. $40.00
C. $45.00
D. $20.00

Answer: B. Cost $30,000; Reg T 50% → DR $15,000. Call price LMV = DR ÷ 0.75 = $15,000 ÷ 0.75 = $20,000 → $40 per share. Check: at $40, EQ = $20,000 − $15,000 = $5,000 = 25%. $45 is a 25% drop from $60 (applying the percentage to the price instead of solving for equity); $30 is the loan per share; $20 misreads the $20,000 trigger value as a price.

Q2 A customer sells short 200 shares at $50 and deposits the Reg T requirement. The stock falls to $40. What is the customer's equity?
A. $2,000
B. $5,000
C. $7,000
D. $3,000

Answer: C. SMV $10,000 + Reg T deposit $5,000 = CR $15,000, which does not move. At $40, SMV = $8,000, so EQ = $15,000 − $8,000 = $7,000. $5,000 is the original equity (ignores the $2,000 gain); $2,000 is only the gain; $3,000 subtracts the gain instead of adding it.

Q3 All of the following accounts could be approved for margin trading EXCEPT:
A. An individual account
B. A JTWROS account
C. A corporate account whose charter permits margin
D. A Roth IRA

Answer: D. Retirement accounts cannot borrow; a loan secured by an IRA is a prohibited transaction. Individual and joint accounts are the ordinary margin accounts; a corporation may use margin when its charter or bylaws permit it, which is exactly the tempting half-right distractor.

Q4 Which statements about SMA are TRUE?
I. It is increased when a rise in LMV creates excess equity
II. It decreases when the market value of the account falls
III. Using it increases the debit balance
IV. Buying power equals SMA divided by 0.50
A. I, III and IV
B. I and II
C. II and IV
D. I, II, III and IV

Answer: A. SMA is a line of credit created by excess equity (I); drawing on it is borrowing, so DR rises (III); each $1 of SMA supports $2 of stock at 50% (IV). It is sticky: a market decline does not reduce it (II is false), which knocks out B, C and D.

Q5 A long margin account has LMV of $30,000 and a debit balance of $18,000. Which describes the account?
A. It has a maintenance call of $3,000
B. It has a Reg T call of $3,000 due by T+3
C. It is restricted; no call is issued
D. It has $3,000 of excess equity

Answer: C. EQ = $30,000 − $18,000 = $12,000 = 40%. Reg T requirement = $15,000, so the account is $3,000 below 50% → restricted. Maintenance requirement = 25% × $30,000 = $7,500; equity exceeds it, so no maintenance call. Reg T calls come only from new trades, not market moves (B); excess equity would require EQ above $15,000 (D).

Q6 A customer buys stock in a cash account on Monday and sells it on Tuesday without having paid for the purchase. What is the consequence?
A. The account is frozen for 30 days
B. The account is frozen for 90 days; purchases must be paid in full before the trade date
C. The account is converted to a margin account
D. The customer receives a Reg T call due at settlement + 2

Answer: B. This is freeriding under Reg T, and the penalty is a 90-day freeze. 30 days is the new-issue margin restriction, not a freeze; a cash account cannot be turned into a margin account without a signed margin agreement; a Reg T call is for margin purchases.

Q7 Which statement about margin regulation is correct?
A. FINRA sets the initial margin requirement under Regulation T
B. The Federal Reserve examines broker-dealers for Reg T compliance
C. Minimum maintenance on short stock positions is 25% of SMV
D. The Federal Reserve sets Reg T, and FINRA enforces it on member firms

Answer: D. The Fed writes Reg T under authority from the 1934 Act; FINRA examines and enforces it at broker-dealers. FINRA's own rule (4210) sets maintenance and the $2,000 minimum, not the initial 50% (A). Short maintenance is 30%, long is 25% (C).

Q8 A customer sells short 1,000 shares at $20 and meets Reg T. Assuming 30% minimum maintenance, at approximately what price will a maintenance call occur?
A. $23.08
B. $26.00
C. $25.00
D. $22.00

Answer: A. CR = $20,000 + $10,000 = $30,000. Trigger SMV = CR ÷ 1.30 = $23,076.92 → $23.08 per share. Check: EQ = $30,000 − $23,077 = $6,923 ≈ 30% of $23,077. $26.00 is $20 × 1.30 (applying 30% to the sale price instead of the credit); $25 and $22 are round-number guesses.

Day 21 · Opening accounts: registrations, KYC and AML

FINRA 3.2, 3.3 · pairs with the Day 21 edition (~115 min)

Before a single share trades, the firm has to know who the customer is, who is allowed to give orders, who owns the assets if someone dies, and whether the money is clean. The SIE tests all four with short scenarios: a joint owner dies, a rep gets a verbal "use your judgment", a customer splits cash deposits. On the institutional side the same questions become prime-broker onboarding, ISDA signing authority and sanctions screening.

The pipeline: from application to first trade

Opening an account is a series of gates. Each one exists because of a specific failure: fraud, money laundering, unsuitable sales, or disputes over who owned what.

Customer data (Rule 4512 new account form)
  --> identity verified (CIP) + sanctions screen (OFAC)
  --> profile built (KYC 2090, suitability/Reg BI)
  --> Form CRS delivered (retail)
  --> principal approves account in writing
  --> trading; AML monitoring runs for the life of it

Two people are involved on the firm side. The registered representative (RR) opens the account and makes recommendations. A principal (a supervisor with a principal registration) must approve the account in writing. The customer's signature is not required to open a plain cash account, but it is required on any agreement that adds risk or authority: margin agreement, options agreement, discretionary authorization.

What the firm must collect (Rule 4512)

FINRA Rule 4512 lists the minimum account record.

For every accountAlso for individual accounts (reasonable efforts)
Customer name and residenceTax identification number (SSN)
Whether the customer is of legal ageOccupation and name and address of employer
Name of the RR responsible for the accountAnnual income and net worth (excluding primary residence)
Signature of the principal who accepts itInvestment objectives
For entities: names of persons authorized to transactName and contact information of a trusted contact person

Trusted contact person. The firm must make a reasonable effort to get the name of someone it may contact about the account: to confirm the customer's health or whereabouts, to ask about a legal guardian or executor, or to raise suspected financial exploitation. The trusted contact must be 18 or older and has no authority to trade or withdraw. If the customer declines, the account still opens.

Employees of other firms. If the customer is associated with another FINRA member, that person must get the prior written consent of the employing firm before opening the account (Rule 3210), and the employer can ask for duplicate confirmations and statements. The logic: the employer must be able to watch its people's trading for insider trading and front running.

Account registrations: who owns it, who can trade it

The registration (the legal title on the account) answers three questions: who owns the assets, who may give orders, and where the assets go at death.

RegistrationOwner / who tradesPaperworkAt death
IndividualOne owner, trades own accountNew account formTo the owner's estate
JTWROS (joint tenants with right of survivorship)Two or more owners, undivided equal interest; any one may tradeJoint account agreement signed by allPasses to surviving owner(s), outside probate
TIC (tenants in common)Two or more owners with stated percentages; any one may tradeJoint account agreement signed by allDecedent's share to the decedent's estate
TOD (transfer on death)Individual owner with named beneficiariesTOD designationDirectly to beneficiaries, bypassing probate
UGMA/UTMA custodialMinor owns; one custodian tradesMinor's SSN; no special agreementMinor dies → minor's estate; custodian dies → successor custodian
TrustTrust owns; trustee trades within the trust agreementTrust agreement (proof of trustee authority)Per the trust document
CorporateCorporation owns; officers named in a resolution tradeCorporate resolution; charter/bylaws must permit marginNot applicable (entity)
PartnershipPartnership owns; designated partners tradePartnership agreementPer the agreement
Retirement (IRA etc.)Owner trades; custodian holdsPlan/IRA documents; no marginTo named beneficiary

Joint accounts

Two smaller registrations: a numbered account (identified by a number or symbol instead of a name) is allowed if the customer signs a written statement of ownership, so the firm always knows the true owner; a sole proprietor account is simply an individual account used for business, since the business has no separate legal existence.

Any joint owner can enter orders, but money leaving the account goes out in the names of all owners (checks payable to all). JTWROS is the usual choice for spouses and assumes equal ownership. TIC lets owners hold unequal shares, and each share is a separate asset for estate purposes.

Worked example — TIC ownership split at death
  1. Ana (60%) and Ben (40%) hold a TIC account worth $500,000. Ana dies.
  2. Ana's share: 60% × $500,000 = $300,000 → goes to Ana's estate (and then to her heirs under her will or state law).
  3. Ben keeps his 40% = $200,000. He does not inherit Ana's share just because he was on the account.
  4. Same account titled JTWROS: Ben becomes sole owner of the full $500,000, and it never goes through Ana's estate.

Custodial accounts (UGMA/UTMA)

Under the Uniform Gifts to Minors Act or Uniform Transfers to Minors Act, an adult gives assets to a minor and a custodian manages them. The rules follow from one fact: the money already belongs to the child.

  • One custodian, one minor per account. No joint custodians, no pooled children.
  • Gifts are irrevocable: the donor cannot take them back.
  • Tax reporting uses the minor's SSN.
  • No margin (so no short sales), and the custodian cannot delegate trading authority to someone else.
  • At the age of majority set by state law, the assets must be transferred to the former minor. UTMA allows a broader range of property and, in many states, a later transfer age than UGMA.

Fiduciary accounts

A fiduciary (trustee, custodian, executor, guardian) manages assets for someone else. The firm must see the document that grants the authority (trust agreement, court appointment, letters testamentary) and keep the fiduciary within it. Margin and speculative strategies are off-limits unless the document expressly allows them.

Who else may place orders: discretion and powers of attorney

Discretion means the rep decides at least one of the "three A's": asset (which security), action (buy or sell), amount (how many). Rule 3260 requires:

  • Written authorization from the customer before the first discretionary order. Verbal permission is never enough.
  • Principal approval of the account in writing, and prompt review of discretionary orders (watching especially for excessive trading).
  • Each order marked discretionary on the order ticket.

Time-and-price discretion is not discretion. If the customer picks the asset, action and amount ("buy 1,000 XYZ today") and lets the rep choose only when and at what price, no written authorization is needed. It is valid only for the business day it is granted, unless the customer specifies otherwise in writing.

Customer picks asset + action + amount?
  yes --> rep picks only time/price
          --> no written auth; good for the day only
  no  --> rep picks any of the three --> discretion:
          written auth first + principal approval

A power of attorney (POA) gives a third party authority over the account.

TypeCan trade?Can withdraw?Ends at
Limited POA (trading authorization)YesNoRevocation, incapacity or death of the principal
Full POAYesYesRevocation, incapacity or death
Durable POA (limited or full)YesPer its termsSurvives incapacity; ends at death

The durable POA exists precisely for incapacity (dementia, a coma). But after death nobody's POA works; authority passes to the executor under the will.

Death and incapacity: what the firm does

Firm learns of death --> cancel open orders, freeze account
  --> collect: death certificate, letters testamentary,
      affidavit of domicile (plus any state tax waiver)
  --> retitle: estate, surviving joint tenant, or
      TOD beneficiary --> executor gives instructions
  • Individual account → estate; the executor acts once the documents are in.
  • JTWROS → the survivor, after the paperwork re-registers the account.
  • TIC → decedent's percentage to the estate; the survivor's share is untouched.
  • All POAs and discretionary authority end at death. Incapacity ends a non-durable POA; a durable one continues.

Senior and vulnerable investors (Rule 2165)

A specified adult is a customer age 65 or older, or 18 or older with a mental or physical impairment that makes them unable to protect their own interests. If the firm reasonably believes such a customer is being financially exploited (a new "friend" draining the account, a scam wire), Rule 2165 lets it place a temporary hold on the disbursement of funds or securities, notify the authorized parties and the trusted contact (unless one of them is the suspect), and investigate. The hold runs for a limited number of business days and can be extended in defined steps; the exact day counts were amended recently and are under review here, so learn the concept: a short, documented, extendable pause, not a freeze of the customer's trading. Rule 2165 is a safe harbour: it permits the hold, it does not require it.

Know your customer, suitability and Reg BI

KYC (Rule 2090): the firm must use reasonable diligence to know the essential facts about every customer, at opening and throughout, including who has authority to act on the account. Suitability (Rule 2111) applies whenever a firm or rep recommends a security or strategy. It has three parts:

ObligationQuestion it asksClassic failure
Reasonable-basisIs this product suitable for at least some investors, and does the rep understand it?Selling a complex note nobody at the firm analysed
Customer-specificIs it suitable for this customer's investment profile?Leveraged ETF to a retiree needing income
QuantitativeIs the series of trades, taken together, excessive?Churning: each trade looks fine; the frequency does not

The investment profile includes age, other investments, financial situation and needs, tax status, investment objectives, experience, time horizon, liquidity needs and risk tolerance. Unsolicited orders (the customer's own idea) carry no recommendation, so suitability does not attach; the ticket is marked unsolicited.

Regulation Best Interest (SEC, compliance from June 30, 2020) raises the bar for recommendations to retail customers: natural persons (or their legal representatives) using the recommendation primarily for personal, family or household purposes. The BD must act in the retail customer's best interest without putting its own interest ahead. Reg BI has four component obligations: disclosure, care, conflict of interest, compliance. In practice: disclosure means telling the customer in writing about fees, the capacity in which the firm acts and material conflicts; care means reasonable diligence, including weighing costs and reasonably available alternatives; conflict of interest means written policies to identify conflicts and disclose, mitigate or eliminate them, and the firm must eliminate sales contests, quotas and bonuses tied to selling specific securities within a limited period; compliance means policies and procedures to make the other three work. Where Reg BI applies, FINRA's suitability rule steps aside; Rule 2111 still governs recommendations to non-retail customers.

Institutional customers. For a non-retail institutional account (for example an entity with at least $50 million in total assets), the firm can satisfy the customer-specific part of suitability if it reasonably believes the customer can evaluate risk independently and the customer affirms it is exercising independent judgment. Reasonable-basis and quantitative suitability still apply.

Worked example — reading a profile
  1. Customer: 72, retired, income need of $3,000 a month, moderate risk tolerance, 80% of liquid net worth in one account.
  2. Proposed: a concentrated position in a speculative biotech, bought on margin. Customer-specific fails twice: objective (income, not speculation) and liquidity/risk (concentration plus leverage for someone drawing on the account).
  3. Proposed instead: a diversified mix of investment-grade bonds and dividend stocks. It fits the objective; now the quantitative test matters: turning that portfolio over monthly would fail even though each individual trade is suitable.
  4. If she is a specified adult and a new "adviser" suddenly asks for large wires out, Rule 2165 and the trusted contact come into play; that is a separate question from suitability.

Form CRS (customer relationship summary) is a short, plain-English document describing services, fees, conflicts, standard of conduct and disciplinary history. A BD delivers it to each retail investor at or before the earliest of a recommendation, placing an order, or opening an account.

Anti-money laundering: the plumbing

Money laundering moves in three stages, and each rule targets a stage.

Placement --> dirty cash enters the system
  (CTR, structuring rules, cash limits)
Layering  --> moved through trades/wires to blur the trail
  (SAR, monitoring, CDD)
Integration --> comes back as "clean" investment gains
  (KYC, beneficial ownership)

The legal base is the Bank Secrecy Act (1970), which created CTRs and SARs, and Title III of the USA PATRIOT Act (2001), which required AML programs and Customer Identification Programs at broker-dealers.

The AML program (FINRA Rule 3310)

  • Written policies to detect and report suspicious activity, approved in writing by senior management.
  • A designated AML compliance officer.
  • Ongoing employee training.
  • An independent test annually (every two years for firms that do not execute customer transactions).
  • Risk-based customer due diligence, including identifying the beneficial owners of legal-entity customers.

For a legal-entity customer (a company, an LLC, a fund), beneficial ownership means each individual who owns 25% or more of the equity, plus one individual with significant control, such as the CEO. The point is to see through shell companies to a real person who can be screened.

Red flags that should prompt review and possibly a SAR: a customer reluctant to give identity or business information; cash in odd amounts just under reporting levels; funds wired in and out quickly with little trading; transfers to or from high-risk jurisdictions; deposits of large blocks of thinly traded stock followed by immediate sale; activity that makes no economic sense for the customer's stated profile; many accounts under different names sharing an address or phone number.

CIP: the four items

Before or within a reasonable time after opening, the firm collects and verifies: name, date of birth, address (residential or business; not a P.O. box for an individual) and an identification number (SSN or TIN for a U.S. person; for a non-U.S. person, for example a passport number and issuing country). It checks the name against government lists.

Reports and lists

ToolTriggerDeadline / rule
CTR (currency transaction report)Cash over $10,000 in one business day, aggregatedFiled within 15 days; not a judgment of wrongdoing
SAR (suspicious activity report)Suspicious transactions of $5,000 or moreWithin 30 calendar days of initial detection (up to 60 if no suspect is identified); never tell the customer
Monetary instrument logCash purchases of money orders, cashier's checks etc. of $3,000–$10,000Record kept
OFAC SDN listCustomer or counterparty matches the Specially Designated Nationals listBlock the property and report; no trading

OFAC (the Treasury's Office of Foreign Assets Control) is different in kind from the BSA reports. A CTR or SAR is a report and the account may keep running; an OFAC match is a prohibition. The firm must not process the transaction, must freeze (block) the property so nobody can move it, and must report to OFAC. There is no dollar threshold, and screening applies to every customer, beneficiary and counterparty, not just cash transactions. OFAC also treats an entity owned 50% or more by blocked persons as blocked itself, even if its own name is not on the list, which is why beneficial-ownership data matters for sanctions as well as laundering.

CTR, SAR and CIP records are kept 5 years. Structuring is breaking cash into pieces below $10,000 to dodge a CTR. It is a crime by itself, even when the money is legitimate, and it is a textbook SAR trigger. No tipping off: telling a customer that a SAR was filed is itself illegal.

Worked example — CTR or structuring?
  1. Monday: a customer delivers $6,000 cash in the morning and $5,500 in the afternoon. Aggregated: $11,500 in one business day → CTR.
  2. Next week: $9,500 Monday, $9,000 Tuesday, $9,800 Wednesday, after asking what amount "triggers paperwork". No single day tops $10,000, so no CTR, but the pattern is structuring → SAR (well over $5,000), and the rep says nothing to the customer about it.
Understand
  • Registrations decide ownership, trading authority and what happens at death: JTWROS to survivor, TIC to estate, TOD to beneficiaries, UGMA/UTMA belongs to the minor.
  • Discretion (asset, action or amount) requires written authorization and principal approval; time-and-price alone is not discretion and lasts only the day.
  • POAs: limited trades only, full can withdraw, durable survives incapacity; every POA ends at death.
  • Rule 4512 defines the account record; a trusted contact (18+) is requested, has no authority; Rule 2165 permits temporary holds for specified adults (65+, or 18+ impaired).
  • Suitability 2111 has reasonable-basis, customer-specific and quantitative parts; Reg BI governs recommendations to retail customers; Form CRS is delivered at the start of the relationship.
  • AML: CIP (4 items), KYC 2090, program 3310, CTR over $10,000, SAR $5,000+, OFAC blocking, structuring is a crime.
MEMORIZE:
  • CIP items → name, date of birth, address, ID number
  • CTR → cash over $10,000 in a business day (aggregated); filed within 15 days
  • SAR (BD) → $5,000+ suspicious; within 30 days of detection; no tipping off
  • OFAC SDN list → block and report
  • AML records (CTR, SAR, CIP) → 5 years
  • Monetary instrument log → cash purchases of $3,000–$10,000
  • Trusted contact person → 18+, no trading authority
  • Specified adult → 65+, or 18+ with impairment
  • Time-and-price discretion → valid only for the day granted
  • UGMA/UTMA → one custodian, one minor, no margin, irrevocable gifts, minor's SSN
  • JTWROS → to survivor; TIC → to estate
  • Reg BI → four obligations: disclosure, care, conflict of interest, compliance
Exam traps
  • "Deposits under $10,000 need no report" → Structuring below $10,000 is itself a crime and a SAR trigger.
  • "The customer said 'use your judgment' on the phone, so the rep may pick the stock" → Discretion requires WRITTEN authorization before the first discretionary trade.
  • "Time and price is a form of discretion needing principal approval" → Time and price alone is not discretion.
  • "A durable POA lets the agent keep managing the account after the customer dies" → Any POA, even durable, terminates at death; the executor takes over.
  • "The trusted contact can authorize a withdrawal" → A trusted contact person has no authority to trade or withdraw.
  • "Reg BI applies to all customers" → Reg BI applies to recommendations to RETAIL customers.
  • "The surviving TIC owner gets the whole account" → Only under JTWROS; TIC shares go to the estate.
Trader connection

Every institutional relationship starts with this chapter scaled up. A prime broker or swap dealer runs CIP and beneficial-ownership checks on a fund, screens every entity and owner against OFAC before the first trade, and repeats the screen on each counterparty and payment. Account structure then decides authority: who may sign the ISDA master agreement and credit support annex, who may pledge collateral, who may direct transfers. That is the corporate resolution and the trustee's authority from today's table. A trade booked by someone without authority, or with a sanctioned counterparty, is not just an error but a loss the desk may be unable to unwind.

Check yourself

Q1 Two customers own an account as tenants in common, 60% and 40%. The account is worth $500,000 when the 60% owner dies. What happens?
A. All $500,000 passes to the surviving owner
B. $250,000 goes to the deceased owner's estate
C. $300,000 goes to the deceased owner's estate; $200,000 remains the survivor's
D. The survivor must buy out the estate before the account can be unfrozen

Answer: C. TIC shares pass to the decedent's estate: 60% × $500,000 = $300,000. A describes JTWROS; B splits equally, ignoring the stated percentages; D invents a buy-out requirement.

Q2 Under the Customer Identification Program, a broker-dealer must obtain all of the following before opening an account for an individual EXCEPT:
A. Annual income
B. Name
C. Date of birth
D. Address

Answer: A. CIP's four items are name, date of birth, address and an identification number. Annual income is suitability/KYC profile information collected under Rule 4512 with reasonable efforts, not a CIP identity item.

Q3 Which statements are TRUE?
I. A CTR is required for cash over $10,000 in one business day
II. A broker-dealer files a SAR for suspicious transactions of $5,000 or more
III. The rep should tell the customer a SAR has been filed
IV. Splitting deposits to stay under $10,000 to avoid a CTR is a crime
A. I and III
B. II and III
C. I, II and III
D. I, II and IV

Answer: D. I, II and IV are the BSA rules. III is illegal tipping off: the customer is never told. Every option containing III is wrong.

Q4 A customer calls her rep: "Buy 1,000 shares of XYZ today; you pick the time and price." Which is correct?
A. The rep needs written discretionary authorization before entering the order
B. This is time-and-price discretion; no written authorization is needed and it is good only for today
C. A principal must approve the order before entry
D. The order remains valid until the customer cancels it

Answer: B. The customer chose asset, action and amount; only timing and price were left to the rep, which Rule 3260 does not treat as discretion. It lasts for the day granted unless the customer specifies otherwise in writing. A and C describe full discretion; D ignores the one-day limit.

Q5 Which statement about UGMA/UTMA accounts is true?
A. Each account has one custodian and one minor
B. The donor may reclaim the gift if the minor misbehaves
C. Income is reported under the custodian's Social Security number
D. The custodian may use margin if it is prudent

Answer: A. One custodian, one minor. Gifts are irrevocable (B), tax reporting uses the minor's SSN (C), and custodial accounts cannot use margin (D).

Q6 Regulation Best Interest applies to:
A. All transactions for all customers of a broker-dealer
B. Recommendations to institutional customers only
C. Discretionary accounts only
D. Recommendations to retail customers

Answer: D. Reg BI covers recommendations of securities or strategies to retail customers. Unsolicited trades carry no recommendation (A); non-retail recommendations remain under FINRA suitability (B); account type does not matter (C).

Q7 A firm learns that an individual account holder has died. In what order does it proceed?
I. Retitle or transfer the assets per the executor's instructions
II. Cancel open orders and freeze the account
III. Obtain the death certificate, letters testamentary and affidavit of domicile
A. I, II, III
B. II, III, I
C. III, II, I
D. II, I, III

Answer: B. Freeze first so nobody trades on a dead person's account, then collect the documents that prove who now has authority, then act on the executor's instructions. C leaves the account live while paperwork is gathered; A and D move assets before authority is proven.

Q8 Which is true of a trusted contact person?
A. May place trades if the customer becomes incapacitated
B. Must be at least 21 years old
C. Must be at least 18 and has no authority to transact in the account
D. Must be provided or the firm cannot open the account

Answer: C. The trusted contact is someone the firm may call about the customer's welfare or suspected exploitation; age 18+, no trading or withdrawal authority. The firm makes a reasonable effort to obtain one, but the account opens if the customer declines (D). Trading authority requires a POA (A).

Day 22 · Retirement, education accounts and investment taxation

FINRA 3.2, 2.1 · pairs with the Day 22 edition (~120 min)

Every account in this chapter is the same machine with the tax switched on at a different point: when money goes in, while it grows, or when it comes out. Learn that frame first and most rules become predictable; then learn the handful of numbers that cannot be reasoned out, which the SIE tests directly. On a desk, after-tax P&L decides whether a client sells, hedges or harvests a loss.

The frame: where the tax hits

Three moments matter: contribution (is the money deducted, i.e. pre-tax, or already taxed?), growth (are dividends, interest and gains taxed each year, or deferred?) and distribution (is the withdrawal taxed, and at what rate?).

AccountMoney inGrowthMoney out
Taxable brokerage accountAfter-taxTaxed yearly (dividends, interest, realized gains)Only the gain is taxed; long-term gains at lower rates
Traditional IRA / 401(k) (deductible or pre-tax)Pre-taxTax-deferredEverything taxed as ordinary income
Traditional IRA (nondeductible)After-tax (creates basis)Tax-deferredEarnings taxed as ordinary income; basis returned tax-free
Roth IRA / Roth 401(k)After-taxTax-freeQualified distributions tax-free
529 planAfter-tax (no federal deduction)Tax-freeTax-free if spent on qualified education

Two consequences worth reasoning out rather than memorizing. First, money coming out of a traditional account is ordinary income, even if it grew from long-term capital gains or qualified dividends; the account launders away the preferential rates. Second, the Roth-versus-traditional choice is a bet on tax rates: deduct now at today's rate, or pay today and never again.

Traditional: deduct now --> defer --> pay ordinary rate later
Roth:        pay now    --> grow  --> qualified exit tax-free
Taxable:     pay now    --> pay yearly --> gains at sale

IRAs: traditional and Roth

Concepts

  • Earned income is required to contribute: wages, salary, commissions, self-employment income (alimony under pre-2019 divorce agreements also counts). Interest, dividends, rent, pension and Social Security are not earned income. A non-working spouse can contribute through a spousal IRA based on the working spouse's income.
  • The contribution limit is per person across all IRAs combined, traditional and Roth together.
  • Whether a traditional contribution is deductible depends on income and on whether the person is an active participant in an employer plan. Roth contributions phase out at higher incomes. The SIE tests that these limits exist, not the income thresholds.
  • Over-contributing triggers a 6% excise tax each year the excess stays in.
  • IRAs cannot hold life insurance or collectibles (certain U.S.-minted coins excepted) and cannot use margin. Limited-risk option strategies, such as SELL CALL against stock owned (covered call) or BUY PUT on stock owned, are allowed.
Traditional IRARoth IRA
ContributionDeductible if eligibleNever deductible
GrowthTax-deferredTax-free
WithdrawalsOrdinary income (except nondeductible basis)Contributions any time tax- and penalty-free; earnings tax-free if qualified
Qualified earnings withdrawaln/aAge 59½ (or death, disability, first home) AND 5 years since the first Roth contribution
RMDs for the ownerYes, from 73 (75 if born 1960+)None

Roth withdrawal ordering: contributions come out first, then conversions, then earnings. So a Roth owner who contributed $30,000 can take $30,000 out at any age with no tax or penalty; only a withdrawal that reaches earnings needs the age-59½-plus-five-years test.

Numbers

Just memorize 2026 IRA limit $7,500, plus $1,100 catch-up at 50 or older (2025 was $7,000 + $1,000, so older material shows those). The deadline is the tax filing deadline of the following year (typically April 15), not December 31, and filing extensions do not extend it.

Employer plans

A qualified plan meets IRS and, for private employers, ERISA rules: it cannot discriminate in favour of highly paid employees, contributions are pre-tax (or Roth), growth is deferred, and assets are held in trust away from the employer's creditors. ERISA (Employee Retirement Income Security Act, 1974) covers private-sector plans only: fiduciary duties, vesting, reporting. Government plans are not ERISA plans. A nonqualified plan (deferred compensation for executives) can discriminate, but the employer gets no deduction until the employee is paid, and the promised money remains exposed to the employer's creditors.

PlanWho offers itKey feature
401(k)Private employersEmployee salary deferral, often with employer match; Roth option common
403(b)Public schools, 501(c)(3) nonprofitsWorks like a 401(k); often annuity or fund based
457(b)State and local governments (and some nonprofits)No 10% early-withdrawal penalty on distributions after separation from service
SEP IRASmall businesses, self-employedEmployer contributions into employees' IRAs; easy to run
SIMPLE IRASmall employersEmployee deferrals plus a required employer contribution
Keogh (HR-10)Self-employed / unincorporated businessesQualified plan for the self-employed

Just memorize 2026 elective deferral limit for 401(k), 403(b) and governmental 457: $24,500; catch-up at 50+: $8,000; enhanced catch-up for ages 60–63: $11,250 (instead of $8,000). Roth 401(k)s have had no RMDs for the original owner since 2024.

Moving money: rollovers vs transfers

The concept: moving retirement money is tax-free if it never lands in the owner's hands, or lands there and goes back in within 60 days. The rules punish the second path.

Transfer (trustee-to-trustee):
  Custodian A --(assets)--> Custodian B
  no withholding, no limit on how often

Direct rollover (plan to IRA):
  401(k) --(assets)--> IRA   no withholding

Indirect (60-day) rollover:
  Plan/IRA --(check)--> Owner --(within 60 days)--> IRA
  plan payouts: 20% withheld; IRA-to-IRA: once / 12 months
Worked example — the 20% withholding trap
  1. Dana, 45, leaves her job and takes her $100,000 401(k) as a check payable to herself. The plan must withhold 20%: she receives $80,000.
  2. To roll over the full $100,000 she must deposit $100,000 into an IRA within 60 days, so she must find $20,000 from other savings. The $20,000 withheld comes back as a credit when she files her tax return.
  3. If she deposits only the $80,000, the missing $20,000 is a distribution: taxed as ordinary income plus the 10% penalty (she is under 59½).
  4. A direct rollover (plan pays the IRA custodian) would have avoided all of it.

Getting money out: penalties, exceptions and RMDs

Concepts

Tax deferral is a subsidy for retirement, so the law penalizes early exits and forces eventual exits. The 10% early-withdrawal penalty applies to taxable distributions before age 59½, on top of ordinary income tax. The exceptions waive the penalty only; income tax is still due on pre-tax money:

  • death (distributions to beneficiaries) and disability;
  • substantially equal periodic payments (SEPP, under IRC §72(t));
  • first-time home purchase, up to $10,000 lifetime (IRAs);
  • qualified higher-education expenses (IRAs);
  • certain unreimbursed medical expenses;
  • for employer plans (not IRAs): separation from service in or after the year the employee turns 55; and for 457 plans, any distribution after separation.

Required minimum distributions (RMDs) force money out of traditional accounts so it is finally taxed. First RMD for the year the owner reaches the RMD age, due by April 1 of the following year; later ones by December 31 each year. Missing an RMD triggers an excise tax that SECURE 2.0 cut to 25% of the shortfall (10% if corrected promptly); older books say 50%.

Nonqualified annuities follow a related logic: withdrawals are treated as earnings first (LIFO), taxed as ordinary income, with the 10% penalty before 59½.

Worked example — what an early withdrawal costs
  1. Eli, 45, takes $20,000 from a traditional IRA; assume a 24% federal bracket. Tax $4,800 + penalty $2,000 = $6,800; he keeps $13,200.
  2. Same withdrawal because Eli is disabled: tax $4,800, no penalty; he keeps $15,200.
  3. Roth instead: Eli has contributed $30,000; the account is worth $45,000. Withdrawing $30,000 costs nothing. Withdrawing $35,000 reaches $5,000 of earnings: tax $1,200 + penalty $500 on that $5,000.

Numbers

Just memorize Penalty 10% before 59½. RMD age 73 (75 for those born 1960 or later). First-home exception $10,000. Roth 5-year rule.

Education and disability accounts

Concepts

529 plans are state-sponsored, so an interest in one is a municipal fund security: MSRB rules apply to the firms that sell them, and the offering document is the plan's disclosure document (an official statement), not a prospectus. Two kinds: savings plans (invested in portfolios, value floats) and prepaid tuition plans (lock in tuition credits). The owner, usually a parent, controls the account, not the beneficiary, and can change the beneficiary to another family member. Contributions get no federal deduction (some states give a state deduction to residents). Growth and qualified withdrawals are tax-free; a nonqualified withdrawal's earnings are taxed as ordinary income plus a 10% penalty. Contributions are gifts, so the gift-tax exclusion applies, with a special rule allowing five years of exclusions to be front-loaded.

Coverdell ESAs (education savings accounts) are small, restrictive trusts: low annual cap per beneficiary, contributor income limits, K-12 and college expenses both qualify, and deadlines on contributing and spending.

ABLE accounts (529A) are 529-style state programs for people with disabilities that began before a statutory age cutoff; qualified disability expenses come out tax-free. Annual contributions are capped at the gift-tax annual exclusion. They are also municipal fund securities.

529 planCoverdell ESA
Annual contributionNo federal annual cap; state lifetime caps; gift-tax rules apply$2,000 per beneficiary, all contributors combined
Age limitsNoneContributions until 18; must be used by 30
K-12Tuition up to $20,000/year (from 2026)Qualified K-12 expenses
Contributor income limitsNoneYes
Investment controlPlan's menu of portfoliosSelf-directed

Numbers

Just memorize 529 K-12 tuition: up to $20,000 per year from 2026 (it was $10,000; older prep books show $10,000). Five-year front-loading: 5 × $19,000 = $95,000 per donor per beneficiary in 2026. 529-to-Roth rollover: lifetime $35,000, the 529 must have been open 15+ years, and each year's rollover counts against the annual Roth contribution limit. Coverdell: $2,000; 18; 30.

Taxing the trades: basis and holding period

Cost basis is what you paid, including commissions; proceeds are what you received, net of commissions. Gain or loss = proceeds − basis. When a customer holds several lots of the same stock, the lot sold matters: FIFO (first in, first out) is the default for stock; specific identification lets the customer choose the lot (e.g., the highest-cost lot to minimize the gain); mutual funds may use average cost.

The holding period starts the day after the trade date (not settlement) and includes the sale date. More than one year is long-term, taxed at lower preferential rates; one year or less is short-term, taxed as ordinary income.

Worked example — holding period
  1. Buy on trade date March 10, 2025. The holding period starts March 11, 2025.
  2. Sell on March 10, 2026: exactly one year → short-term.
  3. Sell on March 11, 2026 or later: more than one year → long-term. Settlement dates are irrelevant.

Netting gains and losses

Net short-term against short-term and long-term against long-term, then net the two results. A net loss offsets up to $3,000 of ordinary income per year; the rest carries forward indefinitely, keeping its character.

Worked example — capital gain/loss netting
  1. Short-term: gains $2,000, losses $9,000 → net ST loss $7,000.
  2. Long-term: gains $3,000, losses $1,000 → net LT gain $2,000.
  3. Combine: −$7,000 + $2,000 = net capital loss $5,000 (short-term in character).
  4. Deduct $3,000 against ordinary income this year; carry $2,000 forward to next year.

Wash sales: the loss is deferred, not erased

A wash sale occurs when you sell at a loss and buy a substantially identical security within 30 days before or after the sale (a 61-day window). The loss is disallowed now and added to the basis of the replacement shares, and the old holding period carries over. Buying a call option on the same stock (BUY CALL) counts as acquiring it. Gains are never "washed"; the rule only blocks harvesting a loss while keeping the position. One desk note: a repurchase inside the customer's IRA also triggers the rule, and there the loss is effectively lost because IRA basis has no tax use.

Worked example — wash sale basis adjustment
  1. Buy 100 XYZ at $50 ($5,000). Sell at $40 ($4,000): a $1,000 loss.
  2. Ten days later buy 100 XYZ at $42 ($4,200). Within 30 days → wash sale. The $1,000 loss is disallowed.
  3. New basis = $4,200 + $1,000 = $5,200 ($52 per share).
  4. Later sell at $55 ($5,500): gain = $5,500 − $5,200 = $300. Economically he lost $1,000 on the first lot and made $1,300 on the second ($42 → $55): net $300, exactly the gain taxed. The loss was used, just later.

Gifts and inheritances

Gifted securities keep the donor's basis (for computing a gain) and the donor's holding period. If the stock is worth less than the donor's basis at the time of the gift, a special lower-of rule applies to losses; learn only that it exists. The donor, not the recipient, owes any gift tax; the 2026 annual exclusion is $19,000 per recipient per donor, so a married couple can give $38,000 together.

Inherited securities receive a step-up in basis to fair market value at the date of death and are always long-term, however long the heir holds them.

Worked example — gift vs inheritance
  1. Father bought at $20. Stock is $70 when he gives it to his son; son sells at $75. Gain = $75 − $20 = $55 per share, long-term (father's holding period carries over).
  2. Instead father dies when the stock is $70; son sells three months later at $75. Basis $70 → gain $5 per share, long-term despite three months.

Dividends and interest

  • Qualified dividends (most U.S. corporate dividends) are taxed at long-term capital gain rates if the shares were held more than 60 days in the 121-day window that begins 60 days before the ex-date. Ordinary dividends (holding test failed, most REIT dividends, money-market fund dividends, manufactured dividends from a short seller) are taxed as ordinary income.
  • Timing: a cash dividend is taxable when paid (the payable date). Exception: fund and REIT dividends declared in October–December to holders of record in those months and paid by the end of January count as received December 31.
  • Return of capital is not income: it reduces cost basis, and once basis reaches zero further amounts are capital gains.
  • Stock dividends and splits are not taxed; the basis is spread over more shares.
  • Municipal bond interest is generally exempt from federal tax (and from state tax in the issuer's state); muni capital gains are taxable. Treasury interest is federally taxable but exempt from state and local tax. Corporate interest is fully taxable.
Worked example — the qualified-dividend holding test
  1. Ex-date June 15. The 121-day window runs from April 16 (60 days before) to August 14.
  2. A trader buys June 1 and sells July 5. Counting from the day after purchase through the sale date, he held 34 days, not more than 60 → the dividend is ordinary income.
  3. Holding through at least August 1 (61 days, still inside the window) makes it qualified. A short dividend-capture trade therefore produces an ordinary-income dividend, while the matching ex-date price drop is only a capital loss, which offsets ordinary income by at most $3,000 a year if there are no gains to absorb it.

Tax-equivalent yield = Muni yield ÷ (1 − marginal tax rate)

Muni yield
the tax-free yield on the municipal bond
Marginal tax rate
the investor's federal rate on the next dollar of income
Tax-equivalent yield
the taxable yield needed to match the muni after tax
Worked example — after-tax return comparison
  1. Muni yields 3.50%; corporate yields 5.20%; investor's bracket 35%.
  2. Muni TEY = 3.50% ÷ 0.65 = 5.38%, above 5.20% → the muni wins.
  3. Check the other way: corporate after tax = 5.20% × 0.65 = 3.38%, below 3.50%.
  4. For a 22% bracket: TEY = 3.50% ÷ 0.78 = 4.49% → the corporate wins. Munis suit high brackets, and rarely belong in an IRA, where their tax exemption is wasted.
Understand
  • Traditional = pre-tax in, taxed as ordinary income out; Roth = after-tax in, tax-free qualified out; earned income is required to contribute to either.
  • Qualified plans (401(k), 403(b), 457, SEP, SIMPLE, Keogh); ERISA covers private-sector plans only; 457 has no 10% penalty after separation.
  • Transfers and direct rollovers are clean; 60-day indirect rollovers risk 20% withholding and the once-per-12-months IRA limit.
  • 10% penalty before 59½ with exceptions that waive the penalty but not the income tax; RMDs force money out; Roth owners have no RMDs.
  • 529 plans are municipal fund securities controlled by the owner; Coverdells are small and age-limited; ABLE accounts serve people with disabilities.
  • Long-term means more than one year from the day after trade date; net losses offset $3,000 of ordinary income and carry forward; wash sales defer losses into the new basis; gifts carry basis, inheritances step up.
  • Qualified dividends get capital-gain rates; ordinary dividends do not; return of capital cuts basis; munis are federally exempt, Treasuries state-exempt.
MEMORIZE:
  • 2026 IRA limit → $7,500 + $1,100 catch-up (50+)
  • 2026 401(k)/403(b)/gov 457 → $24,500 + $8,000 catch-up; $11,250 ages 60–63
  • Early withdrawal penalty → 10% before 59½
  • RMD age → 73 (75 if born 1960+); first by April 1 of the following year
  • Indirect rollover → 60 days; once per 12 months IRA-to-IRA; 20% withheld from plan payouts
  • IRA contribution deadline → tax filing deadline (typically April 15)
  • Coverdell → $2,000/yr, contributions until 18, use by 30
  • 529 K-12 tuition → up to $20,000/yr (from 2026; was $10,000)
  • 529 front-loading → 5 × $19,000 = $95,000; 529-to-Roth → $35,000 lifetime, 15 years
  • Gift tax annual exclusion → $19,000 per recipient (2026)
  • Long-term → more than 1 year; starts day after trade date
  • Wash sale window → 30 days before and after
  • Net capital loss vs ordinary income → $3,000/yr, carry forward indefinitely
  • Qualified dividend holding → more than 60 days in the 121-day window around the ex-date
Exam traps
  • "Roth withdrawals before 59½ are always penalized" → Roth contributions can be withdrawn anytime tax- and penalty-free; EARNINGS need 59½ + 5 years.
  • "A 50-year-old leaving a government job pays 10% on 457 withdrawals" → 457 plans have no 10% penalty on distributions after separation.
  • "Inherited stock held 2 months is short-term" → Inherited securities get a step-up and are always long-term.
  • "Gifted stock takes the value on the gift date as basis" → Gifted securities carry the donor's basis.
  • "The wash-sale loss is lost forever" → A wash sale DEFERS the loss (added to the new basis); it does not erase it.
  • "IRA contributions must be made by December 31" → The deadline is the tax filing deadline, not December 31.
  • "The hardship exception means no tax" → Exceptions waive the 10% penalty; income tax is still due.
  • "Missed RMD penalty is 50%" → SECURE 2.0 cut it to 25% (10% if corrected promptly).
Trader connection

Clients do not maximize pre-tax P&L; they maximize what they keep. An executive with a low-basis concentrated position often prefers a collar (BUY PUT + SELL CALL around the stock) to selling, because a sale realizes a large gain, and the hedge must be structured so the tax law does not treat it as a sale anyway. Tax-loss harvesting is a wash-sale problem: sell the loser, then hold something correlated but not substantially identical (a different issuer or a broad index fund) for 31 days, or accept the basis adjustment. On any desk, P&L reconciliation runs lot by lot: which tax lots were closed (FIFO or specific ID), which were wash-adjusted, and which dividends were manufactured rather than qualified. The same arithmetic sits behind every "after-tax return" comparison you will be asked to run.

Check yourself

Q1 A customer, age 32, becomes disabled and withdraws $15,000 from his traditional IRA, funded entirely with deductible contributions. What are the tax consequences?
A. Ordinary income tax; no 10% penalty
B. No income tax and no penalty
C. Ordinary income tax plus the 10% penalty
D. The 10% penalty only

Answer: A. Disability is an exception to the 10% early-withdrawal penalty, but the money went in pre-tax, so it is taxed as ordinary income when it comes out. B confuses "penalty waived" with "tax-free"; C ignores the exception; D reverses the logic.

Q2 A customer buys 100 shares at $50, sells them at $40 on March 1 and buys 100 shares at $42 on March 15. What is the per-share basis of the new shares?
A. $42
B. $40
C. $52
D. $50

Answer: C. The repurchase is within 30 days, so the $10-per-share loss is disallowed and added to the new cost: $42 + $10 = $52. A ignores the wash sale; B and D use the old sale or purchase price.

Q3 Which statements about Roth IRAs are TRUE?
I. Contributions are not tax-deductible
II. Tax-free withdrawal of earnings generally requires age 59½ and five years
III. The owner must begin RMDs at age 73
IV. Contributions can be withdrawn at any time without tax or penalty
A. I, II and III
B. I, II and IV
C. II and III
D. III and IV

Answer: B. Roth money goes in after tax (I), earnings need 59½ plus five years (II), and contributions come out first, tax- and penalty-free (IV). Roth IRAs have no RMDs for the original owner, so every choice with III is wrong.

Q4 All of the following are true of 529 plans EXCEPT:
A. They are municipal fund securities
B. From 2026 they may pay up to $20,000 a year of K-12 tuition
C. The account owner, not the beneficiary, controls the account
D. Contributions are deductible on the federal tax return

Answer: D. 529 contributions are after-tax federally (some states give a state deduction). A, B and C are true; B is the current figure (older books say $10,000), so do not reject it for looking unfamiliar.

Q5 In one year a customer has short-term gains of $2,000, short-term losses of $9,000, long-term gains of $3,000 and long-term losses of $1,000. What is the result?
A. $5,000 deducted against ordinary income; nothing carried forward
B. $3,000 deducted; $4,000 carried forward
C. $7,000 deducted against ordinary income
D. $3,000 deducted; $2,000 carried forward

Answer: D. Net ST = −$7,000; net LT = +$2,000; overall net loss $5,000. Only $3,000 offsets ordinary income; $2,000 carries forward. A ignores the $3,000 cap; B forgets to net the long-term gain; C uses the short-term loss alone and ignores the cap.

Q6 A customer buys stock on trade date March 10, 2025. What is the earliest sale date that produces a long-term gain?
A. March 11, 2026
B. March 10, 2026
C. March 12, 2026
D. September 11, 2025

Answer: A. The holding period starts the day after the trade date (March 11, 2025) and must exceed one year, so a sale on March 11, 2026 qualifies. March 10, 2026 is exactly one year (short-term); March 12 would count from settlement date, which is irrelevant; six months is a Rule 144 holding period, not a tax rule.

Q7 A 401(k) participant takes a $50,000 distribution payable to himself, intending to roll it into an IRA. What happens?
A. He receives $50,000 and has 60 days to deposit it
B. He receives $45,000 and has 30 days
C. He receives $40,000 and must deposit $50,000 within 60 days to avoid tax on any part
D. He receives $40,000; the $10,000 withheld is a penalty he cannot recover

Answer: C. Plan distributions paid to the participant carry 20% mandatory withholding ($10,000). To roll the whole $50,000 he must replace the $10,000 from other funds within 60 days; the withholding is later credited on his tax return, so D is wrong. A describes a direct rollover's amount with an indirect rollover's clock; B uses the wrong numbers.

Q8 A customer's father bought stock at $20. It was worth $70 when he died, and the customer sold it three months later at $75. What is the result?
A. $55 per share, short-term
B. $5 per share, long-term
C. $5 per share, short-term
D. $55 per share, long-term

Answer: B. Inherited securities step up to the value at death ($70), so the gain is $5, and inherited property is always long-term. $55 is the answer for a gift (carryover basis); "short-term" applies the three-month hold, which does not matter for inheritances.

Day 23 · The rules of conduct: registration, communications and what gets you barred

FINRA 4.1, 4.2, 3.2, 3.3 · pairs with the Day 23 edition (~120 min)

This chapter is about the individual rather than the product: how a person gets registered and stays registered, what they may say in writing, what they may do outside the firm, and the conduct that ends careers. Much of it is pure memorization (30 days, 2 years, $300, 25 investors), and the SIE writes scenario questions that turn on exactly those numbers. On a desk, these rules are the reason for information barriers, restricted lists, surveillance of every order you cancel, and a compliance sign-off on every email you send.

The career life cycle

An associated person is anyone working for a broker-dealer. A registered person is one who has qualified by exam and whose firm has registered them with FINRA to do securities business (sales, trading, supervision). A principal is a registered supervisor.

Pass SIE (age 18+, no sponsor; valid 4 years)
  --> hired: firm files Form U4 + fingerprints
  --> pass top-off exam (e.g. Series 7) --> registered
  --> annual Regulatory Element CE + Firm Element
  --> leaves: firm files U5 within 30 days
  --> 2-year clock: re-register or qualification lapses
      (MQP: up to 5 years with annual CE + fee)
  --> FINRA keeps jurisdiction 2 years after termination

Form U4

The Uniform Application for Securities Industry Registration or Transfer is filed by the firm, not the individual, when the person associates. It carries personal and employment history and a long set of disclosure questions: criminal charges and convictions, regulatory actions, civil litigation, customer complaints, terminations, and financial events such as bankruptcies, liens and unsatisfied judgments. The firm must fingerprint the person. When anything changes, the U4 must be amended within 30 days of learning of it (10 days for an event that creates a statutory disqualification). Willfully lying on a U4 is itself grounds for disqualification.

Form U5

The Uniform Termination Notice is filed by the firm within 30 days of termination, with a copy to the individual. It states the reason (voluntary, discharged, permitted to resign, deceased, other) and answers disclosure questions, including whether the person was under internal review for fraud or wrongdoing. The next employer reads it, so it is a permanent record; if the firm later learns of something reportable, it amends the U5.

Clocks after you leave

ClockLengthWhat it means
SIE result4 years from the exam dateMust pass a top-off exam and register within it, or retake the SIE
Representative qualification2 years after terminationNot re-registered within 2 years → must requalify by exam
Maintaining Qualifications Program (MQP)Up to 5 yearsElect it, complete annual CE and pay a fee to keep qualifications alive longer
FINRA jurisdiction2 years after terminationFINRA can still investigate and discipline for conduct while registered, and require testimony

Continuing education

  • Regulatory Element: FINRA-built training on rules and regulatory topics, due annually by December 31 (annual since 2023). Miss it and the person becomes CE inactive: they may not perform or be paid for registered activity until it is done.
  • Firm Element: the firm's own annual training programme, built from a needs analysis of its business, products and people.

Statutory disqualification and reportable events

A statutory disqualification (SD), defined in the Exchange Act, bars a person from associating with a member unless FINRA approves an application through an eligibility proceeding (the firm must sponsor it and propose heightened supervision). SDs include:

  • any felony conviction within the past 10 years;
  • certain misdemeanors within 10 years: those involving securities, fraud, theft, bribery, forgery and the like;
  • bars, expulsions and suspensions by the SEC, CFTC, an SRO or a state regulator;
  • certain injunctions involving securities, and willful violations of securities laws;
  • willfully false statements on a registration form.
Rep convicted of a felony
  --> firm amends U4 within 10 days (SD event)
  --> rep is statutorily disqualified
  --> firm terminates (U5 in 30 days) or sponsors an
      eligibility application with heightened supervision

Reportable events (Rule 4530): the firm reports specified events to FINRA within 30 calendar days of learning of them: written customer complaints alleging theft, misappropriation or forgery; being named in securities-related litigation or arbitration; charges or convictions for felonies and specified misdemeanors; disciplinary actions; internal discipline for significant violations. Separately, the firm reports statistical data on all customer complaints quarterly, by the 15th of the month after the quarter.

Outside activities: OBAs and PSTs

The firm must know what its people do outside the job, for two reasons: conflicts (the rep's side business may be sold to customers) and "selling away" (securities sold outside the firm's supervision, where customers have no idea the firm is not behind it).

Outside business activity (Rule 3270)Private securities transaction (Rule 3280)
What it isAny paid business outside the firm: teaching, consulting, a restaurant, serving as a paid directorAny securities transaction outside the regular course of the firm's business ("selling away")
Rep mustGive prior written notice to the firmGive prior written notice describing the deal, the role and any compensation
Firm mustEvaluate it; may restrict or prohibit itIf compensated: approve or disapprove in writing; if approved, record it on its books and supervise it as its own. If not compensated: written acknowledgment, may impose conditions
Not coveredPassive investments (e.g. owning stock)Transactions in the firm's own accounts or supervised business

FINRA has proposed consolidating Rules 3270 and 3280 into a single new rule; the exam tests them as described here.

Gifts, entertainment and non-cash compensation

The worry is commercial bribery: paying another firm's employee to steer business. Rule 3220 caps gifts given to a person in relation to the business of that person's employer at $300 per person per year, effective March 30, 2026. From 1992 until then it was $100, and many question banks still show $100. The firm must keep records of gifts given. Business entertainment (a dinner or game where the rep attends as host) is not a gift and has no fixed cap, but it must be reasonable and not so frequent or lavish that it looks like a payoff. A gift with the rep absent is a gift.

Non-cash compensation covers product sponsors paying for trips, merchandise or meetings. Training and education meetings may be sponsored if attendance is not conditioned on hitting sales targets, guests are not paid for, and the location is appropriate for the purpose. A firm's own sales incentives cannot favour particular products, and Reg BI requires firms to eliminate sales contests tied to specific securities.
