Municipal rules run in parallel: MSRB Rule G-20 covers gifts in the muni business (it has been aligned upward with FINRA's change; confirm its current effective date), and G-37 limits a municipal finance professional's political contributions to $250 per election, only to officials the professional can vote for; a violation triggers a 2-year ban on negotiated muni business with that issuer.

Communications with the public (Rule 2210)

FINRA classifies written (including electronic) communications by audience, and the audience decides the supervision. A retail investor is anyone who is not an institutional investor, whether or not they have an account with the firm, so a prospect list counts toward the 25.

CategoryDefinitionSupervision
Retail communicationDistributed or made available to more than 25 retail investors within any 30-day period (ads, websites, static social media posts, mass emails)Principal approval before use; some types filed with FINRA
CorrespondenceSent to 25 or fewer retail investors within 30 days (individual emails, letters, texts)No pre-approval; post-review under written supervisory procedures
Institutional communicationDistributed only to institutional investors (banks, insurers, investment companies, entities with at least $50 million in assets, other members)Written supervisory procedures; no pre-approval

A new member must file its retail communications with FINRA at least 10 business days before first use during its first year. Content standards apply to every category: fair and balanced; risks shown with benefits; no false, exaggerated, unwarranted, promissory or misleading statements; no predictions or projections of performance (narrow exceptions such as hypothetical illustrations of mathematical principles); the firm's name must appear. Interactive social media (a live chat) is supervised like a public appearance, while a static profile or post is a retail communication. Communications are retained for 3 years.

Some items are not communications in the approval sense. A live seminar, radio interview or podcast is a public appearance: no pre-approval of what the rep says, but the firm's procedures govern it, and any slides or scripts handed out are retail communications. Research reports have their own rule (Rule 2241) with conflict disclosures and restrictions on analyst compensation.

Cold calling (Rule 3230 and the TCPA)

Calls to a residence only between 8 a.m. and 9 p.m. local time of the called party. The caller gives their name, the firm's name, a phone number or address, and states that the call is to solicit an investment. A customer who asks to be put on the firm's do-not-call list must be honored for 5 years. Numbers on the national do-not-call registry cannot be called absent an exception such as an established business relationship or the person's prior express consent.

Insider trading

Material non-public information (MNPI) is information a reasonable investor would consider important and that has not been disseminated to the public: an unannounced merger, earnings, a failed drug trial. Trading on it, or passing it on, violates the antifraud rules (Exchange Act Section 10(b) and Rule 10b-5).

Insider (tipper) --(MNPI, for a benefit)--> Tippee --> trades
  tipper liable even without trading
  tippee liable if they knew or should have known
Misappropriation: outsider (lawyer, printer, banker)
  steals MNPI from its source --> trades --> liable

Penalties under ITSFEA (Insider Trading and Securities Fraud Enforcement Act of 1988): a civil penalty of up to 3 times the profit gained or loss avoided, on top of disgorging the profit itself; criminal penalties include prison of up to 20 years. Firms and supervisors who fail to prevent it face controlling-person liability, and investors who traded at the same time can sue. That is why broker-dealers must maintain written policies to prevent misuse of MNPI: information barriers between banking, research and trading, a watch list (quietly monitored names) and a restricted list (names in which solicited trading or research is blocked).

Worked example — ITSFEA penalty computation
  1. An insider buys 10,000 shares at $20 before a takeover announcement and sells at $32. Profit gained = 10,000 × $12 = $120,000.
  2. Maximum civil penalty = 3 × $120,000 = $360,000, plus disgorgement of the $120,000.
  3. Another insider sells 5,000 shares at $50 before bad news; the stock falls to $38. Loss avoided = 5,000 × $12 = $60,000 → maximum civil penalty $180,000.

Market manipulation

PracticeWhat it is
Front runningTrading for yourself or the firm ahead of a customer block order you know about (including trading options on the stock), to profit from its price impact
Trading ahead of researchPositioning before the firm publishes a research change
Marking the closeTrading at or near the close to move the closing price (for fund valuations, option expiry, bonuses)
Painting the tape / wash trades / matched ordersTrades with no change in beneficial ownership, or pre-arranged between colluders, to fake volume and interest
Spoofing / layeringPosting orders you intend to cancel, to push the price, then trading on the other side
Pump and dumpHyping a thin stock with false claims, then selling into the demand
Capping / peggingTrading to hold a stock below or at a level (e.g., around an option strike near expiration)
Backing awayA market maker refusing to honor its firm quote
Spreading rumoursCirculating false information to move a price
Worked example — why front running pays, and why it is fraud
  1. A pension fund gives the desk an order to buy 500,000 shares of XYZ, now $50.00. A block that size will push the price up.
  2. A trader who knows about it buys 20,000 XYZ for his own account at $50.00 first (or does BUY CALL on XYZ).
  3. The block is worked and lifts XYZ to $50.40. The trader sells at $50.40: 20,000 × $0.40 = $8,000 of profit.
  4. Every share he bought first was a share the customer then paid more for. The profit came out of the customer's execution: the firm used the customer's own information against him.

A related customer-protection rule (Rule 5320) forbids a firm holding a customer's limit order from trading for its own account at a price that would satisfy that order without first filling the customer. The customer's order comes first.

Customer abuses

AbuseThe rule
ChurningExcessive trading, measured against the customer's profile, to generate commissions; a quantitative-suitability failure
Unauthorized tradingTrading without the customer's permission or without written discretion
Sharing in accountsAllowed only with written approval of the firm and the customer and sharing in proportion to the rep's contribution; accounts of immediate family are exempt from the proportional requirement
Guarantees against lossProhibited outright, including performance guarantees and personal promises to buy the stock back at a set price
Borrowing from / lending to customersOnly if the firm's written procedures permit it and the customer is immediate family, a lending institution, another registered person at the firm, or has a personal or business relationship outside the brokerage (Rule 3240)
InterpositioningPutting an unnecessary third party between the customer and the best market, so the customer gets a worse price
FreeridingIn a cash account: selling before paying (90-day freeze). In IPOs: withholding hot new-issue shares for yourself or restricted persons
ComminglingMixing customer funds or securities with the firm's or the rep's own
Selling dividendsUrging a purchase just to "get the dividend", which the price drop on the ex-date offsets
Breakpoint salesSelling fund shares just below a sales-charge discount level

Churning has no numeric trigger in the rules. Regulators look at control (the rep effectively decides the trades), turnover relative to the customer's objectives, and how much of the account the costs eat, which is why it is framed as a quantitative-suitability failure rather than a bright-line count.

The guarantee rule catches the friendly version: a rep who says "if it drops below $90, I'll buy the shares from you myself" has made a prohibited guarantee, whether or not he ever pays.

Records retention: the ladder

SEC Rules 17a-3 and 17a-4 require broker-dealers to make and keep records, the first 2 years in an easily accessible place. The logic loosely follows importance: transaction-level paper for 3 years, the firm's core books for 6, the firm's legal existence for its whole life.

PeriodRecords
3 yearsU4/U5 and fingerprint records (after the person leaves), order tickets, confirmations, account statements, communications, trial balances
4 yearsCustomer complaints (FINRA Rule 4513)
5 yearsCTRs, SARs, CIP records (after the account closes)
6 yearsBlotters, general and customer ledgers, customer account records (after the account closes), MSRB complaint records
Life of the firmArticles of incorporation, partnership agreements, minute books, stock certificate books

Just memorize The rungs are arbitrary. A usable hook: 3 = paper trail of each trade and message; 4 = complaints; 5 = the anti-money-laundering file; 6 = the books and the customer's master record; life = the firm's birth certificate.

Understand
  • The firm files the U4 at association (with fingerprints) and amends it within 30 days; it files the U5 within 30 days of termination.
  • SIE lasts 4 years; qualifications lapse 2 years after termination unless MQP extends them to up to 5; FINRA keeps jurisdiction 2 years.
  • Regulatory Element CE annually by December 31; Firm Element is the firm's own annual training.
  • Statutory disqualification (felony or certain misdemeanors within 10 years, bars, injunctions) blocks association; Rule 4530 events are reported within 30 days.
  • Retail communications need principal pre-approval; correspondence gets post-review; institutional communications follow procedures.
  • Insider trading covers tipper and tippee; ITSFEA civil penalty up to 3x; information barriers are required. Front running, marking the close, painting the tape and spoofing are manipulation.
  • Churning, unauthorized trades, guarantees, improper sharing, borrowing and lending, interpositioning and freeriding are customer abuses.
  • OBAs need prior written notice; compensated PSTs need approval and supervision; gifts are capped at $300 per person per year.
MEMORIZE:
  • U4 amendments → within 30 days (10 days for SD events)
  • U5 → within 30 days of termination, copy to the individual
  • SIE → valid 4 years
  • Qualification lapse → 2 years after termination (MQP up to 5)
  • FINRA jurisdiction → 2 years after termination
  • Regulatory Element → annually by December 31
  • Gift limit → $300 per person per year (effective 2026-03-30; was $100)
  • Retail communication → more than 25 retail investors in 30 days
  • Correspondence → 25 or fewer retail investors in 30 days
  • New member filing → 10 business days before first use, first year
  • ITSFEA civil penalty → up to 3x profit gained or loss avoided; prison up to 20 years
  • Records → blotters/ledgers 6 years; complaints 4; AML 5; most other records 3; articles and minute books for life
  • Do-not-call → 8 a.m. to 9 p.m. local time of the called party; firm DNC list 5 years
  • G-37 → $250 per election; 2-year ban
Exam traps
  • "A rep may share in a customer's account with the customer's permission" → Sharing requires firm and customer approval AND proportional sharing (immediate family excepted).
  • "A guarantee is allowed if approved by a principal" → Guaranteeing a customer against loss is prohibited outright.
  • "A PST for compensation needs only notice" → It must be approved AND supervised by the firm.
  • "An OBA requires the firm's approval" → It requires prior written NOTICE, not approval (the firm may still restrict it).
  • "The tipper is safe if he never traded" → The tipper can be liable even without trading.
  • "Emails to a few clients need pre-approval" → Correspondence does not need pre-approval; retail communications do.
  • "The rep files the U5" → The firm files it.
  • "The gift limit is $100" → That was the rule until March 30, 2026; it is now $300.
Trader connection

Front-running and insider rules are why a trading floor is walled off from investment banking and research: a trader who can see a pending deal or a coming downgrade cannot be allowed to position ahead of it, so the firm builds information barriers, keeps watch and restricted lists, and brings people "over the wall" only with compliance logging it. Manipulation rules shape algorithm design: an algo that posts and cancels layers of orders it never intends to fill is spoofing, so order-to-trade ratios, cancel patterns and close-of-day activity are all under automated surveillance, and designers must be able to show each order was placed with genuine intent to trade. The 3x ITSFEA penalty, the U5 that follows you forever, and a firm's 2-year reach after you leave are the incentives that make the walls hold.

Check yourself

Q1 A registered representative resigns from her firm on March 1. Which statement is FALSE?
A. The firm must file a Form U5 within 30 days
B. Unless she uses the MQP, her qualification lapses if she is not re-registered within 2 years
C. FINRA retains jurisdiction over her for 2 years
D. She must file the Form U5 herself within 30 days

Answer: D. The firm, not the individual, files the U5 (and gives her a copy). A, B and C are all true: 30 days to file, 2-year qualification window (up to 5 under MQP), 2-year FINRA jurisdiction.

Q2 An officer buys 10,000 shares at $20 on inside information about a takeover and sells them at $32 after the announcement. What is the maximum ITSFEA civil penalty?
A. $120,000
B. $360,000
C. $240,000
D. $480,000

Answer: B. Profit = 10,000 × ($32 − $20) = $120,000; the civil penalty is up to 3 × $120,000 = $360,000. A is the profit itself (disgorged separately); D adds disgorgement to the penalty; C uses 2x.

Q3 Over two weeks a rep emails the same market commentary to 40 retail customers. How is it classified and supervised?
A. Retail communication; principal approval before use
B. Correspondence; post-use review
C. Institutional communication; no pre-approval
D. Retail communication; filed with FINRA within 10 days after first use

Answer: A. More than 25 retail investors within 30 days makes it a retail communication, which needs principal approval before use. It would be correspondence only at 25 or fewer (B); the recipients are retail, not institutional (C); D confuses the new-member filing rule, which is 10 business days before use.

Q4 All of the following may be permitted under FINRA rules EXCEPT:
A. Sharing in a customer's account with written firm and customer approval, in proportion to the rep's contribution
B. Borrowing from a customer that is a bank, under the firm's written procedures
C. Guaranteeing a customer against loss, with principal approval
D. A compensated private securities transaction approved and supervised by the firm

Answer: C. Guarantees against loss are prohibited outright; no approval cures them. A (Rule 2150), B (Rule 3240, lending institution) and D (Rule 3280) are all permitted when their conditions are met.

Q5 Which records must a broker-dealer keep for 6 years?
I. Blotters
II. Customer account records, after the account closes
III. Order tickets
IV. Written customer complaints
A. I and III
B. I and II
C. II and IV
D. III and IV

Answer: B. Blotters, ledgers and customer account records are 6-year records. Order tickets are 3-year records (III) and customer complaints 4 years (IV), eliminating A, C and D.

Q6 A rep plans to teach a paid evening finance course at a community college. What is required?
A. The firm's prior written approval
B. Nothing, because it is outside business hours
C. Notice to FINRA
D. Prior written notice to the firm

Answer: D. Paid outside work is an outside business activity under Rule 3270: prior written notice to the member firm, which can then restrict it. Approval (A) is the standard for a compensated PST, not an OBA; the hours are irrelevant (B); FINRA is not notified directly (C).

Q7 Which of the following would violate FINRA's gift rule?
A. Sending a client's portfolio manager $400 of wine as a holiday gift
B. Hosting a client's trader at a ball game and attending with him
C. Giving a client a $50 pen engraved with the firm's logo
D. Sending a $75 bottle of wine to a client's analyst and recording it

Answer: A. $400 exceeds the $300-per-person annual gift limit (and the old $100 limit). B is business entertainment with the rep present, not a gift; C and D are under the limit, and D is recorded as required.

Q8 A trader enters large buy orders he intends to cancel, to push the price up, then sells stock into the rise. This is:
A. Front running
B. Marking the close
C. Spoofing
D. Interpositioning

Answer: C. Posting non-bona-fide orders to move the price and trading on the other side is spoofing (layering when stacked at several prices). Front running (A) trades ahead of a known customer order; marking the close (B) targets the closing price; interpositioning (D) inserts an unnecessary intermediary into a customer trade.

Part II · Exam week

Days 24–25 · Weak-area review and the calculation gauntlet

All four FINRA sections · pairs with the Day 24 and Day 25 editions (~100 min each)

By day 24 the tracker knows where you are weak, and the editions will re-teach those topics from a new angle. This chapter holds what does not change: every calculation the SIE can ask, in one place, each with the reasoning and a worked number. The SIE has few calculations, but the ones it has are free points if the method is automatic.

How to run a weak-area day

Open the weakness tracker in the edition. Take the Very Weak topics first, then Weak, Sections 2 and 3 before 1 and 4 (they are 75% of the exam). For each topic: re-read its chapter's Understand box and MEMORIZE box, rewrite every error-log row for that topic in your own words (the rule, not the question), then do the chapter's Check-yourself questions cold. A topic is fixed when you can explain why each wrong answer is wrong, not when you recognise the right one.

Bonds

Current yield = Annual interest ÷ Market price

Annual interest
coupon rate × par ($1,000 unless stated)
Market price
what the bond trades for now, in dollars
Worked example
  1. 6% bond trading at 95 → price $950, interest $60.
  2. CY = 60 ÷ 950 = 6.32%. Above the 6% coupon because you paid less than par.

Price from a quote

Corporate / muni dollar bond
percent of par in eighths: 86 5/8 → 86.625% × $1,000 = $866.25; 1 point = $10; 1/8 = $1.25
Treasury note / bond
32nds: 106-16 → 106 + 16/32 = 106.5% → $1,065.00; 1/32 = $0.3125. A customer buys at the ASK (the higher number) and sells at the BID.
Basis point
0.01% of yield; 100 bp = 1%

Approximate YTM = [C + (Par − P) ÷ n] ÷ [(Par + P) ÷ 2]

C
annual coupon in dollars
Par
$1,000
P
price paid
n
years to maturity (use years to call for YTC, and the call price in place of par)
Worked example
  1. 6%, 10 years, bought at 90 ($900).
  2. Discount earned per year = (1,000 − 900) ÷ 10 = $10.
  3. YTM ≈ (60 + 10) ÷ ((1,000 + 900) ÷ 2) = 70 ÷ 950 = 7.37%.
  4. Order: coupon 6.00% < CY 6.67% < YTM 7.37% < YTC (higher still — the $100 discount is earned faster if called early). Discount bond: YTC > YTM > CY > coupon.

Accrued interest = Par × Coupon × Days ÷ Day-count basis

Days
from the last coupon date up to, but not including, settlement date
Basis
corporate and muni 30/360 (every month = 30 days); Treasuries actual/actual
Worked example
  1. $1,000 6% corporate, coupons Jan 1/Jul 1, settles March 16.
  2. 30/360 days: January 30 + February 30 + March 1–15 = 15 → 75 days.
  3. AI = 1,000 × 0.06 × 75/360 = $12.50, paid by the buyer to the seller on top of the price.

Call premium = Call price − Par · Call protection = years to the FIRST call date

Call price
callable at 105 → $1,050 → call premium $50
Call protection
callable in 10 years at 108, 14 at 105, 18 at 103 → 10 years of protection

Conversion ratio = Par ÷ Conversion price · Parity stock price = Bond price ÷ Ratio

Conversion price
the stock price at which the bond converts
Parity
the price at which bond and shares are worth the same
Worked example
  1. Convertible at $40 → ratio 1,000 ÷ 40 = 25 shares.
  2. Bond trades at 110 ($1,100) → parity stock price = 1,100 ÷ 25 = $44.
  3. Stock at $46 is above parity: converting and selling beats selling the bond (an arbitrage signal).

Tax-equivalent yield = Muni yield ÷ (1 − Tax rate)

Tax rate
the investor's marginal federal rate
Reverse
muni-equivalent yield = taxable yield × (1 − tax rate)
Worked example
  1. 4% muni, 35% bracket → 4 ÷ 0.65 = 6.15%.
  2. A 6% corporate is worth 6 × 0.65 = 3.90% after tax → the muni wins for this investor.

Equities and funds

Split: New shares = Old shares × (New ÷ Old) · New price = Old price × (Old ÷ New)

New ÷ Old
2:1 → 2; 3:2 → 1.5; 1:5 reverse → 0.2
Worked example
  1. 400 shares at $100, 2:1 split → 800 shares at $50. Value unchanged at $40,000.
  2. 10% stock dividend on 400 shares at $55 → 440 shares at $50. Value unchanged.
  3. 1-for-5 reverse on 1,000 shares at $2 → 200 shares at $10.

Cumulative voting power = Shares × Seats up for election

Statutory
shares per seat, one seat at a time; cumulative lets the total be stacked on one candidate

Sales charge % = (POP − NAV) ÷ POP · POP = NAV ÷ (1 − Sales charge %)

POP
public offering price, what the buyer pays
NAV
net asset value per share, what the fund redeems at
Worked example
  1. NAV $9.50, POP $10.00 → (10 − 9.50) ÷ 10 = 5.0% (of POP, not NAV — 0.50 ÷ 9.50 = 5.26% is the trap).
  2. NAV $20, 5.75% load → POP = 20 ÷ 0.9425 = $21.22.

Premium/discount to NAV = (Price − NAV) ÷ NAV

Applies to
closed-end funds and ETFs, which trade on exchanges; open-end funds always trade at NAV (plus any load)

Dividend yield = Annual dividend ÷ Price · Preferred arrears = Missed dividends per share × Shares

Cumulative preferred
all arrears plus the current dividend must be paid before any common dividend

Options

PositionViewBreakevenMax gainMax loss
BUY CALLBullishStrike + premiumUnlimitedPremium
SELL CALL (naked)Bearish / neutralStrike + premiumPremiumUnlimited
BUY PUTBearishStrike − premiumStrike − premiumPremium
SELL PUTBullish / neutralStrike − premiumPremiumStrike − premium
Long stock + BUY PUTBullish, insuredStock cost + premiumUnlimitedStock cost + premium − strike
Long stock + SELL CALL (covered)Neutral, incomeStock cost − premiumStrike − stock cost + premiumStock cost − premium
Short stock + BUY CALLBearish, insuredShort price − premiumShort price − premiumStrike − short price + premium
Worked example — short stock protected by a call
  1. Short 100 at $52, BUY 1 55 call at $3.
  2. Max gain: stock to zero → $52 − $3 = $49 × 100 = $4,900.
  3. Max loss: stock above $55 → you buy at $55 via the call: $55 − $52 + $3 = $6 × 100 = $600.
  4. Breakeven: $52 − $3 = $49.

Intrinsic: call = max(0, S − K) · put = max(0, K − S) · Time value = Premium − Intrinsic

S
stock price
K
strike price

Margin

Long: LMV − DR = EQ · Short: SMV + EQ = CR

LMV
long market value (moves with the market)
DR
debit balance, the loan (fixed except for interest and new activity)
EQ
equity, the customer's stake
SMV
short market value
CR
credit balance = short proceeds + Reg T deposit (fixed)
Worked example — long
  1. Buy 1,000 at $50 = $50,000. Reg T 50% → deposit $25,000, DR $25,000.
  2. Maintenance call when EQ < 25% of LMV → LMV = DR ÷ 0.75 = $33,333 → $33.33 a share.
  3. Price rises to $60: LMV 60,000 − DR 25,000 = EQ 35,000; Reg T requirement 30,000 → excess 5,000 goes to SMA → buying power 2 × 5,000 = $10,000.
Worked example — short
  1. Short 1,000 at $50: proceeds 50,000 + Reg T deposit 25,000 → CR $75,000.
  2. Maintenance 30% → call when SMV > CR ÷ 1.30 = $57,692 → $57.69 a share.

Tax and everything else

  • Capital gain = sale proceeds − cost basis; long-term if held more than one year (count from the day after trade date).
  • Wash sale: sell 100 shares at a $2,000 loss, rebuy 100 within 30 days at $40 → the loss is disallowed now and added to the new basis: $40 + $20 = $60 a share.
  • Net capital loss offsets ordinary income up to $3,000 a year; the rest carries forward.
  • Rule 144 volume = greater of 1% of shares outstanding or the average weekly volume over the prior four weeks, per 90 days (10M shares out, 80,000 avg weekly → 100,000 shares).
  • Markup % = (price to customer − prevailing market price) ÷ prevailing market price.
  • Underwriting spread = POP − price paid to the issuer = manager's fee + underwriting fee + selling concession (the largest piece).
  • Real rate ≈ nominal rate − inflation.

Days 26 and 28 · Practice exams: how to sit the SIE

All four FINRA sections · pairs with the Day 26 and Day 28 editions (75 questions, 105 minutes)

The practice exams copy the real one: 75 scored questions in the outline's proportions, mixed order, 105 minutes. Sit them properly — timed, no notes, every question answered — because the score only means something if the conditions match.

The exam you are sitting

SectionWeightScored questions
1 · Knowledge of Capital Markets16%12
2 · Understanding Products and Their Risks44%33
3 · Trading, Customer Accounts and Prohibited Activities31%23
4 · Overview of the Regulatory Framework9%7

The real exam adds 5 unscored pretest questions you cannot identify (cut from 10 in October 2025 — many books still say 85 questions in total). Passing is 70. There is no penalty for a wrong answer, so never leave one blank.

Pace

105 minutes for 80 items is about 78 seconds each. Take the first pass at roughly a minute a question, flag anything that needs arithmetic or a second read, and come back. By minute 60 you should be at question 50 or beyond.

Reading the stem

  • Find the actual question first. Scenario stems carry decoys (a customer's age, a phone number, a health status). Read the last sentence, then the scenario.
  • EXCEPT, NOT, FALSE, LEAST. Circle it mentally. Three answers are true; you want the odd one.
  • Roman numerals. Decide each numeral true/false once, then eliminate every answer containing a false one. Usually two eliminations finish it.
  • "Best" and "most likely". Two answers will be defensible. Choose the one that answers the question asked, for the customer described, under the rule that applies — not the one that is merely true.
  • Ordering questions ("lowest to highest") reverse the list you memorised. Write the sequence down before looking at the choices.
  • Time zones and dates. An answer can be right in Eastern time and shown in Central. A dividend question can hinge on cash vs regular-way settlement.

When you do not know

Eliminate what you can prove wrong, then prefer the answer consistent with the purpose of the rule: customer protection, disclosure, and preventing conflicts. Answers with absolute words ("always", "never", "guaranteed") are usually wrong unless the rule itself is absolute — guarantees against loss really are always prohibited.

After you finish

Send your letters to the bot as usual (SIE 26: …). The instant reply gives the score and section split; the next edition gives every miss with every distractor explained and changes the plan for the remaining days.

Day 27 · Reviewing an exam so the next one goes better

All four FINRA sections · pairs with the Day 27 edition (~100 min)

A practice exam is worth more after it is marked than while you sit it. The review is where you find out whether a miss was a rule you never learned, a concept you misunderstand, or a slip — because each needs a different fix.

Classify every miss

TypeWhat it looks likeThe fix
Rule-basedWrong number, form, deadline, agency or limitAdd it to your MEMORIZE list; drill it daily until day 29
ConceptualA reversed relationship, the right rule for the wrong account or productRe-read the chapter's teaching section and explain it aloud in a cause→effect chain
CarelessMisread EXCEPT, arithmetic slip, answered a different questionChange the habit: last sentence first, write the arithmetic down

The review routine

  1. Read the edition's section scores. Your weakest section gets the most time today; a section under 70% is the priority regardless of weight.
  2. For every miss, write one line: the rule in your own words. Not the question — the rule.
  3. For every miss, also say why the answer you chose was attractive. That is the trap you will meet again.
  4. Look at your correct guesses too. A right answer you could not defend is a miss that has not happened yet.
  5. Do the targeted drills in the edition, then re-sit the Check-yourself questions for the two weakest chapters in this guide.

Day 29 · The master memorization list

All four FINRA sections · pairs with the Day 29 edition (~90 min)

Every fact in the program that has to be memorized rather than reasoned out, in one place and grouped for speed. It is built from the same reference the daily editions and the answer keys use, so it agrees with them. Items marked Changed are rules that moved recently; many prep books still carry the old number.

Exam, registration and continuing education

MEMORIZE:
  • SIE format → 75 scored + 5 unscored questions, 1 hour 45 minutes, passing score 70 Changed (Many prep books still say 85 questions.)
  • SIE validity → 4 years from the exam date
  • SIE eligibility → Age 18+; no firm sponsorship required
  • Retest waiting periods → Historically 30 days after a 1st or 2nd failure, 180 days after 3 consecutive failures; FINRA shortened these by a rule change effective on filing 2026-06-29 with implementation date to be announced Changed (Verify current status before teaching; do not state new numbers from memory.)
  • Qualification lapse after leaving the industry → 2 years after termination; up to 5 years under the Maintaining Qualifications Program (annual CE + fee)
  • Regulatory Element CE → Annually, due by December 31 (since 2023); missing it = CE inactive
  • Form U4 → Filed by the firm when a person associates; fingerprints; amend within 30 days of learning of a change (10 days for events that create a statutory disqualification)
  • Form U5 → Filed by the firm within 30 days of termination; copy to the individual
  • Statutory disqualification → Includes any felony conviction within 10 years; certain misdemeanors (securities, fraud, theft) within 10 years; bars/expulsions; certain injunctions and willful violations
  • FINRA retained jurisdiction → 2 years after termination of registration

Conduct, communications and records

MEMORIZE:
  • Gift limit (FINRA Rule 3220) → $300 per person per year Changed (Was $100 from 1992 to 2026. Question banks may still show $100 — teach $300 and flag the old number.)
  • MSRB G-37 political contributions → $250 de minimis per election, only to officials the MFP can vote for; violation -> 2-year ban on negotiated business with that issuer
  • OBA (Rule 3270) → Prior written notice to the firm
  • PST (Rule 3280) → Prior written notice; if compensated, firm must approve and supervise as its own; if not, written acknowledgment
  • Customer complaints reporting → Rule 4530: specified events within 30 calendar days; quarterly statistical complaint data by the 15th of the month after the quarter
  • Borrowing/lending with customers → Only if the firm permits it in writing and the relationship qualifies (immediate family, lending institution, other registered person at same firm, personal or business relationship outside the brokerage)
  • Sharing in customer accounts → Needs written approval of the firm and the customer; sharing in proportion to contribution (immediate family accounts excepted)
  • Guarantees against loss → Prohibited
  • Insider trading penalties (ITSFEA 1988) → Civil penalty up to 3x the profit gained or loss avoided; criminal penalties include prison up to 20 years
  • Do-not-call → Calls only 8 a.m.-9 p.m. local time of the called party; firm-specific DNC request honored for 5 years
  • Record retention (SEA 17a-4) → 3 years: U4/U5/fingerprint records (after termination), order tickets, confirmations, statements, communications, trial balances. 4 years: customer complaints (FINRA 4513). 5 years: CTRs, SARs, CIP records (after account closing). 6 years: blotters, general and customer ledgers, customer account records (after closing), MSRB complaints. Life of the firm: articles of incorporation, partnership agreements, minute books, stock certificate books.
  • Retail communication → Any written communication to more than 25 retail investors within 30 days; principal approval before use
  • Correspondence → Written communication to 25 or fewer retail investors within 30 days; supervised by post-review under procedures
  • Institutional communication → Distributed only to institutional investors; supervised under procedures, no pre-approval required
  • New member filing → New members file retail communications with FINRA at least 10 business days before first use for their first year

Regulators and laws

MEMORIZE:
  • SIPC coverage → $500,000 per separate customer capacity, of which at most $250,000 cash; does not cover commodities/futures, currency, fixed annuities or market losses
  • FDIC coverage → $250,000 per depositor, per insured bank, per ownership category
  • MSRB → Created by the Securities Acts Amendments of 1975; writes muni rules; does NOT enforce (FINRA, SEC, bank regulators enforce)
  • FINRA → Formed 2007 from NASD and NYSE member regulation; SRO overseen by the SEC
  • NASAA → Association of state securities regulators; model rules; states enforce blue-sky laws (Uniform Securities Act)
  • Federal Reserve credit rules → Reg T (credit by broker-dealers); Reg U (banks lending on securities)
  • Securities Act of 1933 → Primary market: registration and disclosure of new issues, prospectus, antifraud
  • Securities Exchange Act of 1934 → Secondary market: created the SEC; registration of exchanges, BDs and reps; margin authority to the Fed; insider trading; proxies; short sales; net capital
  • Trust Indenture Act of 1939 → Corporate bonds offered publicly must be issued under an indenture with an independent trustee
  • Investment Company Act of 1940 → Regulates investment companies (mutual funds, closed-end funds, UITs, face-amount)
  • Investment Advisers Act of 1940 → Regulates investment advisers; fiduciary duty
  • Securities Investor Protection Act of 1970 → Created SIPC
  • Bank Secrecy Act (1970) → AML recordkeeping and reporting: CTRs, SARs
  • USA PATRIOT Act (2001) → Title III: AML programs and Customer Identification Programs for BDs

Accounts, KYC and AML

MEMORIZE:
  • CIP minimum information → Name, date of birth, address (residential/business, not a P.O. box for individuals), identification number (SSN/TIN for US persons)
  • CTR → Cash transactions over $10,000 in one business day (aggregated); filed within 15 days (FinCEN Form 112)
  • SAR (broker-dealer) → Suspicious transactions of $5,000 or more; filed within 30 calendar days of initial detection (up to 60 if no suspect identified); never tell the customer
  • SAR/CTR record retention → 5 years
  • Monetary instrument log → Cash purchases of monetary instruments of $3,000-$10,000
  • OFAC → Screen against the SDN list; block the property and report
  • AML independent test → Annually (every two years for firms that do not execute customer transactions)
  • Trusted contact person → Firm must make a reasonable effort to obtain; must be 18+; has no trading authority
  • Specified adult → Age 65+, or 18+ with a mental or physical impairment
  • Time-and-price discretion → Valid only for the business day it is granted unless the customer specifies otherwise in writing
  • Discretionary authority → Written authorization before the first discretionary order; principal approval; orders reviewed
  • Options account → ODD delivered at or before account approval; ROP approval; signed options agreement within 15 days after approval
  • UGMA/UTMA → One custodian, one minor; irrevocable gifts; minor's SSN; no margin; custodian cannot delegate
  • Death of account holder → Freeze the account; JTWROS passes to survivor; TIC share to decedent's estate; POA (even durable) ends at death

Trading, settlement and short sales

MEMORIZE:
  • Regular-way settlement → T+1 for stocks, corporate bonds, munis, options (and option exercise) and government securities (effective 2024-05-28 for equities/corps/munis)
  • Cash settlement → Same day as the trade
  • Ex-dividend date under T+1 → Same day as the record date
  • Mutual fund ex-date → Set by the fund, typically the business day after the record date
  • Confirmation delivery → At or before completion of the transaction (settlement)
  • Account statements → At least quarterly
  • Order guarantees → Market order = execution guarantee (no price); limit order = price guarantee (no execution)
  • Stop/limit placement → Buy limit below market; sell limit above; buy stop above; sell stop below
  • Time in force → Day; GTC; FOK (all, immediately, or cancel); IOC (fill what you can immediately, cancel rest); AON (all or none, not immediate)
  • Muni trade reporting → To MSRB RTRS within 15 minutes
  • Locate → Required before effecting a short sale
  • Short-sale fail close-out → By the start of regular trading on the business day after settlement date (S+1)
  • Alternative uptick rule → Triggered by a 10% decline from prior close; short sales then only above the national best bid for the rest of that day and the next
  • Order marking → Every sell order marked long, short or short exempt
  • Stock-loan collateral → Typically 102% of value for domestic securities (105% for foreign) — market convention, not an SIE rule
  • Manufactured dividend → Paid by the borrower to the lender; taxed as ordinary income, not a qualified dividend
  • Cash-settlement ex-date → The business day after the record date (cash trades settle same day)
  • Order ticket contents → Account, security, buy/sell (sells marked long/short), quantity, order type and price, time in force, solicited/unsolicited, discretion used; commission is on the confirmation

Margin

MEMORIZE:
  • Reg T initial margin → 50% for purchases and short sales (short: 150% of SMV held = proceeds + 50%)
  • FINRA minimum equity → $2,000 to open/extend credit (or 100% of purchase if less than $2,000); $2,000 min for short accounts
  • Maintenance → 25% of LMV long; 30% of SMV short (stocks >= $5)
  • Pattern day trader → Minimum equity $25,000; day-trading buying power up to 4x maintenance excess
  • Reg T payment period → Settlement cycle + 2 business days (T+3 under T+1)
  • Freeriding → Selling a cash-account purchase before paying for it -> account frozen 90 days (must pay in full before trade date)
  • Not marginable → New issues for 30 days after the offering; options with 9 months or less to expiration must be paid in full
  • Rehypothecation → BD may rehypothecate customer margin securities up to 140% of the customer's debit balance
  • Accounts that cannot use margin → IRAs and other retirement accounts; UGMA/UTMA custodial accounts; (also 529/Coverdell)
  • Margin disclosure → Margin disclosure statement at opening and annually

Equities, bonds and munis

MEMORIZE:
  • Bond point → 1 point = 1% of par = $10 per $1,000 bond; 1/8 point = $1.25
  • Treasury note/bond quotes → In 32nds: 1/32 = $0.3125 per $1,000
  • Basis point → 0.01%; 100 bp = 1%
  • Accrued interest day count → Corporate and muni 30/360; US government actual/actual; from last coupon date up to but not including settlement date
  • Premium bond ordering → Coupon > Current yield > YTM > YTC
  • Discount bond ordering → YTC > YTM > Current yield > Coupon
  • Investment grade → BBB-/Baa3 and above
  • T-bill maturities → 4, 6, 8, 13, 17, 26 and 52 weeks (plus occasional cash-management bills)
  • Notes vs bonds → Treasury notes 2-10 years; Treasury bonds 20 and 30 years
  • Commercial paper → Exempt from registration if maturity <= 270 days
  • GNMA → Only agency with explicit full-faith-and-credit backing; interest fully taxable
  • Treasury interest tax → Federally taxable, exempt from state and local tax
  • Tax-equivalent yield → Muni yield / (1 - marginal tax rate)
  • Triple tax-exempt → Munis from US territories (Puerto Rico, Guam, USVI) are exempt federal, state and local everywhere
  • Short-term notes → TAN (taxes), RAN (revenues), TRAN (both), BAN (future bond sale), GAN (grants), CLN (construction)
  • Muni capital gains → Taxable; only interest is tax-exempt
  • Muni quote conventions → Serial bonds (often GO) quoted on a yield (basis) basis; term bonds ('dollar bonds', often revenue) quoted as % of par
  • Municipal Finance Professional → Associated person primarily engaged in municipal underwriting, trading, sales to issuers, financial advisory, research, or who solicits municipal issuer business; an RR selling munis only to retail investors is not an MFP
  • Call protection vs call premium → Call protection = time until the first call date; call premium = call price above par (callable at 105 -> $50 per bond)
  • Commercial paper buyers → Typically $100,000+ denominations; bought by institutions and money-market funds, not small retail investors

Funds, annuities and alternatives

MEMORIZE:
  • Max mutual fund sales charge → 8.5% of POP
  • 12b-1 cap → 1% annually (0.75% distribution + 0.25% service); may call itself no-load only if <= 0.25%
  • Letter of intent → 13 months; may be backdated up to 90 days; shares held in escrow
  • Redemption payment → Within 7 calendar days
  • Diversified fund → 75-5-10: 75% of assets invested so that no more than 5% in any one issuer and no more than 10% of any issuer's voting stock
  • Independent directors → At least 40% of the board
  • Forward pricing → Orders execute at the next computed NAV
  • REIT → Distribute >= 90% of taxable income; 75% asset and income tests; dividends generally ordinary income
  • Fund objectives → Growth; income; growth & income (stocks); balanced (stocks + bonds); asset allocation; sector; index; bond; money market
  • Life with period certain → Payments for life, with a guaranteed minimum period paid to a beneficiary if the annuitant dies early
  • Immediate vs deferred purchase → Immediate annuity requires a single lump-sum premium; periodic purchase payments -> deferred annuity
  • DPP tax items → Real estate: depreciation, deductible interest, pass-through losses. Oil & gas: depletion (and intangible drilling costs)

Offerings

MEMORIZE:
  • Cooling-off period → At least 20 calendar days after filing (can be extended by SEC deficiency)
  • Aftermarket prospectus delivery → IPO: 25 days if listed, 90 if not; follow-on: 0 if listed, 40 if not
  • Accredited investor (individual) → Income > $200,000 ($300,000 joint) in each of last 2 years with expectation; or net worth > $1 million excluding primary residence; or holder of Series 7, 65 or 82
  • Reg D 506(b) → Unlimited accredited + up to 35 non-accredited sophisticated purchasers; no general solicitation
  • Reg D 506(c) → General solicitation allowed; all purchasers accredited with verification
  • Form D → Within 15 days after first sale
  • Rule 144 holding period → 6 months (reporting issuer), 1 year (non-reporting)
  • Rule 144 volume limit → Per 90 days: greater of 1% of outstanding shares or average weekly volume over prior 4 weeks
  • Form 144 → Required if sale > 5,000 shares or > $50,000 in 90 days
  • Rule 144A QIB → Owns/invests at least $100 million in securities
  • Reg A → Tier 1 up to $20 million, Tier 2 up to $75 million in 12 months
  • Reg CF → Up to $5 million in 12 months through a funding portal or BD
  • Tender offer → Must stay open at least 20 business days; 10 business days after a change in price or percentage sought
  • Schedule 13D → Within 5 business days after crossing 5% beneficial ownership (was 10 days before 2024) Changed (Older prep material says 10 days.)
  • Section 16 insiders → Officers, directors, 10%+ holders; Form 4 within 2 business days; short-swing profits within 6 months disgorged; may not sell short
  • IPO restricted persons (Rule 5130) → Broker-dealers and their personnel, and immediate family (spouse, parents, in-laws, siblings, children, anyone materially supported); grandparents, aunts/uncles and cousins are not restricted
  • Offering documents → Registered offering -> prospectus; Reg D private placement -> offering (private placement) memorandum; municipal -> official statement

Options

MEMORIZE:
  • Contract size → 100 shares
  • Exercise style → American: any time before expiration; European: only at expiration
  • Settlement → Trades and exercises settle T+1; index options settle in cash
  • Breakevens → Calls: strike + premium. Puts: strike - premium. (Same for buyer and writer)
  • Max loss/gain → BUY CALL: loss premium, gain unlimited. SELL CALL (naked): gain premium, loss unlimited. BUY PUT: loss premium, gain strike - premium. SELL PUT: gain premium, loss strike - premium
  • OCC → Issues and guarantees listed options; assigns exercise notices to BDs at random; BDs assign to customers randomly, FIFO or other fair method
  • Expiration timing → Standard equity options: expiration date is the third Friday of the month; last trading 4:00 p.m. ET; expire 11:59 p.m. ET

Retirement, education and tax

MEMORIZE:
  • IRA contribution limit → 2026: $7,500 + $1,100 catch-up at 50+ (2025: $7,000 + $1,000)
  • 401(k)/403(b)/gov 457 deferral → 2026: $24,500; catch-up at 50+ $8,000; ages 60-63 $11,250
  • IRA contribution deadline → Tax filing deadline (typically April 15) of the following year, no extensions
  • Early withdrawal penalty → 10% before age 59 1/2 (exceptions: death, disability, SEPP/72(t), first home up to $10,000 from IRAs, higher education from IRAs, etc.)
  • RMD age → 73 (75 for those born 1960 or later); first RMD by April 1 of the following year
  • Roth RMDs → None for the original owner (Roth IRAs; Roth 401(k)s since 2024)
  • Indirect rollover → Complete within 60 days; one IRA-to-IRA indirect rollover per 12 months; 20% mandatory withholding on plan distributions paid to the participant
  • 457 plans → No 10% early-withdrawal penalty on distributions after separation from service
  • ERISA → Covers private-sector plans only (not government plans)
  • Coverdell ESA → $2,000 per beneficiary per year total; contributions until beneficiary is 18; used by 30
  • 529 K-12 tuition → Up to $20,000 per year from 2026 (was $10,000) Changed (Older prep material says $10,000.)
  • 529 to Roth rollover → Lifetime $35,000; 529 open 15+ years; subject to annual Roth contribution limit
  • 529 gift-tax averaging → May front-load 5 years of annual exclusions (5 x $19,000 = $95,000 in 2026)
  • Gift tax annual exclusion → 2026: $19,000 per recipient
  • Long-term holding period → More than 1 year; holding period starts the day after trade date
  • Capital loss limit → Net capital losses offset up to $3,000 of ordinary income per year; excess carried forward indefinitely
  • Wash sale → Loss disallowed if substantially identical security bought within 30 days before or after the sale; disallowed loss added to new basis
  • Qualified dividend holding → More than 60 days in the 121-day window beginning 60 days before the ex-date
  • Inherited securities → Basis steps up to value at death; always long-term
  • Gifted securities → Donor's basis carries over (for gains)
  • Annuity withdrawals (nonqualified) → LIFO: earnings out first, taxed as ordinary income; 10% penalty before 59 1/2
  • Cash dividend tax event → The payable date (when received); exception: fund/REIT dividends declared Oct-Dec to holders of record then and paid by end of January are treated as received Dec 31

Economics

MEMORIZE:
  • Recession (exam convention) → Two consecutive quarters of declining real GDP; NBER makes the official determination
  • Leading indicators → Building permits, stock prices, new orders, average weekly initial jobless claims, average manufacturing workweek, consumer expectations, yield-curve spread
  • Coincident indicators → Nonfarm payrolls, industrial production, personal income less transfers, manufacturing and trade sales
  • Lagging indicators → Average duration of unemployment, CPI for services, prime rate, commercial and industrial loans outstanding, labor cost per unit of output, consumer credit-to-income, inventories-to-sales
  • Fed tools → Open-market operations (most used; FOMC), discount rate, reserve requirements (0% since March 2020), interest on reserve balances. Exam convention also lists changing Regulation T margin (unchanged at 50% since 1974)
  • FOMC → Meets 8 times a year
  • Rates ladder → Fed funds (overnight interbank reserves) < discount rate (Fed lending to banks) ; prime rate (banks' best corporate customers); broker call rate (bank loans to BDs, basis for margin interest)
  • Market structures → Monopoly (one seller, regulated, e.g. utilities); oligopoly (few sellers, high barriers, e.g. airlines); monopolistic competition (many differentiated sellers, e.g. cereal); perfect competition (identical products, price takers)

Still being verified

These are taught as concepts; the specifics are confirmed in the daily edition before they are tested: MSRB gift limit (Rule G-20); Temporary hold (Rule 2165); Stock split approval.

Day 30 · The one-page cheat sheet

All four FINRA sections · pairs with the Day 30 edition (final exam)

The highest-yield facts, relationships and traps, compressed for the last read before the exam. The Day 30 edition adds a version weighted to your own error log.

Market structure

  • Broker = agent, commission, no inventory. Dealer = principal, markup/markdown, inventory risk. Never both in one trade.
  • Primary market: issuer gets the cash. Secondary: investors trade with each other.
  • 1st: listed on exchange · 2nd: unlisted OTC · 3rd: listed traded OTC · 4th: institution to institution (ATS/ECN).
  • Market order = execution guarantee. Limit = price guarantee. Stop = trigger, then market. Buy limit/sell stop below the market; sell limit/buy stop above.
  • Regular way T+1 (stocks, corporates, munis, options, governments). Cash = same day. Ex-date = record date (regular way); cash ex-date = day after record.

Bonds

  • Price ↑ ⇒ yield ↓. Premium: coupon > CY > YTM > YTC. Discount: YTC > YTM > CY > coupon.
  • CY = annual interest ÷ price. 1 point = $10; 1/8 = $1.25; Treasury 1/32 = $0.3125; 1 bp = 0.01%.
  • Accrued: corp/muni 30/360, Treasury actual/actual; last coupon up to, not including, settlement; buyer pays seller.
  • Longest maturity + lowest coupon = most rate risk; zeros most of all, no reinvestment risk.
  • Liquidation: secured → unsecured/debentures → subordinated → preferred → common.
  • Call protection = years to first call. Call premium = call price − par. Call risk rises when rates fall.
  • CP and BAs ≤ 270 days → exempt. GNMA = explicit US backing, fully taxable. Treasuries: state-tax exempt.
  • TEY = muni yield ÷ (1 − rate). GO = taxing power, voter approval; revenue = user fees, feasibility study. BAN/TAN/RAN/TRAN/GAN. MSRB writes rules, never enforces.

Equity, funds, packaged products

  • Declaration → ex → record → payable. Dividends taxed when paid.
  • Rights: short, discount, one per share. Warrants: long, premium, sweetener.
  • Cumulative preferred: arrears first. Convertible: lower dividend. Callable: higher dividend. Par $100.
  • Sales charge % of POP; max 8.5%. 12b-1 max 1%; "no-load" ≤ 0.25%. LOI 13 months, backdate 90 days. Redeem within 7 days. A = front + breakpoints; B = CDSC; C = level load.
  • Custodian holds assets; transfer agent keeps shareholder records; adviser manages; board sets ex-dates.
  • REIT: 90% payout, no loss pass-through. DPP: losses pass through, passive only. Depletion = oil & gas.
  • Fixed annuity not a security; variable is. VA payout rises if returns > AIR. Earnings out first, ordinary income.

Options

  • BUY CALL/SELL PUT = bullish. BUY PUT/SELL CALL = bearish. Buyers: max loss = premium. Naked SELL CALL: unlimited loss.
  • Call BE = strike + premium; put BE = strike − premium. Intrinsic: call S − K, put K − S, never negative.
  • Protect long stock: BUY PUT. Protect short stock: BUY CALL. Covered call = income, capped upside.
  • ODD by account approval; agreement within 15 days; 100 shares; expire third Friday, 11:59 p.m. ET.

Margin and short sales

  • Reg T 50%; FINRA minimum $2,000; maintenance 25% long, 30% short; PDT $25,000.
  • LMV − DR = EQ; SMV + EQ = CR. Long call price = DR ÷ 0.75; short = CR ÷ 1.30.
  • No margin in IRAs or UTMA/UGMA. New issues not marginable for 30 days. Freeriding = 90-day freeze.
  • Locate before shorting; mark tickets long/short; Rule 201 after a 10% drop. Short seller pays the dividend.

Offerings

  • '33 Act = new issues; '34 = trading, created SEC. Cooling-off ≥ 20 days: IOIs only, no sales.
  • Firm commitment = principal, underwriter's risk. Best efforts = agency, issuer's risk.
  • Reg D: 506(b) 35 non-accredited, no solicitation; 506(c) solicitation, all accredited; offering memorandum. Rule 147 = intrastate. Reg A $20M/$75M. Reg CF $5M.
  • Rule 144: 6 months / 1 year; volume = greater of 1% or 4-week average; Form 144 > 5,000 shares or $50,000.
  • Accredited: $200k/$300k income or $1M net worth ex-home. 13D within 5 business days of 5%. Tender offer open 20 business days.
  • IPO restricted: member firms, employees, spouse, parents, in-laws, siblings, children, the materially supported.

Accounts, AML, retirement, tax

  • CIP: name, DOB, address, ID number. CTR > $10,000 cash. SAR ≥ $5,000, 30 days, never tip off. Structuring is a crime.
  • Discretion needs written authorization; time and price does not (that day only). POA ends at death. JTWROS → survivor; TIC → estate.
  • 2026: IRA $7,500 (+$1,100 at 50). 401(k) $24,500 (+$8,000; $11,250 at 60–63). Penalty 10% before 59½; disability waives penalty, not tax. RMD 73. Rollover 60 days, once per 12 months.
  • Coverdell $2,000, contribute to 18, use by 30. 529 K-12 up to $20,000 (2026). Gift exclusion $19,000.
  • Long-term > 1 year. Wash sale ±30 days, loss added to basis. $3,000 net loss vs income. Inherited = step-up.

Conduct and registration

  • U4 on association, amend within 30 days; U5 within 30 days of leaving; FINRA jurisdiction 2 years; licences lapse 2 years (MQP up to 5); SIE valid 4 years.
  • CE Regulatory Element every year by Dec 31. Gift limit $300 (was $100). G-37 $250.
  • Retail communication (> 25 retail in 30 days) needs principal pre-approval; correspondence post-review; institutional under procedures.
  • Records: 3 yrs (confirmations, statements, communications, U4/U5) · 4 (FINRA complaints) · 5 (CTR, SAR, CIP) · 6 (blotters, account records) · life (articles, minutes, partnership agreements).
  • Never: guarantee against loss, share without approval and proportion, churn, trade without authority, front-run, interposition, trade on MNPI (tipper and tippee both liable; civil penalty up to 3× profit).
  • OBA: prior written notice. PST for pay: firm approves and supervises.

Macro and risk

  • Fed: OMO (buy = ease), discount rate, reserve requirements, Reg T. Fiscal = Congress + President.
  • Leading: permits, stock prices, new orders, jobless claims. Coincident: payrolls, industrial production. Lagging: unemployment duration, CPI services, prime rate.
  • Inverted curve = short rates above long, a recession signal. Stronger dollar hurts exporters.
  • Diversification cuts unsystematic risk only. Regulatory = agency rules; legislative = new laws.
